I

WHAT IS MEANT BY PERSONALITY

/ yQUNDREDS of textbooks have been written, M m covering every period of domestic architecture, dX JL interior ornamentation and furnishing, from oldest Egyptian antiquities to latest modern obliquities. But the personality of a house, the quality that appeals not merely to our critical faculties but to our personal emotions, has been left out altogether — overlooked perhaps, or possibly taken for granted, as though a collection of valuable possessions must of themselves confer charm.

And yet charm is precisely what the majority of houses lack. We pass all sorts of adequately designed and neatly cared-for houses, much as we pass all sorts of neatly presentable and might-be-agreeable people on the street, without noticing them at all. Occasional houses attract attention pleasantly or unpleasantly, but rarely do we see a house of such appealing personality that we walk past it very, very slowly, trying to see as much of it as we can, and ardently wishing that we might see it all around, and inside, too.

The appealing personality of a house may be quite apart from its size or its value. It may belong to a structure of importance set in an equally important architectural entourage, or it may belong to a house in a row, or to a little cottage in a tiny patch of dooryard planting. Moreover, the test of its personality is not determined — yes or no — by the question of architectural correctness, but by the question of whether it has the qualities that enchant.

What are the qualities that enchant? This question is twin sister to the age-old "What is charm? Charm has

thousands of times been described as including beauty, taste, sympathy, appealing manner, and so on and so forth. The difficulty of definition begins in attempting to divide further the components of beauty, taste, sympathy and appealing manner so as to be satisfactory to you and to me and to everyone else. Happily, enchanting qualifications of a house are less difficult to determine, because they at least must follow certain definite and immutable laws of form, proportion and — ^most important of all — suitability to situation and to purpose.

SITUATION AND PURPOSE

The first requirement of architectural beauty is suitability to situation. A house should always seem to belong where it stands. If it looks forced upon an unwilling landscape, or if it is in any way antagonistic or uncomfortable because conspicuous or out of scale, then it fails in this first requirement.

hl§k-porticoed white-painted Colonial house on a windswept crag jutting out into the ocean is as unsuitable as an organdie dress worn out in a snowstorm. A low-flung building of massive boulders in a warm and tranquil valley is like a coonskin coat in July.

The house of boulders should stand on a ledge or summit of rock that supports its weight and absorbs its material. The Colonial house would be sympathetically placed on an elm-bordered street, or on its own wide lawn against the green of a wooded background.

The second requirement is suitability to purpose. Unless an intentional camouflage of an ugly utility, every structure, whether a monumental edifice or smallest outbuilding, should suggest the purpose for which it is intended. A prison or a bank should suggest impregnable strength; a dwelling should be suggestive of home. Moreover, the house which is to be your home— in short, your background— should unmistakably suggest you.

WHAT IS MEANT BY PERSONALITY

THE HOUSE THAT EXPRESSES YOU

Its personality should express your personality, just as every gesture you make — or fail to make — expresses your gay animation or your restraint, your old-fashioned conventions, your perplexing mystery, or your emancipated modernism — whichever characteristics are typically yours.

The house that does not express the individuality of its owner is like a dress shown on a wax figure. It may be a beautiful dress — may be a beautiful house — ^but neither is animated by a living personality.

In illustration of type suitable to each individuality, the preciousness of a coldly discriminating connoisseur would be utterly unsympathetic to the unconventional and exaggeration-loving modern to whom riotous confusion of noise and color is merely cheerful. And yet, just as certain women of outstanding fascination charm everyone with whom they come in contact, so also do certain houses exert a vivid appeal — which may be personal, or may not.

In other words, you can intellectually admire a house, exactly as you admire an exhibition of furniture or tapestries assembled in a museum, without in the least longing to live in like surroundings.

Strictly Period houses are wonderfully interesting to look at; but to live in, they are too suggestive of perpetual fancy dress to suit most of us. Furthermore, if it is one’s ambition to have a flawless fifteenth-century house, then one must be resigned to the use of no convenience invented since 1500 — no steam heat, no plumbing, no springs in furniture, no electric light, no modern range, china, glass, table implements, or even modern dress! — in other words, no evidence of contact with the modern world. Which raises the question of how far anachronism may be allowed to go. That one could find true allegiance to purity is rather doubtful. Remembering the wife in the Arabian Nights who never ate more than one grain of rice in the presence of her husband — but was served bountifully between meals — one fears that old chests

would be full of steam pipes, that sliding panels would disclose plenty of bathrooms, and that bouncing springs and downy rest mattresses would wait temptingly beneath fifteenth-century bed covers; and, moreover, that kitchens and pantries and dining-room as well as table-manners would in no detail be faithful to the uncouth barbarism of four hundred years ago.

To determine the type and size and detail of the house that expresses your personality it is necessary to ask a few personal questions.

First of all, how much are you affected by your surroundings? Are you so sensitive to beauty that ugliness is a physical hurt? Will you take any amount of trouble to "fix” whatever rooms you are to live in, no matter how transiently? Must you personally choose every object? Or are you content to believe, on being assured by a professing expert, that what you would never have chosen for yourself is appropriately beautiful? Or are you one of those persons who do not even bother to notice inanimate detail and who are in nowise impressed by it?

WHAT ARE YOUR TASTES?

What is your temperament? Are you conventional, or the reverse? Is your taste conservative, or has the novelty of the modern irresistible appeal? Does noise set your nerves on edge, or are you only happy with the radio tuned in on jazz? Does life express itself to you in the perpetual presence of a crowd of people? Do you want company for every meal? Is Home otherwise a place to dress in and go out from and to return to when there is no other where to go? Or do you like to stay at home with your family alone, and "never ask a cat to the house”?

What is your mode of life? How do you live? Simply, or in much style? "Simply” may mean plainly or it may mean utmost perfection possible on a small scale. "Much style may mean all put on for show and not necessarily well

WHAT IS MEANT BY PERSONALITY

done, or it may mean the utmost perfection attainable. The term ''utmost perfection” means sufficient implements for the varied requirements of complete equipment, and servants sufficient in number and expertness for the size of the house.

The requirements for utmost perfection are precisely the same whether a house have four rooms or a hundred. An establishment imperfect in quality is likewise the same whether unexactingly simple or like a gigantic and overdressed infant, bellowing its shortcomings to everyone but its owner.

It is impossible to overemphasize one qualifying fact: The house of unpretending simplicity attracts the criticism of no one — neither in its correctness of style nor in its manner of service or appointment. But the would-be owner of the house of pretentious style should take serious notice that the first rule of good taste demands not only that the house he lives in should be suitable to his requirements, but that all houses of outstanding importance demand conformity on their owners’ part to them.

Sweaters and day clothes at an eight or nine o’clock dinner at a table set with damask and great display of silver, in a classically Georgian or ornately French dining-room, are as completely out of the picture as formal evening clothes would be at a six o’clock supper on a farm or in a ranch house.

MATCHING YOUR BACKGROUND

Moreover, the exactions of suitability of upkeep would be equally opposed. But with a difierence in this: That in the simplest type of house one may live as beautifully as one pleases — meaning that one may have the most charming table, in the most charming room; also the most delicious food most perfectly served — but in a palace one must have all these requirements and all those prescribed for formality as well.

Therefore, unless you are prepared to conform to the requirements that formality exacts, don’t choose that type

TPIE PERSONALITY OF A HOUSE

of house. If you like to dine in your sports clothes, or pass dishes around from hand to hand, do what you please in your own home by all means, but at least choose your diningroom and your table-setting accordingly.

At the other extreme, a footman behind each chair in a primitive little cottage is not smart, but is in shockingly bad form.

In other words, it is entirely a question of properly matching your background. If you choose to live in a simple house you can have every equipment of luxury, but you must not olfend against the spirit of apparent simplicity. On the other hand, if you choose to live in a palace, not only must all your appointments be of suitable dignity and quality, but so also must you.

Strict observance of these requirements is limited to the two extremes. The great majority who live in average houses can do pretty much as they please, unless too closely approaching the limitations of the one extreme or the other.

In the average house you can be formal on occasions and informal on others. If you have not the equipment for formal service exacted of a great house, what of it? And who cares? The only point to care about is that what you do is done well according to the standards which are suitable to the way you choose to live.

THE FOUR ESSENTIAL REQUIREMENTS OF EVERY HOUSE

^ Every house must respond to four requirements; First, Its outer road or street appearance— that which is plainly on view to the public passing by. Second, its entrance hall and drawing-room of formal hospitality. Third, its intimate rooms satisfying to the needs of those who live in them.

Fourth, Its practical response to modern requirements of comfort.

Charming street appearance most often belongs to low, broad houses. An inordinately high-peaked roof is probably the original source of the slang term "high hat,” which is

WHAT IS MEANT BY PERSONALITY

reason enough for the preference of most of us for roofs of friendly lowness.

Houses that are high in proportion to width, especially if they have overnarrow, deeply set windows and solid front doors, are seldom as invitingly hospitable as those which are more nearly block square or, better still, irregularly rambling. But far more important than height or width or pleasing variety of outline are windows sufficient in number and size to suggest light, sunny rooms within.

Architects of predominatingly masculine taste are noted for their love of blank wall spaces, and usually entertain the opinion that whole walls of windows look like factories without and leave no space for furniture within. But, on the other hand, a house with tiny windows sparsely inserted looks to most women like nothing so much as a suit of armor with visor down. Tempting to live within? Not exactly!

WINDOWS ARE THE SMILES OF A HOUSE

If the windows are generous in outline, they give the same impression as the welcoming smiles of a friend. Of course, if they are out of proportion, so that they seem to yawn or grimace or make a fish-mouth, they are not pleasing at all.

The front door is always of utmost importance to the charm of a house. A front door of lovely design and perfect scale is like a beautiful hand held out in welcome. A hideously untidy door is like a dirty hand that is repellent to touch.

However, if the house is set back from the street its first approach is from its gate. Invariably inviting is a flowerbordered walk of brick or pebbles or flagstones outlined in grass. Unappealing is the concrete walk raised above the surrounding grass; bare-edged and hard as the runway between trains at a railroad terminal. And yet the ugly hardness of such a walk might easily be overcome by sinking it to the level of the grass, with box or privet or other bordering hedge.

On the exterior, color plays a minor part as compared with its importance in the interior. But light-colored houses, sand or cream or oyster or white or the pinkish tones of brick, are to most people more; appealing — perhaps because more cheerful — than houses of dark material.

The serviceable economy of a dark-stone or shingle or half-timbered house appeals strongly to the thrifty, and a house can be dark-colored and at the same time cheerfully beautiful — especially if it be English in type — but not otherwise so often nor so easily as can a light one.

By light is not meant golden oak combined with yellow baked brick — a color scheme especially associated with a certain type of so-called mansion of heavy fatness sitting stodgily on a plot of manicured grass neatly polka-dotted with beds patterned in magentas and scarlet. A color scheme as suitable as any, since this particular shade of oak and brick is notably defiant of garden beautification.

THE ROOM OF CHARM

Usually the first thing the average person notices on entering a room is color, and next invitingness of arrangement; next comfort, and details last. And to everyone

capable of esthetic appreciation, certain beautifications inevitably appeal.

Besides the beauty of color and sunlight and, if possible, flowers, the ideal living-room should have an open fire in wmter, and windows opening on a garden in summer. And there should be at least one long and deeply comfortable sofa drawn near to the fire for warmth when necessary, and near enough to a window for light; and there should be a number of properly fitting easy chairs— not those which assume the human frame to be a block of concrete three feet square! And there should be convenient tables at one’s elbow, with bells, books, magazines, cigarettes, lighters, ashtrays or wha^ver the owners or friends of the house are likely to use. There must also be perfect arrangement of light

FRONT DOOR OF AUTHOR’S MASSACHUSETTS HOUSE [PLATE 2]

A ROOM OF SUPREME PERSONALITY AND CHARM

WHAT IS MEANT BY PERSONALITY

at night to read by, whether lying on the sofa or sitting in any of the chairs. Light that is cut off a foot below one’s book or sewing, or that does not brightly illumine the field of a card table, is not good light. Lights of greatest comfort are standing ones with arms that reach over either chair or table. Table lamps that are intended for use, as well as for decoration, should have white or cream-lined shades that tip at will.

COMFORT - WHICH IS CHARM - AT REASONABLE COST

Every house of comfort must, of course, respond to practical requirements. It must, for instance, be snugly warm in cold weather and well ventilated and cool in summer. It must have windows that create a proper draft when open and that won’t rattle when closed. It must have shutters that won’t bang, roofs that won’t leak, and it must have chimneys that draw. There must be a convenient pantry and a pleasant kitchen with all appurtenances thereto.

And in every house, whether luxurious or simple, each room should have an abundance of light and air.

I have a personal obsession against little windows even in bathrooms. No architect has yet convinced me that the advertised location of the plumbing looks any better on the outside of a house than does a slit of a window from within! True luxury in the upstairs part of a house does not depend upon huge bedrooms and large bathrooms but upon huge closet space for the bedrooms and plenty of no matter how little bathrooms — with big windows!

And in contrast to the house of newly built comfort and ibeauty is the house of unnecessary ugliness. Dinginess, darkness, excess of bad ornamentation, hideosity of inconvenience, are faults that unresourceful people of moderate means accept with resignation in the belief that only a bottomless purse would be able to cope with them.

How many times does one hear a discouraged voice say, "Ugly old house, ugly old furniture — nothing can be done about it. We can’t possibly afford to buy all new!”

Sometimes the house is so completely bad there is nothing that can be done even with unreasonable outlay. But far more often than not, a small expenditure will help a lot or quite possibly can beautify completely. This does not mean that an effect of charm and beauty can be made with hideous furniture against hideous walls, but that the beauty-loving woman will contrive somehow to achieve an effect of inviting personality in her surroundings, even though she accomplish it with saw and chisel, a few cans of paint and some cotton print.

And fortunately the house of inviting personality is not necessarily the result of expenditure, nor always dependent upon intrinsic value of items. Certain types of perfection are far beyond the purse of most of us, it is true. But other types are most often a matter of personal knowledge and taste.

The unknowing person will rent a house that is hopelessly bad but m better apparent repair than another of lower rent that could with the difference saved in rent be put in equal repair and made, moreover, into a house of beauty.

Side by side, two chairs or carpets or tables or rolls of wall-paper are offered to two women. The one who has taste chooses this item and the one who is ignorant chooses the other. Quite often the good article is of less cost than the bad. And yet on every news-stand are magazines filled with much beauty also with some ugliness, it is true. And in every library or museum are books or models showing details of classic forms as well as those of succeeding styles or Periods— knowledge to be had for the asking. In fact, so replete, diverse and scholarly are these books, that the attempt to write one such as this would be presuming were it to pretend to be a work of history, or a guide to the prospective owner of a palace who can say to the best architect, landscape gardener, and art connoisseur, "Build me a beautiful

ru’ ^ house of supreme perfection, embellish it

and fill It with objects of virtu and value, knowingly chosen and placed.”

The object of this book of mine is merely to share the

WHAT IS meant by personality

practical every-day experience of a chronic house-builder who for more years than most of us care to count (beginning at the age of ten) has been unceasingly occupied with the embellishment of houses — whether outside, inside, temporarily or permanently.

Moreover, this continuous practise in building, rebuilding, furnishing or furbishing, has all been of the ordinary sort that comes within the province of the owner or tenant of the American house of average size. My intention, therefore, is to write frankly and from an essentially unprofessional point of view of a subject to which lifelong associations have brought me closest. And it is especially my intention not merely to emphasize the beauty which is achievable with inordinate expenditure (the one and only flaw in the best of the books and magazines) , but to point out as many as possible of the underlying principles by which home-loving people of refinement and taste are able to create an atmosphere of homelike charm with whatever they may have, and wherever they may be.

II

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

T’HE average person is apt to think of architecture as a purely technical subject — as specialized as the manufacture of steel or the practise of surgery. In its ecclesiastical and memorial aspects this is true, but in its domestic aspect it is of vital and personal concern to every house owner, and every hoping- to-be house owner, in the world; in fact, architecture might be considered as a fourth addition to the “three R’s”: reading, ’riting, ’rithmetic — and ’rchitecture! At all events it is not unreasonable to recommend that its elemental principles be included in the curriculum of every school for girls, even if other subjects have to be discarded to make room for it.

Latin and algebra are undoubtedly good training for the mind, but they are both of about as much practical use to most of us as a knowledge of hydraulic mining. A good stiff course in architecture could be quite as useful, calisthenically, to the mind as mathematics or Caesar! And yet, where millions of schoolgirls are obliged to study Latin and algebra, not one in a thousand of them has the least knowledge of the traditions of classic structure and form; not one in a hundred knows the first thing about the beautification of a house in accordance with the standards of good taste. But nearly every girl hopes some day to have a home of her own. She hopes to live in surroundings that are beautiful.

She might as well hope to be a concert pianist without ever having touched a piano, as to hope to achieve a beautiful house by means of instinct alone — unless “instinct” has been trained through lifelong precept and association. One is inclined to think of taste as a natural gift, and in a measure this is true, much as it is true of music. But in addition to

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

natural aptitude for either of these gifts, there must be persistent study and practise to acquire any satisfactory knowledge of either.

As it is impossible to say anything about the beauty of houses without mentioning the Classic Orders from which the standards of beauty have been derived; or to appraise the various styles that we choose for our own homes of to-day without reference to the Periods which have served to produce them, the remainder of this chapter will be devoted to the briefest possible outline of the ancestry of the modern American house.

ARCHITECTURAL HISTORY

Although architectural history begins properly with the Pyramids of Egypt, those gigantic triangles are of no concern to us. The sole example of Egyptian architecture in which we have personal interest is the rock-cut temple at Beni-Hassan, which is quite obviously a near relative,- if not the parent, of the Doric Order of Greece; otherwise there is no trace of relationship between Egypt and ourselves. Neither are our houses descended from Assyria, unless it was from the Assyrians that we learned to make bricks — just as glassmaking came from the Phenicians. And even though the excavations conducted since 1876 in Crete have revealed — especially in the great palace at Knossos — the possibility that the Golden Age of Greece may have owed much of its culture to an earlier Mycenaean civilization, it is with the civilization of the Greeks that we are personally concerned.

THE CLASSIC ORDERS OF GREECE

It is impossible to overemphasize the importance of Greek architecture, which had its beginning in 650 b.c., when the first Doric temple was built at Corinth, and which attained its highest perfection in the Age of Pericles and ended with the death of Alexander, 323 B.c.

The architectural ideals expressed by the ancient Greeks are those of aU the European nations who have come after them. The measurements of the Greek temples remain the standard of perfection; their smallest details are constantly made use of in every classic type of memorial or civic building — and likewise in all formal Georgian or simple Colonial houses of to-day. Doorways, porticos, embellishments of interiors, depend for their beauty upon the reproduction of classic motives and moldings, and above all upon the classic rules of proportion to which perfection is necessarily due.

The supreme beauty of Greek architecture was expressed in the temples of Athens; each temple was built in commemoration of victory, and dedicated to the glory of an especial god or goddess. In other words, they were intentionally built as offerings of beauty that should be manifest to all beholders. Embellishments of columns, and sculptures, were applied to the outside, .and to the rear and front equally.

In general outline the temples were much alike (see Fig. 1 ) : a fairly low and fairly small rectangular building set upon a platform, leaving a margin or porch with steps going up to it on all four sides; a colonnade at the outside edge of the platform, and a roof over all with gable ends at the front and back, leaving the sides straight and plain.

Very small temples had a porch at the front and back only, with a single row of columns along the edge of the

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

IONIC CORINTHIAN

steps. The great temples had a double row of columns at the front and back, and a single row at the sides. The different styles are determined by the design of the columns and the embellishment of the gable ends.

The Greek styles were limited to three — each in turn limited to the use of its own especially prescribed details of column and gable ornamentation. The details and properties of each comprise the design of each Order. The three Greek Orders are the Doric, the Ionic, and the Corinthian.

THE DORIC ORDER

The Doric was the earliest, the least ornate, and is the supreme expression of nobility, strength, and simplicity. To the Doric Order belongs the Parthenon, the most perfect example of classic architecture the world has ever known. The Doric column (see Fig. 2) is composed of a shaft that is without a base, but is embellished with twenty perpendicular "flutes” one-sixth of a diameter wide — grooves set close together, with sharp edges between. The column is eight diameters in height. Close to the top of the shaft are three small lines called fillets that look as though three strings had been wound around and tied until they cut in.

The tapering profile of the shaft curves slightly outward: this curve is called the entasis. The entasis is a notable detail of classic measurement — the design of a curve for the sole purpose of creating an illusion of straightness. The measurements of the Parthenon, seemingly a series of level planes and straight-sided right or acute angles, actually reveal a number of expertly computed curves. In a column that is skilfully designed, the existence of the entasis should not be perceptible to the eye, since it was created for the sole purpose of making the column appear straight. Were it actually a cylinder, it would look pinched in at the waist and broader at the top than at the bottom. The entasis of the Parthenon is three quarters of an inch on a shaft thirty- one feet high.

The reason why modern columns that bulge in the middle are bad is because they thwart the very purpose for which the entasis was created. In the same way, the entire superstructure which is supported by the columns appears to be a series of perfect horizontals, but it actually arches slightly. Were these horizontals truly level, they would appear to sag. But if you can perceive an upward curve, the design is at fault. (Curved horizontals are used only in very large buildings — rarely, if ever, in houses.)

The top embellishment of a column is called the capital. The Doric capital consists of a plain round cushion (echinus) — like a much flattened bead that is cut off above its widest part in order to hold securely a square flat block (abacus) on which the superstructure rests. The superstructure that is supported by columns is called the entablature.

The classic entablature consists of three members: A heavy beam — called the architrave — rests directly on the abacus of each column. Above the architrave comes the frieze, and above the frieze the cornice.

The Doric entablature (Fig. 5) had a perfectly plain architrave (beam) ; the frieze was ornamented with regularly spaced square blocks, each grooved with three vertical channels, called triglyphs. The spaces between the triglyphs, sometimes left plain, sometimes sculptured, were called

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

metopes. The cornice was very simple, consisting merely of horizontal facings of the eaves, the underside embellished with rectangular blocks, ornamented with evenly spaced projecting pegs, or pendant drops, or rounded nail heads. At the gable ends, the lower half of the cornice remained as a finish for the frieze; the upper half followed the angle of the roof, meeting at the peak and leaving a flattened triangular space — really the gable end — which is called a pediment. (This feature is the prototype of Colonial door and window embellishment.) The pediment of the Parthenon was gloriously ornamented with sculptures depicting the gods, goddesses and heroes of Greek mythology.

THE DORIC ORDER

Entablature: A — Pediment; B — Cornice; C — Frieze; D — Architrave. Capital: E — Abacus; F — Echinus. G — shaft.

THE IONIC ORDER

In the second Greek Order, called the Ionic (Fig. 3) the columns were more slender than those of the Doric. The shaft started from a base, and the capital — the outstanding feature — was in the spiraled form of a ram’s horns (called volutes) . The architrave was plain, but in three horizontal bands — like three shutter slats, each overlapping the one under it. The frieze was either plain or sculptured in a continuous band — ^not divided into squares as in the Doric Order. The cornice was elaborated with moldings consisting of rows of small square projecting blocks called dentils. The pediment was plain. An elaboration of the Ionic column was the caryatid or female figure. The spirals of the capital have more turns and there is also an ornamented necking. The most notable example of this beautiful variation of the Ionic

form is that of the porch of the Erechtheum, a temple on the Acropolis at Athens (Fig. 6) .

THE CORINTHIAN ORDER

The third Greek Order, which belongs more typically to Rome than to Greece, is the Corinthian (Fig. 4) . The capital of the Corinthian column is very ornate, and consists of acanthus leaves arranged like encircling ostrich plumes with backs to the column and tips curling upward and outward. At each corner above the acanthus foliation are four diagonally placed double-faced Ionic volutes, supporting a four-cornered abacus like four concave quarter-circlets brought together at the corners. The architrave and frieze are plain; but the cornice — in sympathy with the highly ornamented capital — is greatly enlarged and elaborated.

THE FIVE CLASSIC ORDERS OF ROME

In addition to the three Orders of Greece, classic architecture includes the five Orders of Rome. These eight Orders taken together comprise what are known as the Classic Orders.

In the beginning, the Romans were rather like the owners of an unplowed field who hear everyone continually praising the perfect garden of a neighbor. Fooking over the fence and perceiving the beauty of Athens, they determined to turn their field into a garden that should be still more beautiful — at least, in effect. Beauty for mere beauty’s sake concerned them very little. What they really wanted was to attract the attention of the world from Athens to Rome. So the Romans simply appropriated the Orders of Greece and adapted them to their own use — without really creating anything new. Even the two so-called supplementary Roman orders, which they added to the Doric, Ionic, and Corinthian,

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

were merely rearrangements of the beautiful Greek originals.

They added a base to the Doric shaft, omitted the .flutes, and called it Tuscan (see Fig. 7) . The Roman Doric (Fig. 8) was a slenderized Greek Doric with a base. The Roman Ionic (Fig. 9) was composed of the four-sided diagonal volutes of the Greek Corinthian capital. The Greek Ionic scrolls or volutes faced directly front and back and curled round like a slender-waisted bolster at the sides. The Romans preferred the four diagonal volutes that projected from the four cor-

E

Fig- 7 TUSCAN COLUMN

ROMAN ROMAN

IONIC CORINTHIAN

ners, so that the scrolls of the ram’s horns curved from the sides as well as from the front and back views.

But the especial interest of the Romans was the Corinthian order, which — as their conquests brought them wealth and their love of magnificence increased — they greatly elaborated (Fig. 10). The increased height and width of the cornice was supported at intervals with ornamented S-shaped brackets called consoles.

Their fifth and not very admirable Order, known as the Composite, was produced by combining the Corinthian acanthus leaves with an enlargement of the Ionic volutes above them (Fig. 11).

OUR INDEBTEDNESS TO ROME

But if the Romans contributed little to the Orders, they contributed other details, to which we of to-day are far more indebted — from a practical standpoint — than to those of the Greeks. The Greeks showed us to what supreme height the beauty of architecture can be carried : without the Greek examples we should be without the standard by which to measure perfection. But the Romans gave us all our modern practical methods of building. The Greeks, in other words, were Olympians; the Romans were merely humans — like ourselves. Moreover, Rome gave us many details of greatest modern beauty, such as the perfected arch, the vaulted ceiling, and the arcade. We also owe to Rome the use of concrete, and the present-day method of veneering the face of a building with thin slabs of marble or stone, giving the appearance of a structure of solid blocks. We also owe to Rome many other ingenious methods of getting effects as quickly and as cheaply as possible. It is supposed that we owe the invention of bathtubs to the Romans; perhaps we owe to them our mania for building bathtubs in quantity, but it was the Chaldeans who built them first.

WHAT ONE PEOPLE REFUSED, ANOTHER ACCEPTED

The acceptances and rejections of various forms and methods by each succeeding Period are seemingly haphazard. For instance, the Egyptians, who — so far as is known — created the Doric column and lintel, discarded it completely. The Greeks took it, perfected it, exalted it, and through them it became the ancestor of Classic architecture. The Egyptian column never left North Africa. The Greeks in turn disdained the arch and cared little for the overelaborate Corinthian Order. The Romans elaborated the Corinthian Order to its utmost, and developed that lovely feature of modern embellishment, the pilaster — a square flat column set against the face of a wall for decorative purpose only.

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

Fundamentally, the inspirations of the Greeks and the Romans were diametrically opposed. The Romans loved lavish display ; they cared for effect more than for perfection. They discovered concrete, and with it put up buildings with Aladdinlike rapidity. They carried through gigantic programs for the embellishment of Rome on a scale impossible

ROMAN ARCH TRIUMPHAL

to the Greeks, who continued to build block-on-block, with marble.

OURSELVES OF LONG AGO

The Romans were absolutely the prototype of the Americans of to-day. They were energetic, ambitious, practical, inventive. They built baths everywhere. The arch had been rejected by the Greeks, because the Greek ideal was repose, and "the arch never sleeps.” Rome glorified the arch. Rome did not want to sleepi In fact, it invented the modern slogan, "We never sleep!” Rome had no time for idle dreaming, nor for wasting thought on old-fashioned ideals, which were hampering to enterprise; the Greeks sought beauty above everything — and their ideal of beauty was truth.

One reason why false modern Colonial is in such especially bad taste is because its style is the nearest approach to purest classic, which was that of Greece, and to follow the Greek ideal and disregard its peerless proportion, its purposeful sim-

plicity, and its serene nobility, is simply to create a scarecrow and label it a man.

ANCIENT DOMESTIC ARCHITECTURE

No trace of a Greek house remains standing, so it is to the Roman houses of Pompeii and Herculaneum, whose characteristics were the same, that we owe our ability to know what they were like — otherwise than by description. The plan of each was approximately the same: A small vestibule led to the atrium, an interior court in which the master of the house received strangers and conducted his business. In the richer dwellings there was a second, larger and more private court, also open in the center, but surrounded by a covered colonnade and therefore called the peristylium; this court was the outstanding feature of the house and is the prototype of the Spanish patio. There was a square pool for rain-water (the compluvium) in the center of both atrium and peristylium. The dining-room, called the triclinium, was beyond the peristylium. In the big houses, the peristylium was surrounded by the private rooms of the family, and there were often several dining-rooms. In the smaller, simpler houses, the family sleeping-rooms surrounded the atrium, which, partly covered over at one end, was also the principal living apartment. All — or at least nearly all — houses of the classic period were one-storied and without windows on the street side. Curiously enough, the street side of many of the larger houses was occupied by shops. Although these shops had no communicating doors with the house, they must have been operated or rented by the house-owner for profit — a practise more in keeping with the practical Romans than with the idealistic Greeks!

MEDIEVAL FRENCH FORTRESS

ARCHITECTURE AT A GLANCE

PAST THE DARK AGES

After the classic period, which ended with the fall of the Roman Empire (a.d. 476), the American house of to-day owes nothing further to Greece, and but little to Italy, for nearly a thousand years. Even though churches, public buildings and a certain few palaces of American millionaires have been copied from the succeeding Byzantine, Romanesque and Gothic periods, the American house of small or medium size — which is the one of our personal concern — has little affinity with the colossal fortress buildings of medieval days such as the one indicated in Figure 13, or with the vast intricacies of plan and composition pictured in Figure 14.

But the early years of the fifteenth century, which marked the beginning of the renaissance (or rebirth) of the classic style in Italy, also marked the change in architectural interest from temples and churches or so-called habitations that were actual citadels of defense, to the creation of the Florentine nobles’ palaces with which our houses are closely allied. The earliest of these palaces were massively constructed of great force-resisting blocks of rough-faced stone (see Palazzo Strozzi, Fig. 15), with heavy barricading doors and iron-studded wooden shutters. The interiors, however, had open fireplaces and were furnished with chests and chairs, tables and benches, and the walls and ceilings were marvelously embellished with frescoes and paintings and tapestries — by the greatest artists of all time.

Much as Greek architecture reached its supremacy in the Age of Pericles, art in Italy reached its preeminence in Florence — under the patronage of the beautyloving Cosmo de Medici — ^in the middle of the fifteenth

century. french Romanesque

PALLADIO

An outstanding figure of the Italian Renaissance — one to whom the type of American house which we call "Georgian” owes more than can be gaged — was Andrea Palladio, who not only printed full descriptions and measurements of the Classic Orders but made countless original designs and adaptations that are so familiarly commonplace to all of us as to seem natively our own.

There is not a town in the United States from Maine to Georgia, and westward to the Mississippi, that is without an

MEDIEVAL FLORENTINE - PALAZZO STROZZI

example of the Palladian door — or more commonly the window — of triplex arrangement: a large arched opening flanked by two small flat-topped ones. Palladio devised the block-square house, with four-sided pitch roof (hip roof) and columned portico, and set the Georgian fashion for purely ornamental quoins at the corners (Fig. 34) . In contrast to his many hip-roof designs, he still more especially sponsored the flat or "hidden roof” behind a balustraded cornice, as well as the geometrically balanced plan.

INIGO JONES

Palladio’s influence on our architecture, however, was not direct. The first of our race who discovered and interpreted him was the greatest of English architects, Inigo Jones. Sent

COLORED THREADS

FRANCIS I

As explained at the beginning, this chapter does not pretend to serve even partially as a history of architecture; in fact, it has barely traced a single thread in the world-wide tapestry of the architectural story as a whole. In other words, we merely picked out this long single thread of sand-brown color, as it were, at the portal of a rock-cut temple in Egypt; we followed it from temple to temple throughout three hundred years in ancient Greece; we

COBHAM HALL, BY INIGO JONES

saw it change to a filament of purest gold through the Age of Pericles; we followed it further — still a thread of gold — to Rome, where it gleamed for nearly a thousand years; then it grew background-dark and disappeared for nearly a thousand years behind the threads of other patterns, but emerged, gold again, in the Renaissance m Florence; then disappeared until picked up again a century and a half later by Inigo Jones and carried by him to England. Burnished by Jones and his great successor, wren, and by writers of books who came after them, it ends as a golden strand of Colonial purity, that embellishes every house of classic origin or design that is building to-day. ®

Besides this one thread, there are thousands of others. A parallel one, for example, is the brilliant line of color that marks the progress of English Tudor— the style known as Picturesque which has taken so strong a hold upon the imagination and the taste of our own time. Inis ludor thread

is double; one strand goes back to the first change from Romanesque to Gothic; its other strand begins in the fastness of the Saxon "keep,” continues over the rooftops of the Norman castles and, meeting

This thread was lost to sight under the classic thread of gold which shone first in the days of Anne and gleamed in full splendor throughout the reigns of the Georges, Victoria, and Edward, and has now appeared again, as glowing as before. A third thread that cannot very well be left unnoticed is the red and yellow twisted strand that began in Spain and came with Cortez to Mexico, to California, and to Santa Fe,

causing the color and design of our whole Southwest to be deeply ingrained with the temperament and character as well as the brilliancy of this Spanish inheritance.

ENGLISH ELIZABETHAN MANSION

And some of us, who think of Colonial houses

as none save those evolved by the colonists from England, all too carelessly forget that the Spanish- American settlers were flourishing in St. Augustine, Florida, and in Santa Fe, New Mexico, long years before the Pilgrim Fathers set sail for Plymouth, or the first hut or stockade was begun in Jamestown.

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

first colonists’ homes were not frame-built with I ^^high-pitched roofs, but adobe-walled and flat-topped;

though perhaps the early Spaniards who followed the Rio Grande from Mexico to the table-land of the lower range of the Rocky Mountains, on which they built our second oldest city, Santa Fe, should not be called colonists. Rather were they adventurers — a name at all events best suited to Santa Fe’s outstanding hero of romance, Don Diego de Vargas.

The long, colonnaded, one-storied, flat-topped Governor’s Palace still standing at Santa Fe (founded in 1605), and the restful and beautiful simplicity of the cloistered Mission buildings of California, which came a century and a half later, are native to the inhabitants of the Southwest, exactly as the buildings of white painted clapboards are native to the inhabitants of the Atlantic Coast.

FIRST HOUSES OF THE ENGLISH COLONISTS

The first dwellings, whether at Jamestown, Plymouth, or otherwhere, were of course not "houses” at all, but merely wigwam-shaped huts made of saplings and branches, covered with leaves and sod and whatever the colonists could find. The first hmses built at Jamestown were of rough-hewn planks set up like palings. There were no cabins of logs, laid horizontally, log on log, in the manner of the modern log cabins, except in Georgia. All the earliest houses were boxplain, unpainted, with untrimmed, small-paned windows and plain framed doors, and were of distinctly medieval origin.

Of the typical embellishment of classical decoration, which we associate with Colonial design, there was not a trace.

The most characteristic feature of these houses was the overhang of the upper story, and the end posts of the upper floor were ornamented at the bottom with squarish hickorynut shaped pendants, carved into the wood of the posts. Sometimes the overhang was supported by very simple blocks or brackets, which were the only embellishments, except the multiple form of the massive central chimney.

In the Scotch or Boardman house at Saugus, built in 1651 — the oldest New England house of authentic age (see

THE OLDEST HOUSE IN NEW ENGLAND The Scotch-Boardman house at Saugus, Massachusetts

The windows of the Paul Revere house in Boston are Elizabethan diamond-paned and leaded. The overhang of the Hyland-Wildman house at Guilford is very slight; its pendants are merely shallow brackets, and its door has a row of panes across the top, while the back of the roof dips down like a train. The doors of the oldest houses were made of perpendicular planks on the outside, lined with horizontal

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

ones on the inside — the two fastened together with roundheaded nails forming a dotted pattern of the whole.

Gradually the doorways were elaborated — not in the Tudor manner after which the earliest houses were built, but in the classic style — square columns, decorated, on each side old house in guilford of the door, and an angular,

curved or even occasionally a broken pediment over it. At first a straight row of panes of glass was put over the doors to light the interior. Later fanlights appeared, and shutters or single-sash- width windows were put at the sides. Porticos gradually began to look like small temples, and classical Colonial bloomed into full flower.

THE CLASSIC BEAUTY OF COLONIAL AMERICA

All good examples of Colonial houses are direct copies of the Classic Orders — usually Roman Doric and Ionic. Occasional and supremely perfect examples of Georgian houses are Greek Ionic — which does not preclude the use of complementary detail taken from other orders. In the beautiful illustration, Plate 4, the plain Tuscan order of the door enhances the beauty of the Ionic columns, and vice versa.

The why and how of our skipping all the ages in between and producing no other forms but those known as Classic throughout our successive American Colonial Periods are easily explained, since for more than two thousand years every student of architecture has gone to the Classic Orders for study and inspiration.

American builders (carpenters) of the early colonies did not journey to Greece, it is true. But many of the architects of England did; moreover, they took meticulous measurements of the Greek temples, both of the originals at Athens

and of those patterned after them in Rome, and made equally meticulous drawings and published them in books. And it was these books, brought in sailing ships to our North and Middle Atlantic shores, and studied by our architects and carpenters, which supplied the models for our own Golden Age of Colonial architecture. That the purity, dignity and simplicity of the Doric and Ionic orders should have been so strongly inspiring to the American colonists is easy to understand. Especially since the Greek form of construction — although rendered in stone — had evidently developed from an earlier period of wood, and was therefore especially practical for reproduction in wood.

The Greeks embellished their temples lavishly with color, but that our own copies were invariably painted white was also easy to understand, since, color-printing being unknown, the books and illustrations were necessarily in black and white.

Among the first of these books which influenced the beauty of Massachusetts, Connecticut, Maryland, Virginia, South Carolina, and other New England and Southern settlements was one called Lectures of Architecture, written by Batty Langly, in 1727. It contained, besides carefully written "Instructions of Building,” such definite measurements as: "Tuscan column 7 diameters, pedestal 2 diameters, diminution of shaft diameter at base, inter-columnation 4 diameters of base; Doric 8 diameters,” and so on. A Sure Guide to Builders, also by Batty Langly, brought to America in 1729, begins with a chapter headed: "A;? advertisement to Workmen to be first read by them before they proceed to the folloiving work.” This book also contains complete tables of classic measurements, including detailed working drawings of the variations of the Greek and Roman Orders as they were later interpreted by the great Italian architect, Palladio.

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

DID HE READ HIS OWN BOOK?

But then, alas! after having thoroughly expounded classic beauty,

Langly ran amuck and himself designed complete distortions which violated every principle that he had been at pains to teach! His T reasury of Design, which he declared to be "after the Grecian manner,” was a veritable compilation of the grotesque (the name by which its style came actually to be known) .

One so-called "column” was cut in four sections. In the top was exhibited a bearded head with bananas for ears, from which hung garlanded cloth and long chains falling to the center of the section below. This was decorated with a French sheaf of musical instruments, a sword and arrows, all resting on a barrel. In the next lower section, a squat pilaster with a panel down its center was fastened upon a second panel ornamented with vines. The sides of this section had projecting ears in the form of purposeless consoles. Carvings suggestive of pieces of sod were disposed at intervals upon another dwarf pilaster crowned with filigree and fastened vertically on a sod-trimmed square. And this completed monstrosity he labeled "Doric” — the severest and purest order of architecture in existence.

The books of William Pain were imported somewhat later. The Practical Builder or Workmen's General Assistant was written in 1742; another book of his was The Practical House Carpenter. Taking his work all in all. Pain had far better taste than Langly, and, besides repeating detailed measurements of classic proportions, included lovely circu-

Fig. 2J

ENGLISH GEORGIAN HOUSE BY SIR CHRISTOPHER WREN

TYPICAL COLONIAL

lar and oval stairs and many plans and elevations of "Gentlemen’s Houses” — perhaps the prototypes of some of the most beautiful old houses of Maryland, Virginia and South Carolina.

Of about the same period as the books of William Pain was The Five Orders of W. and J. Halfpenny, which included rriany drawing-room floor plans of London town houses, with typically inconvenient devices and designs suggestive of jig-saw chess pawns. An interesting feature to us is that the stables and coach-houses were often fitted into the ground floor, as garages are fitted in to-day. All big houses were planned on the big box flanked by two little boxes with galleries between. Like Langly, the Halfpennys designed many horrible examples of caryatid grotesques.

Stuart and Revett’s Antiques of Athens was published in England in 1762, and Greek beauty was again brought to the colonists, who borrowed and studied and drew from the book and passed their knowledge on. Happily for Colonial architecture, the hideosities of Langly and the Halfpennys were too intricate for the American carpenters to cope with. They therefore clung to the classic measurements and rendered them with least ornamentation and greatest purity.

The great successor of Inigo Jones was Sir Christopher Wren. New England church steeples, especially, owe their beauty to his designs. Also certain H-shaped English — rather than Colonial —

Georgian houses. The works of Inigo Jones were published in 1727, and those of Wren later. But the designs of the two great Ren- „

for the most part of buildings suitable only to the estates of the richest nobles, and the books chosen for importation to America were devoted to detail that could better be adapted to houses of smaller and simpler style. (Figs. 18 and 25.)

THE MODERN COLONIAL EXTERIOR

The various methods by which one might attempt to classify Colonial types are quite as numerous as the types themselves. One might, first of all, put them in two general divisions:

Class A: Those which have been the development of native need; the straightforward adaptations by the various settlers — whether they came from England, Ireland, Wales, or from Holland, France, Italy, Germany or Sweden — who naturally adapted the architecture of their neighbors to their own modes of life. This class, therefore, includes the Colonial houses whose development has been utilitarian.

Class B: This includes houses of imported elaboration created solely for purposes of beauty — those, for instance, of the style known as Georgian, and later those of the Neo-Grec — and the various adaptations which were strongly influenced by Renaissance Italian, and, latest of all, by the French, though the last-named influence rarely goes beyond the interior.

A second way of making a classification is that of the art experts who divide Colonial architecture into First, Second, and Third Periods. This is simple enough in its segregation of the early houses of Tudor origin into the First Period, but is far from satisfactory — or even intelligible — to most of us in its later manifestations.

A third classification, most easily understood — and it . seems to me the most logical — is that which lists all houses of the early period in three simple classes that developed in the northern, middle, and southern sections of the Colonies. These three types are still fully marked and easy to distinguish. They are:

1. New England Colonial.

2. Dutch Colonial.

3. Southern Colonial.

NEW ENGLAND COLONIAL

Those of New England are compact — box-like in shape, with a chimney in the center and the rooms grouped as closely around it as possible — for warmth. Some of them are shingled, some are clapboarded, some have lean-tos at the back or an L addition at the side. A few of them are of brick. These are usually called Georgian, though one and all are of the same Greek- Roman-Palladian-Jones-Wren family. All have classic doors. Many have beautiful details of cornice and window-trim. Typically they are dignified, conventionally well-bred and restrained. (See Figs. 26 and 30.)

A m’intire door

DUTCH COLONIAL

The Dutch houses in New York, Pennsylvania, and New Jersey were low, broad, and more homelike in aspect than the austerely stiff -roofed and classically circumscribed houses of New England. The Dutch colonists took the gambrel roof, which occurred in several of the early medieval houses, added dormers to the steep lower half of the slope, carried the bottom outward „„„„ colonial

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

in a curve beyond the face of the building, and put a row of square columns under the edge of it, thus devising a porch. (Fig. 29.) The authentic New England house has neither porch nor dormers.

The so-called Dutch Colonial house, which, excepting for a door that divided horizontally in half, had nothing especially suggestive of Holland about it, was almost always built

THE GIBBS HOUSE, CHARLESTON, SOUTH CAROLINA

of Stone, at least as high as the second story; sometimes the stone was left plain, sometimes whitewashed. The wood of the upper story was painted white.

SOUTHERN COLONIAL

In the South the houses were exactly the opposite, in purpose of plan, from those of New England. Chimneys, instead of being centrally placed, with the principal rooms surrounding them, were put as far away as possible and built on the outside of the farthest walls. The hall, eliminated from the typical New England house, was enlarged as much as possible in the Southern one; in houses of importance the compact squareness of the New England plan was discarded in favor of entirely separate units connected by corridors or

galleries. The moderate porch invented by the Dutch became an unrestrained feature of the Southern house, which in more than one instance ran around all four sides of the house, and on every story.

In Charleston, South Carolina, the porch was frequently made the connecting link between the street and the house by having the front door open not into a hall but onto an end of the porch. The Colonial houses of Charleston are, of course, noted the world over for their homelike charm as well as for their architectural beauty.

In such examples as the Gibbs house (Fig. 30) or the Brewton or Edward or Horry house, Georgian perfection of detail had reached its highest point in prerevolutionary days. Built about thirty years after the revolution, Homewood, in Baltimore, Maryland, is considered by the majority of critics to be the Parthenon of America”; in other words, the most perfect example in existence of classic Colonial architecture. (For Colonial Interiors, see Chapter XIII.)

WROUGHT-IRON BALCONIES, NEW ORLEANS

THE FRENCH HOUSE

The oldest native American-French houses are the serenely beautiful plantation villas of Louisiana, including a few in New Orleans itself. Very many of these lovely old houses are tumbling to pieces, but a number of the best are as perfect to-day as when they were built— about a hundred years ago. Most of them, imbued with the spirit of the "Greek revival,” are closely related to Georgian in their classic tranquillity, but suggest a spacious comfort, a friendly hospitality, that compares with Georgian formality much as the homelike friendliness of

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

the Dutch Colonial compares with the austerity of the early architecture of Puritan New England.

All the most beautiful examples had two-storied colonnades, the railing of the upper floor fitting between the Doric columns that, rising from low square bases on the ground, supported the roof. The long French windows gave easy access to deep galleries, which, crossing one or more sides of

every floor, often served in place of hallways; the stairway often built at one end of the gallery was sometimes open, but usually screened by shutters from the lawn or the street.

Others had interior halls and wide, curving stairways. These houses were entered from a paved gallery at ground level or by high flights of outside stairs that led to the gallery on the second floor. Occasionally, as at Rienzi Plantation, they were in a double curve. In these instances the basement floor was used for kitchen offices, and sometimes as quarters for the house slaves. But when the house was entered at the lower level, the parlors and dining-room were on that floor. In occasional instances, upper balconies — more fragile than

usual — were seemingly hung like wall-brackets on slender wooden columns suspended from the roof, apparently reversing the usual procedure of column support.

But in the Vieux Carre of New Orleans, the heavy classic two-storied columns of the villas — such as Belle Alliance, Seven Oaks, Burnside, Welham, and that most alluring house of fairy-tale beauty and name, "Shadows of the Teche” (Fig. 32), were often replaced by slender iron colonnettes and wrought-iron railings and embellishments in floral designs.

The typical color of all the plantation villas, like that of their English Colonial cousins, was white or cream, with

AMERICAN GEORGIAN

green blinds. Several in creole defiance of the fixed Colonial prejudices were actually pink with white marble trim, a color combination that, set in the dense shadows of tropical foliage, was lovely nevertheless.

The great beauty of the old houses of the French South lies in their simple but generous proportions, in their unassuming evidence of having been built by gentlefolk of taste and cultivation, and in the languorous luxuriance of the gardens upon which the balconies look out or down.

The present American version of the French house is distinctly that of the open timbered type of Norman cottage, which takes the place of a very simplified form of Tudor style. The French farm and manor houses of the Provinces include most charming and adaptable styles for American use, which have so far been almost entirely overlooked; at all

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

events, they have not been developed. The French highroofed chateaux (modifications of Fig. 14), although we have many admirable examples of them in this country, are suitable only to dwellings of palatial size and to owners who carry formality to its furthest French extreme.

THE ENGLISH HOUSE IN AMERICA

This is not the Georgian of classic origin, but the type of building that is founded on the Gothic style, which in the fifteenth century in England became the fountainhead of the great Picturesque style of architecture — a style of entrancing beauty and flexibility, which is at the moment so greatly in favor as to threaten America’s two-hundred-year allegiance to Colonial Georgian. Casually speaking, the Picturesque style of English house is divided into two periods: Tudor or Elizabethan, and Jacobean.

The characteristics of Tudor (really English Gothic) and Jacobean are much alike. Both are of stone or halftimber construction; both have oriel windows or square projecting bays and gable roofs, the whole following a picturesque "rambling” design.

Elizabethan is somewhat more formal in appearance. It is either E-shaped or H-shaped, and its fagade is almost invariably a series of bays, while the entire building is crowned with a balustrade or parapet of stone. A typical Elizabethan house is usually a gigantic country seat built of smooth-faced stone or brick, with mullioned windows occupying more than half the walls. The doorways are usually set in deeply recessed round or Gothic framing; the chimneys are wonderfully columned, twisted, reed-like and ornate. See illustration of Madiugley Hall (Fig. 22) and title page, another example of Georgian

The Jacobean style may, for all need of this chapter, be said to continue the general effect of Tudor until the coming of Inigo Jones.

The modern English house in America, whether it is called Tudor, Elizabethan, Jacobean, or English cottage, is characteristically irregular in outline and is therefore adaptable to irregular sites. Its best style is rather limited to houses of importance, because it is of very expensive construction. The stone of which its walls are built are beautifully trimmed and mortised; its casements are metal-framed and the panes leaded; the roof is gabled, copper-valleyed, and bronzeguttered; its chimneys are tall and often elaborate, and its

THE H-SHAPED HOUSE - FRENCH OR ENGLISH

roof is of slate or of the same stone as that of the walls, cut into thin shingle-like slabs. Of equally high cost of construction is that which shows its actual timbers, filled in with brick precisely fitted — usually laid in herring-bone or other pattern. The eaves are either cut off' flat in American fashion or curved in suggestion of thatch. But the shingles must on no account be mechanically wavy and roll at the edges like bolsters. An architect who is a past master in the art of producing beautiful shingle thatches is H. Lindeberg.

A much less expensive open-timber effect is obtained by building a surface of stucco, with exposed timbers simulated by flat boards stained to look like beams of weathered oak, and set into the stucco walls in imitation of timbered design. (Small houses of this type are listed under Norman Peasant. For Interiors, see Index.)

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

THE ITALIAN HOUSE IN AMERICA

Italian architecture is less in vogue at the moment than it was some time ago, perhaps because of the present interest in Spanish, which, being interpretable with complete freedom from formality of style, appeals much more to the modern mood.

The general outline of the Italian plan is usually much the same as Georgian — in a different dress. In other words, the house is block-square, with windows evenly balanced. But stucco — sometimes painted in Arabesque design — takes the place of wood or brick. An arcaded loggia is a characteristic adornment, balustrades are used instead of iron handrails, the walls are embellished with decoration. Roofs, if they are of tile, should be very low in pitch or hidden behind a parapet. (For Italian details of furnishing or garden, see Index.)

THE AMERICAN SPANISH HOUSE

Native mixtures of Mexican, Pueblo Indian, and Spanish adobe houses are typical of the southern half of California, and American adaptations of Spanish origin completely

dominate the architecture of Florida, where it has become exceedingly ornamental, elaborate, and often palatial. Many American-Spanish houses are entrancingly beautiful, per- Pig 37 fectly appointed and in

CALIFORNIA SPANISH best taste. On the other

hand, numerous examples are inclined to be vulgar — and many are far more than "inclined.”

Too many varieties of tiles are as bad for digestion as too many varieties of cakes for tea; too much color can become quite as torturing to the eye as blistering sunburn is to the skin, and the Rococo tendencies of spurious motives too loudly proclaim illiteracy. But, handled with taste, knowledge and restraint, the Hispano-American house, in a hot climate, is ideal. Its overthick walls are designed to keep out the heat ; its doorless openings encourage the circulation of air; its untrimmed surfaces are beautifully suitable to the embellishment of tropical foliage; its patio — especially one that is arcaded on its northern side — grants a shaded outdoor retreat — at one side of it or another — for every hour of the day.

(Various ways of beautifying this most lovely feature, as well as details of the Spanish interior, will be found in Chapter XIII.)

AMERICAN-BORN, LEFT-OVERS, ADAPTED PERIOD,

AND "just houses”

The term American-Born, in architecture, includes, the adobe pueblo of the Southwest American Indians, the log cabin, the false-front shack, and the bungalow.

The story of the rise of the Adobe Pueblo to prominence is as fantastic as a fairy-tale. Not only has it become the "Spanish castle” of the Southwestern millionaire and the prototype of New Art’s monolithic modern house, but it would seem to have been the inspiration for the soaring, story-above-story, monolithic skyscraper, the most outstanding achievement in the architectural beautification of utility that America — or any other country — has produced.

The Log Cabin, on the other hand, has shown no adaptability to further development. It is often enlarged, made multiple, and fitted with every modern comfort in the Adirondacks and other mountain resorts of fashion, but it remains as unchanged in detail as in construction.
