THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

One wonders why the log cabin is not made use of more as a mountain home of comfort and suitability. One finds not half as much use made of it as there might be. The wooden house in a mountain region always is rather suggestive of a packing-box dropped along the trail. One is inclined to call up the expressman and tell him about it, so that it can be claimed and taken away!

The False-Front Shack has no place in our pages, really, except that it was such a typical feature in every new settlement of our Western pioneers, who seemingly inherited an instinct for ancient Rome’s love of effect. The idea was to make a row of one-storied houses look two-storied. Almost every Main Street of every town in America had — or still has — examples of this “making the town look grander” feature. In fact, there are examples of it even in New England, whose people are as truth-loving as the Greeks.

A Bungalow is any building of any period — recognizable or not — that is one-storied. If two-storied, it is a cottage, castle, house, or what you will, but not a bungalow.

The dictionary definition of a bungalow is a building of one story, with a veranda around it, that is found in India. Not a word about its having become the most typically American small house possible to think of, nor about the fact that its only suggestion of India is when it is two-storied and thereby disqualified.

The bungalow of charm is most usually a one-storied dwelling built of shingles or of stucco, with a shingled roof — on occasions a roof of not too heavy tile. The more nearly it comes to looking cottagey or quaint, the more attractive it is. And yet if it have dormer windows, it supposedly turns into a cottage. For that matter, if the roof be flat, it then ceases to be a cottage! But, after all, what difference in a name?

So let us say that a very ugly example of house, whatever it is called, is the turtle type that is low and squat and closely covered by an enormous roof — a roof supported on chunky wooden spools or concrete sausages, giving the impression that, like the turtle, its defense is against attack from above —

which may perhaps be merely the precaution of the wise against tumbling airplanes. As a matter of fact, these overheavy roofs are obviously derived from the thatch of English cottages. But the material of the latter, being what it is, gives no uncomfortable sense of weight such as the same measurement of solid wood or, still worse, tile, produces.

A new style of roof that threatens the Middle West is exactly like a mushroom in shape, thickness, and support at its center. This style, in faithful copy of the vegetable, is the production of a leading modernist who refuses to join the ranks of "mere copyists of architectural precept.” Which naturally suggests the question: Which would one rather copy — a Greek temple or a vegetable?

Another bad type of bungalow is the one that looks like a railroad car or a Noah’s ark. Sometimes, it is true, if the lot is very narrow and the space available to build on is of bowling-alley shape, very little can be done but to build a Noah s ark type of house — if it must be one-storied. But the monotony of a too-long-and-narrow house can be somewhat broken with any one of a variety of bay windows — little grilled squares on a Spanish or Italian house, or boxshaped ones of leaded panes on an English or Normandy house, or cottage casements, or village-shop ones on almost any sort of house.

Still another unpleasing bungalow is the roofless Mexican type that gives a scalped-by-the-Indians impression. Moreover, the surface of adobe or of stucco can easily be hideous; if it looks too patty-caked and smoothed round the edges, it looks overheavy as well as overhandled. It also is hideous when its surface is lumpy and rough, as though children had flung its material at it by the handful. Plaster or stucco should be coarse or fine — but much better too fine than too coarse — in proportion to its size. A very small wall surface should be

very fine and have fairly sharp edges so as not to look. overthick.

Also a warning as to color: In spite of the fashionable appreciation of sunburn as an embellishment for young

THE STORY OF HOUSES IN AMERICA

women of to-day, no less than for young men, a deep shade of terra-cotta is not a beautification of the exterior of any house — be it a bungalow or an Aztec temple.

Left-Overs are houses which belong to periods which have gone — perhaps temporarily, perhaps permanently — out of style. A list of them includes the Mansard mansion, the Victorian jig-saw house, American Gothic structures, Wood- Lizzies, French chateaux built of shingles, and all types that we no longer find pleasing. Many of these will be considered in Chapters VI and XIX.

Adapted Periods is a term that includes the majority of new houses. A few examples that are building to-day are faithful copies or interpretations of old examples. But nearly all are adaptations — reductions in size, usually — to modern American needs. Sometimes adaptations are so liberal as to be scarcely traceable to their originals. Sometimes this is excellent, sometimes the contrary. The rule for adaptation should be to interpret the spirit of the model — or make an independently new design. To attempt to build a house of a certain style and then to add details that are diametrically opposed, or to leave out items of characteristic importance, is to produce a mongrel.

If one builds — or remodels — a simple house of no great allegiance to precise details of style, one can have an entirely charming house of its own quite personal variety, without causing criticism. But to take a Tudor design, for instance, and copy its detail carefully except for a too-high and toosharp roof which comes eccentrically down on one side with a swing-to-the-left curve, for all the world like a huge Spencerian capital A, is to produce ugliness. Or to build a bastard Colonial house, with a higher second story than first story, and with the entire fagade broken by jogs and angles, is to proclaim that architecture was not included in the owners’ studies at school.

It is really harder to build an ugly small house to-day than to build an attractive one. Books and magazines as well as most neighborhoods are so full of lovely examples, one

would have to be totally unseeing not to choose well. And really bad new examples are very few.

Just Houses means just that! It means houses that attempt no definite period, though they perhaps suggest relationship to a certain style. Among them are certain bad ones already noted among the "left-overs”; but as a type "just houses” are those that have been built by local carpenters, usually, of local style and of local material — than which there can be nothing better for the building of a suitable house. Most of the styles that we especially cherish are so constructed. The houses of Pennsylvania, built of local stone, straight and plain and comfortable; the houses of clapboards and shingles in all woodland districts; the houses of brick in Maryland — block-plain, most of them, with plain, straightforward plan, and additions built as needed by the enlarging families who occupied them — cannot well be other than good, likeable and personable.

IV

THE FUNDAMENTAL PRINCIPLES OF ARCHITECTURAL

BEAUTY

ly matter to which type or period a house may

/ 1 /belong, the fundamental requirement of every archi- » tectural design is that it shall combine utility, strength and beauty.

In addition to the three requirements, the ancient Greeks included truth. This meant that each item of building material actually served the purpose to which it pretended. Blocks were truly blocks, not facing; they disliked pilasters, which had no depth and carried no weight. Greek columns actually supported the entablature, and so on. The exact opposite of the Greek ideal is the modern building of steel with the facade actually a trimming.

UTILITY

Utility means sincerity of design to the purpose for which it is intended. Meaning that a dwelling, whether it be a palace or a cottage, should look homelike. Anything more useless than a placid suburban house built in imitation of a medieval castle, with bastions and turrets designed for purposes of defense, is not conceivable. And yet every steepletowered and moat-surrounded feudal chateau in France was faithfully copied in hundreds of wooden houses during American architecture’s darkest hour about fifty years ago. A modern example of architectural insincerity is a Spanish patio in a cold Northern climate, where it serves principally as a container for half-frozen rain and snow.

The purpose of any jail door is to bar entrance — or exit. The purpose of a house door is to invite — though its invita-

tion is naturally more reserved than that of the sign-shouting indiscrimination of the entrance to a shop.

The purpose of a roof is shelter and not an erratic display of angles, though an exception, is allowed in designing the picturesque sky-line of a house on the summit of a hill or mountain. And although the more protectingly it fits down upon a house the more snug and secure it seems, it should, of course, not smother it.

The ideal of architectural purpose was perfectly expressed by Inigo Jones, who exacted that a building be "solid, proportionable according to rule, masculine and unaffected.”

STRENGTH

Strength means obviously sufficient solidity for weight, size and purpose. To continue the portrait of the roof, a one-storied bungalow should not be buried under its weight of roof supported on fat sausage columns and the whole suggesting that, like Atlas, it might bear the weight of the world. Any solid chunk of a house so massively built that you feel its weight might easily be converted into a vault for safekeeping or into an ice-breaker in the Arctic, has strength that is out of scale, and is therefore not good architecture.

On the other hand, if you feel that a building might topple over, or blow down, then there is equal fault in its structure or design. The Leaning Tower of Pisa was an accident and freak of use only as an example of the law of gravity. It is not a model of architectural perfection. The ground upon which it was built gave way on one side. Had it given way a little more the building would have fallen flat.

The house of ideal strength is one that gives the impression that it is standing at rest in tranquil security.

BEAUTY

Beauty that is worthy of the name must delight the eye, exalt the spirit, and satisfy the intelligence. But, first of alll there must be suitability to purpose and to situation.

PRINCIPLES OF ARCHITECTURAL BEAUTY

Every chapter in this book will probably emphasize the necessity for suitability, because suitability is always the keynote of good taste. As beautiful clothes must be suitable to the hour, the place, the occasion, and the wearer, a beautiful house must be suitable to its situation, to the personality of its owners, to their manner of living.

It is true that there might be utilitarian suitability without a vestige of beauty, but it is equally true that there cannot be a vestige of beauty without suitability. Suitability to purpose has been noted above; suitability to situation includes style, scale, and building material.

There are two directly opposed ideals in architectural design: the Formal, which expresses the Greek ideal, and the Picturesque, which expresses the Gothic. The Greek ideal was of a building majestically at rest. It was never flung on the ground, but was ceremoniously placed upon a terrace or platform, exactly as a statue is placed upon a pedestal designed not merely to support it, but to display it. The platform on which a Greek temple stood emphasized the building as a work of Art. The Gothic ideal, on the contrary, is of a building that rises spontaneously from its foundations, as though it had of itself sprung to life in marble or in stone. Its base is seemingly part of the rock or hillside it has sprung from; its roofs, which are usually multiple and are of varying heights, seem part of the landscape’s own profile. An ideal house of self-born type stands on two great rocks that descend precipitously into the waters of a New York lake. It is impossible to tell where the boulders cease and the mortised stones of the matching walls begin.

The Picturesque house is especially suitable on a sloping or irregular hillside, because being itself irregular it adapts itself to the conformation of the landscape.

Native building material — especially stone — is more sympathetic to location than foreign materials, which are often difficult to naturalize. Gray boulders, cut so as to leave moss-and-lichen-covered surfaces, are characteristic of moss-and-lichen-covered surroundings of our Eastern

S2

mountains. The adobe houses of New Mexico, the Spanish style prevalent in Florida, the thatched-roof cottage on a wooded lane, the Colonial houses of the Southern or New England towns, the Normandy houses on sloping hills, Italian villas overlooking formal gardens, Georgian houses of importance on important city streets are all types suitable to location. The ideal of suitability to situation is the house that belongs so exactly where it stands that it cannot be moved elsewhere without loss to the site as well as to itself.

THREE STRUCTURAL ATTRIBUTES

The three structural attributes of beauty are Form, Proportion and Scale, and Balance.

Form is dependent upon structure; structure is dependent upon material to be used; and material is dependent upon location. For example, the adobe houses of the desert — whether in the East or in our own Southwest — are naturally expressed in very primitive structure and limited to primitive form.

Form means the outline of design or shape; the successful materialization of the idea. A Spanish house in nothing the form characteristic of Colonial. An attribute of form is texture: smoothness or coarseness of material appropriate to the style of design. The walls of a Spanish house are rough; those of a Georgian one are smooth. There are also innumerable conventions which define the colors suitable to certain types of buildings, such as that Colonial houses are never painted pink, though Italian villas may be.

Proportion and Scale — There is no greater test of an architect’s inherent talent than the infallibility of his sense of proportion. Proportion means fitness and harmony in all relations of measure. Scale means size of decoration in proportion to object decorated, or of detail to the whole of which it is a part.

For instance, a room is in proportion when its height is suitable to its length and width. Its details are in scale when

PRINCIPLES OF ARCFUTECTURAL BEAUTY

they are in proportion to the size of the room and to one another. A room may be in beautiful proportion, and yet a detail too small, an ornamental design too big, a door too low, may be out of scale.

A ceiling either seven feet six inches high or eighteen feet high would be equally out of proportion in a room twentyfive feet square. An ideal height would be between eleven and twelve feet. A seven-and-a-half foot ceiling could not be borne in a room bigger than about fifteen by twenty feet, or fourteen by twenty-two feet, which would then have to be very "knowingly” furnished to obviate the feeling of the ceiling coming down on one’s head. Ornamentation on a very low ceilmg makes it even lower. An eighteen-foot ceiling, on the other hand, would make a medium-sized room feel like the bottom of an elevator shaft. If flat and untrimmed, the effect of height would be increased. An eighteen- or nineteen-foot ceiling in a room thirty by forty-five feet is perfection — either with a proportionate cornice, if in a Georgian room, or a groined or timbered and otherwise relieved overhead decoration, in an Italian room.. A mantel in proper scale would be at least six feet high and seven or possibly eight feet wide. The trims or pediments of doors and windows must also be of proportionate depth and height. Under certain circumstances the effect of size is appreciably increased or diminished by means of weight. A mantel, if of sufficiently fine and delicate design, may be overlarge in measure and yet fit with perfect proportion in a low-ceilinged medium-sized room. Or one which is somewhat small would be adequate in a larger room if it had sufficient weight. This would not answer, of course, if an object were too greatly out of scale.

Texture means surface finish or material suitable to use. You wouldn’t trim a chiffon evening dress with bands of tweed, or put a covering of coarsest weave on a fragile satinwood chair. Texture includes fineness or coarseness, whether of walls, trim or furnishing; both of design and execution. Texture is purposely rendered in fineness or crudeness, in sim-

S4

plicity or sophistication, according to each detail of period or style.

Balance, sometimes called repetition, is the equilibrium between one side of a house — or a room or piece of furniture — and another side. It is most easily illustrated in furnishing. All tall pieces gathered at one end of a room produce the feeling that the room is capsizing. One tall piece on one side may, however, be balanced by an object of weight on the other. If you want to put a little picture on one side of the mantel and a big one on the other, the little picture can be made to balance the big one quite easily, if on a table under the little picture you put a lamp, and nothing under the big one.

In the same way, if one side of an irregular house is short and the opposite side is long, evergreen trees against the short side will restore its harmony. A steeple on a church or a tower at the end of a building is equalized by sufficient length of building to balance the height of the steeple or tower.

Other attributes of architectural composition include Bhythm, meaning that the eye is carried easily from contour to contour without being brought up sharply by an obstructing height or gap, and Emphasis, meaning that which attracts and holds the attention for instance, the lavish ornamentation of a Spanish door set in an otherwise unbroken surface of wall. Unity means that each separate item or part of a building or series of buildings is designed in harmonious adjustment with every other item of form and scale, so as to enhance the appearance of the whole.

NEIGHBORHOOD DESTROYERS

In marked opposition to unity are the out-of-key houses of a certain species of householders to be found in almost every town in the United States — a species for whom there is no better name than neighborhood destroyers. Curiously enough, they are almost always persons who consider them-

PRINCIPLES OF ARCHITECTURAL BEAUTY

selves honorable members of the community — who wouldn’t take a mill not rightfully theirs, nor take advantage of a neighbor in any manner of dealing whatsoever. And yet they will unthinkingly take something of far greater value than the neighborhood’s petty cash by putting up a discordant building or splashing it with a violently jarring color and robbing the whole town of its beauty.

On an old South Carolina street, for instance, a rich man of this type — they are nearly always rich men — put up a pile of distorted architecture that was not only a monstrosity of unsuitability itself, but destructive to the beauty of the entire street — exactly as a great ink-blot on the face would destroy a beautiful portrait.

In a New England village, on a hill which dominates the clustering white- and green-shuttered houses, one who is obviously insensible to neighborhood beauty has built a gigantic bungalow with pink stucco walls and a blood-red roof. A short time before, it was a village of exquisitely fragile Colonial beauty that motorists were wont to go miles out of the beaten track to see. It is utterly ruined. Even the pink-faced, red-turbaned monster looks uncomfortably insecure on the seeming foundation of the fragile little dwellings huddled at its base. Yet the owner of the monster came to live among the fragile dwellings because he thought them beautiful! How could he then destroy them? What can be done about it is not very clear, since each house-builder has the right to build what house he pleases!

If each of us in every town would care something about the unity of the streets we live on, the towns of America would be the most beautiful in the world. But a haphazard collection of unrelated styles of architecture can never look anything but mongrel. Just go and look at them! Spanish, English, Colonial, Rococo, Byzantine, Italian, German, French, and the uncompromising cube plainness of the new monolith — parading down a whole street length — discord if there ever was discord! Perhaps the only way to create a town of beautiful accord would be to start with a brand-

new development and lay out its streets with restrictions of each, to the type of its name.

COULD IT BE DONE?

Washington Road would be all old Colonial. Via Appia would be Italian. On Old Norman Road, English cottage and Norman peasant might mix quite nicely. In such a town the Browns or the Perugis or the Latours choose the style of house suitable to their ancestors or their taste, and buy a lot in the midst of neighbors whose houses will be in unity with their own. Makeshift Way could be a segregated section for any sort of near-type house of untraceable pedigree, with permanent corners for those who cannot be trusted to keep their architecture the required percentage pure.

The above suggestion is not written in jest. One need merely bring to mind the beauty of French New Orleans, or the enchantment of so many New England or Southern houses that are so truly lovely because the type of old Colonial pervades the entire place; in fact, having a personal preference for Colonial, I myself live half the year in an enchanting New England seacoast town where a whitepainted, green-shuttered and flower-bordered house of my own is duplicated by all its uncles and its cousins and its aunts!

V

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

/F you are a prospective house-owner, three courses are open to you: you can build, you can remodel, or you can buy ready-made. The advantage if you build is that you can decide upon each separate detail of design, so as to suit your taste and requirements. The disadvantage is that you have to pay for the house as its construction progresses, and yet are homeless throughout the time it is building; moreover, it cannot be seen except "on paper,” which is, for some people, difficult. There is also a popular belief that all custom-made articles are of high cost (not necessarily true) , and that the final cost of house-building is always more than you think it is going to be (99 times out of 100, true) .

On the other hand, if you buy a new house (or an apartment) ready-made, the advantages are, that you can see it actually without having to imagine what it will look like; that you can move into it at once; and that you know exactly what it is going to cost. The disadvantages are, that it is "as built”; that you must judge it by appearance only; and that its price is not — value for value — likely to be economical.

The cost of a house that you build ought to be less than the price of a house put up by a building corporation, because, although building in quantity is cheaper, the profit to the promoter — which includes not merely principal and interest on the money he has put into it, but also reasonable compensation for risk — must almost necessarily raise the total to a higher figure than the cost of building for yourself ought to reach.

The third course — that of remodeling — is becoming every year more popular. In addition to the beautification of full-grown — even if neglected — shrubs and trees, the ad-

vantage of buying an old house, especially one out of repair, is that you can usually buy it for a song. The disadvantage is the uncertainty as to how much the song; may mount up to by the time the renovations are completed. If no structural changes are necessary beyond the putting in of a partition or two and a small amount of plumbing, estimate of improvement and repair is simple enough. But the moment you begin tearing down — that is another story (which will be discussed at length in the next chapter) . Meanwhile, let us begin with the problems of building:

If you have found a site that you like, you have perhaps already visualized the style of house you want to put on it. Perhaps you know so definitely the house you want that you are thinking of building without an architect. Of course, if the house is to be very simple and unpretentious; if you have had a certain amount of experience in building; if the new house is to resemble others in the neighborhood; and, finally, if you have a reliable contractor, he may be able to produce the ideas you have in mind. Or you may be able to do your own designing; or perhaps you are buying ready-made plans which can be used as they are, or modified slightly to suit your requirements.

But under normal circumstances, no matter how well you think you know what you want, the wisdom of engaging an architect cannot be overemphasized. Not merely because the architect is likely to make a more beautiful house, but because, in spite of his ten-per-cent.-over-all fee, the chances are greatly in favor of your saving money in the end.

CHOOSING AN ARCHITECT

House-building is not a simple process for the inexpert. The details that come up for decision are unceasing, and more often than not they involve conundrums which only the technical expert can answer. And if you "guess wrong” the result is ugliness, inconvenience, unsuitability, or that most wasteful of all extravagances, tearing down and doing over.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

A good architect foresees the difficulties, saves the redoing here, the wasteful and unnecessary outlay there; he sees, from the first setting up of the frame, that the outline of the building is right. If something seems wrong, he does not "hope it will look better later on.” He knows either that it will or that it won’t, and in the latter case he adjusts it at the beginning. He also sees that the materials used are those which the specifications call for, and that each detail of the house is assembled as it should be, straight through the entire operation. The architect is personally responsible, and should, of course, be held so.

Without an architect the building progresses exactly as a ship at sea with no one at the steering wheel. Few people can even visualize size, let alone form; fewer still know anything at all about detail. Not one in a thousand knows good building from bad; and not one in ten thousand knows or can produce, even when he knowSy the qualities that give a house personality and charm. Any builder can put up a hard block of a house that looks as though made of cast iron or tin. Only an architect of talent (or an owner of genius) can produce the subtleties of line and proportion that alone create a building of beauty.

Thousands of houses of charm — one might almost say the majority of those that have most charm — are furnished without the services of a decorator; but to build a house without an architect is another matter. If you, as an amateur, have ever spent days of intensive thought and effort trying to draw a perfect plan — let alone an exterior to fit the plan — and then had a really good architect in a few moments reshape your plan and obviate each of your difficulties as though by magic, you will understand why you should have your house built by a good architect.

True, all architects are not good ones! Not by any means! And bad ones are a total loss! Moreover, a poor or inexperienced architect gets possibly the same fee as the best one. Bad ones may be very charming neighbors or friends, or relatives. It is difficult to discriminate against one who is a

near neighbor or a dear friend, in favor of a stranger who is nothing to you at all; but, after all, delight in the comfort and beauty of your home surroundings is too vitally important to risk, even to neighborly interest or dear friendship. Surely this is a sufficient reason why you should choose an architect whose work you like, and not let liking for a man whose work you don’t like warp your judgment.

Quite aside from a question of taste, business relationships are best kept on a business basis. It is much better to keep Jim your friend, apart from his profession, than to make him your architect and be miserable about a dozen things that you cannot bear to tell him because he is your friend. It is very hard to say: "Jim, I think less than nothing of the way your are looking after our job!” But you can say to a total stranger: "Mr. Blank, we are not satisfied with the supervision that has been given our house. "Whoever is to blame for the plastering will have to have it redone.” Or: "The wood that is being used for the Ubrary paneling will all have to come down.” Or — and much worse: "The roof line is very stiff and awkward; it is not a bit like the picture you made of it.”

Nothing like this may occur, it is true; the house from beginning to end may progress without a flaw or hitch. The probabilities in that direction, however, are not strong. In building, it is almost inevitable that there will be a certain number of mistakes, delays, additional charges; these are readily handled on a business basis, but may easily create an uncomfortable strain on an old friendship.

Remember also that architects are more or less specialists. Some have genius for gigantic buildings, some have a beautiful knowledge of a definite style. One man’s taste is for the picturesque, another’s for the severely classic, a third is happiest in a city house plan, a fourth specializes in country places, a fifth builds nothing but Spanish houses.

If you are building a small or medium-sized house it will be necessary to select an architect who lives within a reasonable distance, or who has other work in your neighborhood.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

because the cost of overseeing a small building from a great distance is obviously out of all proportion. This consideration is, of course, apart from the still more essential requirement that your architect be one whose special taste expresses your own. If possible, you should consult him before the land is bought, so that he may give his advice on the suitability of the site, both as to beauty and as to the natural adaptability of its conformation and exposure. He would be able to point out, for instance, that while the north slope of a mountain may overlook an entrancing view, the sunless exposure would make an appealing house impossible there — unless you prefer perpetual shade. He might also call your attention to important considerations of cost, such as the fact that foundations on steep hillsides are always expensive. Foundations on rocky mountainsides, I may add, are liable to cost fabulous sums. Foundations on flat sandy land are least expensive — unless in a quicksand district. Friends of my own, who bought a beautiful shore-front site, found a quicksand in the very place where the cellar was to go. It drank up thousands of dollars before bridging methods were found that were secure enough to assure the safety of the house. This, however, was an unusual experience.

YOUR LIST OF REQUIREMENTS

The first thing that the architect will want to know is how big a house you must have, and what are the requirements necessary for your convenience and comfort. It is very necessary for him to know whether you run your household according to fastidious standards or whether you live in the way known as "simple.” It might also be well to ask your definitions of the words "fastidious” and "simple.” Do you want a little room or a big one set apart in perpetually perfect order for the reception of friends or acquaintances? Must there be a study for your husband, a play-room for your children, or are you willing to have whoever-it-may-be come into the living-room where you or various members of

your family may be cluttering up desk or tables with any of the many writing, pasting, sorting or game-playing pursuits? Would you be comfortable in one big informal living-room to sit in, and eat in, and dance in, too? Or must you have a dining-room that will seat twelve, eighteen, twenty-four or more? In this case do you prefer a formal drawing-room of size and a snug little book-room to sit in when alone? Or, on the other hand, would you be content with a small-sized sitting-room and a tiny dining-room to seat no more than six at a pinch?

Are you going to fill your house with so many objects of virtu and so much furniture of tender fragility that the dusting and polishing of each of these treasures will take many hours of one person’s time every day? Or are your rooms going to be fairly empty so that sufficient dusting can be done in scarcely any time at all? In other words, shall you need many servants’ rooms, or few, or one, or none at all?

Do you want people passing by to be impressed by the size of your house — and its cost — or do you prefer to have its street view present as inconspicuous an effect as possible? Do you love beauty of interior for the pleasure it gives you personally? How do you spend your time? Do you read much? Do you play cards much? Do you have much company, and if so, of what sort? Must each member of the family have a room and bathroom of his own? Must you have closet room for endless clothes and personal possessions, or will a dozen hangers be more than enough? How much silver must you find safe or shelf room for? How much linen do you use? How many servants must be provided for? How much space is required for special storage, games or hobbies?

The answers to these questions naturally determine the size of house necessary to your comfort. The next question is that of style.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

WHICH STYLE OF HOUSE?

If you are entirely uncertain — perhaps somewhat confused by thinking of "styles” as a series of chronologically selected details, grouped and catalogued according to the when and where of their origins — this question may be much easier to answer if you merely divide all styles of architecture into five general physical types:

1. The Severe Formal.

2. The Minor Formal.

‘ 3. The Balanced Picturesque.

4. The Irresponsible Picturesque.

5. The Blank-Face.

The outstanding characteristic of the Formal house is undeviating regularity. A line drawn perpendicularly through the center of its front elevation will go directly through the center of the front door and every detail on one side is repeated in exact reverse on the other. In fact, it is a drafting-room custom to draw only one-half of the facade, and, holding a strip of mirror on the center line, let the reflection complete the design. Houses that belong in this category may be either rectangular E-shaped or H-shaped, half H, or three-block English, Italian, French, or American Georgian. The Formal house requires an absolutely flat setting, which may be an artificially built terrace or platform, or may be a natural park or table-land.

T he Minor Formal means merely a simple house of unpretentious size that is evenly balanced in design. Thousands of small houses that have a door in the middle and one or two evenly balanced windows on either side, with regularly spaced windows above, should sit tranquilly also on platforms of land.

The Balanced Ficturesque is suitable to all hillsides. By "balance” is not meant "repetition,” but adjustment of height to weight. A house should give the feeling that its balance is comfortably secure. Its heaviest — central — weight

is placed either on the crown of the hill, with supporting wings packed close against it, or on the middle level with a lower wing on the higher, and a smaller but steeper wing (or possibly a tower) on the lower. The roof of the largest unit is slightly higher than those of the smaller units, but the general outline follows the contour of the hillside against which it is placed. Such houses are sometimes built of stucco or half-timber, but the finest examples are usually of stone that is natural to the location. They include English Tudor, replicas or adaptations suggestive of the Feudal French, German (Rhine castles) , Flill-Town Italian, or our own American rendering of moss-and-lichen-covered graystone. Although especially suited to wooded-hill surroundings, these houses may quite perfectly be placed on level ground and are for that reason fair more adaptable than the inflexibly Formal house, which must never be thrust upon stilts on one side, with chin dug into the earth on the other.

The Irresponsible Picturesque includes every low, unassuming building which, pretending to neither style nor form, can literally perch or huddle or ramble at will almost anywhere; in a meadow or on a hillside, on a sand-dune or in the woods. Its type includes the Spanish hacienda, a collection of small buildings unevenly surrounding a central courtyard. It also includes all dwellings of styleless category, built in irregular units or composed of a multiplicity of wings and galleries; or a collection of log cabins or farm buildings grouped closely together or connected by galleries or colonnades.

The Blank-Face includes every house that turns its back to the street, or that surrounds itself in Spanish, Chinese or other Far-East fashion with blank walls. Many important houses of the old nobility on the left bank of the Seine in Paris do this. On the street, there is nothing to be seen but a high wall with an ordinary or even shabby little workman’s house which is entered through big wooden doors. But when the doors are opened to you, you see a neat porter’s lodge on the inside, and find yourself in a garden facing a beautiful

Peabody, Wilson tfe Brown, Architects

A DIGNIFIED AND HOMELIKE HOUSE OF THE COLONIAL TYPE

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

house entirely hidden from the street. This same intention, though somewhat differently expressed, is found in the city or suburban house that has a perfectly plain fagade set flat to the street, with a front door on one side, a kitchen door on the other, and as many windows as need be with straight hanging curtains. Houses of this type are rapidly growing in popularity in America. By means of slight detail and characteristic doors and windows, almost any type of architecture might be indicated in conformity with neighboring houses. The kitchen and all other service rooms would face the street, while the living rooms of the family would face toward a garden at the back.

If the house is plain and the plot is wide, a high wall continues the facade on either side, and a door in this wall should lead by way of a kitchen yard into the kitchen. But if there is no side lot, the kitchen door opens on the same fagade as the front door.

This turning of the house front-side to the back is greatly to be commended, since the fagade overlooking the garden allows an architect infinitely greater possibilities for the development of charm and beauty than a fagade on the street. Moreover, a plain exterior that says nothing about the beauty within is always sympathetic to best taste, whereas an outside ornamented for show and an interior that is neglected or cheap is like nothing so much as a woman in a sable coat and ninety-eight-cent underclothes.

, SUITABILITY TO YOUR PERSONALITY

If you are a formal type of person; if you care overwhelmingly for a house that is never otherwise than precisely so; if you never glue or cut out or spread things about; in short, if disorder of any sort is distressing to you, then any of the examples of formally balanced design would be suitable to your personality. If, on the other hand, you sit on the center of your spine, “wear” a cigarette in the corner of your mouth, and dress in the last exaggeration of fashion.

expressed in the latest clash of color, then modern French or Viennese or American monolith is obviously yours — which, if separated from the conservative choice of neighbors by toy-village trees or other garden screening, will save both them and you.

Whichever type of house you choose, be sure that you are choosing each detail because it appeals to you, and not because you are a slave to Rule. In other words;

WHEN RULES HAMPER - DISCARD

Let us say you own a piece of property that has a flat plateau, and below it a second table-land. Here is an ideal site for an Italian house; the house on the upper terrace, walled or balustraded, and the garden spread below. But suppose that you are an out-and-out American, that you live in characteristically American style, and that Itahan surroundings — even cypress-spired gardens — would "say nothing” to you. In that case an Italian villa is not for you; it would be idiotic to suppose that a misfit setting, no matter how beautiful it nught in itself be, could ever produce perfection.

Far, far better build a low, rambling American house with these or those characteristics — whichever best suit the type of person you are. Build the house on the upper terrace with a fenced-in flower garden around it, and turn the lower terrace into a tennis court or a swimming pool — or both. Or, if the difference in height is not great, put the house on the lower level and make a garden with a plunge pool, if possible, reached easily by steps going up from the first and down from the second floor.

But, whatever you do — if you really want a house of charm do not be hidebound and imagine you have to build the sort of house the obvious person would put on such a site. Unless you are an obvious person!

Or suppose, on the other hand, you love a French house but hate a French garden. Then don’t have such a garden. Don t think of having it! If the landscape gardener says you

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

must, get another landscape gardener with more ingenuity and sense. With clever adaptation you can have anything you want — that is, almost anything — except deformity, or a red and yellow Spanish house in the heart of a New England town!

architect’s drawing of the plans

When the site has been bought, the size of the house determined, and its style and material chosen, the architect will submit a plan of each floor and draw or paint a picture — called a rendering — of the house as it is going to be.

It must be confessed that this portrait is apt to be somewhat flattering, much as a fashion-plate drawing is inhumanly slenderized and lengthened; the drawing of a house, on the contrary, is lowered, the lines softened, and floral decoration artfully placed. A photograph (later) of the actual house will be harder, higher and sharper in outline. On the other hand, the straight, hard elevation drawings of the front and back and each side that are always included with the final floor plans (and are used by the contractor to build by) are drawn without perspective or the softening values of light and shade, and look much harder than the actual house ought to. Elevation, cross-sections, and especially drawings of full-sized details are quite unintelligible to the average client, who can only "see a picture,” and to whom all working drawings are unmeaning.

Very important public buildings are always — and private houses are sometimes — modeled in plaster or wax or other materials by people who make these miniature models of houses as a profession. Such models are necessarily very expensive. But if you can draw straight lines with a ruler, or even fairly straight ones — in other words, if you are at all adept in the use of a pencil, paint-brush, glue and scissors, a little paper model drawn to one-eighth of an inch scale is not unreasonably hard to make, and very helpful when you cannot quite see how the house will look.

6S

A GLANCE AT THE PRACTICAL SIDE OF BUILDING

Let US return to the architect, and take up considerations of cost. When you have determined the size the house must be, and have bought the land, and have decided upon the style of the house you prefer, the chances are — alas! — that your estimate of cost is only one-half, possibly only one-ithird, of what must be allowed for the size and style of house you have in mind.

If you say nothing about cost, and the architect neglects to mention this most necessary detail, the result is apt to be a site for sale, and an architect’s bill for drawings disputed!

But in the usual situation you name the sum you expect to spend and the architect explains that the style or size you describe will cost double your price, whereupon the sum must be increased or cost of building reduced. In the end a compromise is usually arrived at. But if the architect is young or money-conscienceless, he designs a house regardless of the amount you named — and meant — taking it for granted that you will gladly pay for what you want. Maybe you will, maybe you won’t, maybe you can’t.

If an architect knows a man to be enormously rich, he may let his plans run over the amount at first discussed, in order to give him what he feels sure would be more satisfactory. Not because he wants to run up the price and thereby increase his own commission, but because, when he draws the plan, he sees the opportunity to create beauty or comfort or durability that — to one who can afford it — may be well worth the difference in the cost.

When he shows the drawings to the owner, he naturally explains what he has done. If the owner agrees, the details are included in the final drawings; if not, they are reduced in size or quality, or taken out. On the other hand, if the architect knows that the sum the owner specified is all that he can afford to pay, he must check his dreams of perfection, and try only to get the utmost beauty and comfort to be had for the sum available.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

PLAY FAIR WITH THE ARCHITECT

Misleading one’s architect is for this reason a mistake. The make-believe-poor, rich man, gets a substitute house, with which he is afterwards dissatisfied. The make-beUeverich, poor man, is ever afterwards dissatisfied likewise, because of the necessary cutting down of the plans that entranced him and that he was unable to have.

Misunderstandings of cost are not always the fault of the architect (nor of the builder) . If a stranger going into an architect’s office tells him he wants a French Renaissance house and gives him a list of twenty-odd rooms that must be large and high-ceilinged, with ten baths and other details that indicate no evidence of economy, and takes him out in a very expensive car to look at an important site, and says nothing whatever about the cost, a young unthinking architect, taking it for granted that a business man knows what such a project necessarily mounts to, makes a preliminary sketch and floor-plans for a house that will cost in the neighborhood of a hundred thousand dollars probably more. He is quite as bewildered when told by the owner that half of that amount is the utmost sum permissible, as the owner is to hear that what he wants is not to be thought of for twice his intended outlay. A more experienced architect would have avoided such a misunderstanding, perhaps by starting a dialogue such as this:

Architect: "You know, of course, that a house of — let us see, twenty rooms, built of limestone, with enrichment of ornamentation, and with a slate roof, will run well past a hundred thousand for the building — apart from decorations of the interior. Is that all right?”

Client: "Not right at all!”

Architect: "What price had you in mind?”

Client: "Fifty thousand is the utmost that I will spend

on the house, including a garage.”

Architect: "Then you will have to build a very different

type of house.”

Client thereupon builds a stucco or wooden house, with rather simple interior, or else reduces the house in size — or gives up the project of building.

FIGURING THE APPROXIMATE COST

It is difficult, on the other hand, for an architect to estimate exactly. But he must be either irresponsible or inexperienced not to be able to come within a fair approximation; in fact, you can easily make a rough estimate by determining the cubic footage of the building, and multiplying it by the probable cost of its type of construction. In a location where labor is highest, and for completely fireproof construction, such as apartments and office buildings in New York City, one must allow from seventy to seventy-five cents a cubic foot. In the country, where building materials are local or easily available, the price for a simple frame house can be as low as forty cents — possibly even lower — s. cubic foot. A house twenty-five feet wide by fifty feet long with a sevenfoot cellar, nine-foot first floor, and eight-foot dormered second floor allowing a foot each for cellar, first and second floors — would figure out about as follows: Height, 7 plus 9 plus 8 plus 3, equals 27, which, multiplied by 25 (width), equals 675, and this, multiplied by 50 (length), equals 33,750 cubic feet, which, at thirty-five cents a foot, equals $11,812.50. This would be the least amount possible, and, of course, is exclusive of paint — which is considered decoration and is usually extra. The most expensive construction would come to $23,625, and other specifications would come variously between.

It might be well to attempt some such calculation as this before deciding to build at all. Also, before consulting an architect, you should make a careful list of your personal requirements. Unless you do this you will forget to mention half of the details you "cannot live without,” the architect will omit them from his preliminary plans, and you will be shocked by their additional cost.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

RECKONING WITH EXTRAS

The additional costs because of "extras,” which nearly every house-builder experiences, can be overcome in but one way: study the plans, visualize the details thoroughly before the contracts are let, and then make no changes. It is the endless adding of this item or that, that runs up the bills like boiling-over coffee.

"While we are about it, let us make the house two feet longer, let us have the windows six inches wider. Two feet canH cost much! Six inches are scarcely enough to bother about.” And yet — two feet added to a frame house of average construction, forty feet long and two stories high, will cost about two thousand dollars more, counting only fifty cents a cubic foot, and three to four thousand for a house of more expensive construction. The original windows were probably to be of "stock” size; adding six inches is not merely a question of so many inches of glass and frame, but an alteration in manufacture which exacts that they be hand-made, one by one, instead of machine-made in quantity, and therefore obviously cheaper.

Remember also that everyone from the architect down to the workmen’s helpers is apt to underestimate both time and amount of material, and the resulting cost. This, of course, is true of all of us, and in every situation in life. It always takes longer to do whatever we are doing than we think it is going to, unless it is part of a daily (and therefore timed) routine. This, therefore, is of utmost importance: either hiild your hotise according to a definite contract, and make no changes, or be prepared to pay more than the average person would expect or imagine. Remember that every shelf put up, every hook, rod, fixture, cupboard or coat of paint — every item of decoration, floor polish or linoleum laying, electric fixtures, special hardware, mantles, additional quarter-round of wood, whether added to cornice or to wall — ^is an extra if it was not itemized in the original contract.

It is the architect’s duty to tell the client this or that extra

will cost so much more. Generally he does. But unless each extra is written down, signed for and clipped to the contract — as it should be — ^the client has not the slightest idea that the cost is boiling over until he has paid out the amount of the original estimate, and finds that there is anywhere from ten to fifty per cent, additional cost still due.

HOW TO KEEP WITHIN THE ESTIMATE

If you make no changes the builder cannot charge more than the amount specified in his signed contract. Personally I have known of but two owners who made no changes. The first accepted the architect’s plans for the house, including its interior decoration, much of its furnishings and all of its domestic equipment, signed the bids of the contractor and the landscape architect, and then sailed for Europe and never even wrote a letter about the work. ^JC^hen he returned the entire place was completed, even to the unpacking of items of furniture which had been collected in Europe.

The other client stayed in the neighborhood, but made only one minor change, for which a clause in writing was added to the builder’s contract. In both cases the final bills were necessarily identical with the amounts agreed upon in the contracts.

One of the reasons for extra costs is that many owners cannot visualize the house from a plan, so that when its walls are up (it always looks half its size in rough plaster) he insists upon enlarging this and changing that, without giving a thought to the hours of labor and items of material that are mounting and mounting. Persons to whom mere figures— so many by so many feet— mean nothing, should have the sizes explained to them by measuring rooms that they are at present living in; closets, bathrooms, and all other details should be gone into, especially for a house that is small. The architect, to whom a plan is far more distinct than a finished building, rarely if ever understands that a client, no matter how much he looks at the plans, may quite possibly visualize

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

almost nothing, and therefore begins to make changes after the walls are up !

However, let us go back to the beginning, and follow the procedure of building in detail. Before making one decision as to location, style, or size, the average property owner must first make his arrangements for financing the enterprise.

FINANCING THE BUILDING

To the inexperienced, the very word "mortgage” brings to mind the favorite plot of melodrama — the "old home” sold over the heads of the aging mother and father by the flint-hearted rich man who holds them in his power by means of the fatal mortgage.

To mortgage your home in order to buy a pleasure car or to take a trip abroad — unless the first is to further the family’s bread-winning assets, such as a car for a physician, or the second is to make the necessary preparation for a career, such as that of an artist or architect — is to court the melodrama’s danger in earnest. But to finance the building of one’s home by means of a mortgage or building loan is to do something that is done by at least ninety-eight persons out of every hundred.

In fact, all business men — even those who can easily, if they choose, pay for the building many times over — prefer to finance the operation rather than tie up that amount of capital in a form that will make it inactive. And for the countless salaried business men and women who earn good incomes, but who have little or no capital, building a house to be paid for in instalments is far more practical than continuing to pay rent, since the renter has no prospect of betterment — ever.

In order to build, it is necessary to have a certain amount of ready cash to start with, the amount depending upon the prospects of the property, and upon whether you have a fixed permanent mortgage or a building loan. In the first case, you can borrow as much as fifty per cent, of the selling price

from any bank. This leaves the other fifty per cent, to be paid in cash at the outset, from your own savings. If you are going to buy an old house to improve, the bank may lend you as much as sixty or seventy per cent, of the cost, if the improvements are extensive and the bank considers that you have greatly enhanced the value of the property.

TWO TYPES OF MORTGAGE

A permanent mortgage is one that runs for a long period at an unchanging rate. When it finally comes due, the bank may be willing to renew it — if the value of your property has been kept up — but is sure to demand a reduction of the principal if the mortgage was for as much as half of its original value and the property has run down. This type of mortgage, if too large, is therefore obviously dangerous.

The other type of mortgage, known as an amortizing mortgage, and available through reputable building and loan associations, will let you have a loan amounting to sixty per cent. — sometimes even seventy or eighty per cent. — of the value of a well-built structure in a location of especially good promise. An amortizing mortgage of this kind is paid off gradually at stated intervals — exactly as payment is made for anything else on the instalment plan. You pay, usually, twelve per cent, a year on the amount of the mortgage. Six per cent, of this is interest on the money borrowed, and the other six per cent, is paid-back principal.

In other words, if you borrow ten thousand dollars, you pay twelve hundred dollars the first year; six hundred dollars in interest, which is rent of money, instead of ordinary rent of house, and six hundred is paid back on the mortgage. So that at the beginning of the second year you have a mortgage on your house of nine thousand four hundred dollars instead of ten thousand. The amount of the amortization varies from as little as two per cent., on business buildings, to as high as ten per cent, on dwellings and apartment buildings, but the average amount paid by "home builders” is six per cent.

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

Although it is usually best to own the house you live in, it might be well — in order to make sure you are wise in buying rather than renting — to do a sum in simple arithmetic, because situations vary and advantage is sometimes on the renting side. If, for instance, you are paying $1000 a year rent on a piece of property worth $20,000, you are paying only five per cent, on the actual capital invested, and the landlord, while receiving only five per cent, gross, is paying at least $600 a year in taxes and repairs. In other words, he is receiving only $400 net — or two per cent. — on his investment. In this case you would be idiotic to change places with him.

But if you are paying $1000 a year on a house worth $10,000 the case is different. While you would have to pay a higher rental to buy it, the rent would decrease slightly each year, and at the end of a set period you would own your house outright. And if you are paying $1000 a year rent on property worth $5000, the sooner you become its owner the better. In other words, the desirability of owning increases as the value of the investment decreases in proportion to the rental.

Suppose that you are paying $1200 rent on a $12,000 valuation, and that you have only $2000 saved as a building fund. A loan association, however, is willing to let you borrow $10,000 on the property. If you take that loan and buy the house, your rent is increased to $1500 the first year, and each year thereafter decreases slightly. Taking the sum of $10,000 that you borrow as an approximate figure (because it is the easiest sum to figure on), and supposing that the house and land will cost $12,000 complete, the table of the operation will be as follows:
