After the seventeenth year the expense of buying is ended, and the house is owned in full.

The amounts allowed for repairs in this case are rather low; but on the other hand, those of a small house, whose owner is capable of making minor ones himself, may not be even as high as noted. Repairs and interest are a little like a seesaw; as one goes up, the other comes down. A new house, if at all well built, should have no repairs of any account. As the interest decreases, the repairs grow larger, so that the one is somewhat offset by the other.

The foregoing table merely indicates what it would cost — plus barest fixed charges — to buy a $12,000 house on which you borrow $10,000. If you borrow less, your payments are less; or if you keep a permanent mortgage, as noted in the first instance, you pay six per cent, on the money borrowed without adding the six per cent, for paying off any part of it. Your interest on $10,000 is fixed at $600 a year. Or on a $10,000 house for which you pay $5000 in ready cash and $5000 in borrowed money, the interest on the loan is $300 a year. Your rent is represented at $600, however, because you should allow for the interest lost on the $5000 cash you put in.

A house costing $120,000 would multiply every figure by ten, while a little bungalow costing $1200 would divide by ten instead. If the amortization on $10,000 is ten per cent, instead of six, you pay $1000 "on instalment” and $600 for

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

interest, the first year. The second year you pay on $9000, and so on.

At all events the loan — whether large or small — is arranged by your real estate agent; or if you own the land, the bank will make the loan for you.

PLANNING FOR SOME DAY

There is another way to pay for your house gradually, without doing any "financing” at all — but only if the size of your family permits, and if the house you are building is larger than your minimum need. Of course, if your family consists of many members who cannot be packed into less space than you have allowed for in the finished building, borrowing on an amortizing mortgage is the best answer to your problem. Or if, for reasons of your own, you choose to keep a fixed mortgage of from thirty to forty per cent, of the value of the house on your building, that, as already noted, is a sound procedure. But if, on the other hand, you are a family of two — newly married, perhaps — no way of acquiring a home is as sound and as economical as that of building by instalments.

This means building the least amount of your house that you can manage to be half-way comfortable in, and completing it part by part as you save. In the bank-loan method, you get what you want to start with, and pay afterwards, little by little, plus interest — which at six per cent, on $10,000 totals $5304 — or more than half again as much as the amount borrowed. In building part by part, these interest charges are saved, the house is cleared of debt sooner, but you have to wait for its completion instead of getting it at once.

Or suppose you do not wish to borrow any money. You have six thousand dollars and are going to build a six thousand dollar house instead of the ten thousand dollar one you want (or any other amount — the sum does not matter) . It would be far better to build a first unit of your ultimate ideal

than to build at once a completed but inadequate house that is not at all what you really like, and that can never be made into anything different.

If you are going to adopt this course, you naturally explain your intention to your architect, so that he can make his design with this end in view. Unlimited varieties of plans and exterior beautifully adapt themselves to this sort of instalment building, but there are certain unelastic others that do not take kindly to piecemeal building, either without or within.

SOME HINTS ON THE PIECEMEAL PLAN

And yet the best way to approach this method of building is first to complete the plans of the eventual house, exactly as you hope some day to have it, and then to begin with the barest essentials. If you are going to have a coal range, it is doubly necessary to build the kitchen. On the other hand, if you are going to use oil or gas or electricity to cook with, the future pantry might serve as temporary kitchen. Or if you build the permanent kitchen with chimney for the range, the pantry of the future is perhaps used as a dining alcove, to be closed off later from the dining-room — which is now serving as the living-room. In this selection, be sure to lay the piping for the future pantry’s sink.

Or possibly you build the front hall and stairs before completing the back; or perhaps your original entrance is a side or rear one, and back stairs serve to take you up to your present sleeping quarters, which will some day be the servant’s wing.

Or perhaps you choose — and are able — to build the front hall and permanent stairway and your own bedroom with bath (or baths) , closets, and sleeping porch complete. Perhaps you build the big living-room at once, in which you eat as well as live, and have a small temporary kitchenette that later is to become a lavatory and coat-room.

But however much you build, it must be divided ver-

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

tically — never horizontally — into sections. Each unit must be built from cellar to roof. In other words, never build a bungalow, intending later to add a second story which will necessitate tearing off the roof and building a new one. You can leave second-story space, if desired, in the shape of an unfinished attic ; but ceiling height, windows and dormers should be put in at the beginning.

Wherever a room or other addition is to go, the connecting door frame must be set and temporarily plastered in, so that no future tearing out of walls need be resorted to; and of course all piping and wiring must be laid, and preparation for future connections boxed in on the outside, so that when new building is in progress, wires and pipes are all at hand without coming into the rooms you are living in.

There is one quite inevitable trick of the imagination that offsets impatience, and even to a great degree mitigates the makeshift of those who must look forward rather long to the completion of their some-day house — which is that the vision of the house is inevitably supplied by the blank doors, the beam-supported wall-space roughly plastered over, but ready to be broken through the moment the room or hall or loggia on the other side is ready and waiting.

Irregular styles of architecture lend their help somewhat better to unit-by-unit building than do those which are very regular.

The English house of the general Tudor type, or the simple farm-house type of building, is pliant, tractable and amiably yielding to the retrenchment or enlargement of its owner’s needs. It is especially suitable for the plan that is to be completed in sections, since one complete building can be added to, by indefinite numbers of sister buildings. The blockshaped Georgian, or any type of Formal house, on the other hand, looks maimed if built only in part, and lends itself no better to afterthought additions.

About the only way to plan for a Formal house to be built piecemeal is to begin with a central block and add a wing first on one side and then later on the other. Or even

begin with a wing and a slice of the back, putting on the front next and the balancing wing last.

AGAIN WE RETURN TO THE ARCHITECT

\

Apart from making a suitable plan for unit building, financial arrangements are entirely outside of the province of the architect, although it is ethically his duty to see that you are given best value for what you spend on building and on whichever part of the decoration he designs. You must be sure, therefore, to fit into the total sums you have arranged for, a margin for extras, for furnishing, and for putting the grounds in order, and for the architect’s- fee, which is usually from six to ten per cent, of the total cost of the building that he has designed. This includes excavations, grading — only in so far as it is preparation as a foundation for the house — all building by carpenters, masons, brick-layers, plumbers, electricians, and the work of painters on the exterior and on the architectural decorations of the interior.

If the architect completes the structural decoration of the rooms — which he should — or designs the garden, which he does rarely, except for very important Italian gardens or Spanish patios which are fundamentally architectural, these items are also included in the total on which he gets ten per cent.

VARIATIONS OF COST

The least expensive material that can be used is wood (barring adobe in desert sections). The cheapest construction is that which is most like a box. Bay windows, dormers — all things that cut into or stand out from wall or roof surfaces — add to the cost of construction.

The house of greatest beauty at least expense is the "frame house” with either shingle or clapboard walls and shingle roof. The frame house includes every variety of Colonial that is not of brick, as well as endless varieties of "just houses”

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

and bungalows. Log cabins are cheap only when built in a forest of pine or spruce or other tall, straight-growing trees.

The frame house of highest type is built of brick or hollow tile covered with wood, and has a well-bred quality of being better than it pretends. The reverse of this construction is the house of brick veneer over an under-frame of wood. There is no actual objection to this, however, even though it lacks the ethical quality of the reverse just noted.

Woods vary in value from spruce and fir to cedar and oak for use on the exterior, and from fir and pine to oak and mahogany, walnut, cypress, maple, and pine for the interior. They vary, moreover, in quality from planks of rough and porous surface that splinter into hang-nails and drink paint or stain like a sponge — and so unevenly that the finished result looks woolly — to fine woods of firm and even surface that in time becomes as smoothly glazed as Chinese porcelain.

There should be no fear as to the durability of wood. It will last, at all events, for a dozen generations, which is longer than the house-builder or his children or grandchildren need worry about. It is true, wooden roof-shingles have to be replaced every twenty years, and wooden steps about every other spring; but they are exceptions. I would recommend that front steps be of brick or stone as a measure of economy, since wooden steps get down at heel with amazing facility. But wooden repairs are inexpensive when continuously attended to. A frame house will tumble to pieces if not looked after, but so will a neglected brick house, or even a stone one.

Stucco over hollow tile is of about the same cost as either combination of wood and brick. Stucco over wire lath fastened to wood-frame costs considerably less, and, properly done, is usually quite satisfactory. But if one can afford to pay the difference, hollow tile is much to be preferred, not only because it is fireproof but because it is practically an insurance against cracking, which is the flaw of stucco when applied to an unstable foundation.

The walls of nearly all Italian and Spanish houses, and of nearly all "apparently” open-timbered Normandy cottages

and English houses, as well as those of endless other varieties, are of stucco with roofs of tile (real or composition) . Several modern renderings in stucco of Colonial, including Georgian, have been very successful — especially when the stucco surface is almost plaster-fine.

Brick is higher in cost than tile and stucco. The cost of brick increases according to quality, and according to whether the brick-yard is at a distance or near by.

The price of all building material is greatly increased if brought by freight; a fact easily understandable if one considers the weight, for instance, of even the very smallest brick house! Fortunately, the ordinary pinkish "common” brick is often far more beautiful in its porous surface, varying color, and faculty for acquiring the "softened look of age” because of its tendency when close to the ground to acquire a tinge of — let us call it patina. More expensive bricks are sometimes beautiful but quite as often they are merely hard and tight-grained and sharp-edged and as beautifying as blocks of iron.

As for the threatening Persian Invasion of black bricks, brown bricks, maroon bricks, and leanings toward patterns and shadings and blendings of peacock and yellow and rose and green — such ingredients do not make beautiful houses, but hideosities. Therefore, let us copy gardens and murals from the Persians, but leave their bricks alone!

Stone — The cost of stone is much higher than of brick — unless poor stone- work is taken in comparison with best brick- work. Smooth-finished stone is the most expensive material (next to marble) that there is. The rougher the stone, and the more local its nature, the less the cost. In localities where field-stone abounds, especially when it is found on the same property, a house of stone can be built at a very reasonable cost in the way known as rubble. (Do not confuse rubble with "cobble.” Rubblework has great charm, whereas round, rain-washed stones jutting out from the walls like pans full of biscuits are an offense to good taste.)

The cost of stone naturally increases enormously in pro-

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

portion to its distance from the quarry and to the method of setting. Walls of evenly squared stones beautifully mortised mount up to many times the cost of those which are built of irregularly dressed field-stone, set into a wall of mortar like plums in a pudding.

Mountain graystone or granite that must be quarried is always expensive — but magnificently durable. The cost of steel-frame, hollow-tile and concrete construction, with stone facing and with stone or slate or tile roof, runs from about eighty cents a square foot — with wood-trim — to a dollar, even a dollar and a half or more, for a house of great elaboration of detail, such as certain Tudor country estates of marked magnificence.

WHAT PRICE TUDOR?

The Enghsh Tudor house of perfectly imitated old weathered stone, with rain-and-wind-polished carving, and patined metal, has been achieved in many examples by one of to-day’s greatest architects — but at a PRICE! Very straightforward rough-dressed stone houses with wood-trim and first-class fireproof roof can be built as low as seventy cents a cubic foot. Brick for about sixty cents. These figures are merely approximate, because details of finish and of location greatly affect the cost.

Half-Timber — Real half-timber with brick nogging is very expensive — even as expensive as stone. "Real” open timber means that the studs and beams and supports are set up of solid wood (weathered oak usually) with their front faces left exposed and the spaces between filled in with brick, laid in various patterns.

Imitation half- timber houses are built of stucco, and then boards stained dark are put on top of the stucco in imitation of actual timber construction.

The most expensive building material is marble — very rarely chosen for a complete house, but often used for part, or all, of the trim of Georgian houses of brick. Trim includes

quoins — blocks of marble set into the brick corners, originally devised to interlock right-angled walls (as in any small wooden box) , but now merely ornamental.

The less expensive brick house has doors and trim and quoins of wood, painted white.

The materials of the exterior necessarily influence the cost of the interior. A brick-and-marble Georgian house would naturally promise a lovely oval or double, or that most beautiful feature of a Colonial interior, the free-standing stair; spacious, high-ceilinged rooms with elaborate cornices and trim; paneled walls and marble — or hand-wrought wood — mantles. Stone and half- timber houses of Tudor or Elizabethan importance presuppose an important and richly carved staircase of pollard oak, and high wainscoted rooms and open-timbered ceiling. Real open-timbered ceilings show the actual floor beams overhead, requiring that they be smooth-finished beams instead of the rough-finished boards set edgewise of usual construction. Ceilings are often made of plaster in marvelous imitation of wood.

Italian and Spanish houses are expensive chiefly because of tile roofs, floors, and stair treads, and beautiful wroughtiron work. Roof tiles are pressed one by one over the knees of the workmen who make them. The trim, however, is lacking, doors are often absent, walls are left in the rough.

The least exacting style of house — barring the bungalow— is the simple one of shingles or clapboard with shingle roof and plain board trim inside, and with equally plain furnishing. The utterly primitive house of Tudor example with unpamted open beams, unpainted wood without and within, may easily be quite expensive, since timbers hand-hewn and planed are in this day quite the opposite of reasonable in price. The element of price, indeed, segregates the Tudor, Jacobean, Georgian and French chateau as suitable only for those whose purse is very deep; the tile-roofed Italian, Spanish, and all stone and brick houses as being of considerable outlay ; and houses of wood, of whitewashed field stone, or of stucco with Spanish or Italian accents as being moder-

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

ate in cost. Houses of wood are cheapest. Every deviation from straight flat walls and straight back and front gable roof adds to cost.

THE OWNER AND THE CONTRACTOR

If you have no architect, you and the contractor build the house, and you assume the responsibility that belongs normally to the architect. But if you employ an architect you have no personal business relations with the contractor except through the architect.

When the architect’s plans have been approved — at his own estimate — and detail drawings have been completed, you and your architect together decide upon the contractor. Sometimes a contractor is selected from the beginning and no "bids” are given out. But more often — especially for a house of any importance — duplicate blue-prints of plans and carbons of specifications are sent out by the architect to several contractors who bid on them, meaning that each names the price at which he is willing to sign a contract to build the house according to the plans and specifications that were sent to him.

The bids, which are received together at a certain hour on a certain day, are always contained in sealed envelopes, and these are opened by the architect in the presence of the owner. The advantages of each are then discussed and one of them is selected.

It is usually supposed that the lowest bidder secures the job, but this is not necessarily true. For instance, if a contractor sends in a bid that is much lower than any other — lower than the architect’s own quite expert calculations make possible — this bidder’s willingness to build at an obvious loss would warrant a close investigation of the matter. One reason might be, in the case of an unusually important job, a legitimate willingness on the part of the builder to stand a loss as a pure matter of advertising, or of keeping his organization going in slender times. He may believe that

his loss on this building will be repaid to him in reputation and future jobs. This reason cannot well be offered, however, in the case of an unimportant house. Another reason is that a builder may be so inexperienced or so incompetent as to make a serious miscalculation.

A third reason is one of which the architect of experience is always chary. A too-low bid from a builder of experience is sometimes an evidence of the unscrupulous contractor who underbids, with intent to freeze out his more conscientious competitors. This type of "squeezer” is usually one who gambles on being able to substitute inferior material and workmanship or to make the extras so numerous and so confusing in their unending repetition of "hours’ labor,” lumber, "hours’ labor,” nails, cement, "hours’ labor,” paint, "hours’ labor,” bricks, "hours’ labor” — page after page — until no one, not even the architect, can make head or tail of it. It is impossible to keep tab on such a builder, unless the house is big enough to pay for a superintendent who never leaves the job and who makes itemized lists of materials and daily time-sheets of "hours’ labor.”

DETAILS OF AN ARCHITECT’S COSTS

Before leaving the subject of costs it might be well to explain a few details of cost to the architect which unaccustomed house-builders rarely take into consideration.

A bill that countless clients consider unfair is the bill for drawings which have to be discarded because the client changes his mind. When an architect makes plans and sketches and they are accepted — even after considerable alteration — the cost is included in the architect’s regulation percentage on the finished building. But when the house is discarded for an entirely new design, or the project possibly is given up altogether, the architect necessarily sends in a bill for office cost in preparing these drawings.

To the inexperienced, this bill often seems enormous and unjust, because not one person in fifty has the least idea — or

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

stops to think — how much architectural drawings cost. In a business office, the average salary is moderate; many clerks occupy small space, and, as often as not, work by artificial light. An architect’s draftsmen and tenderers are all experts who get high salaries; each must have space for a big drafting table, and brilliant daylight is essential. At present-day rentals, the overhead of a drafting room is appalling. Paper and blue-printing alone are items that run into thousands of dollars a year in an office of average size.

There is another phase of cost- — social wastage, we might call it — to which architects, more than men of other professions, not even excepting lawyers, are subjected. A client calls on a business man or lawyer and discusses business. When the discussion is over, the client leaves. But an architect is, for reasons that have never been explained, looked upon as an addition to the social life of the family. If an architect is agreeable, cultivated, intelligent, as architects are rather notably apt to be; and if he is young, the house-builder assumes that he is his (or more especially her) property.

Let us say, for example, that John Robinson, a young architect of talent, is given the Jones house to build. He is much pleased. Mrs. Jones is a charming woman. Mr. Jones is a likeable man. The house promises to be a good one; moreover, it will help carry office expenses, so that he can work on an important competition, which, should he win it, would "make” him. He will have plenty of time to complete his design for the competition by devoting five or six hours a day to it, until the contest closes. By working ten or at most twelve hours, he can easily (he thinks) take care of the Jones project and continue to superintend the jobs he has in hand — jobs that are small and time-taking. The Jones house is seemingly a gift from Heaven.

And then! Mrs. Jones comes to his office and talks and talks. He finds that she is rather vague, but inclined to like the idea of a frame Colonial house set in a field. So he makes tentative plans of very simple construction. Mrs. Jones is enthusiastic. Mr. Jones thinks they look all right, and asks

Robinson how much the house will cost. Robinson says it should cost approximately so much. Mr. Jones is pleased and everything is sunshine. Then, little by little, Mrs. Jones decides she wants a lot of changes, and is entirely indefinite as to what they shall be. She changes her mind about Colonial and thinks she prefers a French house. She has some French chairs and a set of Louis XIV bedroom furniture. The Colonial house is discarded and Robinson designs a double-winged Provengal house. She does not recognize it as "French,” so he sketches a chateau type with high slate roof. She is delighted with the sketch, but seems to understand little or nothing of the plans. He draws sketches of interiors so that she can better understand.

She likes to watch him draw, praises his pencil facility, but seems as vague as ever in her acceptance or rejection of his plans. Gradually she assumes that young Robinson is a species of paid companion. She calls him up at all hours to come at once and decide about the house. When he arrives she talks ten minutes about it and hours about herself, her artistic tastes, her appreciation of the beautiful. What she won’t talk about is how big each room must be, how many rooms she must have, or whether the first, second or third floor plan is satisfactory. Finally, she definitely decides on a stone Cotswold type of house, and, acting on a rather hazy outline of her requirements, the architect designs a house that he thinks would be suitable to the tastes and needs of the Jones family. He completes a set of plans and elevations, spending about six hours in sleep and the other eighteen trying to get the Jones house definitely decided upon, and struggling to finish his drawings for the competition. By this time Mrs. Jones has added innumerable rooms and increased sizes, besides changing from rather primitive wood construction to excessively expensive stone — which is not quarried within hundreds of miles of the Jones location — and Mr. Jones is absolutely aghast at the price. Remembering nothing but the roughly estimated total of the farm-house plan Mr. Jones is convinced that young Robinson is incompetent, if

IF YOU ARE GOING TO BUILD

not wilfully trying to force them to build an unnecessarily expensive house. At all events, negotiations are terminated, and Robinson sends in his bill for costs of rejected drawings, which is protested. Robinson sues; Mr. Jones pays, but Robinson has npt only lost the Jones good-will, but he has also lost his chance in the competition because the Jones house took too much of his time.

This is an extreme example, but cases approaching it can be counted by the hundreds.

One last word from the point of view of the architect: Since the operation of building is essentially a partnership between yourself and him, it is not unreasonable to ask that you give at least a little thought to his side of the case.

WHAT YOU CAN DO FOR OR AGAINST YOUR ARCHITECT

How many times has a house-owner said to you: "The design of the house was entirely my own. I merely had an architect to work out the mechanical details.” Which is very like saying: "I diagnosed appendicitis myself — the surgeon merely operated.”

The worst thing you can do against your architect — something much worse than claiming credit for his design — is to discuss the plans for your house with every friend, neighbor and acquaintance, and change your mind about this detail or that with each opinion.

And what you can do for your architect is to discuss privately all points about which you and your family can’t agree, thresh them out by yourselves, settle them, and then tell him. A still more important thing to do for him is to trust him, and to realize that unless you do, he can’t look after your interests in the way you expect him to, and in the way he has every intention of doing. Moreover, do not put him in a false position by giving direct orders either to the contractors or to their workmen. The architect, who is alone responsible for the success of the building, must be in sole control of the job.

VI

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

T^O one who genuinely loves houses — both inside and out — there is no occupation so fascinating as restoring a once beautiful house that has been maimed, or discovering the possibilities in one of seemingly hopeless ugliness, or solving the problem of a honeycomb plan. Of course, the chief attraction of remodeling is not merely that the operation can, in many instances, be attempted by the owner, but that few new houses can compete with the appealing personality of an old one. Not only are its trees and bushes — if it have any! — inimitably becoming, but there are always telltale evidences of its long past — apparent in slightly softened edges of window-frames, chimneys, door-sills and the color of its stone or brick — that are missing in the blank regularities of the house that is still an unprinted page.

The problems of remodeling naturally vary from the simplest ones of making a few interior changes or an addition at the back, to complete remodeling — either in harmony with the original design or in accord with a new design that changes it into a different type of house. Between these two extremes the first of which can be done quite easily by almost any one, and the second of which, if the house is at all important, should be attempted only by an expert — are hundreds of midway remodelings that can be done by almost any owner of cultivated taste assisted by a good builder.

If it is hard for you to see possibilities in imagination, then by all means take an architect with you when you go house-hunting; but if, on the other hand, you are of those who mentally reconstruct every not-pleasing house that you

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

look at, your difficulty is not likely to be in finding one worth redoing, but in deciding which to choose of the many ’that offer enticing possibilities. If you are looking at this place and at that, however, and have no idea which of several will take most kindly to becoming your home, it may perhaps be easier to make your choice if you realize that houses of definite style, especially formal ones, necessarily exact adherence to the conventions of their types. You can’t tag runways and lean-tos and odd outbuildings to "mansions” of brick and stone or marble. But you can take a barn and a chicken-house and almost any odd farm-building, move them about and put them together in whichever grouping a perceiving eye directs, and achieve a pleasing irregularity that lends itself to delightful planting of other unconventionalities of a similar nature. Moreover, hundreds of insignificant, unpleasing or downright ugly houses can be turned into dwellings of charm — if their proportions are within reason — sometimes even if they are not.

But let us leave all considerations of outside beauty or ugliness for a later part of this chapter, and begin by assuming that if you are so much interested in a house that you are thinking of buying it, the probabilities are that the outside is attractive as it is. Therefore, let us choose a lovely old Colonial house; and in order to make our first example easy, let us say that its plan is of the hall-through-the-middle and rooms-on-either-side variety; that it looks unattractive because its wall-papers are in varieties of shabby brown, and that it seems uncomfortable because it has neither electric light nor plumbing — but let us add quickly that both are available, (^^e will take up their lack, later.) Even when considering the simplest plan, it is best to lay it out on paper. This is the first thing every architect does; one of the easiest things in the world to do — but one which very few prospective house-buyers ever think of or seem to know

about.

You will need a few sheets of cross-barred paper and a rule or tape measure of some sort; if you buy one, get a

fifty-foot surveyor’s tape that winds up on a covered spool, because it eliminates laying a lot of short rule-lengths end to end across the floor and adding them up in your mind — • with occasional mistakes. ^J^ith a long tape you merely get some one to hold the end at each point that you want to start from, and then the distance to which you pull it out, whatever it may be, is marked in feet and inches. With a two-foot rule you have to add six twos and seventeen; on a surveyor’s tape you read *'13 feet 5 inches.” At all events you measure every wall length and mark it on the crossbarred paper. Regular engineers’ or architects’ paper, subdivided into eighths of an inch, gives you a standard scale; but any sort of cross-barred pad-paper will do, no matter what the size. In any case, you count one square as a foot, and mark the total feet of wall length as so many squares. Put down the exact length of each wall in each room, and the positions of windows and doors. In making this rough "layout” it is not necessary to bother about the thickness of walls except where blocked by a chimney or other obstruction.

It is necessary to measure all irregularities in shape and to put the rooms next to each other as they belong, so that with the plan of each floor (which is automatically accurate) in front of you, you can see the pattern of it as a whole,

and know exactly how much space there is, and how hard _

or how easy— it will be to open doors through, or to add or move partitions, as the need may be.

If it lays itself out” nicely, it may be unnecessary to consult an architect. For instance, let us say that the plan we are considering is about like Figure 38. Then, on the first floor, let us say that we decide merely to knock out the wall between the front and back parlors and to add a sun-porch at the left side, making the most typical modern small-house first-floor plan known. A garage on one side possibly balances the sun-porch on the other— if not spoiling to the kitchen.

Or if there be a garden at the back, it might appeal

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

to you to change the kitchen and dining-room about; to omit the kitchen-screened porch at the right side, but to put a sun-porch or terrace or both across the back.

TYPICAL PLAN OF HOUSE TO BE REMODELED

For such a simple change of interior as either Figure 39 or Figure 40, an architect would not be necessary; but per-

A SIMPLE PLAN FOR REMODELING THE SAME HOUSE

haps if you make as much exterior change as that shown in Figure 41, with the addition of bay windows and two balancing outbuildings, it might be well to consult one — especially if you have to add dormers to the third floor for servants’ rooms, or if you build two-story wings. At all events, with a straightforward plan of the sort that calls for a hall through the middle and rooms on either side, changes are seldom difficult to make.

But a honeycomb plan is quite another story. For instance, a plan that is typical of Colonial New England is shown in Figure 42. This is a big house with twelve bedrooms but with uncomfortably adjusted space from the point of view of modern living. When these houses were built, mother and father occupied the central bedroom, and the children were put in cubicles all around and were unable to get out or come in without going through their parents’ room. Unless you, who buy such a house, use the principal bedroom on the second floor as hall space, there is no way for the occupants of six rooms to get out except through room No. 1 ; and the occupant of No. 8 must pass through No. 7 as well as No. 1. Many houses of this plan have an attic in place of bedrooms 7 and 8. Others have only one floor, but nearly all have the small rooms around the diningroom. In a few examples, the only second-floor room reached by the stairs is No. 2, in which case No. 1 has no direct entrance. Of course, endless solutions are possible according to the needs of the owner. For example, let us say that you need three masters’ rooms, all large, and three servants’ rooms and a guest room. One change would be as in Figure 43.

This or any other plan is capable of being altered into dozens of patterns, though the working out of the inside

NUMBER ONE - MY OWN HOUSE

The original dining-room, which was the biggest room, was naturally chosen for living-room, and the water view side was chosen for dining-room and sun-room. The kitchen was left unchanged. The only place possible for the servants’ rooms was in the old parlor space. The necessity of getting from the kitchen to the front door made an elbow-hall imperative — an undesirable feature which^ in this instance, I could not overcome. Even so, I think the space is pretty solidly packed and no essential of convenience is left out.

As already said, it is fairly safe to change an Interior without consulting an architect, but if you are making changes on the outside — that is apt to be quite another story!

TWO SIMPLE WAYS TO STUDY HOUSES

In case you are one of those who share my own obsessing interest in rebuilding, it may be well to explain two pet methods of studying houses; they are not at all professional methods, but as they have been very useful to ine, they might perhaps be useful, sometime, to you. For in-

Stance, let us say that there is a lovely old place for sale with a rather ugly house on it, and that you would buy it if the house could be made less unattractive. Or let us say that you are considering improving the exterior of your own house — that you want to solve the problem yourself, and not turn it over to an architect. You know something is wrong with its outline, and you have long considered whether it would look better (or worse) were you to make this change, or that!

My pet method No. 1 is to take snap-shots of it, face on, both side faces, and back, then have a dozen prints made, and experiment by cutting away what might better come off, or by drawing on what might be changed or added. It is quite extraordinary how accurately the results can be seen, even with the crudest alteration of the photographs. You can see perfectly what a raised or lowered roof will do by cutting the picture off at the roof edge and either pushing it up or pulling it down. You can see how it will look with a new door (drawn and pasted on) ; with a gray Faber pencil — No. 57 — you can make white paper look just about the color of a typical solio print, so that you can fill in blank gaps and make the facade seem whole. At all events, you can try cutting off, adding on, and remaking to your heart’s content, and if yoif succeed in getting what you like, then measure the size of whatever is necessary, by measuring from known point to known point on the picture. In other words, instead of having to know the proper proportions by means of tables or slide-rule or other forms of mathematics, you

when it looks right, then find out how big it must be made by seeing where the height or width comes on the building. Although foreshortened distance must be allowed for, with a few known measurements to go by, you can at least come close enough to decide what can — or can not — be done.

My pet method No. 2 is making a paper model. This is not merely of use to an architect in showing an owner who is unable to see the house plan from an elevation drawing,

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

but invaluable in straightening out perplexities if you are doing alterations yourself.

In remodeling the second house just described, I not only wanted to show the owner how it would look when finished, but I wanted to see how the roof with the flat-topped dormers between the gables was going to look. For this purpose I made a paper model. A little paper house is not difl&cult to make — on a scale of one-eighth of an inch to a foot. You merely draw each side very carefully as to measure, but roughly as you like in detail. Tint it with colored pencils, and cut the sides with wide margins to glue with. Bend its walls forward and back at each corner, and then glue on as many pieces of paper as necessary to make each slant of the roof. Naturally, you must allow for all irregularities — which are most easily added as separate boxes — and make separate patterns of the additions or changes you contemplate.

However, let us go back to the subject of exterior beautification, where we are faced, in the very beginning, with this practical question;

IS IT ALWAYS PRACTICAL TO BEAUTIFY AN UGLY HOUSE?

Taking ugliness as meaning structural defect, it must be confessed that operations involving structural alterations of the exterior are apt to be expensive, possibly too expensive for the average owner to attempt. Of course, if a dwelling of tasteless mediocrity can be changed into a gem of beauty, it is worth a good deal of effort — even sacrifice. But to make it undergo a lot of purely facial surgery without assurance of compensating return for the operation is scarcely worth

the price.

An outstanding example of the hopeless kind of house that would best be left alone is the mongrel of assorted rounds and jogs and angles put together in a patchwork of styles— all of them bad. A smaller variety of this same nobreed species to be found in almost every village and town of

the United States is the lop-eared, Neapolitan striped, strungbead-columned "wood-Lizzie” house, forerunner of the "tin-Lizzie” automobile in that it was factory-made in quantities and bought ready to set up! Its vintage began somewhere in the later eighties and continued until the bungalow took its place. Another bad subject for reconstruction is the "mansion” built of bad-color brick in a bulbously ornate design.

On the other hand, the seemingly ugly mid-Victorian clapboard house with heavily jig-sawed trimming is often quite easily transformed into a building of genuine beauty. If we cut off its lumbering veranda and its hack-sawed trimming, and restore its outline by pasting it on paper and coloring the cut places to look like the plain surface of the photograph, we shall see exactly how good — or bad — the structural form of the house may be. A mid-Victorian fault is apt to be the height of the windows in proportion to their width. Sometimes wrought-iron balconies, like those typical

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

of New Orleans, with flower-boxes or pots set behind the tops of the railings, will add apparent width by means of horizontal trimming, which helps to bring down the height. Or if the house is excellent except that the pulled-off porch shows too high foundations, an admirable treatment is a lovely flat-pilastered front door with pediment, with a descending double-curved stair in the manner of so many beautiful Southern Colonial houses, notably those of Charles-

THE SAME VICTORIAN HOUSE REMODELED

ton. Or a simpler porch may be replaced two or three steps below the floor of the interior, and a moderate amount of grading done to raise the land. Again, a typical Victorian house like the one shown in Figure 49 can be changed into something modern and attractive, as shown in Figure 50.

It is rather difficult to show really hideous houses in illustrations, because if photographs were taken of the properties of others and used as examples of ugliness — that would never do! But I am impelled to say that no old jig- jaw or patchwork mongrel of the ugliest eighties can approach a few outstanding freaks that are building to-day.

One hou§e that I have recently seen looks like a

two-headed frog under a Chinese coolie’s hat. As I stood in front of it, a native told me: "That was widow Brown’s house — used to be same as the rest of them round here — some fixin’s!” A person who could take a harmless little house and turn it into such a monster ought to be forced by the state to take a course in fine arts, as a means of prevention against cruelty to civic pride.

Another example — elsewhere — was of an unnoticeable house of maroon brick and brown wood that was turned into a freak of vulgarity by a roof of wavy-lined, villainous, imitation thatch with eaves that curled around exactly like a trimming of gigantically fat inch-worms laid end to end.

Not that you are going to do anything like these, but the thought of them suggests an earnest warning against combinations of disagreeing materials — even against new ones that are at variance with the old. For instance, new composition shingles, which are unquestionably far more durable than wooden ones — and which are even exacted by the fire laws of certain townships, must be chosen with painstaking care as to texture as well as color, or the house will look like a beautiful woman wearing a flagrantly apparent wig. Substitution of anything in place of the wood shingling that is the natural head-covering of a Colonial clapboard house is a wig, and therefore, unless skilfully matched and adjusted, is a misfit.

There are some new asbestos shingles on the market, by the way, that have all the quality of the old hand-split ones. These are unequaled for beauty as well as use.

The addition of foreign-looking building material is easily fatal to beauty. Be careful even of new woodwork as being too self-assertively nen/ when put next to the old. Above all, be careful of brand-new brick laid with too great skill and therefore suggestive of having been set by machine in frames of cast iron. Avoid all brick that is too hard and even — in other words, too perfect to fit into a house of other days when bricks were full of irregularities. The pinkish tone and soft appearance of common brick have twice the

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

charm (but not the lasting quality) of the smooth-glazed, finer-texture brick.

If it is impossible to buy old bricks and you are tearing down a chimney, you can have it done carefully and save the bricks to build at least the outside of a new one, or steps, or walks, or whatever you need bricks for.

BE SURE THAT THE STRUCTURE IS SOUND

Turning to even more essential details that should be considered before buying an old house to remodel, it would be well to make sure that the structure is reasonably sound. If the house is a very old one, you should have the advice of an expert whom you can trust. It makes no difference whether your expert is an architect or a contractor or a mason or a carpenter, but you should have advice from some one as to the chances of expensive repairs that may be necessary. Walk heavily across the floors — or even jump on them — to see whether they are solid or whether they threaten to break through. If the timbers are old, or look at all dilapidated, you would do well to thrust your own penknife into various parts of the woodwork before buying! Used with moderate force, a knife-point will not penetrate sound wood to more than scratch depth, but it will cut into rotted timber up to the hilt! Soft wood, no matter how sound, is always penetrable to a moderate degree, but that which is sound holds tight to a knife-blade. Rotted wood is no more tenacious than earth.

Sometimes an old house is bought solely for its beauty by some one of great means, and cost is an item of no importance to him. But more often than not, the principal object in buying an old house to remodel is to get a dwelling of comfortable size at an outlay that is less than to-day’s cost of building a much smaller house, new. Successful remodeling, therefore, is that which achieves the best results with least destruction of the existing building — or with the fewest changes in items of cost. You must, however, be prepared for

contingencies that are unavoidable in all "making over” and leave quite a margin of safety between the contractor’s approximate estimate and the probable amount of the final bill.

No conscientious builder will give you a contract price on an alteration that involves much rebuilding. He can’t. If you insist upon a fixed contract in spite of his reluctance, he must — to save himself — either put the price at a higher figure than any upright man is willing to name or any owner willing to pay, or run the risk of loss. The chances are that the situation will turn out very much as he thinks it will, but he cannot tell within something of a gambling margin what the condition of the hidden structure of the house really is until he begins to tear down. Beams and joists may be sound or many of them may have to be replaced. A sagging floor-board may mean that one beam has rotted or it may mean that the entire structure will have to be braced. A few shingles removed to make room for new windows may reveal siding and even studs that are rotted through.

On the other hand, the structure may be splendidly sound and the work finished at less than the tentative estimate. (This last is unusual, but it has been known to occur.)

IN CASE YOU MUST COUNT YOUR DIMES AS WELL AS YOUR

DOLLARS

Other questions that should not be left in doubt, at the time you agree to buy, are those of water, light, heat, and sewage. Many beautiful old houses in the country suggest such obviously luxurious beginnings that an enthusiastic buyer is sometimes apt to forget to ask about the quantity — also the drinking quality — of the water supply. Truthful answers to these questions may easily be negative. In thousands upon thousands of cases the "good well that the family always got water from” may prove upon analysis, or even upon questioning, to be in such condition that unless you are willing to face periodic droughts and the possibility of typhoid, you will have to meet the uncertain but always

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

considerable cost of sinking an Artesian well. Or if you are told that you are within the radius of the township’s water system, do not take this as a satisfactory answer until you find out how near to you the township lays its piping.

How near also do electric light and telephone wires run! The poles of one will serve for the other, but make sure — unless your purse is deep — of the distance over which you must put your own poles up.

Heat is the next consideration: Shall you burn coal in range and furnace, or what other method of heating must be prepared for? The least expensive thing to do is to repair the hot-air furnace that is probably in the house. If a new heating and cooking system must be installed, go into this thoroughly and be sure to get definite estimates.

The only sewerage system known to the countryside at large is the cesspool, and if the house you buy has one, and it is in good order, there is no need — unless you choose — to change it. But by far the best country drainage system — unless drinking water is supplied from a well near by — is the septic tank. It costs less than a cesspool to install, and it never has to be emptied; moreover, if its dry- wells are put under the beds of the rose garden, you will have all the neighbors wondering how you have the "luck with roses” that you do!

Country people — for reasons known only to themselves — mistrust septic tanks completely. So don’t let any of them persuade you to use any other method for the disposal of sewage, unless your land is solid undraining clay. In parts of New Jersey, and above all in Iowa, the advisability of installing one may have to be settled by an expert; but in average soil, especially if it be sandy, you need merely install one and — never think of it again.

HOW MUCH REMODELING MAY THE INEXPERIENCED

ATTEMPT?

If the house you are doing is "just a house,” you can certainly do as much of your interior remodeling as you

please, and quite possibly that of its exterior also. You can certainly add almost indefinitely out at the back, or push out the gable end, or build a somewhat diminished duplicate of the house at the side — if you choose a beautiful example and follow its scale.

For instance, if you have a simple Colonial square-box type of house, there would be little risk in carrying its roof out over a colonnade and adding a smaller replica of itself in the manner of the beautiful example illustrated in Plate 14. With a perfect model such as this to go by, you could not easily produce anything bad. And yet, if the house you have bought is truly a gem, it would be more prudent at least to ask an architect whether he approves the scale and proportion of your plans, since in a house of genuine beauty it is important that the intentions of its original architect be sympathetically understood and knowingly adhered to. It would be vandalism to lift it up or stretch it out, or to add an ill-fitting annex, or in any way to spoil the roof line.

Roof lines especially should be placarded with a warning to amateurs to "keep off,” since nothing is more important to the beauty of a house than its roof detail, and nothing is more exacting of skill. The right heights, the right pitch, the right width of eave, the right cornice, the right roofing, and the perfect adjustment of one angle to another — these are not within the knowledge of the inexpert.

No one but an expert should, moreover, attempt to change a house of one type into another. You can, of course, take a shingle or clapboard structure, cover it with stucco, and make it Italian. But this also necessitates an Italian door, a flattened hip or hidden roof, and the removal of all evidences of American origin both without and within.

Primitive Colonial, such as the old Boardman house sketched in Figure 23, easily changes into casement- windowed Tudor, because that is what its ancestry really was. Or, as already said, if a house to begin with is thoroughly mongrel, it does not especially matter which of its possible progenitors is chosen as its origin of style. But, again, to repeat, if

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

a house — no matter what its style — have any beauty to preserve or to bring out don’t change it into another sort of house and make it a mongrel! In other words, don’t cover a typical old farm-house with stucco and make it bad Italian or Spanish. Let it remain a good farm-house.

And yet, since there are few — if any — old houses in America of other styles than Colonial, and if you personally dislike this style or any of its "nearly” variations, it is senseless, certainly, to remodel an undistinguished old house into what can at best be one of a pseudo-Colonial style merely because its parentage is more Colonial than anything else.

Because they are entirely unhampering — being "nothing” to start with — outbuildings such as sheds, barns, and chicken houses, are ideal to spend your imagination on, whether you put them into a costume that is English, French, Dutch, Swedish, or Italian. Beware, however, of adding details that belong in the tropics to a dwelling that was built in a northern climate, or vice versa. Perhaps it is not so much a matter of climate as of environment. An adobe bungalow looks quite perfect in its natural desert surroundings, but among neighbors of finer detail it looks like a man in rough ranch clothes sitting on a fragile Louis XIV bergere, with his rawhide boots resting on an Aubusson carpet.

THE COLOR OF THE EXTERIOR OF A HOUSE

The exterior painting of the smaller American houses has developed a slight degree of gaiety and a greater general uniformity in pleasing appearance. The utterly depressing suburban house of greenish chocolate, once known as mongrel because it was produced by all the left-over paints around the barn, and the ugly multiple-color effects, such as pumpkin-colored side-walls banded with red, a brown trim, and olive-green shutters and slate-gray roof, are at present rivaled by pretty bad examples of violent green shingle-staining and despoiling roofs. But most house-painting is happily limited to one color for the house, with a contrasting color

for the blinds; or with the side- wall, trim, blinds, and roof different but in close harmony.

The Colonial house in best taste, whether large or small, is still painted white with green shutters, or possibly cream with chalk-blue shutters. The shutters, which used almost invariably to be painted so dark a green as to be nearly black, are now, quite as often as not, mitis-green, applegreen, new leaf -green, or any light or bright green the owner fancies. The cream-colored or natural unpainted gray house, with blue shutters, has of late been known to display a blue roof; and it looks very gay and attractive, especially when the blue is not too dark and solid — if it does not destroy its neighborhood. Alone, surrounded with trees or in a group of houses of similar coloring, it is a relief, perhaps, from the uniformity of white and green.

An unusual and most enchanting color scheme for the exterior of a cottage at the seaside, or in a scrub-oak or other not-too-green woodland, is made by staining roughshingled walls a warm tannish-pinkish-gray; the roof, darker gray; the trim, deep cream; and the solid (Dutch) shutters, pink! The pink must be as much like the glow of sunset as possible, or like pink luster. Needless to say, it must be grayed with more white than black. Start with vermilion, and add much gray. It sounds pretty bad, but a certain house painted like this is so lovely that it is almost worth going to Martha’s Vineyard to see! I really mean it! And this in spite of the fact that I so much like houses painted white, with fairly light green shutters and neutral gray roof — if possible of shingles that look hand-split — that I have no second choice.

It is easy to understand that many houses were stained or painted dark — or left to weather — because it was economical. A house painted white must be continually washed off, and must be repainted every so often; since the only things worse than dirty white houses are dirty white collars and cuffs! That the frugal New Englanders painted their houses white is less evidence of their extravagance, however, than might be supposed. New England coastal districts are

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

sandy and very often wood-burning, so that a white house stays white longer there than otherwhere. White also permits touching up without danger of piebald effects, which are impossible to avoid in attempting to retouch a sun-faded color with new paint. Even if you seemingly match the old shade, the new is apt to fade differently and some fine morning shout "Look at me!”

Apart from the white and green Colonial houses — ^with preferably silver-gray roofs — those in best taste include houses of stone in all tones, or of brick, or of cream or graywhite, or of sand-colored stucco, either self-colored or with dark "open” timber. The Spanish craze has added increasing depths of yellow terra-cotta and even pink tints to stucco, besides vast expanses of brilliant red roof.

Whether you like these roofs or not is a matter of your personal opinion. In certain surroundings, and where they have quality of texture and blending of color, they can be beautiful, of course. But in the wrong environment and made of cheap material that looks like tar-paper, or even in hard color, shape, and surface, suggestive of machine-cut sections of drain-pipes or flat slabs of cast-iron, and of heavy turkey red, they look far from comfortable to the house that must support them, and are equally far from being beautifying to the neighborhood.

Roofs, by the way, do not weather in polka dots, but in streaks and veins. Instead of finding the weathered effect sought from varicolored tiles, all too often your eye is caught by one of these polka-dot roofs and you follow a tint or a shade unconsciously row after row, spot, spot, spot! It is much more eye-arresting than a spotty wall-paper, because furniture breaks the wall, whereas nothing interferes with the expanse of roof.

HOW ROOF SHADINGS SHOULD BE LAID

The directions given by the manufacturers of one of the best makes of tiles that reproduce the genuine discolora-

tions on weathered clay is to lay them as nearly as possible in imitation of the way a roof would naturally stain with the dripping of water. The ridge, which receives no drip from above, naturally dries first, and the eaves last. The lightest tiles should therefore be placed nearest the ridge, and those most deeply discolored at the eaves of the widest roof. The eaves of small gables should not be laid with tiles of deeper than medium discoloration.

Some of the composition roof materials — especially those of the cheaper grades — are unpleasingly hard and flat and mean-looking. These are, however, counterbalanced by many beautiful tiles of softness, thickness, and beauty of color, as well as by tiles of other materials, such as slate, stone, or copper. There are some unusually beautiful shingles made of lead. I have lately seen some composition shingles that, in addition to being fireproof, have the look and quality exactly of the old hand-riven shingles, which still make the most beautiful roof possible for a Colonial house.

If you are building, you will do well to look into the subject of manufactured roofings and insulation. Roofing is of vital importance to the beauty of a house; more vital than most people suppose — until too late! Insulation is of equally vital importance to coolness in summer, to heat in winter, and to fuel-saving.

One suggestion as to arrangement of plan: The usual method is to build a small group of rooms in the ex-barn and a smaller group of rooms in the ex-chicken house, and join the two with a long narrow hyphen containing the kitchen. If the family rooms all go into the barn, and the servants’ rooms into the chicken house, this does very well. But otherwise a much better arrangement is to let dining-room and hving-room occupy all of the hyphen, with windows on opposite sides. Otherwise the two ends are blockaded from each other by kitchen and pantry, and guests or family are obliged to go outdoors.

Or if you prefer that the ex-chicken house be used for

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

children or guests — so that they can be off by themselves — then be sure to add a connecting gallery on the court side, so that those who dwell in the smaller end can go over to the larger without going through the kitchen section or out into the grounds.

A FEW DETAILS THAT THE NOVICE MIGHT AS WELL BE

WARNED AGAINST

First of all, building an addition is somewhat different from readjusting existing space. The difficulty in adding on a room is that, unless one end or side of a hall happens to be blank, the room next to which the addition is placed loses its utility, since it becomes a passageway between the addition and the house.

A small sitting-room, at the end of which is put a huge living-room, is a sitting-room no longer. But it can perfectly well become a "parlor” or reception room, or it can have a corridor taken off one side of it and the remnant of it (if there is any) can become a little book-room or writingroom. A bedroom that can be reached only by going through another is of no use at all — ^unless the other is turned into an upstairs sitting-room.

The difficulty is to keep the full value of the space you had without sacrificing it to that which you add, and thereby making much of it futile. You can, of course, add a dressing-room or bathroom or sleeping-porch beyond a bedroom, and a sun-porch beyond the living-room. But a big new living-room beyond a small old living-room spoils the old one so completely that it would in many cases be far wiser merely to double the old one’s size.

CERTAIN TYPICAL PREJUDICES OF WORKMEN

It also might be helpful to the novice builder to be forewarned against certain prejudices of country builders. The first is the objection of many carpenters to allowing a house

to sit on the ground. Whether there is any truth in the fear that houses, like children, will "catch their death” unless kept oflf the damp grass, I don’t know; but I do know from very long experience that the more conscientious the mason or the carpenter, the more surely will he stand a building on its tiptoes — on stdts — if he has his own way. The more surely, too, will he carry up his roof until it perches far above the eyebrows, like a woman’s hat of the gay nineties.

Another characteristic of every good carpenter is a great appreciation of the solid and substantial, and a detestation of the flimsy no matter how clearly it may be labeled "quaint.” A master carpenter will deliberately throw away your specification for match-board barn-doors with 4 x board battens, and show you a heavy, solid-paneled door that he has arranged to give you instead of "that no-account makeshift you had contracted for”; I have known them to do it for genuine architects just as well as for half-way ones like myself!

In the same way, the mason will take it for granted that you will gladly pay a little extra in order to add three or four feet of height to the foundation walls, or raise a lower wing so as to bring it level with the main building, or make some other "improving” change that utterly destroys the beauty and quaintness you had in mind.

If you are on the spot, it is easy enough to stop the mason s wall, start the roof where you want it, and flatten its angle and restrain its eaves. But if you are absent, you come back to see a wide-brimmed, high-crowned grain-elevator where you intended a Dutch-capped hutch, and a massive barn-door that would suffice to confine elephants instead of Bossy and Dobbin, who would stay contentedly in their stalls without any door at all.

Good masons will also worry themselves to death over the cheap-grade brick you contract for (and like), and do their best to save on something” so as to surprise you with brick of better grade. In building a garden wall once I used

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

.the commonest brick I could get, which was in my mason’s obvious belief a streak of economy. He could not believe that I could like bad brick until he offered to substitute good brick for my old wrecks and I refused flatly. Even so, it was not until at a later period, when I offered to pay double the price of new pressed brick for a few hundred old ones to build a front walk, that he believed me — and at the same time no doubt believed me not quite right in the head.

Another detail which architects, no less than contractors, take very casually, but which drives a woman who hates wastage almost to distraction, is the unnecessary confusion in which the finishing of the job is done. Under ordinary circumstances every variety of workman is working at the same time, and each man’s work is being held up or spoiled by others. The plumbers finish laying the pipes, and then go away leaving tubs and wash-basins and all other fixtures lying around everywhere that they don’t belong; and they are much too heavy to move. Painters, as likely as not, spatter paint all over them. Then the plumber gets even and lays his hands all over the paint. The electricians gouge out the finished work of the carpenters, and all of them combine to throw everything heavy and handy out into the flowerbeds that have been hopefully planted. And the owner, knowing that every item delayed or destroyed is just so much loss to him, gets more and more nervous.

One might suppose that the unceasing repetition of this experience would get on the nerves of the workmen, but apparently they like it.

HINTS FOR THE PROMOTION OF A TRANQUIL MIND WHILE

BUILDING

If you would be tranquil throughout the process of building, remember to have electricians and carpenters entirely finished before the painters come on the job. Every one who has ever built can probably remember an instance where

the painting was finished beautifully, and then — hardware had to be fitted or electric lights attached, with the result that the area surrounding each object was smudged and finger-printed. To a woman nothing in the entire operation of building is so hard to bear. While the house is in a state of unfinished confusion, smudges and knocks and disorder are not in the least annoying. But after the painters have finished a wall or trim or floor, then to have it all ringed round with black, so that the painters have to retouch it all over, and paper-hangers have to put on patches, is a severe strain on one’s sense of fairness. And worse still are smudges around drop-cords on calcimined ceilings that cannot be cleaned at all!

Whoever is in charge of the building should see that everything is finished completely by the time the painters and paper-hangers and floor-finishers come in. It is far better that the painter or paper-hanger should unscrew plates, or loosen electric fixtures and the hardware, than that the electricians should come in or the carpenters stay on after the painters, who are finishers. .

If Hnoleum is used instead of tiles, it is unfortunately necessary that it be laid in the kitchen, laundry, pantry and bathrooms before the plumbers set the fixtures — which is not very good for any of it, but is especially bad for the finer soft “Surface grades. If the floors are tile, of course, no harm is done. If they are covered with new linoleum, the only way to achieve tranquillity yourself — disregarding that of the plumbers is to follow these workmen incessantly throughout the short proceedings of putting in the fixtures, and to supply them with old coverings of some sort for their shoes to rest on. It is not their fingers, but the edges of their heels and the soles of their shoes that dig into the linoleum and streak and cut it permanently, exactly as though with an indelible pencil sharp enough to cut. I know one plumber, a very real friend of mine, who, whenever he sets fixtures over linoleum for me — in mockery, no doubt — ^wraps his shoes and those of his helper in pieces of awning tied round

THE HOUSE THAT YOU REMODEL

with string. Needless to say, I prefer the chances of mockery to streaks in the linoleum!

After the plumbers are gone, and the linoleum-layers (in town) or the carpenters (in the country) have cemented the linoleum, and all hardware has been put in, then the painters finish ceilings and walls and trim. After that the paper-hangers put on the wall-papers — if any are used — and then the painters or floor specialists finish the floors. If the floors are painted or varnished, the painters should lock the doors of each room as it is finished and let the varnish harden for at least one or two days. After this the house is ready for its furniture.

But the story does not quite end here. You will be very lucky indeed if the house suffers no destruction in the way of nicks, bangs, scratches, and smudges by the movers! Highgrade professional "movers” are marvelous, and can take every blockading piece of furniture up stairways and through doorways that are seemingly about half the size of the object to be taken up or through, without leaving the slightest mark. But this is a feat beyond the skill of the average local truckman, a statement that can be certified by many a marred stair- wall and door- jamb.

And yet, to one who is a house-building addict, nothing is so fascinating as to get into precisely this turmoil again and again, because of an insatiable interest in seeing how each new adventure in building will turn out.

VII

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

y ^OR each danger that follows in the wake of mod- AV ern progress there invariably follows a device of -A. safety. The danger that confronts the inexperienced house-buyer, with the resultant necessity for salesmanship - resistance, is the theme of this chapter.

No woman with the least common sense or halfway knowledge of suitability could think of buying a beaded evening dress to wear in the daytime on the street. Yet this is precisely what inexperienced buyers of houses do every day. Hundreds upon hundreds of sensible people buy a "pretty picture” entrance, electric ice-box, or colorfully tiled bathrooms, instead of the house, and its setting — which is exactly why such selling features are "featured.” We shall get to this phase of the subject presently.

As the first and essential preparation for your own ultimate satisfaction and contentment in the house that you buy, therefore, you should — before going to look at any house at all — prepare two lists. Make them on separate sheets of paper or in a small note-book. The first should contain every item possible to think of that is necessary to your own and your family’s comfort. The second list is entirely concerned with questions of construction and need not be consulted until the first list has passed your personal-requirements test.

And whatever you do, don’t buy a house as thoughtlessly as you might buy a hat or a pair of shoes, since a house is far too important and permanent a responsibility, as well as an expenditure, to be chosen because its color or effect pleases at first sight. It should not be bought without carefully trying it on (by means of your note-book) and making

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

sure of its becomingness and fit. No matter how "sweet” a house seems, do not think of signing the contract that will ipiake you its responsible owner until you have checked all the items that are necessary to your mode of life. If these are satisfactory, do not buy even then until you have checked your second list of building details.

For example, although your first list is much the same as that which a house-builder makes to show his architect, there is an immeasurable difference in this: If you are building, the object of your architect is to insure your permanent satisfaction in the house he is designing for you, and he will help you to the utmost of his professional knowledge to this end. Making a pro-architect list is merely an economical insurance against the possibility of a few forgotten items, since the architect will probably have thought of most of them for you.

But the agent on the premises is solely concerned with making a sale. Having made it, he cares no more whether you eventually like the house or hate it, than you care what ultimately happens to postage stamps you put on your letters. Making an anti-agent list is therefore a vitally self-protecting measure, because he is certainly not going to help you to remember items that are missing in the house he is trying to sell you. This is not a criticism of the agent; it is a mere statement of the positions of players in a game. In the game of house-building, or buying, the architect is your partner; but the sales-agent is your opponent. The point to remember is that buying a house from an agent is a game that must be played with your wits about you.

If you buy a house through your own real estate broker, whom you know, and know you can trust, that is again another matter; but even so, lists will do no harm.

LIST OF NECESSARY COMFORTS

To go back to the making of your first list, however: you should try to imagine everything you use each day,

sleeping, dressing, eating, sitting, resting, writing, reading, as well as on party-giving occasions. You must make a note of the things needed by the members of your family in following their characteristic pursuits. You must fit your servants as well as your family and yourself into your mental picture, and try to imagine them during the varying activities of their days.

Above everything, you must notice whether every working part of the house is practical. If you have children, where can they eat — play — sleep? How much room do they need for their toys and clothes, books and possessions? What other things must you find room for? Take measurements of everything. Measure the furniture you will have to place. Take the dimensions of the rooms you like in your present house, of closets you know, so that you will have a clear understanding of what sizes really mean.

Comparatively few people have the least idea of exactly how big a room 10 x 12, 15 x 17, or 20 x 24 feet may be. Few people standing in an unfurnished room can guess how much furniture it will hold. Take a tape-measure with you or — in any hardware store — buy a fifty-foot measure that rolls on a reel. Beginning with the upstairs (which is important) , instead of the entrance hall (which is not) , put down in your bedroom requirements not merely the number of beds you must have, but the size they must be. A room that is to have twin beds, with a night table between, will require a minimum wall space of 7^ feet over all; bed length is usually 7 feet. Twin beds close together with a night table on either side, 9 feet. A double bed is 4^2 feet wide, a single bed 3 feet or 3 feet 3 inches wide. Make a note of where they will be placed.

Ninety-nine bedrooms out of a hundred have a long wall for the bed space opposite the windows, so that the only way you can sleep is with head to the wall and feet toward the light; meaning that, should you breakfast in bed or ever have to lie there in the daytime, a book or whatever you look at will be lighted on the back, while the page or object

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

facing you will be in total darkness. This arrangement is perhaps preferred by so many architects because it provides the least amount of draft from windows opened at night. Perhaps you are opposed to drafts? Or perhaps you like to sleep with your head on the window sill? At all events, if you very much care one way or the other, make a note of it.

Perhaps you care a great deal about an open fire in your bedroom — or perhaps you don’t. If possible, there should be room for a sofa. If the closet space is large enough to be equipped with plenty of drawers as well as hangers, sofa space in a small room may be allowed for by omitting a bureau or chiflFonier.

THE ALL-IMPORTANT QUESTION OF CLOSETS

On the other hand, if closet space is moderate, be sure to measure closet depth. Remember that a clothes pole is not practical in a closet 1 8 inches deep, because coats and dresses will be marred at the shoulders. Men are inclined to belittle a woman’s insistence upon closets and then appropriate as many of them as they can. Men’s boots and ulsters, and especially their coonskin coats, can take up as much room as the average small-house closet space allows for the whole family. If the linen closet seems small and narrow, measure it by your biggest stack of folded towels, table-cloths and sheets. A shelf deep enough to hold their width will do, if the shelves be long enough for your supply. Laying them lengthwise is better merely for looks.

If there is no attic, is there a trunk space? And a storage closet? Is there a kitchen-stores closet? Is the kitchen potcloset and dresser ample? Is the ice-box — or ice-box space — big enough? We are taking it for granted that the kitchen is light and pleasantly livable. Pantry? How much room for china? Is there a housemaid’s closet on each floor?

How many open fireplaces are there? Do not forget to notice this, unless you are one to whom a picture of "home” and a fire burning on the hearth are not the same!

Notice the shape of the living-room. Does its principal group of furniture naturally arrange itself in an undisturbed side of the room, or do doors open in such a way that the mantle and windows — near which you would naturally place your big sofa and most comfortable chairs-^ are in the direct passage between entrance and door to dining-room? Also notice by what means a servant can go from the kitchen end to the front door. Is the living-room big enough to deserve the name? Is the dining-room too small or too big in proportion? How many bridge tables can you put in the living-room (if this interests you) ?

Besides questions of space and arrangement, make notes of the size and make of the kitchen range. Is there a hood over it to carry off the smell of cooking? What sort of heating system? What make and grade? How do the windows open and shut? Do they stick or rattle, or do they slide easily? Does the pantry door knock and rock and bump, or does it swing smoothly?

How many outlets for electric fixtures? Are there plenty? Count them. Notice most especially the light and convenience of the servants’ quarters. Are their rooms pleasant? Is their bath of usable size? Is there a dining-room for them — and, in the country, a porch for them to sit on?

TESTS OF CONSTRUCTION

The foregoing are suggestions for notes on personal requirements. But of equal importance are the buying questions concerned with construction, such as: How thick are the foundations?
