There should be at least two bricks or an eight-inch thickness of concrete for a two or three-storied building, or twelve inches for one that is four or five-storied. If the cellar walls are built of concrete, are they smooth and dry, or full of pebble patches and damp? Is the floor dry? Are the outside foundation walls wavy or streaked or starting to crack or stain? Can you see stones in their surface, or horizontal board-marks from the forms they were cast in?

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

If the outside walls are stucco, are there signs of "map crack” or stains? If they are of stone, is the mortar the actual surface with a few stones in it? Are the leaders of copper or tin, or are there none at all? Are the roof valleys lined with copper, zinc, or only tin? If not tin, will the building company guarantee "copper” or even "zinc” in writing?

Then, indoors: Are plumbing fixtures porcelain or enameled iron? First grade or third? (This is not important except as an item of cost.) If they are likely to be upsetting to judgment because of bewildering color, at all events notice the size of the piping, and whether it is brass or iron painted over. If the bathrooms and kitchen are tiled, does the tiling fit snugly against the trim, or is there a quarter of an inch left for a roach promenade? Notice all joinings throughout the house. Are they beautifully fitted or do they come together with individual indifierence? Is the lumber smooth, hard, high-grade, or does it look hang-nailed and likely to split?

At all events, armed with two detailed lists, you should now be quite ready for the game of house-buying and be able to hold your own even against Bildem & Sellem’s agent on the premises, who will display any of the company’s houses with all the skill of a conjurer taking rabbits and roses out of a hat before your bewildered eyes. That firms such as Bildem & Sellem or the Allforsho Construction Company have need for necromantic salesmanship is obvious. Third-grade value can be sold at first-grade prices only by rabbit-and-rosebush leger-de-langue of the most conscienceless character; and this in turn can be most readily paid for by profiteers whose returns soar far above the moderate profits of the conscientious building company which gives its customers 100 per cent, of the value that it claims to give.

So that, although the above paragraphs have done their best to warn you against too easily believing in the reality of magic roses and rabbits, you will do well also to cling tightly to the lists in your note-book and to carry the book with you; for this is a well tried method by which to see how the tricks are done and to avoid being taken in by them.

Another sales-resistance aid is to remember that just as many an old automobile has been sold because its body was newly enameled and its worn-out engine covered with silver paint, hundreds of houses equally lacking in essential worth are sold every week because of little "arty” effects or brain-paralyzing schemes of color.

To your list of items to be noted you might add one more: If you are tempted to buy, WHY are you tempted? Because there is a showcase window in the dining-room that is too darling for words, or a trick cupboard that turns out to be a door, or a sweet little stairway, or a romantic grille of wrought iron, or colored tiles and plumbing fixtures in the bathrooms which so capture your fancy that you do not even notice that quite possibly the tint or texture of the one is far from being in harmonious accord with the other? Was the actual impulse to buy because of an electric refrigerator free with the house? (Two or three or four hundred dollars put down to salesmanship account!) Was it, after all, the orgy of colors in the bathrooms that benumbed your good sense — to say nothing of your good taste? (Which last may not be true — since tiles and colored fixtures can be ravishing, but they are not likely to be in a made-to-sell house) .

Another question important to answer: Is the painting genuinely beautiful finish or is it artful disguise? This is the real rose-tree delusion, since who among millions of housebuyers can possibly know how deep the shiny new surface quality goes below its made-to-sell appearance — until the outside wears oflf and the worthless inside sham falls to pieces like stucco and cardboard buildings of a gone-by exposition? The importance of getting an expert whose knowledge is salesmanship-proof to advise you, on a building made to sell, cannot be overemphasized.

CONSCIENTIOUS BUILDING COMPANIES

Even though there are more building companies of the Bildem & Sellem variety than there should be — so many that

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

warning against them could not possibly be omitted in a chapter such as this — on the other hand there are plenty of absolutely honest and conscientious building companies, who, by buying up a large block of land and putting up houses or apartments in quantity, are able (and willing) to build to the advantage of the buyers as well as of themselves.

A company of this type has no objection to your calling in whatever expert advice you choose; in fact, the more expert it is, the more they welcome it, whether it is that of a consulting architect, builder, or firm of accountants. A company of Bildem & Sellem type will not welcome expert examination (its unwillingness will be quite apparent if you are on the lookout for it) , nor will its sales magician tranquilly agree to your proposal to "think it over.” He will not be willing to let you go home, as you should, and compare your list of requirements with those you checked, and to compare the building details with those you know to be sound or standard. He will at once begin taking other things out of the hat; the increased value of the property; the fashionable, or the rich, or the influential people who have bought next door, and whose friends are going to buy the very house you are looking at, the instant your back is turned.

He will also produce from his hat an answer to every objection that you can possibly find. You are not quite satisfied with the doorless openings, nor with their frames; you do not like the hardware nor the trim very much, nor the balusters of the stairs. You do not, perhaps, know why. He will tell you that "good taste demands simplicity.” An expert would answer that they are not simple. They are crude, and cheap, and in very bad style. All the work is of the lowest grade. There are no doors between any of the rooms because the price of doors can be saved by leaving them off. Ornamentation — in painted plaster — trims the mantles and simulates the timbered ceiling. If the ornament had been left off and the beams were real, it would be infinitely "simpler” and better.

ASSORTED IFS AND ANDS

On the other hand, if the building is pleasingly planned and seemingly well done; if you know enough about construction or can be advised by one who does; or if, above all, you know that the builders have a reputation for integrity, you are taking little risk in buying; apart from being persuaded by adept salesmen that finding room for your treasured secretary-desk and your comfortable long-pillowed sofa, your old dining-room sideboard, your personal six-footwide bed, or a playroom for the children or other essentials, is of no account.

W^hen you go home and talk it over, you should make a double-columned account, write down on one side the assets, and on the other their lack. If the essentials of comfort are in the asset list, and the shortcomings are nonessential, the chances are that you will be contented if you buy. But be sure to remember that if it have incurable faults of discomfort, the prettiest home in the world is an abomination that will never be anything else.

One last word: Under normal circumstances, the remodeled house costs least, the house that you build costs more, the house ready-built costs more still, and the house bought on the instalment plan costs most.

It is obvious that a house built ready to sell must cost more than bare cost of land and labor, because it must pay a profit (and a good one) to the promoters who risked their money to build it and who must be reimbursed for their risk, their loss of interest, the cost of advertising, and the sales department — including the skilful gentleman with the magic hat. In other words, you ought to be able to build your own house for less (even plus the building loan and interest loss) than you can possibly buy it for from a company which built it to sell at a profit. And, quality for quality, you can; in fact, you very much more than can — if you know what you want and go straight ahead and build it according to unchanged contract.

A LOVELY COLONIAL EXAMPLE

William Lawrence Bottomley , Architect

THE HOSPITABLE AS WELL AS DECORATIVE QUALITY OF A GRAPE¬ VINE OVER A LOVELY DOOR

IF YOU BUY READY-MADE

The house bought on the instalment plan costs most in actual cash, but it is a boon to thousands upon thousands who by this method are enabled to own their homes, and who would otherwise be rent-payers all their lives.

Every normal person longs for a home, and no one more than the worker who boards, or rents, and lives among furnishings that are not his own. And to all such the "buy your own home” appeal is irresistible. Very, very often the price asked is reasonable, the house honest, and the site one that will appreciate in value. And to one who is cautious enough to ask for expert advice — every one knows some one who knows something of values — it is a very good thing to do. You can see the house actually as it is, and where it is, and you are not led into the changes and extras that those who build or do over invariably run into. So that even if it costs a little more than the house that is remodeled or built, it costs infinitely less than the permanent outgo of rent spent throughout a lifetime for the transient expenses of gone-by yesterdays with neither a floor board nor a shingle set up for still homeless to-morrows.

VIII

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT OR OTHER COMMUNAL

DWELLING

the sales-resistance advice in the last chapter is ^ merely a fraction of that which applies to apartment-buying. In fact, becoming a "tenant owner” in any communal dwelling is beset with far greater danger than is possible to one who merely buys a house which does not prove as satisfactory as he thinks it is going to be, or who merely pays a higher price than it is quite worth.

In an apartment house where you are one of, let us say, twenty owners, you have a one-twentieth interest, or right, in whatever may arise; but in the average instance you have no personal control except through the Board of Governors — whom you help to elect, willingly or indifferently, as the case may be — and in their acts you are without prerogative of your own except to share fully in all liabilities.

For these reasons buying a cooperative apartment about which you know nothing — as to its construction, its management, and, above all, its financial solvency — should be labeled in huge red letters "T.N.T.”

The financially sound, capably managed, honestly built and conservatively owned cooperative apartment house of the 100 per cent, plan is now being recommended even by conservative trustees; but not without being very sure that the rating of the house is Al, and not Z26.

Let us begin, therefore, with a "T.N.T.” or "Z26” or whatever we may call the type of cooperative apartment that those of limited funds and inexpert knowledge should avoid even approaching — just as a mouse should avoid approaching the cheese in a trap. By taking a look, from a safe distance, at this "trap” kind of cooperative apartment, we may per-

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

haps learn in detail the characteristics whereby its reverse can be recognized. The one to avoid is put up by a building syndicate of unsavory reputation solely for its own profit. Let us say that it is promoted by Bildem & Sellem, the same firm that put up the block of new houses which illustrated the wrong house to buy, in the last chapter.

THE WRONG KIND OF COOPERATIVE BUILDING

A dangerous cooperative building is one that is put up as cheaply as possible; it stands without falling, and that is about all. Its financial basis is just about equally solid. Its outward appearance has a camouflage finish of stucco trim in the entrance hall, supplemented by the overbraided uniforms of the doorman and front elevator man. The financial statement is a supreme exposition of optimism and avoidance of facts that might foreshadow deficits. Its optimism is emphasized by our old friend, the agent of rabbits-and-roses skill, who sold houses in the last chapter. He now suggests that you briefly pass over "tiresome figures,” since all you need to know is that apartment 4B will cost so much. Unless you know there must be one, and ask him how much it is, he may possibly not think to mention the maintenance payment at all. But when directly asked, he will say delightedly, "Only ten per cent.,” but fail to tell you by what means it is to be kept at that.

Having made the cost entirely clear by "leaving out the merely technical details,” he now begins his actual selling method by flourishing the list of magic names of the great and near-great who are all going to become partners in the enterprise which you are privileged to join. Remember and never forget — the difference between "going to buy” and "have bought,” and even when the words are have bought,” verify them.

This list of owners or tentative owners is the first thing that the prospective buyer should investigate, because it is the chief selling factor through which at least fifteen of the

twenty owners are given sufficient confidence to buy. For instance, Mr. and Mrs. Rentings decide that rather than move every time their landlords put up the rent, they will buy an apartment of their own. In this mood they see a brand-new building with a cheerful white-lined brick fagade, inviting casement windows, and a huge diagram on a pink or light green ground, showing a checker-board of rectangular spaces, more than a third of them filled with the big block-lettered word "SOLD.” A notice at the bottom announces "Agent on Premises.” The Rentings go in. The agent greets them cordially, and shows them an apartment. He distracts their attention with his well-known conjuring skill throughout their visit to the kitchen end, but lingers in the living-room or the principal bedrooms, or whichever happens to be the best room to put foremost, and produces his baitlist — a duplicate of the sign outside, except that many more spaces are filled than the few marked "SOLD.”

He holds it exactly as he would the pack of cards from which you are to choose whichever one you please, and the Rentings are made to notice that "Mr. Notables has bought on the tenth floor, Mr. Eminent is closing to-day, the Toplofty-Smiths of Long Island have bought No. 4B, Mrs. Socially Prominent has chosen the fifth,” and so on. The Rentings naturally think — as they are intended to — that if prominent people such as these are all investing their funds and making their homes in the building, certainly it should be safe for people like themselves to do the same. Knowing nothing about cooperative methods or maintenance, they merely notice that at about the rent they paid before their late landlord raised them, they are getting a brand-new and much more smartly run apartment, the rent for which — so they think — can never increase. Without advice from any one, they take a large part of their nest-egg of savings, buy the apartment, and move in.

Little by little, the Rentings discover many things. They cannot keep a cook because the kitchen is airless, and the dining-room, on the same court, gets all its smell and that of

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

Other kitchens as well. The fireplace in the living-room is a fake in which they can burn no logs.

Moreover, they discover that their fellow-owners are not the personages they had supposed them to be. It seems that the Notables sold their apartment and bought a larger one elsewhere; the Eminents and Mrs. Prominent decided not to buy — though they had held options at the time the Rentings bought. The Smiths, who alone had bought and stayed, were no relatives of the Toplofty-Smiths of Long Island, as the agent "had been led to suppose,” but were Fieldmice-Smiths from Fieldville Junction.

Nevertheless, all goes fairly well so long as the house remains brand-new. But gradually the camouflage of transient decoration wears through, and the skimped construction of the building becomes increasingly apparent. The steam pipes add leakage to their chronic habit of knocking, the shrinkage of unseasoned wood leaves cracks in nearly all door frames, the short-measurement tiles recede from the walls. The plumber becomes a frequent visitor, as well as other workmen, who bear witness to the money that went jnto the pockets of Miessrs. Bildem & Sellem instead of into sound construction.

Nor are the flaws in the quality of building the only ones. The style of upkeep falls off; the management of the building, which was permanently assigned to a subsidiary company recommended by Bildem & Sellem, has taken away the original front-door man to improve the appearance of a newer building. In the Rentings house, the men are changed every few weeks, and the once much-braided tailcoat uniforms are now of jumper grade. The halls are dirty, the building is gradually running down at heels.

And then the Rentings get a notice that the rate will have to be increased to 14 per cent. Six months later they get an assessment. The Rentings learn for the first time that some of the apartments were never sold. They were temporarily rented to help pay the upkeep, and are now vacant. There is, moreover, another assessment to pay for an amort-

ization of the second mortgage. For the first time the financial statement is made quite clear. The property is mortgaged to the utmost limit, and nothing has been put aside for amortization; there is not even a fund for depreciation and repair.

Realizing only too well that their investment was neither profitable nor sound, the Rentings decide to sell. But, the house being by this time obviously a patchwork building, with finances very close to ruin, the Rentings must either accept an offer of half the amount they paid or face continuous assessments for a building not worth being assessed on. All of which should certainly be a warning to the unwary against buying into an uninvestigated building.

AN EXAMPLE OF SOUND COOPERATIVE BUILDING

The foregoing example, of course, is purposely chosen as the worst possible to encounter; but it is by no means exaggerated to the point of impossibility. However, having presented its hazards, let me now give an example of sound cooperative building.

Superficially, the characteristics may seem much the same, especially since the wrong methods imitate the appearance of the good. But one difference, to start with, is that the financial plan in a properly drawn prospectus has been prepared by a responsible firm of accountants or lawyers, and if a buyer does not understand the meaning of maintenance and amortization and sinking fund, it is thoroughly and clearly explained. A complete list of wages to be paid is drawn up, and all allowances for heat, light, and repairs are estimated in such a way that comparison with costs in like buildings may easily be made. The building is put up, moreover, by a notable architect, and a builder whose standing is equally high. The names given of the owners are in no way other than they seem, and you are, moreover, assured by the reputations of those who sponsor the project, that your interests will be conscientiously looked after.

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

HOW COOPERATIVE APARTMENTS ARE FINANCED

The financial basis of a cooperative organization is very briefly this: A building company — of whatever it is composed — puts up the building and sells the apartments in the form of stock in the corporation. In other words, instead of buying an apartment for so many thousand dollars, you buy so many hundred shares of stock in the XXX Park Avenue Corporation. (The name is usually that of its address, but it can be anything agreed upon by the organizers.) Fof rather technical reasons this is considered the best way to incorporate such an enterprise, so that instead of receiving a deed for apartment No. lOA you receive a certificate of stock to the amount and value of the space occupied by apartment No. 10 A, and a lease for apartment 10 A, which is customarily allotted for a period of ninety-nine years. But, as it is clearer to speak of your apartment rather than of the shares of stock which represent it, let us put the stock out of mind until you are about to sign a contract, when — if you care to know — ^your lawyer can far better than I explain the reason why.

It is, however, important that you understand that every owner of a cooperative apartment assumes his share — based on the cost of his apartment— of certain fixed charges above the buying price that he pays. The proportion of the buying price to your share of the free and clear cost of both land and building varies in each corporation.

In a superconservative building the buying cost is high and the maintenance rate low, because the mortgage is very small and the interest to be paid on it not only less in amount but at a lower rate. For example, let us say that if there were no building loan or mortgage of any kind, the free and clear cost of the apartment would be fifty thousand dollars. In an ultra conservative building you would pay as much as thirty thousand dollars cash, and yearly interest on twenty thousand dollars of the mortgage, which is your share. If you pay as little as twenty thousand and youi* charges for

mortgage are for thirty, the situation is evidently one of hazard rather than of safety.

The amount of mortgage compared to the value of the building is very important, and one which a prospective buyer should understand. Moreover, he should find out the actual cost of the ground and building, and not accept the rose-and-rabbit agent’s inflated valuation — again a danger of the wrong cooperative. In the properly presented prospectus these costs are so clearly stated that verification is a simple matter. In the wrong building, promoters’ profits are covered over in pretended cost. If what is called the maintenance charge can be kept at 10 per cent., it should mean that you paid at least thirty — possibly thirty-two — thousand dollars for an apartment whose total value is fifty thousand dollars. But if the maintenance charge is 16 per cent., you should not have paid more than twenty-two thousand dollars on a fifty-thousand valuation.

In other words, your total yearly cost balances like this: If you pay $32,000 cash, maintenance charge at 10 per cent, would be $3,200 yearly; allowing 5 per cent, on your $32,000 investment, or $1,600 (the interest that you naturally have to give up), this, added to the maintenance charge, makes your actual yearly cost $4,800.

Or if you pay $22,000 for the apartment, 5 per cent, on this capital is $1,100, and the 16 per cent, maintenance rate is $3,600, which, plus the $1,100, makes your yearly share $4,780. This is less than the other way, but not as wise if there be not sufiicient provision in the rate of maintenance for amortization of a mortgage that is too high to stand permanently. The amount of mortgage and method of paying it — or keeping it — ^varies according to the preference of the majority of the owners. Cooperative apartments are rarely if ever paid for in full. In those of the soundest conservatism about 60 per cent, is paid for, or the amount borrowed is gradually reduced to a level of safety.

Maintenance charges, therefore, include interest on the mortgage, yearly amortization of at least two per cent., taxes,

Firm of Kenneth M. Murchison, Architects

ENTRANCE HALL IN A NEW YORK APARTMENT [PLATE 16]

MANTEL IN AUTHOR’S NEW YORK BEDROOM [PLATE 17]

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

repairs, fire and employee insurance, heat, hall and basement light, wages of employees, uniforms, and the various items that are necessary in the housekeeping of such a building no less than in a private house. If the apartments were paid for entirely, there would not be any interest charges, but there would still be taxes and all running expenses.

These costs vary considerably according to plan and style of house. In a well appointed house, each elevator adds more than twenty-five hundred a year to the costs; very well appointed houses pay higher wages, employ a greater number of men, pay more for their uniforms, and buy more of them. On the other hand, a conscientiously constructed house has almost no repairs. The only means whereby a buyer can be assured of the quality of the building is by the reputation of the architect and builder, and the known integrity of the promoter.

PUTTING UP A COOPERATIVE BUILDING OF YOUR OWN

But if you want a superfine building at a price below that of its counterpart made to sell, there are a dozen reasons why you should consider building it yourself.

Exactly as in the house that you buy ready made, the apartment put up by a promoting company must contain, and quite justly, a profit as well as reimbursement to the promoters. Therefore, every now and then a certain number of would-be owners put up a building by themselves. This is the soundest, sanest and most satisfactory thing to do, not only as a means of getting best value for least cost, but as the means of getting an individual plan that is completely satisfying to your personal requirements of beauty and comfort.

If you have looked at a hundred or so New York apartments of supposedly high quality that rent or sell to represent the typical New York rental a thousand dollars a room a year** — it is inevitable that the hackneyed phrase, "Queen Anne in the front and Mary Ann in the

back,” will continually din in your mind. In that state you enter an overtrimmed, indigestibly furnished street-entrance ^hall, and go into an ornate elevator more or less in keeping with the hall. A moment later you emerge either on a tiny enclosed landing containing a section of adobe wall and two iron doors, painted walnut, or else you find yourself on a sausage-meat stone-floored iron-staired corridor that looks like the bare back stairs of a hospital or factory. From either of these you are shown into the apartment. If it is, let us say, nine-roomed, there will be a fairly big living-room (but one inclined to turn its narrow side to the window) , a semidark dining-room, and a quadruplet of bedrooms, each the exact rectangular counterpart of the other — also narrow end to windows. A long season of apartment hunting makes one wonder what they do with the wide sides! There is always one less bath than bedroom; this is so that bedroom No. 4 may be used as a library, or den — or what you will! If the house is very new and what in trade is called "de luxe,” the window of one of the baths will be full-sized and all of the plumbing good. As you look at it, you perhaps wish the windows were on the long walls, and you realize that its likeness to every other apartment robs it of personality; but it is all perfectly satisfying until you are led blandly into the cramped gloom of a Hghtless kitchen and two dark little cell-like bedrooms.

HIGH COST FOR NO COMFORT

Not more than one apartment in twenty-five possesses more than two tiny servants’ rooms. Not more than one in fifty has a servants’ sitting-room to go with an apartment of apparent grandeur — judged from the front.

If you can pay a somewhat startling price, nothing is easier than finding apartments with good-sized front rooms, adequate closets, and first-class plumbing; but finding one with a light kitchen and servants’ dining-room and four or even three bedrooms for them goes only with a front part

THE cooperative APARTMENT

of enormous size and a rental that is, as Alice would have said, enormouser. There is one kitchen that I could name on a court about twenty feet from a wall. The advertisement had said it would be white-tiled. It was. It was also nightdark! There were the two ubiquitous and equally night-dark servants’ rooms.

I said to the agent:

"Do you mean that you can rent an apartment such as this?”

He replied light-heartedly:

"Oh, I think this kitchen is as good as any other, for cooks to walk into and out of!”

I pass the building every now and then, and it looks occupied; but I always wonder on how many of its floors the cooks have just walked in — or out!

All of which is merely intended to illustrate the reason why I myself became one of a group of apartment builders, and why I urge you to do the same. After months of apartment hunting and of finding absolutely nothing either to rent or to buy that was possible both to pay for and to live in — one or the other could be found, but never the two together — I one day discussed my difficulties with a group of friends only to find that each of us seemed to be looking for the same thing. We each wanted an apartment suitable to certain requirements of living, and one in which the back should not be different from the front. Suddenly, then and there, we decided to build our own. We gathered all possible cooperative details, and found that the only risk we ran was that of not being able to sell the house out quickly and thus of being forced to carry the expense of unsold space. As it turned out, every apartment was sold before the steel-work was completed, and there has been a waiting list ever since.

We built exactly as an individual builds a single house. Our object was not to build cheaply and sell at a profit, but to make the best building possible to put up — and to live in. It can be duplicated in the same way, but in no other,

because no one is going to undertake a project of that sort for profit and make none. That is obvious.

THE SAFEST COOPERATIVE

Nothing, as a matter of fact, is easier than putting up your own building, if you are strong enough financially either individually or by force of numbers — to carry it through. The method of procedure is this: You first of all find a suitable piece of land, and get an option on it. About 10 per cent, of the price has to be put up at once. This amount is risked only if you cannot definitely sell enough apartments to be able to see it through. Here is the one and only danger.

Let us say, however, that you and nine friends are agreed as to the size and type of apartments you want, and the sums you are willing to be responsible for. Ten people would pay ten different prices if each selected a different floor. Taking the ninth floor of a fifteen story building as the average, each floor decreases about 8 per cent, on the cost of the ninth floor as it goes lower, or increases about 8 per cent, as it goes up. (This, however, varies in every building and must be calculated by a real estate expert.)

Using the 8 per cent, floor rate merely as an example, the future owner of the fifteenth floor would add 48 per cent, to the cost of the ninth, and the owner of the first floor would subtract a similar or larger percentage. As already explained, the price varies according to the distance of the apartment from the ground, the outlook, and other considerations; but no matter how steep or how slight the line of increase, the top floor is to-day the most expensive.

A few years ago extra servants’ rooms used to be built on the roof, and a penthouse was a remnant, as it were, of the era of moderate cost. Penthouses to-day sell, space for space, at from a quarter to a third more than that of any other space in the building.

Having found your group and your site, the next thing

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

is to decide upon your architect. Or perhaps you decide upon your architect before doing anything else (we did) , because he can give you an idea of the size and shape of lot best suited to the space you need. He can give you a fairly accurate idea of the building cost, and even of how much the different apartments will probably be. He also knows what land in this location or that is selling for. But he cannot draw plans until he knows the size and shape of the plot and surroundings.

When you have found the land, the architect should approve of it before you take an option. He then draws the plans. To draw a stack of apartments suitable to the tastes and requirements of ten different people is difficult, but by no means impossible; so let us say that in a short while his drawings are approved.

You then take up your option and buy the land. You talk about your lovely apartment that is going to cost so much, describe its location, and show its plan. If what you claim for it is true, one person invariably tells another, and that other — or another one still — comes and buys one, and the remaining apartments sell like hot cakes. There should be no difficulty whatever in selling them IF the plans are really inviting in arrangement and comfort, and IF the location is good and the financial responsibility of the original committee is assuring.

A FEW DETAILS MORE

There are two plans of cooperative ownership, the 'TOO per cent.” and the "50 per cent.” In the 100 per cent, plan, every apartment in the building is owned outright and there is none for rent. In the 5 0 per cent, plan, half of the apartments are purposely bought in addition to their own by the owners jointly as a means of income. Whether you think this is a good thing to do, depends upon whether you like an element of chance or not. In this plan, the rentals of half of the apartments go towards paying the expenses of the

owned half. When all the apartments are advantageously rented, it works very well — so well that in certain cooperatively built and owned apartment houses, owners get their own apartments for no rent at all. This is possible, however, only in the rather rare situation of those who built long ago, when the cost both of land and of construction was much less than to-day; and this situation for those fortunate ones will last as long as to-day’s rents remain exorbitant. Should rents and costs go down, they would lose instead. If the building goes too far out of date, or its location out of fashion, and the apartments in a 5 0 per cent, plan cease to rent, the owners will naturally have to pay the upkeep for whatever unrented space there may be. So that while there is a chance of no rental at all, there is at the same time a chance for high assessment for vacancies, plus expenses for upkeep and decoration of apartments to lease, and agents’ fees.

In some cooperative agreements, the promoter of the building company is responsible for any losses accruing through failure to sell the total amount of stock to the tenant-owners. In others this loss devolves upon the owners. It is of utmost importance to find out which is in force in any building you are thinking of buying into. When you build your own, this loss, divided among yourselves, is, as already said, the one uncertainty of owner-builders. But after the apartments are sold, if one is vacated, the responsibility remains with the owner exactly as responsibility for a dwelling-house remains with the owner or his heirs, and not upon his neighbors.

Apartment owners are necessarily more closely affected by the character of the other owners in their building than are property owners on a street, but there should be no danger of financial loss.

There is properly a clause of safety in every cooperative agreement; if an owner fail to pay the maintenance charges, the apartment may be sold by the Board of Directors (owners elected to office by their fellow owners) , the arrears paid, and the balance turned over to the late owner — or his estate.

THE COOPERATIVE APARTMENT

THE COMMUNAL COUNTRY DWELLING

Another type of cooperative is the communal dwelling — really a spread-out country apartment. It is capable of much greater latitude in outline and plan than a city apartment that must be built within the limits of 80 x 100 feet — more or less. The country cooperative is concerned with conserving upkeep costs in the way of heating and building. Kenneth M. Murchison, one of the leading New York architects, developed this project a year or two ago in a design that was very interesting; it was suggestive of the Pueblo Indian communal dwellings merely in that the roof of each floor made a terrace for the floor above. It was attractively suggestive also of the hanging gardens of Babylon! There were divisions of garden space, and a community sports building, with bowling alleys, squash court, and winter swimming pool and tennis courts. A scheme such as this, built of four or six units, with the sports building in the center and connected with each by galleries, would be rather fascinating and quite feasible.

In any event, cooperative ownership, which is now the outstanding characteristic of house-ownership in New York, is capable of indefinite development.

/

IX

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

J ^HAT is loosely — and quite unintelligibly — termed "interior decoration” is actually divided into three ^ f distinct provinces: Interior Architecture, Decoration, and Furnishing.

Interior architecture, which includes all embellishment that is structural, is an inalienable province of the architect — if there be an architect! Decoration in the form of permanently applied color belongs equally to architect or decorator. But all decoration that is movable, such as the hanging of material or the placing of furniture that is not built in, is in the province of the decorator — if there be a decorator!

When an architect of highest reputatipn builds a two or three hundred thousand dollar house, there is never any question as to his right to complete the structural decoration. But in the house of ordinary size, built by an architect of unestablished reputation, the point of separation between the territory of the architect and that of the interior decorator is to many well-meaning but uninformed owners not clearly defined. It is true that certain architects have neither interest in nor taste for interior detail. But even these complete the doors and windows, with their trim, as well as ceilings, floors, and stairways.

Quite possibly an architect himself suggests that a certain French or English room be bought from a certain firm of decorators who specialize in the importation of antique interiors. In this case he may perhaps leave the room — or rooms — floorless, ceilingless, and with walls rough-plastered. Usually, however, he instals the interior himself. That every architect should be expected to complete his own creation is too obvious for question, nor is there any question ninety-

PILASTERS, MANTEL AND CORNICE

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

nine times out of a hundred. But there is the hundredth occasion when an unwitting owner hands over the architecture of the interior to a decorator to complete.

If, for any one of many reasons, an owner would like a certain decorator to do a certain room, he should tell the architect at the time of his first talking of the plans. Nothing could be a greater infringement of the rights of an architect than to wait until the house is half-way up and then announce to him that Miss Drapery is to put pine walls in the living room and an old wainscot in the hall, and set the mantel and a series of painted panels in the drawing-room. Such a breach of ethics is exactly the same as saying to a doctor: "Dr. Brown is coming to-morrow to take over the case!” There are persons who know no better than to do just this; they are the same persons who would send for a decorator before her turn has come. And her turn properly comes when structural decoration is completed, never before, except by the architect’s own agreement.

If an owner sees a set of panels or any other detail of interior embellishment that he would like to put into his house, the proper procedure is to take his architect to look at it. If the architect finds it suitable, it is bought, and he prepares his walls to fit it. Or if it is for the hall, he builds a stair to suit it. If he does not like it, and the owner insists on buying it, the architect must, of course, make for it the best setting that he can. But it is just for the avoidance of such disagreement that it is important in the first place that the owner choose an architect whose taste he likes. If, in his opinion, the architect has no taste, then under no circumstances should the house be given him to build. If, on the other hand, the owner admires his architect’s taste and respects his knowledge, then he should do him the justice to believe him when he declares the old paneled room is bad either in design or in execution, or else unsuitable to the style or size of the house.

It is not necessarily certain, but the chances are rather strongly in favor of the average architect’s knowledge as

against that of the average decorator. It is willingly conceded that really good decorators have much knowledge, and a few have supreme taste; but where there is one of these last there are hundreds who know nothing whatsoever beyond a patter of phrases like that of every other salesman the world over. It is also true that there are architects who have little taste and no great amount of knowledge; but they are greatly in the minority compared with the many who grace with their knowledge and their talent the most beautiful profession in the world.

DETAILS OF THE INTERIOR THAT AN ARCHITECT COMPLETES

In the house or apartment that is built-for-sale, interior architecture necessarily consists of the plainest possible trim, so that it may be added to or torn off by the buyer or his decorator; the walls are plain, smooth-finished plaster, which can be made rough as the surf of the ocean by the application of plastic paint, or covered with wood or marble or whatever else the buyer prefers. Such a house, at this stage, has an unfurred ceiling; a fireplace opening without mantel, facing, or hearth; base-plugs finished, but electric wires in side walls and ceiling outlets protruding through roughedged holes. The kitchen and pantry are completed, with dressers and with plastered or tiled walls, and the wood or cement floors are ready for linoleum or other covering. It is not practical to complete the interior further than this, since the taste of the buyer is unknown.

If you who buy such a house ready-made are not in any way a client of the architect who built it, you either decorate it and furnish it yourself or you put it in the hands of a decorator. Or if you do over a house of your own, you do the same. But if an architect is building — or remodeling — your house, he should always complete the stairs, doors, windows and their trim, columns, pilasters, cornices, chair-rails, wainscots, paneling; all reveals, whether arched or straight; niches and lunettes, screen partitions, mantels, and built-in

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

furniture, such as cupboards and book-cases, as well as iron, bronze or wood grilles, doors, balusters, gateways, and hardware.

An architect always designs every detail that is new, though he may buy old rooms — or mantels or ceilings or floors — and fit them as does a decorator. And if old woodwork is to be bought, it is necessary that he be told of it at the earliest moment possible, so that he may design a setting in which it will be properly at home, instead of building a room without knowledge of its existence, and then being obliged to cut it up and refit it into a space that takes to it neither kindly nor becomingly.

It is also necessary that he adjust his wall surfaces to accommodate overlarge pieces of furniture, or keep their style and size in mind even at the beginning of his design for the house. Therefore, it is essential that he be shown as much of the furniture to be used as possible, since it will not do at all to house Spanish furnishings in Georgian or French surroundings. Nor may he design open-raftered, roughtimbered rooms, in which are to be put furniture in the style of Sheraton, Hepplewhite or Duncan Phyfe. He finishes each room elaborately or simply, in accordance with the intended expenditure of the client and the style of the house.

Certain styles — such as those of the French Louis or Renaissance periods, or the Elizabethan or classic Georgian — naturally necessitate greater elaboration of interior detail on the part of the architect than houses of more primitive type. In ,a simple type of house, the architect constructs a simple stairway, and more than likely composes mantels as well as trim by merely assembling ready-made moldings. He may perhaps find old planks or shutters, with which he panels one end or side of a room into which he fits a mantel as well as cupboards; or he may panel or wainscot one or all of the rooms. He, of course, designs the trim, selects the hardware, and lays the floors. That is about all.

But in a Georgian, French, Italian, or English house of consequence, the architectural interior including the em-

bellishment in color — can quite possibly equal, both in cost and in importance, the structure of the building.

Let us, therefore, rather briefly indicate what is meant by interior architecture in a few of the most typical styles. Since stairways are never designed by any but architects, let us put them at the head of the list.

STAIRWAYS

Beginning with a cottage of most primitive Colonial farm-house type, we have the plainest, steepest, go-up-toattic stairway. Sometimes the spindles are set in a sort of frame between the wainscot and the rail, but usually the risers are so high and the steps so narrow that a hand-rail is set between newel and posts. The beauty characteristic of the Colonial stairway lies in the fragile slenderness of the rail and spindles. If it becomes heavy or loses the graceful hand-made curve of its rail, it becomes charmless and commonplace. A very simple and good type of Colonial stairs is illustrated in Plate 32.

The circular stairway is illustrated by one in my own house (Plate 54) and by the one in Plate 12, which I designed to replace the stair illustrated on the same page. Such stairs are quite suggestive of Cinderellas in gingham frocks, compared with the superlative beauty and elegance of the outstanding feature of a Georgian interior — the unattached or “flying stair.” In its simplest form, the balusters and handrail are of wood; sometimes, in finer forms, the spindles are of wrought iron, though the hand-rail is of wood — a combination of much greater sophistication.

A stair that might almost be called multi-styled is one with stone steps, wrought-iron spindles, and a wood handrail, and is equally suited to Georgian, French or Italian surroundings.

Closely related to the Georgian flying stair is the unsupported Italian stair, which is set into the wall like a shelf, but has no support except that which is made by riser and

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

tread closely joined together. The most beautiful of these stairs are made of marble, with balustrades of wrought iron.

Classified as Tudor or Jacobean English, but equally suitable in primitive, unpainted houses, are the square-turning or straight-mounting unpainted stairs of chestnut or oak or other wood, with hickory-nut-shaped carving at top and bottom of the stair posts. When restrained and simple in design, they are good; but when overheavy, hard and shiny, and machine-turned, like the newel post in the upper half of Plate 12, they are bad.

Tudor houses — also certain German houses — have huge hollow-square staircases of dark-stained oak. Often the posts go up to the ceiling, so that the whole stairway is set ill a frame or enclosed in screens of intricate carving or pierced work.

A stair that is charming in a Spanish house might have tiles set’ into the face of the risers. When the balusters are varied, they are especially delightful, as all things which show hand-made irregularities are apt to be; meaning that the spindles are of the same height and thickness, but the spool-turnings of no two are alike. Their variety rather inexplicably adds quality to the variegated tiles.

French houses naturally suggest stairs of Caen stone or marble with wrought iron, bronze, and gilded rail.

CEILINGS AND WALLS

Ceilings and walls are necessarily details of interior architecture that are more or less determined at the time of the drawing of the first plans. Often they are included in the original estimates — especially if the elaboration of their finished detail has been determined. At all events, their general character has been considered from the start, and preparations made for their being plastered, open-beamed or faced with other material. If an old ceiling has been bought, it must be fitted to the room — or the room to it.

If the ceiling is to have ornamented plasterwork, the

architect designs it, and with it the cornice which joins the ceiling to the walls. Cornices are often made of wood, but unless the walls are of wood also, plaster is more satisfactory. Plaster is, in fact, the outstanding material of interior wall and ceiling decoration. With it every building material known can be imitated, and with it every possible detail of molding or ornamental full-relief or bas-relief be executed at least expense and with best effect.

PLASTER

Few people, perhaps, realize how large a part plaster plays in the embellishment of most rooms. Not only are ceiling motifs, cornices, and pilasters usually of plaster, but shell-shaped ceilings of niches and cupboards, and even open-timber ceiling beams, especially when decorated with carving or painted design in the Spanish or Italian manner, are of plaster six times out of ten. Beautifully detailed French paneling is of plaster almost as often as not! The question, "When is marble plaster?” might quite truthfully be answered, "More times than not!”

Somewhat like the precarious balance of the Leaning Tower of Pisa is the question of how much imitation material may be used without giving the whole composition an effect of sham? Wood painted in imitation of marble, gold paint instead of gold leaf, plaster painted like bronze, plaster in imitation of wood, linoleum in imitation of tile or marble — it is a nice question whether to say, "The better the imitation, the greater its offense,” or to decide that "The nearer its approximation to the real, the more admirable it is.”

In other words, is there an ethical difference between a plaster-beamed ceiling which would deceive the most knowing eye, and rubber tiles which, no matter how perfectly produced, could never be taken for anything but what they are? Is it because they are eye-deceiving that the first are admitted in the best rooms of houses of the highest class, while the second are granted entrance only to the back? Or

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

is the ban on the type of material? Plaster ornamentation, at any rate, has always held its place without need of being other than itself, while rubber as an imitator of marble or tile is so completely different in texture from either, that it does not really imitate at all!

Or is its worth a matter of situation? In great buildings — likewise in palatial houses — one exacts that fine materials be what they seem. Plaster undisguised is real. It becomes imitation when it is gilded to look like carved and gilded wood on mirror frames, or marbleized on walls, pilasters and wainscots. But if whatever it is made to seem, is beautiful, what more should one ask? Far better install beautifully designed plasterwork, expertly molded or painted, than wood or stone, or even marble, that is unpleasing in color and ugly in design. Besides, who amongst us can live in a marble room? Go into it, oh, yes; but live in it — ?

There is, at all events, the best possible precedent for the use of plaster in imitation of marble wainscots — also of pilasters and walls in Georgian or eighteenth-century English — no less than in Italian rooms. There are likewise the precedents left by the best Florentine, Roman, and Venetian architects in the use of plaster to imitate wood in ornamented ceilings. Without plaster the ornamentation of our houses would be reduced to the plain painted or papered walls, with wooden picture moldings, chair rails and baseboards; or else to the pine room — at present not merely a fashion, but a craze — and an occasional room of Jacobean paneling. After all, there is no reason why plaster should be belittled; it is a medium by which smooth-finished fineness is obtained, without which most of our loveliest interiors would be as plain and flat as the inside of a bandbox.

On the other hand, rough plaster surfaces share with varnished golden oak the responsibility for more ugliness of interior than that produced by any other medium. Whether they be stucco, plastic paint, or plaster of sorts, rough walls of this kind — suggesting maps of the moon or a beach that has been violently pitted with rain — have not even the excuse

of utility, since they are as dirt-catching as they are vulgarizing to everything brought into contact with them. A sanded finish, at most a trifle coarser than flat wave-washed sand of a smooth beach, is an admirable background for furniture of the cruder varieties, and for hangings of Indian print, tapa cloth and Navajo blankets — possibly also for the cruder tapestries — but is scarcely suitable to needle-point or to fine brocades and velvets. It would be difiicult to combine two textures that produce more cheapening results than velvets and fine silks against a stalactite wall.

Each style of house dictates more or less distinctly the details of its interior. It is never necessary to follow a period so closely that individuality is lost. A house should never’ be looked upon as though it were one soldier in a regiment — Spanish, Georgian, French, Dutch, or whatever the soldiers’ uniforms proclaim them. But it is necessary — not because of any esthetic decree, but merely for becomingness — that primitive things be put together and placed against a background that shall bring out their beauty instead of their crudeness; and that fragile, smooth, fine things be assembled against a smooth, fine-detailed background. All of which explains why certain styles have gradually made their own definite selections, and why custom has naturally classified like with like.

You can classify as much as you please — and not mrrely with individuality, but with esthetic success — so long as a feeling of suitability and becomingness is preserved. No one is less regimentally exact than an architect of talent. If there is one thing he takes to less gladly than to another, it is putting b precisely after a, and c after b. But, naturally, if you have asked him to build you an Elizabethan house, he will instinctively suggest a great square staircase with oak wainscot and Jacobean rooms, and design a living-room with linen-fold paneling, and Old English arched carving over the mantel. But, if you give him any encouragement, he will do any one of a dozen other things; he will leave the walls plastered plain and put in a vaulted ceiling, or he will leave

James W. O’Connor, Architect

A DELIGHTFUL ROOM WITH A HUNTING PERSONALITY

CASEMENT WINDOWS AND QUAINT CHINTZ EQUALLY SUITED TO COLONIAL OR ENGLISH INTERIOR

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

the ceiling flat and prepare the wall for paper. Or if furniture is to be of mahogany rather than oak, he will choose an interior with smooth walls and classic pilasters that is perhaps quite Georgian.

Even if the house you have chosen is Italian, it can be in accord with Georgian, since Georgian is purely classic, and it was from Italy and Greece that classic architecture came.

For a Spanish house, let us say that he designs lovely spindled grilles of wood, and courtyard balconies, and that he builds you door-frames that are neither too floridly Rococo nor Moorish nor Venetian — nor set round with violent-patterned tiles! Let us say that he leaves the door openings plain, or framed with wood in the age-old posts and lintel construction, with carving or painting of the cross-beam.

In a French chateau of the Louis periods he has less leeway. Yet even so there are endless modes of paneling and of painting French interiors. For a few of these see Chapter XIII.

MANTELS

Mantels naturally are in the province of the architect quite as much as the flues for which they are built. And since a knowledge of proportion is the first requirement of ability in an architect— just as a sense for color is the first requirement of a decorator — let it be urgently asked that mantels be chosen, or probably designed, by the architect and by no one else. An ill-fitting mantel is one of the most common faults of owner- or decorator-furnished rooms. The mantel may be beautiful in itself, but that is no reason for thinking it an object of becomingness to a wall against which it looks either like a monument or a postage stanip.

For a room of standard size and height, an architect quite possibly buys a mantel, either old or new; but, for a room that is especially low or high or wide, he designs one of suitable proportions to go in it; this unfailing sense of proportion, in fact, is one of the things that we ask of an architect.

If a decorator is to complete the rooms, the architect seldom puts in any color, unless he has an unusual taste in color and is asked by the owner to complete the painting. If there is to be no decorator, the architect completes the foundation color, or at least helps his client in the selection of colors that are harmonious with her furnishings, and she does the rest.

HOW THE ARCHITECT AND DECORATOR MAY COOPERATE

An architect’s cooperation with a decorator would be something like this: Let us say the client, the architect and the decorator together have decided that there is to be a pine room, a green-walled French-paneled living-room, and a cream-white dining-room, with hangings that the decorator has suggested of green and cream brocade. There is to be a scenic paper in the hall that the decorator is to put on. In this arrangement, the architect finishes the pine room complete — walls, cornices, ceiling, doors with hand-wrought latches and hinges, window framing, mantel, built-in cupboards, bookcases, and floor. In the hall he builds a flying stairway, designs the trim with broken pediment over-doors, cornice, chair-rail, and lays the marble floor. In the livingroom he designs the mantel, the ceiling, the paneling, and the floor. In the dining-room he designs a mantel, cornice, ceiling ornamentation and pilasters, lays a herringbone wooden floor, and his job is ended.

The decorator then comes in, puts on her scenic paper — an old one in her own collection — and paints the woodwork that has been built by the architect to tone with a tint in her paper. She paints the living-room green, the color of her brocade, and the dining-room the cream that is the background of the green. She decorates and finishes all the bedrooms, and all the service end. In the principal rooms she puts down rugs, puts up the hangings, and places the furniture, as well as the rugs, lamp-shades, and ornaments throughout the house.

INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

To repeat: The greater the importance of the house, the greater is the owner’s obligation to let the architect complete his own creation. In small or unimportant houses, the architect considers that his work is ended when he has finished the stairs and doors and window trim. But in a great house, an architect of high professional standing in consultation with the taste of the owner completes all the lower rooms, leaving the decorations of bedrooms and the service end of the house to be completed either by a decorator or by the owner himself.

Few architects buy furniture for private houses; occasional ones do, and all do occasionally.

X

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

UCH as style, form, and proportion mean to the beauty of every interior, nothing means so much to the personality of a house as color. Color can so enchant you that all other details are overwhelmed. Or it can so distress you that there are no compensations possible.

Without color, every object that surrounds us, everything we own, or look at, is lacking in beauty, warmth, gaiety, charm, sympathy, strength, and vitality. Without color, even we ourselves are uninteresting. A colorless house or a colorless person is equally antipathetic, dull, drab and lifeless. But because some people are inarticulate in their expression of color, it does not follow that their love for beauty of color is less than that of others who are skilled in its arrangement. Nor is there proof that those who unknowingly surround themselves with discord might not greatly prefer harmony were they given the opportunity of esthetic cultivation.

The phonograph, the player piano, and even more notably the radio broadcasting of best music, have brought knowledge of musical harmony to thousands who otherwise would have little or no opportunity to cultivate appreciation of that which is good, instead of being immunized to that which is bad. But there is no way as yet of broadcasting harmony of color. One inevitably associates music with color, because both appeal to the emotions, both create visions of beauty quite as much for those ^ho merely look or listen as for those who design or compose.

Of course, there are those who, without imminent danger of insanity, can endure strumming and banging upon a neighbor’s never-closed piano. (Whoever it was that im-

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

pressed American parents with the idea that excruciating piano-playing by their utterly talentless offspring is evidence of “culture” has more than much to answer for!) And the same nerves that can, with seeming indifference, endure assault and battery from an out-of-tune musical instrument, are not likely to suffer greatly from the silent jangles and visual discords of color.

Let us, therefore, leave the discord endurers to themselves and consider color from the point of view of those who not only love beauty of harmony but appreciate this quality too much to be content to go through life without having it around them.

TALENT OR CULTIVATION

It is true, of course, that certain persons — they are very rare — are born with what is called a notable color sense, just as others are born with a notable talent for music. And it it also true that these few can never be equaled in original composition by the thousands upon thousands who have slight facility or aptitude, but no talent to speak of at all. But the point to be brought out is this: That just as it is not necessary to have a heaven-born ear for music in order to learn to listen to music with appreciation and pleasure, or to play the piano with fluent ease, neither is it necessary to have a heaven-born eye for color in order to understand the principles of its harmony.

Much as musical facility is accomplished by learning to read notes, practising with the fingers, and cultivating an appreciative intelligence by means of a listening ear, color skill can be acquired by learning the rules of harmony from color charts, and cultivating an appreciative intelligence by means of an observing eye. This chapter, therefore, is written especially for those who really love beautiful color arrangements when they see them, and who would like to make such arrangements for themselves, but have never happened to be shown the mechanical process by which color harmonies are created and discords avoided.

Those of you who have made a study of color in fine arts classes will find nothing new in this chapter until you come to Figure 5 4, which is a device of my own. But to those who do not happen to know about triad harmonies, monochromatic neutrals, and complementary accents, as well as barricaded family groups and near-relation discords, I will try to explain as briefly but as clearly as possible the composition and use of color charts.

MAKE YOUR OWN COLOR CHARTS

Although the most important chart is reproduced in this chapter in full color, so that its principles may be understood by merely looking at it, I would suggest that you take the trouble to make a set of charts yourself, because the practical experiment in color-mixing gives a better understanding of how to make unlimited color-blendings of your own by merely following the same principles.

The directions for making the charts suggest kindergarten work, but they are not quite as easy as they sound. Because, unless you are as painstakingly accurate as a chemist following a formula, an unequal mixture of the colors (or weakness of their intensity) will so alter the combinations that the results will be all wrong — and the charts useless.

However, this is how to begin: Go out and buy a sheet of white drawing-paper and six small-sized tubes (or little square dishes) of any reliable make of water colors, one tube (or dish) each of cobalt-blue, alizarin-crimson, cadmium-yellow, violet, white and black, and a small paintbrush. Also collect a pencil, a pair of scissors, a five-cent piece, a sheet of thin cardboard, a dozen cups and saucers or other receptacles, a salt-spoon, and a bowl of clear water.

TO PREPARE YOUR COLORS

Squeeze half a tube of yellow paint into a cup; squeeze precisely the same amount of blue paint into another cup,

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

and the same amount of red paint into a third cup. Measure the amounts by the length left in the flattened end of each tube. Add ]mt enough water to each to make the paints fluid. A tube that is dried out will take a little more water than a tube that is half liquid. The paints must be about as thick as medium cream, because the colors must be as intense as possible.

Cut a twelve-inch square of drawing-paper; cut an even-sided triangle, about six inches on each side, out of the piece of thin cardboard; lay it in the center of the drawingpaper; put a pin (or a thumb-tack) in the exact middle of it — so that it will revolve on this center but cannot side-slip. Turn it so that one point rests at the top and the other two at the corners of the base. Cut a small pattern of cardboard an inch wide by five-eighths of an inch high, lay it across the top point, and draw around it. Do the same at the other two points.

CHARTS AND COMBINATIONS

Fill in the rectangle at the top with the yellow paint you have dissolved, the one at the lower right with blue, and the one in the lower left with red. These three colors, painted as strong as they can be, are the colors, from which

all colors are made without any other addition (excepting white or black) . Next, turn the triangle so that the points come midway between the rectangles drawn. Cut threeeighths of an inch from one end of the oblong pattern, thus making it a square; put this square at each point of the triangle, and draw around it.

Now take a salt-spoon and put two spoonfuls of yellow on a clean saucer; rinse the spoon and add two spoonfuls of red, and mix them thoroughly. The result will be orange (called a secondary or binary color) . In the same way, mix equal quantities of yellow and blue, which will give you the secondary color, green. Violet, the third secondary color, is made by mixing equal parts of red and blue. This can be done if your paints behave nicely, but they so often insist upon

separating into red and blue granulations that won’t mix, that this one combination is much safest and easiest to buy mixed by a paint chemist, in a tube called violet. At all events, whether mixed by you or by the manufacturer, paint pure violet in the bottom square you draw; put green in the upper right one and orange in the upper left. It is very important to

BEGINNING YOUR OWN COLOR CHART - PRIMARY AND SECONDARY COLORS

notice that these mixtures are even; that no binary favors one primary more than the other. Remember that the three primary colors are in the rectangles, and the secondary colors in the squares (Fig. 51).

Now, in six other saucers, mix a spoonful each of two neighboring colors — yellow and orange, orange and red, red and violet, etc. Turn your triangle so that the points come exactly between the yellow and orange, red and violet, blue and green. Place the five-cent piece at each point and draw around it. Turn it again and draw a circle between yellow and green, blue and violet, red and orange. In these circles

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

paint a half-and-half mixture of the primaries and binaries that each is between. These are the tertiary or third-degree colors. The one halfway between yellow and orange is made of an equal mixture of yellow and orange, and is called yellow-orange. The one midway between red and violet is

DIAGRAM OF COMPLETE CHROMATIC COLOR CHART

made by mixing even quantities of red and violet, and is called red-violet. The third, between blue and green, is bluegreen. Yellow-green is between yellow and green, blue- violet between blue and violet, and red-orange between red and orange. These twelve hues complete the chromatic color scale in its highest intensity.

Remove the cardboard triangle and draw a five-cent-sized circle at the hub of the chromatic circle — where the pin went through. In this hub-circle paint the color "neutral gray.”

This color (or colorlessness) is made by mixing equal portions of your original primary red, yellow, and blue. Theoretically, this combination should make white, but the only way this can be achieved is by fusing the three colors without mixing them. To prove it, take a small circle of paper painted yellow, red and blue in three equal divisions, like a pie cut into three pieces, and fasten it on a child’s top. When it is spun, three colors thus fused but not mixed will turn to white. But in mixing paint, or even in looking through the three colors of glass, the result is neutral gray — when the primary quantities are equal.

Having painted this gray in the center of your circle of colors, mix equal portions of the neutral color and yellow, and paint a narrow strip (or spoke) between the yellow rectangle and the gray center (Fig. 52) . This color is called grayyellow. Mix half gray with half yellow-green and paint a spoke of it between the yellow-green square and the gray hub; and label this spoke or strip, gray-yellow-green. Repeat this with the twelve colors; mark each of the neutral tones gray-red or gray-yellow-orange, or gray-blue-violet, and the chromatic chart is finished. The colors can equally well be put in uniform squares or circles, but the shapes as indicated define primary rectangles, secondary squares, tertiary circles, and pastel spokes, at a glance. The use of this chart, now that it is complete, will be explained as soon as we make a value chart.

HOW TO MAKE A VALUE CHART

This is very simple. Rinse all the cups and saucers except those containing the colors painted in the three rectangles and three squares. Take a fresh piece of paper and divide it into six up-and-down sections. In the center of one of these, paint a horizontal block of undiluted yellow (in the space marked "intense” in Figure 53). Now mix one-quarter of white with three-quarters of yellow, and paint a strip of the mixture above your primary yellow. Then mix one portion of yellow with one pbrtion of white and put the result in a

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

strip above that. Then mix one-quarter of yellow with threequarters of white and put that above the others. Then take black and do the same: three quarters yellow and onequarter black just below the primary yellow; half-and-half below that, and three-quarters black to one-quarter yellow at the bottom. Repeat this procedure with each of the primary and secondary colors.

COLOR VALUES

These various tints and shades are known as high and low values. The original undiluted color is known as middle value — or full intensity. A tint that is a quarter white is called low light. Half white is called light. Three-quarters white is high light. A shade that is one-quarter black is high dark; half black is dark, and three-quarters black is low dark. (See Fig. 53.)

Technically, the colors that have been mixed with white are known as tints, those that are mixed with black are shades. Professional painters and all teachers of fine arts make a great fuss about the ignorance of any one who asks for a shade of palest red or blue. An expert would hand such a person primary red or blue mixed with the least bit of black; but all the rest of us would select an almost white tint. Not one person in a million bothers in the least about the meaning of "tint” or "shade or even "hue” — beyond explaining them, as I am doing now.

Each separate color on the chromatic chart is a separate hue. Nile green and dark leaf -green are the high light and low dark of the same hue. Nile green and yellow are different hues.

There are three ways of changing a color: mixing it with another color pig. 53

changes its hue; mixing it with black or value chart

white changes its value; mixing it with its complement makes it a pastel color.

COMPLEMENTARY COLORS

Put the chromatic chart in front of you, and. in place of the triangle cut a strip of cardboard in the shape of an arrow; put a pin through the middle of it, and pin it so that it can swing around like a weather-vane. To find the complement of any color, swing the feather end of the arrow in front of it, and the color the arrow points to is its complement. Complementary colors are occasionally called opposed colors, which would certainly seem more sensible, since the complement of each color is that which contains none of its own hues. Red is the complement of green, which is a mixture of blue and yellow and contains no red. Orange is made of red and yellow, and contains no blue; therefore blue is its complement. Yellow and violet, which haven’t a trace of the same blood, are complementary colors. Yellow-green and redviolet are too remotely connected by the kinship of blue to be classed as relatives. Blue green and red-orange are utter strangers.

Complementary — or opposed — colors have a curious effect on each other that is interesting to experiment with if you make your charts yourself. They lose all brilliancy if any portion of one is mixed with the other. Equal parts of vivid yellow and strongest violet mixed together result in neutral gray. The same gray is the result of mixing the strongest blue with orange, or green with red, or any of the intermediate hues, such as red-violet, with yellow-green. One of the uses of a chart is to see by it which colors are safely blended and which others cannot be mixed without burning to ashes.

A small amount of every color’s complement mixed with itself turns the original color to a pastel hue. And yet, put near each other but not mixed, complementary colors are the most brilhant contrasts possible to produce. It is very necessary to know the complement of the keynote of each color

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

scheme, because a complementary accent is to harmonious color arrangement what salt is to food. If there is none of this ingredient, the composition is apt to lack flavor, but too much of it is a violent emetic! And now to explain;

HARMONIOUS AND DISCORDANT FAMILY RELATIONS

On a piece of thin paper, trace an outline of your chromatic chart and color it. Next, draw a long line through the center of each rectangle and square except orange^ con- • tinuing through each pastel spoke; and connect these barri-

CHART SHOWING THE DIFFERENT COLOR FAMILIES

cades with encircling lines, so that the chart is divided into one double and four single sections. (See Fig. 54.) Each of these sections is occupied by a single family, dwelling in complete accord. The safest recipe for harmony, therefore, is to keep within the immediate family section as marked by each of these boundaries. For instance, the line running through

the center of yellow and gray-yellow, and connected with the line through green and gray-green, enclose the family of yellow married to green and the various yellow and greenhued children of that pair. The sister of green, in the same way, is married to blue. Blue’s brother is married to violet. Violet’s sister to red. But red is married to yellow, and orange is one of their children.

In each of these family sections (one double-sized and four small) , discord is impossible; the two parent colors, with their intermediate-hued children, live in perfect harmony with each other, and with their own neutrals and their values, including every shade and tint — creams, browns, slates, and grays — in the big yellow-red family. Gray-reds and grayviolets are lightened to pink-violet-cream and violet-silvergray in the smaller red-violet family, or to whatever combinations the hues of each group alone (or with the addition of white or black) accomplish.

But just as the families within the enclosures made by each barrier are in undisturbable accord, the nearest neighboring cousins on either side of the barriers are discords. Redorange and red-violet fight like Kilkenny cats. Red- violet put next to blue-violet, and blue-violet put next to blue-green, and yellow-green put next to blue-green, all have hysterics. Only great masters of color can make discords behave harmoniously, and then only with the most perfect adjustment of hue and value as well as neutral predominance. The warning, therefore, cannot be too prominently posted that those not supremely color-gifted — unless purposely attempting jazz — would better leave these cousinly discords alone.

TRIADS OF HARMONY

If you have made no chart, cut a paper triangle of the size indicated by the triangle outline and pin the middle of it to the middle of the gray center circle of the colored chart in this chapter. If you pin it so that it is held at the center of its own outline and at the exact center of the gray circle, no

THE PRINCIPLES OF COLOR HARMONY

matter where it swings its three corners will infallibly point to colors that are in harmony with each other. Yellow, red, and blue; violet, orange, and green; red-orange, blue-violet, and yellow-green, and so on, are each triads of harmony, which may be combined with safety if modified either in quantity or intensity. This is an unbreakable rule.

The confident belief by the unperceiving that all triads are "safe” would seem rather to explain why so many interior decorations attempted by the totally taste-deficient are so colorfully horrible! No school of design ever told its inattentive pupils that triad combinations were to be taken in equal parts and at full strength! Yet this is exactly what certain so-called designers do with their color-box in front of them and not a vestige of tradition behind them! And that is not the way they should be taken.

In a closely related color scheme, such as one that is confined within the limits of its immediate family circle (see Fig. 54) , colors may with safety be used in high intensity — combined, of course, with their own grayed hues and with any mixture of white or black. But the unrelated colors of a triad harmony must be softened down (grayed) or diluted either in value or in quantity.

Combining the triads in full strength is excellent for posters, since they are intended to hit you in the eye as hard as they can. But by modifying the intensity of these colors with black or white, or both, there are unlimited combinations of beauty that you can make.

For example, let us suppose your triangle is pointing to primary red and blue and yellow.
