Let us purposely begin with this triad because the first recipe given by every school of decoration is to put something red, blue, and yellow into every room. But this does NOT mean equal quantities or colors of greatest intensity!

Lighten the yellow to cream, gray the red to rose, silver the blue with high-light gray for the biggest masses; add the smallest accent of red’s complementary color, green; and you have a lovely combination. Or suppose we make a personal

triad in detail. Let us say that you are thinking of a color scheme built on blue-green. Swing your triangle so that one point of it is at blue-green. The other points will indicate yellow-orange and red-violet. And this again demonstrates the necessity for modifying intensity or quantity. Few could possibly endure to live in a room that had blue-green walls, red-violet hangings, and yellow-orange furniture, with a splash of red-orange, which is blue-green’s complementary color, added for salt.

No matter which triad of which two-color family or which monochrome you choose for your theme of color, all additions of white and black are considered colorless. Gold as well as silver is also considered colorless.

WEIGHT OF COLOR

Weight of color must be considered in order that a color scheme shall be in balance. Heavier color must go on the floor, lighter color on the ceiling, with medium color between the two, on the walls. If this rule is reversed and heavy color be chosen for the ceiling and light color for the floor, the room will threaten to turn bottom side up.

Weight of color is measured by its nearness or distance from yellow, which is the color-equivalent of sunlight. Yellow is the highest in luminosity or light-giving quality. Orange ranks next to yellow. Next in order come green, red, blue, and violet last. White and black are unique in their aloofness from color. No matter how almost white a tint or how almost black a shade, the least amount of color is rated as color. Black- violet, the darkest color possible, is rated purple. The lightest cream is more closely related to yellow, which makes 1/1 00th of its total parts, than to white, which supplies 99/lOOths of its component parts.

XI

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

y^PART from hue and value, every color has a defi- ^ nite emotional character that is restless, stimulat-

JL ing, soothing, charming, irritating or depressing, as well as temperamentally friendly or formal. Moreover, certain colors behave beautifully under all circumstances, while others do nothing of the kind.

As explained in the previous chapter, yellow represents light.

Red is the elemental color expressing fire, energy and primitive passion. In its brightest tones, red is the most brilliant, stimulating and approaching of all colors.

Vermilion is the strongest, brightest, most exciting color that there is. It, of course, affects people in different ways. It not merely stimulates and revivifies, but its cheering warmth comforts as well as delights those who happen to love it. If you are one of these, you are never quite happy without at least an accent of it somewhere. If it is not your color — if, you, for instance, are in sympathy with blue, all vivid yfellow-reds are probably jarringly violent. In fact, an unexpected encounter with much of it might be something like meeting an uncaged lion roaming through the”house.

Blue is ice, the coldest, most formal and most distant and retreating of colors. Blue and yellow make green, a combination of light tempered with shade.

Green, since it is the color of leaves and intended by nature to harmonize with every color, will do so if properly blended. Remember, however, that flowers have a quality of texture which modifies their hue. Remember also that greens are as varied m shade and tint as though composed of countless hues.

RED AND AGAIN RED

Red with gray' gives us a deep wine-red, or darkened with black and accented with yellow it gives us the brown- toned crimson — sometimes called old Italian red — that is richly warm, dignified, restful, friendly and becoming as is no other color, to a very large, very high-studded room, with dark wood paneling. Old Italian red is the perfect background for objects of size and weight. It is the violoncello of color music.

Yellow and red — light and fire — ^produce orange and the most vivid blaze of heat and light.

Red lighted to an orange tone or used in full vermilion strength is like a cornet, inspiring and strongly accented, but is at its best when accompanied by a full orchestra of creams and blacks and browns. A room of waxed cypress, or of pine with embellishment of vermilion or of orange-red — sometimes called flame — in velvet, taffeta, brocade or wool damask, is satisfying as nothing else to the temperament to which this color appeals. Badly handled or taken out of its own family group, it is unendurable.

UGLIFICATIONS AND BEAUTIFICATIONS OF BROWN

Brown is a color of fourth intensity and has no right whatever to be listed next to red, which is a color of first intensity , but It IS so closely associated with vermilion and its orange-tinted sister, flame, that its immediate consideration is logical, at all events. Brown can be the ugliest color 5 or perhaps the truer way to put it is that it is more often made ugly than any other color.

It is a tradition to regard brown as of mud-and-mustard homeliness, and therefore the least amount of thought spent on its beautification is considered good enough for it. Certain tones of brown do seem desperately ugly, but the ugliest brown is seldom as ugly— merely as a color in itself— as its treatment and environment make it appear.

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

All earth is brown, tree trunks are brown, shadows are brown — or brownish purple. Millions of people have brown hair or eyes, other millions on every summer beach in the world are turning their skins as brown as may be! Brown is an essential of beauty. Without brown and its dilutions of snuff, tan, fawn, beige, and sand, color harmonies would be like a musical score without a base. Nearly all brown can be reduced to ugliness or elevated to beauty according to its situation and use.

The old milk-bench and hanging shelves illustrated in Plate 5 5 are not beautiful either in quality or in color. They are made of combined woods in at least seven different tones of browns, pinkish, yellowish — two planks are greenish — with innumerable dents and scars of black. A quantity of this coloring used as the whole trim or wainscoting of a room would be not only ugly but squalid; and yet, used as an accent, it has value; the-obviously long service in dairy and scullery of this one unit of furniture gives character to an otherwise merely "pretty little” cottage room.

Even black and its dilutions of gray would be cold and anemic substitutes for the warm depths of tones that are produced by deep browns, light browns, gray browns, golden browns, dark browns, tan browns. But brown more than any other color is perhaps dependent for its beauty upon texture rather than upon tone. A flower-bed newly cultivated, so that it is dark and rich and soft, adds immeasurably to the beauty of the plants seen against it. The same earth, hardpacked, is ugly in itself and detracting from the plants that uncomfortably stand in it. Most woods — as they are finished — are brown or dilutions of brown.

What is known as a patined surface of wood, which is produced by the polishing of time — combined with oil and wax — upon smooth, even, and carefully selected wood, is the most beautifying element of furnishing known. Brown at its ugliest is represented in coarse fiber surface and a greenish cast of hue, or yellowish green tan, or in oatmeal-gray cast of color and finish.

Brown wood becomes ugly, because vulgar, in smooth "high light” finish, or in rough dull bumpy wall-paper, or in combination with other vulgarities of design or color. It is at its best when the wood is expertly chosen and treated, and smoothly finished in its proper hue.

If a great amount of very dark wood is to be used for the walls, it must be balanced by nicely adjusted relief of becoming color, so that the room shall be homelike but not homely. It was not by accident that the somber, dark oak paneling of the Elizabethan period was relieved by windows, the size of the whole of the room’s width, that admitted a counterbalancing amount of light.

The soft brown of cypress or the often unstained finish of pine are amenable to a reduction of window space in direct proportion to the lightness of their finished color. In reducing your window space it must be remembered, however, that all wood darkens with age — unless it is shellacked — which Heaven forbid!

APPROACH-ME-NOT BLUE

Blue — ^meaning not navy or Delft, but the more sophisticated French or Italian blue — by reputation, as already noted, is cold, restrained, and intellectual. One would expect it, therefore, to behave calmly, serenely, irreproachably under all circumstances; instead of which it is more impossible to manage than a temperamental opera singer, and more unamiable than Katherine the Shrew.

One blue article is charming, be it wall or hanging, sofa coveHng or chair; but bring a second blue article into close proximity with the first, and the chances are that there is a fight forthwith — which ends in the destruction of one or the other, or both. Attempt to assemble ten separate items of blue— and complicate this attempt by the test of daylight and lamplight and you will believe Hagenbeck’s control of ten tigers the easier accomplishment.

It is impossible to imagine the ravages that individually

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

beautiful blues can wreak upon each other! And if out of a hundred samples you find two or three that behave well together in the daylight, puU down the shades and light the lamps and watch their behavior then!

From the point of view of amiability and kindly behavior, yellow is if possible even better behaved than red. Reds often quarrel in the daytime though in perfect accord at night. But yellows of every tone can be put together with becomingness to themselves and equal becomingness to each other. Yellow-orange and orange-red, with every tint of yellow and cream, can be counted on at all times to beautify each other to the utmost.

GREEN

Green tints and shades of the same family go well together, though the family to which they belong often changes by day and by night. Green is known as the most restful of all colors, and in its endless variation of hues and shades and tints can be chosen to suit every human mood. It can, however, like brown, be very ugly and utterly light-absorbing — especially at night — ^if its shade be too dark.

VIOLET

Red and blue — fire and ice — consume each other and leave ashes, which is the reason why violet is the color of mourning, the color of nothingness, the color of twilight. The standardized violet-gray-black hue called neutral tint is the color of shadows.

Violet is also known as the color of mystery, because, besides being the nearest to lack of color, it is, next to black, the heaviest color known. Therefore it would be suffocating if used in unrelieved quantities, either on the walls or on the furniture. And yet in its intangible manifestation it could give to a wall an effect of immeasurable distance. This, howgYcr, IS a problem in texture and value too difficult to be suggested to a novice, since the wrong value or the wrong texture

with the wrong lighting and the wrong furniture might all too easily produce the impression of being shut inside of an Egyptian tomb.

A FEW OTHER CHARACTERISTICS OF COLOR

Green and blue are known as receding colors ; red, orange, and yellow are those which advance, meaning that rooms painted blue or green should seem larger than duplicate rooms with walls painted red or yellow. It has often been noted in hotels, where the rooms on different floors are identical in size, that persons who have at one time been given a room painted light blue or green insist that the duplicate red one they are in is only half as large as the other. That red comes forward is true, but not to a degree comparable with the difference of high or low value. Red, in other words, must be compared with green and blue of the same value — crimson with dark leaf -green or with a bright dark-blde or pumpkinyellow. And in comparing a room of high-light Nile-green or pale sky-blue, it would be necessary to take pink or lemon.

Primary or binary colors in full strength are seasonings which, like salt and pepper, must be used with discretion except when a deliberate irritant is sought.

The grayed colors are the useful colors whose quantity is unlimited in rooms of distinction.

An unrelieved monotone is already defined by its own derivative monotony. But a room fitted with so much relief that it looks like a spilled box of paints is equally unappealing.

THE COLOR OF THE BACKGROUND

The part which one color plays in every theme of decoration is much the same as that played by one instrument in an orchestra. Not that the simile is exact; but it will perhaps serve to explain what is meant, if we say that the background of a room is a bass viol, and the hangings and furnishings to be put into the room are the violins, cellos, cornets, French

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

horns, and so on. A background that is as strident as a saxophone will be suitable in a cocktail-room or a dancing-room that is of intentionally jazzed design. Saxophonic colors would be primary and binary colors in undiluted intensity.

Apart from these six banned hues, the degree of strength that can be used with harmonious results depends entirely upon the modifications — or irritations — of the other colors with which it is brought into close relationship.

There are two ways to choose color for a painted background. The first is to paint the background the color you like and suit the furnishings to it. The second is the reverse. It is also the simplest method — and the safest. There are two reasons only for considering the background first. One, if a painted decoration, or a scenic oi^ other decorative wall-paper is to be used, it is chosen first and the draperies and furniture covering are selected to go with it. Reason number two, if you have a color in your mind and nothing can stop your impulse to mix it and have it put on, you will do the wall first and then find things to put against it — which may be easy, or may not.

But, taking the normal procedure for our example, let us begin with the most important, or better, the most appealing object that is to be put into the room. It may perhaps be a rug that you have to use — no, let us not begin with a problem of ugliness in a new room! There will be a whole chapter, later on, on the beautification of ugliness theme; so let us begin with an object of beauty, a picture, a Chinese bowl, a pair of vases, or perhaps best of all, a piece of chintz or brocade that you saw in a shop and couldn t resist. This is the easiest possible method of deciding upon the color of your wall. Merely choose from the colors of this central object of beauty, either taking one of its more neutral colors or lightening one of its stronger colors to a tint of wall suitability.

In choosing the color for your walls, it is necessary to bear in mind the various temperamental characteristics of colors and to pick out one that will behave as you want it to do. The best colors for backgrounds are all the tints of sand and cream

and pinkish taupe-cream, which are better when mixed with an undertone of light than when painted solid. For instance, you can build your wall on yellow orange, a color not to be used either in quantity or in strength; but, lightened with white and neutralized with gray, it will give you a safe background of grayish cream in which the yellow-orange element is unnoticeable except to give it an effect of glowing light.

A light-producing process in painting such a wall is to paint a first coat of yellow-orange in "high light” value, and then glaze or stipple it over with grayish cream.

Theoretically, white would seem a bad background, since the rule for a background is that it shall stay back, and not come forward and gobble up whatever is put before it. But against a white or lightest cream background you can put the clearest colors, such as watermelon, blue-green, emerald, or orchid. Against deep cream or pale lemon these colors are slightly creamed or lemoned.

One is taught that colors are more brilliant upon a background of white, which is composed of all colors, than upon black, which is complete absence of color. But if you place two identical bouquets of flowers, one against white and one against black of the same texture of surface, your eye certainly tells you that the flowers on the black background are seemingly the more brilliant. This is perhaps because their values are apt to be high, and therefore in greater contrast to lowest value, which is black.

Merely to give an example of the way to use a key; Let us suppose you have a pair of Lowestoft vases of puttycolored porcelain, decorated with raspberry-pink and red, blue-green and gold. These give you your background of putty-gray, with — if you like — paneling gilded and then wiped off, or the accentuation glazed in neutral tint. With these go raspberry-taffeta hangings, and blue-green smallsptigged brocade. If you use your triad chart you will see that raspberry, blue-green and orange are in harmony. Be moderate of this last. Let it be gold, or let it be diluted, or let it be in a copper bowl, book bindings or flowers.

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

Or suppose — still illustrating your vases and their triad — that you reverse the arrangement. You cannot very well attempt raspberry walls; but you can make your walls grayed blue-green with woodwork to match, and fill the room with gaily flowering but finely designed chintz on a putty-colored ground, using this to cover furniture of walnut or fruit wood. Or you may choose one of the light grayish browns that are actually dilutions of orange and neutral gray. Perhaps the material on a very small chair might be of the Lowestoft raspberry, the accent of missing orange being supplied in the chintz ; if not, it can be furnished as already noted.

However, let us return to backgrounds by themselves, since furnishings must be put aside for later chapters of their own. Next to the various beige and cream tints, the easiest color to get right in painting a room is either blue-green or sea-green-blue. Your difficulty begins in adding blue-green or sea-green-blue fabrics, but with solid walls and trim there will be enough of it, and any object added should be of contrasting value. The hardest color to paint is pink. A tint so pale that it seems almost white becomes unendurable the moment the four walls reflect each other. It must always be remembered, when selecting wall-papers or paint samples, that four walls are at least twice as intense as one alone.

You have surely had this experience: The painter shows you a sample of color on a strip af board or on a patch of wall. You think you would like it a little more blue or a little more yellow, or you suggest that it be darker or Finally, it is exactly what you want. You go away. By and by you come back to find a room that is dark as night or shriekingly violent. You indignantly ask the painter why he has changed the color. You wanted just a touch of orchid—- not a purple wall! Or you wanted it soft flesh, not intensified salmon. The painter shows you the pail of paint he is using. You look from paint to wall, and back to paint again; the wall seems to be covered with a red rash, and yet the paint in the pail is pinkish cream. All colors look many shades darker when four times reflected on the walls of a room;

but pink when wrong is more vulgarly wrong than any color one is likely to attempt — unless orchid is worse!

Many years ago, I had a room painted completely over four times. I had had a rather small pink-papered room at home that I liked, so I had a paneled bedroom that was fairly large, painted pink. It was horrible! I had it lightened to a higher value. No better! Then I had it tinted orchid. It was worse! Thep I had it painted peach — which turned out salmon! Had the room been many degrees lighter, it would have been all right; but by this time its color had got on my nerves, so I gave it up, had it all repainted French gray — and hated it forever.

The rule is obvious: Choose a tint that you think much too light — no matter what color it is — and it will probably be what you have in mind. It is easy to glaze it or stipple it darker, but it takes much more paint ( and time) to lighten a wall that is too dark than to darken a light one.

Green that has yellow in it should have a sample chosen for it that looks almost colorless, because, in spite of the fact that green is supposed to retreat, it can behave exactly like a candle-snuffer in its effect of gloom. The feat of applying deep green paint and producing an effect of emerald that is brilliant and luminous, both by night and by day, is possible, because it has been done; but the art of producing deep green without an effect of dull solidity, and of making it equally light-giving at night, is not within the knowledge of many.

One secret, perhaps, of luminosity of such a wall is a polished texture. Moreover, the effect of depth in painting a wall is obtained by means of glaze. A transparent color is painted on top of another, or a final solid color is stippled — or brushed partially off so that the under color shows through. But what the tint of the under, and what the hue of the upper, and how many of them, is not easy to know. Usually a green room — unless of a very light tint — ^snuffs out every light put into it. There is a certain room about twenty feet wide by thirty feet long; its walls are covered with green Italian brocade; there are ten lamps and four

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

groups of side-lights, and the room is merely glimmering with patches of light in a surrounding of darkness.

A FEW WORDS OF ADVICE ON WALL PAPERS

If you are going to use any papers, especially any that have much pattern, hang up a number of strips of each, so as to be sure that the general effect will not be splotchy. Many papers that are entrancing in small pieces are unendurable when the pattern repeats so distinctly that you can’t help counting the rows.

Another detail to watch is a surprising appearance of prominent color that you had not even noticed. The reason for choosing a corner of a room to hang your test lengths in, is that here you can see if the paper’s background — unless white — is likely to be too strong or too dark.

Although papering will give you the most "furnishing” wall-covering at least expense, be very careful about buying too cheap a quality of paper. The cost of hanging is greater than the paper — usually. And too-cheap papers are pretty sure to fade. Good quality non-fadable papers last for years, even at the seashore; whereas a lower grade paper will turn into a rather sickly symphony in tans in one season. There are any number of papers at about a dollar and three-quarters or perhaps two dollars a roll that are guaranteed sun-fast. If you happen to live in a color-trying climate, you will know by experience that it is really a waste of money to buy either papers or materials that are not guaranteed to keep their color.

SCENIC PAPERS

These are very expensive, and they do not fit in every house ; but anything more beautiful in, or more suitable to, a Colonial house than a scenic paper cannot be imagined— when properly chosen and properly placed. To those who have always lived with them, nothing takes their place ; moreover, they last more than a lifetime, and are easily moved

from house to house. Therefore, if you buy a scenic paper — especially an old one that is priceless, it is wise to prepare for moving it by having the wall pasted over with muslin, and to hang the paper on that. Then, if you ever want to take it down, cut through the muslin as well as the paper in the corners of the room and also at convenient intervals with a sharp knife and pull it down. Have the paper-hanger lay it face downward on his table and wet the muslin. It will peel off easily. The old printing of scenic America shown in Plate 39 has been moved three times.

As a scenic paper is a panoramic picture, nothing must ever be hung upon it. You should no more hang a clock or side-lights on a scenic wall than you should hang them on a tapestry or a painting. Even objects standing against it should not interfere or be out of scale with its scenes. In halls or dining-rooms or drawing-rooms that have no other decorations they are especially happy hung above a very low chairrail with paneling or painted plaster below. They look best usually with cream or oyster- white trim, and with hangings of taffeta in one of the softer colors of the scenes. They look very badly with fancy or overcolored woodwork and with confusing objects in front of them.

If a tall piece of furniture, such as a secretary, is to be put in a room with a scenic paper, the design of the paper should be especially worked out by standing the secretary where it is to go, and then letting a break of scene come behind it and having the pictures proceed from that point. A certain amount of adjustment must be arranged for, also, so that the principal scenes are centered on the principal wall space. (For other wall-paper suggestions see Index.)

IN THIS DAY OF COLOR RIOT

There is less need to-day for advising against what used to be known as American "mud-and-mustard” dinginess than for advising against color insanity. Whether the pictures illustrating innumerable advertised commodities are taken by

THE EMOTIONAL BEHAVIOR OF COLOR

every one as "posters” or whether they are accepted by those of shock-proof sensibilities as "patterns” only the power to see into millions of American homes could reveal. But let us at least hope and pray that no man of sensitive taste tired after a harassing day of turmoil and coming home to find comfort and repose — is obliged to spend his hours of relaxation amidst jangling discords of color suitable only to a madhouse.

Imagine the restful comfort to be found within walls of green-yellow, splotched with a crimson rash, amidst furniture covered and painted in lightning-struck scarlet or orchid and orange, with cushions of blue and olive and magenta, all resting on a carpet of black with disks of turquoise, lemonpink and garnet! Imagine trying to find repose in a bed that is grass-green with ornamentation in violet and yellow, in a bedroom with pink walls, turquoise-blue curtains, and scarlet slip-covers! Fancy, a floor of shining red, purple, and jadegreen tiles, a mantel of green and yellow tiles, wall decoration of blue and purple and orange, with emerald-green leather furniture studded with brass nail-heads and resting on a pink and purple rug! Imagine — but it is not necessary to imagine, merely look into a room set forth in the color-pages of some magazine advertising sections and seriously consider what *'home” would be like were it one of these!!

By this I do not mean that all advertisements in color are bad, by any means. In fact, some are lovely. There was a bathroom-dressing-room, for instance, that appeared in ha a dozen magazines and that was entrancing with jade porcelain and watermelon toile de jouey and half-closed shutters that you know must open out on a garden in Paris. But there are others that go to unimagined depths of vulgarity, and absurdity quite as great as that of the cannibals who fantastically bedeck themselves in the clothes of the missionary that they have eaten for supper.

XII

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS IN MAKING PORTRAIT

INTERIORS

essential object of this chapter is to indicate some of the practical uses to which the color charts described in Chapter X may be put. For the purpose of illustrating the principles of color harmony as applied to actual furnishings, it might be amusing to select a few individual personalities and attempt what may be called portraiture in house decoration.

To begin with, therefore, let us imagine a number of people who are as different in type as they can possibly be, and then express the personality of each by means of a triad, a related family, a monochrome with complementary accent, and possibly attempt a discordant cousin theme. In other words, let us suppose that we are momentarily a firm of decorators, and that we are commissioned to decorate:

1. A town drawing-room for a social leader of utmost formality.

2. A country lounge-about room for outdoors-loving moderns.

3. A boudoir for an exotic beauty.

4. A country living-room for the stay-at-home mother of many children.

5. A dressing-room bathroom of unlimited extravagance

and display of color.

THE TOWN DRAWING-ROOM

Each of these is to be as expressive of an individual personahty as possible. Beginning, therefore, with the town

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

drawing-room, we must first of all visualize the personality of its owner. To express the restraint of her innate formality, let us neither overload her room with ornamentation nor overdo it with rich color. Let us also banish the consideration of children, or at least admit them only for a little while when they are on their best behavior — at tea or at Sunday lunch.

We should select a Period — French, most likely, or possibly Eighteenth-Century English, or its American derivative, Colonial Georgian. Blue would surely be her color. We therefore choose the primary triad of blue, red, and yellow, but must modify it to the utmost. It will be safe to panel her walls and paint them pale silver-gray, since her favorite blue is certain to be the blue of the sky mixed with the gray of silvered clouds, this combination being the coldest, most formal monotone possible. Therefore, it is to be hoped that this drawing-room has a little of the western sun to cheer it at tea-time, especially as we must add gray furnit-i-e— or possibly cream — in gracefully restrained French curves or the satinwood fragilities of Hepplewhite. (Satinwood would supply our needed yellow.) To the blue and silver brocade of hangings and furniture — which, to avoid any chance of disagreement, we make of the same material — we will add two chairs of blue velvet which we may quite probably have to go to France to find! There should be an Aubusson tapestry carpet in gray-blue slightly oranged, yellow, and rose. A few odd chairs, settees or stools should be covered with needlepoint in rose and blue on a beige background. (Both rug and needle-point illustrate the triad.)

There would be, of course, one or two glass-shelved cabinets displaying French or Chinese porcelains, miniatures, and snuff-boxes against a background of opened fans. Flowers always play a part in such a room, though their profusion is seldom lavish: Ophelia roses in a bowl, or delphiniums in tall vases, with other flowers in strength- avoiding shades.

The drawing-room of such a person is invariably twice and often three times curtained: glass curtains next to the

184 THE PERSONALITY OF A HOUSE

panes; heavy outer curtains of the French blue and silver brocade; flesh or deep cream-color taffeta curtains between the two. There are lambrequins, either of brocade with silver galloon, or of carved wood. A small open fireplace lightens the hearth, giving a charming but restrained note of welcome that exactly matches the quality of the lady’s smile. On the walls there should be a portrait or two suggestive of the French or English school; also several mirrors in gilt frames, rather small. The room may suitably have a French clock of Apollo driving his chariot, a fine needle-point fire-screen, gilt appliques with musical instrument motifs, and a small writing-table, prettily but not too adequately equipped.

If we are doing the whole house, we know that this owner will choose for her bedroom another tone of blue — ^with peach, perhaps, and a note of egg-plant purple. If there be a dark-paneled library, she will choose a monochrome of greens rather than one of reds to complement the wood.

Her dining-room might be in cream, orchid, and grayblue-violet — a single color family with complementary accent. Whatever its colors, however, each room is necessarily feminine but restrained, luxurious but precise — and utterly impossible to imagine with anything out of order or even moved out of place.

A COUNTRY LOUNGE-ABOUT ROOM FOR OUTDOORS-LOVING MODERNS

Such a room would, of course, be utterly unlike the fragile gray blueness of the drawing-room sketched above. The owners would probably like their house to be in the modern manner — a manner, let us add quickly, that shall go no further than a certain up-to-dateness and that shall express neither clumsy crudeness nor decadent distortion, nor yet drab monotone in brown and steel. It shall be very gay and colorful and alive and youthful. (For monotone decoration and other expressions of the strictly Modern interior see Chapter XXII.)

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

If the owners are sunburned, active, outdoors-loving young people, probably the very best background that we could choose is one that, as much as possible, brings outdoors indoors. Selecting the sand of a beach as our background, we might make the walls a combination of rough and smooth plaster in a color of dry sand or cream. The rougher plaster, which is sanded in surface, contrasts but slightly with the smooth, which is to be used for bas-relief portrait panels, each depicting the owners of the house engaged in a different sport — polo, yachting, diving, hunting, tennis — and one of them a scene at the bridge-table in this identical room.

We must, of course, give them two studio windows with large square panes — windows to look out of, and to bring the unshaded sun in. Moreover, let there be also an enormous glass door with openings hinged to fold entirely back like screens across one wall, so that in good weather the room can be turned into a loggia. The frames of both windows are faced — at right angles to the sashes — with ground glass, behind which are receptacles holding bands of electric lights to be turned on at night. If these do not produce an effect of the blaze of midday, other bands of lights might also’ be set behind the cornice.

With this strictly neutral background of sand and colorless decoration, almost pure primary or binary colors could be used. The floor could be of orange or emerald tiles — let us not mix them! — subdued by rugs of sand and black. Honeycolored wood furniture may be upholstered with materials combining red-orange and green, with an accent of grayed violet. In such a room there should be no useless ornaments, but there could be brilliant flowers in one or two copper bowls or pots, and perhaps an orange-red lacquer writingtable of Modern design. No curtains are on the windows or doors in the daytime, but curtains of thin semi-transparent pale orange silk, drawn close at night, diffuse the brilliant lighting of the window-frames behind them, producing the impression of bright daylight. Or these curtains could be decorated in transparent color. The fireplace might be of

U6

black glass, and there might also be one or two occasional tables of glass and steel, holding ash-trays and cigaret-boxes.

Or let us suppose that we are wrong, and that our young house-owners are less glare-loving, less heat-enduring. Let us consider that they might prefer inviting coolness as a respite from torrid outdoor heat and glare. In that case we could completely change this same room’s personality by changing the color to that of the blue-green family. The walls then may well be grayish green, and the floor of black and white marble or oyster-gray stone. The furniture might be changed to gray or jade or turquoise, and the entire color scheme to one of black, gray-white, greens and blues, with curtains of oyster-white and occasional lights in the window-frames that can be turned on separately — of blue.

There is a chintz that for an effect of delicious mid-ocean coolness is unsurpassed. Its design is of sea-gulls in gray and white against a background of ocean waves in almost binary green and primary blue.

As already explained, when keeping a color-scheme within the boundaries of a small color-family such as that of blue married to green and all their blue-green children, their family hues can be used in full strength — especially when there are wide neutral spaces.

If windows are too sunny, Venetian blinds are coolest, but green-lined awnings give an illusion of space. If the room is definitely a loggia, nothing is more decorative than a fountain against a wall, with ivy trained as a background and pots of growing plants around the base.

In choosing textures for either suggested decoration of this room, it should be remembered that they must not be too fine, since such a room should not lose its suitability as a background for sports clothes.

A BOUDOIR FOR AN EXOTIC BEAUTY

Our third personality design, a boudoir for an exotic beauty, is also necessarily modernistic, but in a quite different

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

key. (A boudoir, by the way, is not a bedroom, a bathroom, or a dressing-room, but a small private sitting-room on a bedroom floor.)

This should be the sort of room that scarcely one person in a thousand could live in, and yet it must be the ideal setting for the person its coloring represents. Let us suppose, then, that the woman who is to live in it is fragilely thin, white-skinned, with red-gold copper-colored hair as flat as copper luster and as polished. Let us suppose her languid, exquisite, and utterly sophisticated. And this room must be so like her that any one, having seen her, would at once know it was hers. So we will face the walls with highly polished black glass, with mirror panels engraved in designs of converging streets. The ceiling is, of course, of silver. Side-lights are of steel and crystal. The "studio” window has glass curtains of orchid chiffon and heavy curtains of magenta satin. The carpet would be black, and the mantel of green-veined black marble. Over it might be a portrait of the owner in black against a curtain of emerald green and framed in silver. A broad divan covered with jade-green velvet should be set in a recess of engraved mirrors. A dozen very large square-boxedged plain satin cushions in flesh, orchid, magenta, and deep black purple should be packed too tight to slip against the mirror-back and sides — but not propped about on the floor! The lamps are of green quartz or of crystal with creamcolored shades; the ornaments all of jade, crystal, amethyst, and silver — or stainless steel. The exotic beauty s own hair actually, and in the portrait— would supply the missing accent of yellow-orange that completes the triad of green and orchid and magenta. Or this entire room might be removed from this chapter on color, and translated into black, white, gray and steel.

A COUNTRY LIVING-ROOM FOR THE STAY-AT-HOME MOTHER

Now let US define an entirely different type of room, since we can scarcely imagine black and emerald and magenta as

a background for a country living-room for the stay-at-home mother of many children. The woman for whom we are doing this room is one whose hands and thoughts are chiefly occupied with her husband and children, who are to occupy the room as much as she. Their house is big and rambling • and Elizabethan in type. The sun streams into the livingroom through diamond-paned casements. Everything in it must be chosen with two prime qualities in view — cheerful homelikeness and unspoilability. The most unspoilable furniture possible to devise will be of oak, in Tudor design, with just enough carving to avoid plain surfaces that could be marred by whacks of toys and kicks of shoes.

If the sitting-room is to be extravagantly done, suitable walls will be of linen-fold paneling, with book-cases abounding in beautiful levant and morocco bindings, and with needle-point furniture coverings, palatial but unspoilable.

On the other hand, if the purse is not one- quarter deep enough for such a lavish scheme, the same room at reasonable cost could be achieved with walls covered with plain cypress boarding, filled and waxed. And there are numerous sprigged tapestry materials that are good in color and show no marks of fingers — not even spots. Velveteen wears well, as every one knows. And slip-covers of endless varieties of material, plain, figured or flowered, are utterly beautifying and practical, because they can be taken off and cleaned or washed.

In a room that is in a monotone of browns, color is beautifully supplied by colored cushions, ornaments and colorful book-backs.

This reminds me of a story that is true: The wife of an eminent author who had enlarged their library living-room was annoyed at the distressing emptiness of so many of the new book-shelves. So she went to a store that had advertised cheap editions and asked for "lots of books.” Said the clerk: "Which books may I get you?” To the horror of the clerk, she answered, "Red ones!” She giggled afterwards at the clerk’s obvious estimate of her literary taste.

In a room such as this — the family living-room — a

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

serviceable floor-covering is very essential. Of course, we all know that for wear and unspoilability an Eastern rug, whether Persian, Indian, or Chinese, is best. But it is beautiful only if one can afford to buy one that is in soft shades. (Few can.) On the other hand, figured rugs and carpets — in sharply defined colors — rise off the floor and hit you in the face! Plain carpet in one of the tones of dust-brown is always good, especially in a winter house. For summer use or for a simple cottage an excellent floor-covering for almost any type of room is Scotch wool carpeting, and it comes in a great variety of colors. One kind, made of camel’s hair, is especially beautiful and durable. (For further discussion of Floors see Index.)

A DRESSING-ROOM BATHROOM OF UNLIMITED EXTRAVAGANCE

For our last "portrait” interior, a dressing-room bathroom of unlimited extravagance and display of colors, let us see how jazzingly colorful we can be; but at the same time let us see if we can make our theme resplendently pleasing.

On the theory that a combination of all colors produces neutrality, let us combine all the discords and present them with utmost brilliancy by covering the entire wall surface with mirrors gorgeously decorated, not with color applied to the surface, but with designs painted on the back of the glass before it is silvered. A process of long ago it is, but now revived with new and vivid effects by many modern artists; so let us ask the most modern of artists to paint for us a series of Chinese scenes in yellow-green and blue-green, olive and peacock-blue, blue-violet, red-violet, red-orange and magenta.

Let us have every violent contrast possible m tne plumage of birds and the flowering vines, and in the robes of the Chinese figures. Let the shadows and tree-trunks be^ o purple and brown and black. Let us have a vaulted ceiling of brilliant chromium plate— like the silver-glass balls on

Christmas trees — and many hundred candle-power lights reflected upon it. Let us have a floor of amethyst glass, and a sunken tub big enough to swim in, lined with — if we are multi-millionaires — rose quartz; or if merely millionaires, with rose-colored glass. Let us have a dressing-table of emerald glass, chairs of lapis-blue glass upholstered in shagreen, and toilet articles of green quartz and chromium steel.

Or, instead of our Christmas-tree fantasy, we might have a ceiling of copper, a vermilion tub, and upholstery of leopard, tiger cub, or piebald pony skins — left natural, or cut and sewn in modernistic bands and rectangles or other geometrical figures.

At all events, let it not be said that this book is mid- Victorian!

As a matter of fact, this last interior, although intentionally extreme, is no exaggeration of rooms that are actually being done. Julian Howay — who has made an entrancing speciality of painting under glass — has created a number of marvelous designs of just this type. To the not fabulously rich, such rooms are out of reach, since the cost of one runs into many thousands — and, unless amazingly well done, they are amazingly bad.

The expert can always dare — and successfully achieve — what the inexpert may not attempt; but on the whole we shall be safer — and far more likely to attain to something of beauty — if we stand by such rules of convention as that "the smaller an object, the more vivid it can be.” Large surfaces must be neutral — or at least so counterbalanced as to seem neutral — unless you want your room to look like a foursided bill poster! For certain occasions perhaps you do. The present fashion for an amusement-room, a coffee-room, or a cocktail-room, might purposely and suitably be as vivid an interpretation of jazz as you care to make it. But a room that you spend much time in, day in and day out, should be suitable to other moods than those of gaiety and light-heartedness which prevail when you are surrounded by a horde of friends.

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

COLOR BEAUTIFICATION OF THE UGLIEST HOUSE FOR THE

LEAST MONEY

Having finished these imaginary interiors in illustration of the method of assembling colors by means of color charts, it might be well to describe in detail my own methods in the selection of color in a few actual instances that were either especially easy or especially hard. And since the five "imaginary” themes were very expensive, I will begin my actual experience list with the ugliest house that I ever beautified for the least amount of money.

About ten years ago we rented a seashore cottage on a three-seasons’ lease without ever having been inside of it. W e knew its location, and I studied its floor-plan. Its rent should have impressed us as suspiciously low, but the agent wrote us an even more than normally eulogistic agent’s letter, so we felt that it couldn’t be very bad. But on the inevitably cold, raw, rainy day that we took possession, the why of the rent was quite self-evident. I had never imagined that any series of walled enclosures could be so hideously hopeless! The color scheme of the whole house was olive-green, purplebrown, mustard-yellow and maroon, and in the midst of this coloring loomed the bottle-shaped chimney breast and fireplace of the living-room, a thing of white-mortared orange brick. The trim was varnished golden oak and the furniture a mixture of maple, oak, and purplish imitation cherry. Walls and furniture coverings writhed and twisted in mustard red, maroon, and a variety of discordant greens, like the serpents of Medusa.

The immediate question was, what could I do on a reasonable outlay? And next, what would the landlord let me do?

I went out in what was now a deluge to find the landlord. He cared very little what I did to the walls, but he prized the golden oak trim and the orange-brick fireplace. So, taking these two "fixtures” as the foundation of the problem, I went to work. I took out every piece of red-purple pseudomahogany and ptit in all the articles of maple and oak.

Happily the rug in the dining-room was a greenish-brownish nondescript not especially noticeable. But the violent one in the* living-room had to be rolled up and banished to the attic. (This was distressing, as floor covering is always high.) However, I had the wall papered with a very pale coral-brickcolored cartridge paper, much lighter than before, but in tones of the brick of the fireplace; and I covered the floor of the living-room with a plain brownish-sand-colored klearflax rug. In the three principal bedrooms upstairs I put gaycolored papers, and, the trim being not valuable, had it painted to match the papers. Then I went to Boston, where I found a very effective chintz with a tree-branch design that had clumps of brickish red and coral berries and plenty of foliage. I covered the furniture with slip-covers which I made myself. The windows were too tall for their width, so I took some strips of wood from a packing-box and nailed them end to end across the top of the window trim, like a piecedtogether shelf. To this lambrequin foundation I tacked, first, deep pleatings of cheese-cloth, disguising the narrowness of the window frames, and then tacked the chintz a foot beyond the cheese-cloth. A long hemmed strip of chintz, about ten inches deep, tacked flat around the cross-board as lambrequins (Fig. 154), shortened the windows and hid the lack of rods.

I bought a big second-hand drop-leaf kitchen table, and put a tape-bound sand-colored denim cover over it. On this was a coral-colored sheet of blotting paper, with my writing materials, books and magazines, and a bowl of flowers. Scruboak branches in preserve jars decorated the barest spots in the room. As the last touch I stood geranium pots on the windowsills, and the room was quite enchanting.

The dining-room I papered in light sand that toned with the oak, and to this I added plain coral hangings made of unbleached muslin dyed by my laundress. (No colors dye so easily as pinkish or brickish reds. But, whatever you do, donH attempt any of the purples.)

The pseudo-mahogany was put upstairs in a grayish green

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

room with a little twenty-five-cent diaper-print cotton in orange, red, violet, and blue-green. Of course, so many things went into the attic that the house was fairly bare. But it was amazing how little was spent in comparison with the effect produced. And as we had the house on a three-year lease, the addition to each year’s rent was less than fifty dollars — and the landlord offered me $500 to cancel my three-year lease!!

As nearly as I can remember, all the papering and painting of the rooms upstairs came to about sixty dollars. The livingroorn chintz was a re-block at 50 cents a yard thirty-five yards — and cost seventeen dollars and a half. Cotton print to neutralize the pseudo-mahogany in the room I took for myself was twenty-five cents — ten yards, two dollars and fifty cents. The other two rooms, as well as those I made no attempt to do over, had ruffled curtains at a dollar a pair.

I made the shades for five lamps of light-colored manila paper, with cut-out applications of chintz and pictures cut from frontispieces of magazines fitted over three white glass globes bought in the village and two wire frames found in the attic. (I shellacked them, of course, to keep them stiff and smoothly round.)

This house was done solely for effect, but with the exception of the lamp-shades, which were rather makeshift, and the fact that the paper in the bedrooms faded out in doing it over again I should buy sun-proof papers the embellishment was satisfactorily permanent.

THE HARDEST PROBLEM IN COLOR ARRANGEMENT

The hardest problem of this sort that I ever attempted to solve was the drawing-room of a certain New York apartment. The architect— who had the most perfect color sense of any one I ever knew— had completed the interior architecture, including the color of the walls, before I arrived on the scene, and on no account would I have asked him to alter whatever it might be. But I must say I stood aghast in the middle of that empty room, lovely in its size and paneled

detail, but with the sun blazing in each of its five windows on highly polished pinkish-pumpkin- copperish walls!! How to quiet it? As a color it was as beautiful as it was amazing, but as a room — ! How to put anything with it, was no small problem. Of course, I turned to the triad at once, but it was of no use; neither were any other formulas that I have ever heard of. It resolved itself into a problem of the eye.

Each day I tried samples of color in every sort of material; the room destroyed each, and each destroyed the room. Even the architect was helpless. In fact, he had decided to treat the walls with buttermilk to fade them, when quite by chance I hung up a length of flame-vermilion tafieta, a color so much brighter than the walls that they went back quietly where they belonged. So curtains of taflfeta were the beginning. After that I found a red-orange brocade for a small sofa, and an old Sung roof-horse in orange and emerald solved the rest of it. I grayed the emerald for upholstery and found that the room accepted old Sheraton furniture, English portraits, Lowestoft and Chinese porcelain, as well as beautiful book-bindings. And in the end the color of the walls was a great success. But I can’t explain the colors that proved harmonious according to any chart. If it were called a redorange-yellow family, that would do; but then blue should be the complement, not green. Or if it was a triad, it should have included violet, whereas a pitching ship to a bad sailor would be calm by comparison with the effect that violet or blue or bright yellow — and, of course, crimson — ^had upon it.

A bedroom in this same apartment was also not quite a triad, but it was very easy to do. According to the charts, it should belong to the red-yellow family, with the complements made of green-blue instead of blue. But the complement, in this case, is the principal color instead of the accent, since the entire wall is green-blue. The next color in importance is lemon-yellow, which is the color of the taffeta of the bed and window curtains. The chintz is bluish-green — which is where the chart goes distinctly sideways — with a bird, treebranch, and flower design of orange flame, light orange.

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

cream, browns, grays, and an accent of blue. The furniture is apple wood.

In attempting to explain the tendency of one triad corner to move a degree nearer to the other two, I can only say that I have noted it many times — though I have never heard its chart explanation, and therefore hesitate to offer it as a rule. But it would seem safe to say that a closely adjoining color within the family circle of one point of a triad may be substituted or included. This may explain the becomingness of orange-red with orange — though it does not explain the hideosity of violet and blue in the drawing-room problem described above

THE SPLIT COMPLEMENT - A TRIAD THAT WAS OMITTED

There is also a certain triangle of color that was purposely omitted from the charts, because, when this triangle is harmonious, its short side indicates two parents in one family, and its two long sides point to their split complements, opposite. For instance, green and yellow, with red-violet. Or violet and red with yellow-green. Or violet and blue with yellow-orange. \f^hen it indicates a triangle of discord, its points indicate the colors on either side of a family barrier, with the split complement opposite them.

In these triads you usually make the two related colors dominate the complement, but you can equally well place them the other way round — which is the way I used greenblue in the bedroom just described. In other words, the room is not red-orange and yellow with an accent of blue-green; it is a blue-green room in which there is much pale yellow, and an accent of orange and orange-red.

Of course, we can never know whether the vibrations which manifest themselves as color are to any two of us the same. Is that which A finds beautiful and B finds ugly presented in identical vibrations to the mind of each? Or is B s vibration-receiving station slower than A s, and the message received quite different? Who knows?

COLOR AS A BACKGROUND OF PERSONAL BECOMINGNESS

Another aspect of color which should be considered by every house-owner who has an atom of personal vanity — or by those who love her, even if she herself has no such pride — is the choosing of color as a background of personal becomingness.

Although the concern of this book is with the beauty of a house and not with the beauty of its owner, it would be incomplete without suggesting that a woman should choose surroundings that are becoming, rather than thoughtlessly spend her life amidst colors that possibly detract quite actually from her looks. In order, therefore, to consider background colors from the point of view of personal becomingness, let us divide women into three types — blondes, brunettes, and mediums — and divide these three types again into subdivisions. There are, for instance, four types of blondes — the noonday blonde, the moon blonde, the drab blonde, and the red-haired blonde.

BACKGROUNDS FOR BLONDES

The noonday blonde is she who has white skin, golden hair, brilliant cream-and-coral coloring; she is the "woman beautiful as established by the Greeks when they set up their temples to golden-blonde Aphrodite. One’s first thought of her is that nothing can be either taken from or added to her glorious radiance. And yet one danger threatens her: the vulgarity of too much gilding. Just as she must be careful to stay slim or she will become too full-blown, she must choose surroundings of distinction and simplicity or she will make them as well as herself look tawdry.

Therefore, she must assiduously avoid robin ’s-egg blue; true, this is usually her favorite color, but it exaggerates rather than becomes her. Grays, taupes, gray-blues, very gray yellow-greens, and certain rather pallid yellows are all becoming. But, above all, she must be surrounded by fineness

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

of detail. You see, she is herself so colorful that one degree beyond restraint — unless she is thin to attenuation, as we imagined our exotic beauty to be when we designed her boudoir — puts her over the edge where the peony becomes a cabbage. A room such as our first portrait interior does not express a noonday blonde at all, and yet the conventionality of such a background would give quality to her beauty, and her beauty would illumine such a room.

The perfect example of a moon blonde is one whose coloring is porcelain fine, but so pallid that she should be surrounded by every color that enhances her own robin segg blue at high light, apple-green, white, black and steel with emerald and lemon. But she must avoid strong values, which would wash her out. Feminine rooms suit her ^French ones and certain very modified modern ones that are carried out in pale blues or greens or mauves and silver. Colonial, French, or not too imposing Georgian houses suit her best; or very little cottages furnished fragilely. In fact, so feminine should her own surroundings be that her husband should have a plain wood-lined room to counterbalance the rooms that are too obviously hers. But let him not have heavy Tudor or Jacobean furniture if she is to be admitted— ever.

The drab blonde — seemingly qualified by a very unflattering adjective — is really one of the most essentially thoroughbred as well as the most natural of women. She fits into the sort of house that a man likes with the suitability of a boy. She fits into the most elaborate house with the suitability of race; and, being devoid of affectation, she general y contrives a background more or less in monotone, but vfith homelike qualities that attract. A pine room with gay but small-patterned chintz, a room with red in it, or orange or yellow, is far more becoming to her than green or mauve or blue. At night a deep cream-colored wall with curtains of yellow — or better still, a background of dark wood and

crimson brocade. , i • j •

In this day of jazzed color — no less than jazzed music

the young woman whose hair is red has come into her own.

since few other colorings are accentuated enough in themselves to be put in the center of decorative interest. Belonging to the red-orange-copper family, her discord is red, violet, mulberry, or bluish crimson.

BACKGROUND FOR BRUNETTES

Brunettes are divided into two classes: those with white skins and blue eyes, those with dark skins and brown eyes. Let us say that there are no colorless brunettes, since lip-stick is used freely by all and rouge by those whom it embellishes. To the dark-haired, fair-skinned, blue-eyed Irish type, all colors are equally becoming. But in designing the living-room of her house, let its color include blue, and let there be a highbacked chair or a cushion of blue — slightly deeper than the shade of her eyes — against which she leans her head.

To the Irish brunette, who in her youth has skin like nothing so much as snow and watermelon-ice, with eyes that are blue, blue-gray or blue-violet, almost all colors are suitable; there is no color that does not suit her, in fact, except those that are thickly mixed. She should keep the water-ice transparency in mind. Orchid is more becoming to her than salmon, apple-green than olive; she is loveliest of all, perhaps, in a white room with blue hangings, in the presence of brilliant flowers with outstanding masses of watermelon-pink. She is one of the few who are beautiful against a wall or curtain of apple-green. It will turn her eyes from blue to bluegreen, but be otherwise becoming. She can stand magenta, too. In fact, she can triumph over colors that despoil nearly every other type.

The true brunette looks at her very best in the entire range of reds and yellows — plainly the reason for the constant use of these colors in Spain. A woman with black hair and dark skin should never be seen far from some shade of yellowred or red-yellow. Let her on no account do a room in blue, blue-green or violet, and let her avoid the smart modern room of black and gray. Very yellow green to which are

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

added the tones of russet red would be becoming — very. Instead of attempting triads, she must follow the scale of yellow and avoid tones of color in which yellow is absent.

EMBELLISHING BACKGROUNDS FOR WOMEN OF FADING

BEAUTY

For those who are young and beautiful, selecting a becoming background is — except within certain marked types, as noted — not important. But when one grows older, choice of becomingness is essential. A woman whose skin is inclined to be at all red should avoid green as she would the plague. The best color for a woman whose face is florid is red, since red is inclined to make lighter red pallid.

A proverb has it that "yellow next to yellow makes yellow look white.” The right yellow, yes. The wrong yellow, decidedly no. A sallow skin surrounded by tan is as bad as the mud-and-mustard formula. But sallow coloring next to pinkish orange, or to the red that has a strong accent of terra cotta, looks its very best.

The problems of the roan-haired period, meaning that period of middle-aged beauty-doldrums through which all light and medium shades of hair must fade, are best answered by backgrounds in pastel shades that have a slight amount of yellow in their mixture. The ugliness of roan hair is that it is not steel-gray but mousy-yellow. Put next to steel or to pure white it looks its worst; therefore the present use of steel in modern furnishing should by the roan-haired be avoided.

All the colors shown in the spokes of the color chart are becoming to women whose skins are fading. Other softening backgrounds that are becoming to nearly all women whose coloring is not as young as it used to be, are old ivory or yellowed cream paneling, outlined with rubbed-off gold; gray paneling that is yellower than the tone of her hair; gray-violet, French blue, periwinkle blue, ashes of roses, brownish raspberry, and old Italian red.

Old Italian red, noted as the most becoming background

THE PERSONALITY OE A HOUSE

possible in an opera house, is likewise the most becoming background possible where an effect of dignity and brilliance i is the object. Women’s clothes, no less than their complexions, are beautiful against it; even men’s tail-coats acquire distinction. It is necessarily a color that exacts evening dress and, if possible, much candle-light.

In choosing her own most becoming background, the woman of taste is merely a stage director who skilfully presents — ^herself. The object of these pages is not so much to tell you what color of room is becoming to you, as merely to attract your attention to a point that may very easily be overlooked. Other things being equal, why not look your best at home, where you are seen every day by those you love most, rather than think of your appearance solely when you choose a street or party dress?

THE EFFECT OF SELF-EXPRESSING HUES ON TEMPERAMENT

Let US say that you are a somewhat restless, overenthusiastic, excitement-loving person, and that the color you like best — as well as the one that expresses you — is orange-red. The question we now confront is whether you should be surrounded by the perpetual stimulant of red, or should be grayed or quieted by the influence of blue or neutral tones instead. In other words, being yourself overcolorful by temperament, could you increase your own tendencies to the point where nervous breakdown threatens? Or could you find restfulness in other surroundings?

On the other hand, if you are very reserved — purposely, perhaps, or possibly inarticulate by nature — would gray or gray-blue-violet increase your innate aloofness? Would you be more relaxed in a room of yellow or rose? This is included because the answer is part of the theory of color, and at least you can think about it! My own opinion is quite beside the point, but such as it is I include it also. Personally I believe in matching, rather than complementing, your background; that is, if you happen to care really very much about color

HOW TO USE COLOR CHARTS

in the first place. If you do not, then it is a good idea to use complementary colors for your background. But, if you are depressed or annoyed by the color that should correct your own tendencies, you would certainly be lacking in ordinary common sense to be governed by the theories of other people in the matter.

SELECTING COLOR ACCORDING TO SITUATION

According to the typical interior decorator, yellow, which is the substitute for sunlight, should always be chosen for north rooms. Reds and oranges are warming, and therefore should be chosen for cold climates and for half-northlight rooms. Cold blues, violets, grays, and blue-greens are for the hot climates and rooms facing south.

These conventional rules are good to know, but good also to break, if you prefer, since choice of color for situation depends entirely upon whether you like an effect of light that

is evened or prefer a contrast.

Personally, I like a bright room in the sun, a cool one in the shade. If I feel a need of the sun, then I like lots of it. Yellows, reds, and creams are a hundred times more beautiful in the sun, of which they are symbols, than they ever can be in the shade. And what is so restful, when the heat and glare of the sun become a nether-world torture, as a cool grayblue room facing the cool gray-blue north? These sentences express my opinion. But my opinion is of no concern whatever in any detail of your house.

The color of my house is suitable if it delights and expresses me; your house, to be perfect, must delight and express not your decorator, hut you. If its color is becoming as a background to your looks, if it is suitable to your use, if you find it restful when you are tired, if you find it soothing when you are ill, if it satisfies your need of beauty and that of your family, then you have achieved a personal scheme of harmony that supremely fulfils its every requirement.

XIII

THE PERIOD STYLES

X^IKE the forms of classic architecture and the theory M of color harmony, the Period Styles, although threadbare to students, may perhaps be of interest ^ to those who do not happen to know their history. It may be well, therefore, to explain the very simple story of how and why the styles of this or that period came into being, and why we are still making use of some of them.

Their origin goes back to ancient days, when countries and even localities were segregated from each other by distances impracticable for travel, let alone for the hauling of building material. Each locality was dependent, therefore, upon its own resources. The wood or stone or clay which happened to be near at hand was naturally chosen for the construction of dwellings, and practise in the handling of this material or that, gradually suggested the most practical method for its use. Plans and details were furthermore developed as civilization progressed. Wattled huts were replaced by houses of logs hewn and lashed or mitred together. Clay that had been daubed on to fill the openings between branches was made into mortar to hold stones together, or shaped into bricks and sunbaked; or rock was split and squared into blocks that could be laid securely one above the other.

Gradually dwellings, influenced by climate and personal taste, as well as by the needs of the people of this or that locality, followed certain definite forms or styles. Communities that needed protection from the cold or from hostile neighbors, or from dangerous animals, built closed-in shelters or strongholds. Other communities that dwelt in mild climates amidst friendly surroundings and great beauty of nature were encouraged in the creation of beauty them-

THE PERIOD STYLES

selves, and that was the beginning of Art, which is, after all, the creation of beauty by man. Naturally, the taste and dexterity of one community exceeded that of another, and visiting neighbors, noting the beauty that had been achieved, borrowed such ideas as were suitable to themselves; and others, in turn, borrowed from them. Little by little, those communities which developed the highest excellence began to be looked up to and to be talked about, and travelers came from afar to admire their achievements and study their methods. It was in this way exactly that the civilized world went on artistic pilgrimages to Athens, to Rome, to Florence, to France, to China or otherwhere.

ART THAT STANDS THE TEST OF TIME

Why do the creations of one people endure, while those of another are discarded? Because they stand the test of time, which inevitably chooses that the fittest shall survive. In every ?gc, new ideas are expressed in new forms, designs, textures and colors, and are temporarily accepted as useful or beautiful; but when these are subjected to the immutable test of time, most of them fall by the wayside and only the best and truest survive. And that which endures becomes a standard of beauty; that which fails to endure is usually classified as having been of little worth — unless it is from time to time revived.

It is perhaps not safe to make a definite statement that the excellence of an object which is chosen for the second time after a long interim is thereby established, but it is at all events not unreasonable to declare that those things which are rechosen cool-headedly after their emotional furore is past, are more likely to be enduringly good than the great majority of objects of a new style in the high tide of its

vogue.

Barring certain recurrent fads such as the present mania for kitchen antiques, or for modern Tut-ankh-Amen replicas — which must be classified as very distinctly rage-afm

TIIE PERSONALITY OP A HOUSE

fected — our belief in the beauty of that which has been selected generation after generation, and age after age, leaves little room for doubt as to its intrinsic worth. All of which is a roundabout way of saying that our belief in the beauty of the Taj Mahal of India, the Parthenon of Greece, the Cathedral of Burgos in Spain, the Elizabethan manor, Montacute, in England; the Maxfield Parrish beauty of Carcassonne in France, or any one of the hundreds of supreme examples of architecture, as well as tens of thousands of objects of interior embellishment, is founded upon established principles of taste and judgment — supplemented by the test of time.

HOW STYLES ARE SHAPED

Present-day styles, meaning those of latest and untested creation, always appeal to the large class of people who are attracted by whatever is new. But over against these lovers of mere newness stand those conservatives who find it difficult to endure any form of innovation. Without the first group we should never have the infusion of fresh elements, which are as necessary to the vitality of Art as fresh water is to an aquarium; without the other group there would be no check upon what might easily become an incurable epidemic of tasteless novelty.

It is, in fact, the balance between these two forces that shapes the course of Art. The cool-headed, conservative element, seeing that public sentiment is bent upon new Art — whatever its momentary expression may be — selects whatever may be capable of development, or uses the lever of its judgment to dislodge that which is completely bad.

(Our present Modern decorative art craze will be discussed in its own chapter.)

THE BEAUTY OF THE WORLD TO CHOOSE FROM

In contrast to the people of this or that Period of long age, whose choice was limited to the artistic accomplishments

THE PERIOD STYLES

of their nearest neighbors, we of to-day can make our choice from the whole range of examples that thousands of years of esthetic endeavor in all the nations of the world have been able to produce.

Suitability to use and appeal to good taste are the sole requirements in making our choice. Suitability to use includes suitability to situation, and under this head we must consider, in turn, harmony of aspect, durability, comfort, and protection. If these qualities be present, there is no reason why we should omit beauty in whatever form we find it and whatever its source, whether this be near by or in some distant country — such, for instance, as those of the far East, whose culture had reached a point of highest rank thousands of years before the white-skinned people of the earth had emerged from a state of primitive savagery.

We do, as a matter of fact, choose many beautiful objects from Japan, India, and China, though we have rarely, if ever — as yet — built a completely Eastern house; but that is only because Eastern modes of life differ too much from ours to make their houses or their furnishings comfortable for us. We have not chosen to erect fragile, one-storied houses with paper walls, such as they build in Japan, charming though they are to look at, especially when surrounded by an entrancing rock-garden; but this is merely for the utilitarian reason that they would be incompatible with the proper installation of our plumbing, steam-heating and electricity, to say nothing of the fire laws! Moreover, we prefer to wear our shoes in the house, to sit on chairs, and to warm ourselves at fires instead of putting on an extra half-dozen of wadded kimonos. But there is no reason why we should not — and quite suitably — build a Chinese house, especially now, when the influence of Chinese art dominates the best motifs of the present Modern style. One should say again dominates,” because Western appreciation of Chinese form, texture, and color rises every so often in a tide of enthusiasm. Not only was Chinese influence manifest throughout the eighteenth century in France and in England, but the influ-

ence of China would seem to have been marked in the inner court-gardens of all the ancient peoples of the Mediterranean borderlands, no less than in certain sculptural forms of Greece. Perhaps the small-headed, arch-necked horses of the Parthenon frieze, which we know as Classic, received their Classic outline from the roof-tile horses of China! Their similarity, at all events, is striking, and the Chinese prototype antedates the Greek by more than a thousand years.

THE RIGHT WAY TO CHOOSE

We should consider an object of beauty, no matter what its style, exactly as we consider an object displayed in a shopwindow or on a page of advertising. The only sensible and sane way to choose any of our belongings is to pick out each item either because it delights us or because we have need of it — or both.

To select a house, a chair or a table because it is tagged "Cinque-Cento,” "Elizabethan” or "Francois I” is exactly like buying a hat or a dress because of the name sewed into the lining! If it is an object of beauty, and suitable for the place where we intend to put it, what earthly difference does it make what its name is? This is not meant as an objection to knowing its name or recognizing its style, but is a protest against judging it solely by label, instead of appraising its value according to the evidence of its merit.

THE LABEL READERS

Appreciation is a thousand miles ahead of label-reading, or even of label-memory, and the way to cultivate it is to discover the beauty of an object first, and then find out afterwards what it is. If you mistake a piece of paste for a gem — or overlook a gem — the cultivating thing to do is not, even then, to memorize the genuine label, but to find out, if you can, what it was that made you like the counterfeit, and why it was that you missed the real gem, and thus,

THE PERIOD STYLES

through knowledge and practise, to become acquainted with the subtle details that distinguish the good from the bad. If those whose habit it is to concentrate on labels will only learn to recognize beauty of form and of texture and of color, and thereby learn to separate that which is an expression of art from that which is an expression of utter ignorance, they will have taken a long step in the right direction!

To know the name of something is useful — at times even essential. It is simpler, for instance, to tell a salesman, "I would like a Spanish vargueho,” or "I would like a Sheraton sideboard,” than to say, 'T want something for a house.”

The point to bear in mind is merely this: There are a thousand label readers to every one person of uninfluenced knowledge or taste, and it is this one, and not the nine hundred and ninety-nine others, who achieves a house of beautifully assembled personality instead of a tagged collection of unimaginatively assembled monotony. If you see an Italian or Spanish or English or any other sort of desk that you think would look well in your house — then choose it!

The only object in knowing about the styles that were evolved at certain times and in certain places is that knowledge of every sort is always an advantage. But knowledge that has been digested by means of thought and understanding and appreciation, is of greater advantage still.

The following sections, each devoted to a different period or style, are therefore intended to point out a few of the details that contribute to their charm (or the contrary), instead of emphasizing chronological history. In other words, our sole concern is with houses or objects that have qualities of beauty in contrast to those which are despoiling. For every reason, therefore, let us begin with the Spanish style.

1. THE SPANISH HOUSE

In the first place, it is suitable to put at the head of our list of styles the house that belongs to our own oldest period

of Colonial; a house that was not of English descent, built by the colonists at Jamestown and Plymouth, but one that came to us out of Spain, with something of Aztec and Indian origin added, through much earlier settlers — in New Mexico and California — who were Spanish.

In the second place, the subject of Spanish-American architecture and furnishing is one to be especially emphasized because no other style is so extreme in its contradiction of beauty and hideosity, of virtue and villainy. On one side the Spanish house of true type has beauty such as is possible to no other order of building, because the spirit of its creation is the spirit of Romance.
