On the other hand, the house that is sometimes labeled "Spanish” can be the most tawdry exposition of pretentiousness possible to perpetrate.

THE CHARM OF MYSTERY

Spanish houses of greatest beauty are like persons of quality, in that they do not proclaim their personal affairs nor parade their possessions in public. Moreover, the revelation of their charm of personality is reserved for those who, through ties of family or friendship, are admitted to the inner rooms and walled enclosures.

It is for this reason that the Spanish house of quality flaunts no attention-attracting ornamentation to the passer-by, gives no inkling of its character, its intentions or its possession. On the street side the fagade is of whitewashed stone or stucco, unbroken on its lower floor save by occasional windows, small in size and heavily shuttered, latticed, or grilled; from the vantage-ground of which those within may look out, but through which those without may glimpse no detail within.

Because of its mystery, the charm of this type of Spanish house is as tantalizing as the fleeting glimpse on an upper balcony of its own Castilian or Andalusian beauties, whose dark eyes gleam above their fans held in slim jeweled fingers. The allure of mystery and romance belongs, moreover, to the

Firm of Kenneth M. Murchison, Architects

GENUINELY BEAUTIFUL SPANISH FURNISHING: THE AUBUSSON RUG, MADE FOR A GRANDEE OF SPAIN, HAS A WINE-RED GROUND WITH BRILLIANTLY COLORED DESIGN

WalkeT 6c Gillettej Architects

EVERY POSSIBILITY OF BEAUTY IS EXPRESSED IN THIS ITALIAN ENTRANCE TO A PALATIAL HOUSE

THE PERIOD STYLES

Old Spanish house alone. Cold Northern blank- walled buildings are just that — and the reason for their unresponsiveness leaves one incurious. But low rambling Spanish walls that encircle gardens — the telltale trees and vines of which escape here and there above a coping — have all the allure of a shutin paradise.

This is why it is just about as suitable to tack the label "Spanish” on a commonplace, vulgar, white or pink or blue or yellow pile of chunky, topless squares, perched upon a bleak plot of unfenced land, with windows on all sides open to the ground, as it would be to build an adobe pueblo dwelling, trim its parapet with a Greek cornice, stand some Greek or Roman columns against the front of it, and call it "Colonial.”

The rules governing Spanish architecture, of course, have plenty of contradictory exceptions. If one happens, for instance, to dislike the suggestion of living in walled seclusion, one may with propriety and with best taste interpret the Spanish style as one pleases, so long as its quality of reserve be not lost. The result could be any plain-walled building with a beautiful doorway and deep-set windows with spoolturned spindles covering a casement that opens inward. It might even be something suggestive of plantation architecture, in that it could have wide second-story verandas and its roof-tiles laid shingle-flat.

Beautiful Spanish houses are always those which are simple in effect and low in proportion to their widespread area. Most ugly are those closely bunched together, like a handful of a child’s building blocks surmounted with heavy red-squared Chinese coolie’s hat-brims; equally bad is the house of great pretense that looks like a semi- Venetian casino with rococo trimming, Venetian Gothic-arched windows, Italian balustrade, or ornament in stucco work copied in full size from the wall of a church ten times the dimensions of the wall or door-frame it is made to decorate.

Other bad examples are those with bulbous bulges of the walls and great buttress-like thickenings of the base, and

with parapets that form wavy top pieces. Some of the New Mexican houses, which are modeled after the pueblos of the Indians, look much better in their own environment than pictures of them suggest, because they fit in with their desert setting. Otherwise the unornamented parapet of a roofless house is apt to give the appearance of a jar without a top.

The beauty of the Spanish roof depends upon the quality of its tiles, which can have indescribable beauty of color when produced by blendings of yellowish-pinkish-grayishburnt orange and faded brick. Moreover, their colors are as dependent upon softness and irregularity of texture as upon pigments.

GALLERIES

Galleries play an important part. Little ones on the outside above the entrance, or at one side, or on both sides. On the patio side long arcades or galleries often take the place of the upstairs hall, allowing bedrooms to open through two sides of the house. Generally there is an arcade on the first floor also serving as a hallway, supporting the colonnaded balcony above it. Wrought iron is often used, but spoolturned balustrades of wood are equally if not more in character.

SPANISH DOORWAYS

Whether good or bad, the doorways certainly run to extremes. One type is anchorite-plain and fortress-solid; another type is as decorated as a birthday cake. The embellishment of doorways and windows in great blank wall-spaces was the dominant feature of Spanish Renaissance. Sometimes the design in high-relief stucco rises in pinnacles higher than the walls of the fagades. Sometimes, when the door is low, a cartouche is placed over it; at other times the door is surmounted by an elaborate lintel, supported on columns or ornamented side-posts, with perhaps a balcony and window over it.

THE PERIOD STYLES

UNSUITABLE TO A COLD CLIMATE

The reason why the Spanish house does not belong in a northern climate is that the purpose of its design is protection against the tropical heat and dazzling sun. In less brilliant light the thickness of the walls produces an interior of dungeon-darkness and chill; moreover, the use of open galleries instead of corridors, with the featuring of a purposely shaded courtyard as the chief living-room of the house, has little appeal under cloudy skies, and none at all in a good hard downpour.

And yet, with a moderate amount of ingenuity, especially if one has a generously filled purse, a house of Spanish character could easily be adapted to the northern climate. The patio, if big enough to cast comparatively little shade, could have a glassed-in, steam-heated conservatory garden on its southern side, filled with vines and winter-blooming flowers. There would be a fountain, of course, and a small swimming plunge, perhaps; and the uncovered part of the patio would be beautified throughout the year by a thick planting of evergreens. But unless it is a question of housing a Spanish collection or of suiting a family of Spanish descent, one would do well to think twice before facing the necessity of overcoming liabilities which in more suitable environments are the outstanding assets of the Spanish style.

ABUSE OF THE PATIO

The patio is an inner courtyard garden completely surrounded by the rooms or galleries of the house, supplemented when necessary by high walls, and is a direct descendant of the atrium or peristylium of ancient Rome and Greece. In Southern California, Florida, and other localities where adaptations of the Spanish house predominate, the patio, as often as not, is enclosed on three sides only. The original intention of the patio was a secluded outdoor living-room for the use of the family. Its surrounding walls insured complete

privacy, and, furthermore, insured shade on one side of it or another at every hour of the day.

But, whichever its form, the possibilities of its beauty and enrichment have been realized by an amazingly small percentage of those whose houses include them. Too often an American attempt at a patio looks like a factory-exhibit of tiles, or a home for incurable feather-duster palms and scrubby little plants in cluttering flower-pots, with not a plant or vine or tree of luxuriance in the whole enclosure; it might be called a courtyard, a clothes-yard, a ceramic compound, or a feather-duster alley, but it cannot — ^unless one intends to be sarcastic — be called a patio!

THE PATIO OF PERFECTION

Too small an enclosure that is nothing more than a roofless vestibule or small yard defeats the object of the patio, which is meant to serve as a cool and pleasant living-room where the down-slanting breezes may blow through a wideopen space. Too large a patio, on the other hand, loses its living-room quality.

The principal area of the patio should be paved with tiles — preferably unornamented — or with broken flagstones, or with herring-bone-laid bricks. In any case, there should be left wide earth openings in the corners or at intervals against the building or gallery walls for planting.

In patios of size, the tiles or flagstones are taken up at effective distances, and trees or shrubbery or flower-beds are set in. Every patio has a fountain — which can be a feature of entrancing beauty, especially when the water descends by means of spills into a pool deep enough for a plunge. At one side there should be an unplanted space of at least small-room size, protected by an awning that can be let down for shade or in case of rain, and this space furnished as a living-room. But the surrounding distances of the patio should be treated as a garden. Galleries should be twined with vines, and the beautiful flowers of the tropics should be planted in profu-

THE PERIOD STYLES

sion — oleanders, Camilla trees, hibiscus, myrtle, bougainvillea, and roses growing as high as one’s head.

The possibilities in awning-sheltered flower-bedecked fountain-embellished gardens are so rich and varied that the ugliness of a bare, uninviting enclosure becomes doubly disappointing if such chance for beauty be wantonly — or ignorantly — thrown away!

THE SPANISH INTERIOR AND ITS FURNITURE

An outstanding quality of a good Spanish interior and its furniture is an effect of hand-made softness of the carving and surfaces of the wood. Such an interior may quite well be almost bare, but its few objects have individual character and are beautifully mannered in their consideration of each other. Walls of tin hardness are hideous, as are equally those which are lumpy or scratched. Those streaked and smeared with colors and cobwebs are simply unspeakable and need not be discussed.

There should be the same softening of the plaster edges of a door -opening that there is in the surface of a handhewn beam. There is seldom any trim, and only such necessary doors as those that lead outside or into the service wings or bedrooms. The walls are nearly always plastered white or cream. Heavy wooden beams of the ceiling are supported on posts or columns, at the top of which brackets, called zapatos, are often fitted, filling the angles between the columns and the beams that they support, and making a very simple but effective ornamentation^ The spaces between the beams are plastered or filled with strips or twigs of wood, herring-boned or set in some other straight-lined or rightangled pattern.

Whatever wood there may be is embellished with ''linework” or "flat-work” carving that follows a herring-bone design, or one of match-sticks laid in little groups, first this way and then that; or perhaps it follows an outstanding design which suggests a pattern that might be notched by

the drawing of a rounded knife-blade with little jabs across the smoothed surface of mashed potato.

FLOORS

The floors are sometimes of wood, but more usually of tile, either figured or plain. Figured floors, whether of tiles or covered with rugs, belong in the eye-hitting category — so be careful! Because the Spanish used rugs made by every country to which they sailed, the rugs suitable to a Spanish house are unlimited in variety. Most effective, of course, are those with colorful heraldic devices, figures of people and animals, and every sort of floral pattern.

Bold use of brilliant color on the floor must be carefully adjusted to a repetition of its keynote elsewhere. But fixed rules can not be made. Ordinarily two challenging masses antagonize each other. A gorgeous rug, gorgeous embroideries, elaborately decorated leather and tiles — a combination of these is apt to be frightful, and yet it can be beautiful. The rule of safety is that if figured tiles or embroideries or tapestries are to be put on the walls, the floor covering would better be plain.

In the dining-room shown in Plate 22, in which the furniture is Spanish, the outstanding color is in the Aubusson rug. This rug was especially designed for a palace in Spain, and its color is wine-red, cream, yellow, green, and rose. The walls are cream, the furniture soft brown; the hangings of old damask exactly match the wine-red of the rug. The quality of every item contributes its utmost to a result which is as appealing as it is in perfect taste — and this in spite of the not at all Spanish setting of an average apartment interior.

COLOR AND ARRANGEMENT

The most effective arrangement of a Spanish room is to put a magnificent embellishment on one wall — a tapestry or a painting, or a hanging of brocade with a mirror over it, as

THE PERIOD STYLES

in the dining-room just described, and under it place a sofa or a table or other low piece of furniture. Place a desk against another wall, a leather or velvet - covered, trunk-shaped chest under another hanging. Stand a long table against another wall, with a mirror or painting over

SIMPLE SPANISH TABLE . lie i • i i

It, add a lew chairs, and the room is finished. A piece of embroidery is hanging over a balcony, high up on one wall, perhaps, or there is a splash or two of vividly colorful flowers in big jars of glass or pottery or copper.

And yet the furnishing of a Spanish house is far from easy. You can find a thousand examples of vulgar Spanish furniture for each single item of herring-bone or knife-notched carving — properly called flat-work — or for each bit of wood of mellow surface, or each set of proper brace-irons made for support and not for spiraled ornamentation. (Fig. 5 5.)

The one great difficulty for the unknowing is that while good Spanish furnishings are invariably expensive, expensive Spanish furnishings are by no means invariably good. For one thing, furniture, and in fact the entire interior of the Spanish house, must give the impression of being hand-made. Of course, all beautiful furniture of other styles should be hand-finijhed — and therefore is liable to be prohibitively expensive. But Spanish furniture is different. It need not be fine — it can, in fact, be quite crude; but its wood-carving and even its structure must not suggest the precision of a machine.

CHAIR

Happily there is much good Spanish furniture that is entirely within the ability of an average craftsman to reproduce. By this I do not mean that the more elaborate varieties of furniture, such as the inlays and flat-work of Spanish varguenos — drop-front desks that are fitted on the interior with a solid checker-board of small drawers, the face of each carved or inlaid — can be made by the nearest carpenter! Far from it. But merely that it has been within my

BEAUTIFUL SPANISH CHEST

personal experience to prove that, given a good design and some timbers of old, seasoned walnut or other wood, any skilled carpenter who is anything of a cabinet-maker can make many varieties of Spanish furniture that are pleasing and satisfactory. Not alone the table (Fig. 5 5), but even the chest (Fig. 57) and the chair (Fig. 58) can be copied in proportion and outline — with as much or little ornament as may be practicable.

Leathers and stuffs and embroideries are not so easily substituted. There is a quality belonging to old velvets and brocades that is difficult to imitate. At all events, the worst

THE PERIOD STYLES

possible interior to describe is the one with rough or streakycolored pseudo-Spanish walls, hung with gold-gallooned cotton-velvet banners, and curtains embroidered with imitation coats of arms, and hung on double-spear fixtures behind highlighted machine-made furniture!

Admonition to one inclined toward the Spanish or Italian style: Be moderate in the use of oversized Gothic outlines, exaggerated Moorish details, splurgy cartouches, overwrought stucco, crudeness of finish, and massive structural effects that suggest the housing of a hydraulic plant, or at least of a herd of hippopotami.

TILES

If don’ts in the use of tiles were set in electric lights they would not be overemphasized. Variegated tiles stamped — or composed — in mosaic patterns are the favorite trimming of the incautious, and are often used with dire results; so if you must use tiles, be careful! However, if you really like that sort of trimming, there is no reason why you may not frame with tiles the doors or niches or recesses of an otherwise bare room or loggia, or vestibule or hall. They are often decorative set into the treads or the risers of the stairs, and many people like them as dados and as fountain-backs in patios. Nevertheless, it is well to keep in mind that a mixture of tiles indiscriminately used is more apt to look like the Spanish Inn scene of a musical comedy than the abode of a family of cultivation and refinement.

Remember also that good Spanish furniture is carved in continuous bands of shallow cut-work, more suggestive of fancy stitching than of carving, and that, as in all good furniture, the surface and color of the wood count at least sixty if not seventy per cent.

GOOD EXAMPLE OF SPANISH CHAIR

Tastefully done, no house lends itself to economical beautification more easily than does the Spanish. With very little furniture against its very plain walls, with the patio elaborated to its utmost by means of the dark green foliage of shrubs and trees, and with comfort-inviting furniture and a few splashes of strong color as an especial accent modified by neutral textiles — the various browns of old wood and iron — it can be lovely.

2. THE ITALIAN HOUSE

Considering that the Italian Renaissance was at least godmother — if not grandmother — to all the neighborhood Periods of Europe, this greatest Period of art and decoration plays a comparatively small part in our houses of to-day. (Except in its Palladian interpretation, adopted by England and later by us, and noted elsewhere. See Index.) But, defining the house in this section as distinctly Italian in character, we might almost say that in our present enthusiasm for Spanish, English, and Normandy styles — not to mention the modernist monolith that is bearing down upon us with the implacability of a traction engine — one of the best opportunities for a house of personality and charm is being overlooked.

Because certain classic villa examples express the "frozen music” definition of architecture as few other styles can, we are apt to think of the Italian house as having no other personality save that which is imposing and formal. To regard the quality of formality as inseparable from the Italian style is as unthinking as it would be to insist that clouds must unvaryingly be "fleecy” and water be "blue.” If clouds are fleecy when they merge into heavy, even blankets of fog; and if water is blue when it is slate-gray or green or silversurfaced, then perhaps the Italian palace, or villa, or house, is always formal.

The perfectly balanced aspect of an American-Italian villa, set upon a marble-balustraded platform, with stiff,

THE PERIOD STYLES

starched gardens that are geometrically precise, is formal, certainly. A great, block-shaped palace is much more than formal on its exterior. The case of resplendence vs. formality of the interior will be noted presently ; at the moment let us merely remark that the informal Italian house can express one of the most homelike dwellings possible to imagine for you or for me — in any neighborhood.

But before considering this latter type of house, we must at least briefly focus our attention on the palaces and villas of the Renaissance, which were the ancestors of the modern house.

ITALIAN SPLENDOR OF THE FIFTEENTH CENTURY

In its most brilliant and forceful manifestation, the fifteenth-century palace is very suggestive of a Kohinoor diamond that most of us look at in a museum display-case with never a thought of becoming its owner. But for the few of us who are buying world-renowned diamonds or duplicating world-renowned palaces, no other Period that we could possibly select lends itself to such colorful magnificence.

At the mention of an Italian palace, we think of floors elaborately designed of varicolored marble; walls unbelievably embellished with paintings and sculpture and frescoes, by the greatest artists the world has known; we think of ceilings no less than walls that are vast panoramas of history or mythology in deeply subdued colors, embellished with enframements of gildings and sculptures and inlays of marbles. We think of rich-colored damask brocades and

Genoese velvets. _

We think, too, of high relief carving of wood — perhaps

finished with a smoothing of gesso (hard plaster) in preparation for the painting and gilding that was applied either to walls or to furniture. We think of great chests some of them with backs and arms. We think of high candlesticks, gilded and carved, and of high corner cupboards and low credenze; we think of refectory tables; all of these paneled

or carved or inlaid or finished in polychrome — or with these embellishments applied to one piece.

We think of severe high-back chairs upholstered in blue, dark red or deep green damask brocades. We think of crossframed chairs with strapped backs and loose cushions of velvet; we think of chairs with twisted wood spirals and arm-posts terminating in carved figures. We think of tapestries, of course, worth a rich man’s fortune; but first and foremost we think of the supremacy of Italian sculpture and painting.

THE MAGIC DIGESTION OF THE RENAISSANCE

Small wonder that such overrichness is more than dangerous for any but an expert to assemble — and that even the expert is likely to fail in producing a quality that might be called the magic digestion of the Renaissance! No rooms intended to be lived in were ever so overpoweringly ornamented as were those in the palaces of the rich nobles of Italy — especially throughout the sixteenth century. And yet, for all their ornament — so lavish as to defy description, even though one copy every synonym for splendor in the dictionary — they were HOMES. They were suitable backgrounds for the great families who’ lived in them, and who, in many instances, are still living in them.

You can go to-day into many of these supergorgeous apartments, into which modern comforts have been installed, and be made conscious of no overpowering background. To me, this is sheer necromancy — and perhaps, like the Indian rope which hangs down out of empty air, and on which a small boy climbs heavenward — a figment of my own imagination. At all events, I offer the picture as it appears to me.

In the eighteenth century the virile style of the Italian Renaissance went through the phases of baroque decadence and rococo flippancy. It should be added that the term baroque is not necessarily a synonym for decadence, but is merely the name of a style that easily lends itself to decadence

THE PERIOD STYLES

because it is beyond the bounds of classic regularity. It was originally applied by Portuguese jewelers to unevenly shaped pearls, and its meaning is unevenness. Rococo is composed of two French words, rocaille and coquaille — rocks and shells.

In its best interpretation baroque is an imaginative and picturesque release from the cold restraints of classic form. In other words, it denotes freedom and exuberance of expression. Rococo runs somewhat to the florid and the flippant, but at its best it is gay and youthful. At their worst, both the baroque and rococo — like the little girl with the curl are "horrid.”

Throughout every Period the pendulum of popular taste swings from classic, or regular, to baroque or picturesque. In a house that has what might be called flexibility of charm, an accent of the one should complement the other. A purely classic house, without a human deviation from set regularity, is as unlivable in quality as a tomb or any other memorial edifice. Unrestrained irregularity that "spills all over the place” is equally uninviting because repose is impossible in an

atmosphere of restless confusion.

The Palladian influence which was expressed in the Florentine palaces and villas was a direct return to the classic orders, and although of utmost importance to us, belongs so far as this chapter is concerned — in the sections apportioned to France and to England.

VILLAS

Many of the Palladian villas in Italy were permanent country houses. But curiously paradoxical is the fact that many of those which were built with most chaste formality were retreats to which the nobles, escaping from the gayeties of city life, went with their week-end house-parties to take a rest cure, the regime of which is said to have consisted chiefly in looking at the view, wandering in the pleasure gardens, reading poetry, and leading the sort of life ^at had been suggested — again it is said — by Boccaccio. The

palaces of Rome and Florence, on the other hand, for all their florid exuberance, were the scenes of purely patriarchal family life. Therefore, had we chosen our pattern fittingly, country life in America would far better have found its example in palaces than in villas.

But since we have made the coldly classic villa the accepted standard for our interpretation of the most gloriously colorful period of history, we might at least see what manner of building this is likely to be.

THE FORMAL TYPE OF ITALIAN HOUSE IN AMERICA

The outstanding features of its exterior include the platform on which the house is placed, and the surrounding gardens — laid out with architectural symmetry — of greenhedged vistas and cypress spires which serve as settings for fountains and sculptures of marble.

The house is either block-shaped or E-shaped, directly facing the terrace, which is balustraded in marble and has wide marble stairs descending to the gardens below. An inevitable feature is the loggia overlooking the garden, or commanding a notable view.

A typical Italian house of dignity and distinction would be severely classic and probably white or oyster-gray or cream. Another equally beautiful but less formal type — one that might have walls of very deep cream or sand, and that might quite possibly be warmly embellished on its exterior with frescoes or scraffito design — is found in the unusually enchanting example whose hall and stairway are shown in Plate 23.

The interior of such types as this illustration suggests may be appropriately rendered with great elaboration or utmost simplicity of background. Elaboration can go as far as that of the marble and fresco- walled palaces of Venice — rarely, if ever, do they go as far as those of Rome. Usually the walls are left plain, though the ceilings are embellished with woodwork or with plaster or vaulting. The floors are of

THE PERIOD STYLES

marble or tile or stone. But even though the walls be without ornamentation, there must be considerable embellishment in the way of pictures and tapestries and beautiful stuffs, and furniture that is intrinsically good. This is the one demand of the Italian house of importance.

It is true that an Italian house, if it be formal, is in many ways exacting — formality always is! But it is not true that the Italian house of beauty is necessarily a formal one. Neither is it necessary that its gardens be formal — unless its architecture be notably classic. A copy of a villa of definite type would necessitate the garden that belongs to it, since the word villa connotes a house with a garden that matches, exactly as country place denotes a house surrounded by a certain amount of land.

THE INFORMAL ITALIAN HOUSE

This is like any other simple house or low, rambling farm building, with stucco walls and hip-roof, a plain door, plain deep-set windows (less deep-set than those of the Spanish house) , and without ornamentation of window or door frames and without grilles.

Such houses as these are far from being formal or exacting, and few houses can be so full of charm. A house such as this might be set in a cottage garden with a space at the front door — or better at a side one — that is flagstoned and shaded by a grape-covered pergola. It looks at its best on a hill with a high wall — making at least part of the boundary of its enclosure — against which vines or trees are trained; but it can equally well be spread on level ground.

One of the loveliest houses in America — judged by its quality of appeal — was a converted stable, barn, shed and chicken-house, with connecting buildings set between. At one side was the shut-in perennial flower-bordered garden, somewhat suggestive of a Spanish patio — only ' somewhat because an awninged loggia took the place of the paved area, and the garden itself had paths of grass. Moreover, since its

location was near New York, there was no tropical planting. The trees were cedars and the flowers those of the hardy garden perennials and biennials.

Such a house may have floors of stone, but they could quite as suitably be of wood. Its ceilings are probably plastered flat, its windows square, a few objects of carving or color shown to advantage against plain plastered walls. An old chest with a priest’s vestment hung over it; a double breadth of delphinium blue or bright rose-red brocade or embroidery; a mirror in a Florentine gilt frame. Any variety of Italian chairs, a sofa for comfort, and some colorful stuffs such as the Fortuny cotton prints — or any other materials of foliage or diaper pattern in monochrome of blue or red of green or possibly yellow. Or the walls of a room can be battened for stuffs — as old ones often were — and hung with silks or cottons. Or they can be decorated with an Italian scenic or other wall-paper. A little hall or room papered in the small sheets of hand-blocked paper that are still made in Italy (in Varese, especially) for book-binding and endless decorative purposes, would be very much in character, since these "domino” papers of Italy (so called because they were used to line boxes that held chessmen and dominoes) were the rage in other days. The marble patterns must be shellacked after they are hung, and this treatment improves them all. Covered walls make a more furnished, and therefore homelike room — especially if there be no tapestries or other decorative embellishments — than walls that are white. Ceilings may be left flat and plain; or, if high enough, open beams are suitable, whether left plain or decorated. Vaulted, plastered ceilings, either plain or with ornamented brackets, are ideally suitable if there be sufficient height. Great height with no relief is as bad as overtrimmed lowness.

In other words, if a ceiling is low, it is best to leave it the color of the wall with no dividing cornice line. If walls are moderately high, leave them plain or open-timbered; but if they are very high, they should be either decorated or vaulted.

Taylor & Levi, Architects

THE SORT OF HOUSE THAT APPEALS TO A MAN— FRIENDLY, SUBSTANTIAL, WELCOMING AND HOMELIKE

A LOGGIA THAT IS AS INVITING AS IT IS ARCHITECTURALLY FORMAL

THE PERIOD STYLES

THE SPANISH VS. THE ITALIAN PERSONALITY

Considered according to its inherited tendencies, the allure of the Spanish house is emphasized by its walled-in mystery. To open its doors and windows wide is like tearing the veils from the faces of favorites of the harem, dressing them in Paris clothes, and making them as commonplace as the rest of us.

The Italian palace was a patriarchal stronghold of domestic dignity; the villa was a secluded retreat. The Spanish and Italian houses have many points of resemblance.

The beauty of Spanish furnishing is dependent upon plain wall surfaces — meaning wide spaces between the furniture so that each object can be displayed against an unencumbered background. The placing of Italian furniture is the same.

A gallery-shaped room is especially adapted to Italian furnishing, with windows down a long side, hung with colorful brocade that decorates the otherwise bare plastered walls. An ornate mantel is centered on the wall opposite, with small sofas drawn forward on either side. A chair under a mirror, a sofa under a tapestry, two pictures in two even spaces; no items too close together, no detail out of scale, and no objects moved too far forward from the walls. A long table at the end of the room with two majolica jars made into lamps.

This description sounds like a Spanish room, but its personality should be quite different in the effect of color. The Italian interior, whether palatial or primitive, should be subtle in its harmony. If its walls are white, its hangings emphasize a note of blue. If its walls are cream, its hangings would probably be yellow or yellow-green. If the walls are sand or verging on the brownish (many walls in Italy are actually close to stale chocolate) the hangings would undoubtedly be red. With the color chosen as the dominant one, whatever it may be, there should be blended the grayed colors of its own triad and complement. At its best, one color should be predominant, but not tyrannical.

The Spanish characteristic is one of much stronger accent — a splash of color, red and orange by preference, or any primary or binary hue put close to the contrast of its complement (see Color Chart) . In the Spanish house, vivid flowers look best: Solid masses of brilliant yellow with blue, or masses of orange with red — the warm colors generally prevail. In the plain-walled Italian house, flowers can never be too many, but they should be arranged in blended harmonies •ather than in strong contrasts.

A WORD OF WARNING TO THOSE LIKELY TO BE LED ASTRAY

Although the Italian house is not at the moment breaking into violences of vulgarity as the Spanish one is on occasions doing, the impulsive buying of oversized and overexuberant

FLORENTINE RENAISSANCE

THE PERIOD STYLES

examples, which can too easily deteriorate into meaningless scrolls and bulges, is so characteristic of the novice traveler that one who is thinking of going to Italy with any intention of buying objects of art, should make preventive preparations before setting out. The Italian style, as it is encountered in its palaces (and every building one goes into is a palace!), is so colorfully breath-taking in its mellowed magnificence that any one who comes within range of its spell is very apt to lose all sense of proportion, restraint, and taste. And then he or she wonders why bulbous baroque Cupids, large-patterned brocades, massive mirror frames — or wall cabinets as ornate as the animal wagons of the circus — give violent indigestion to the living-room at home.

Away from home one forgets that the average ceiling built to-day is between eight and nine feet — twelve feet seems almost too high! So one confidently buys a beautiful

ceiling that, compared with all the sculpture-embellished others, seems fragilely simple, only to find, when it is put up in the new dining-room at home, that its size and weight give the impression of a giant’s waffle-iron pressing down on one’s head.

The rule of safety in furnishing an Italian room, of course, is to put any ornamentation verging on the florid against a blank-walled contrast. The method of danger to the inexpert is the putting of painted carving or otherwise highly embellished furniture against an equally ornamental back-

OPEN-TOP ITALIAN CHEST

ground. In fact, this latter procedure is very apt to produce the same effect upon one’s senses as eating butter without bread. And yet this dish, as set before us by the Italians, is palatable.

The reason why the old Italian palaces are so unmatchable is that the Italians were — and will ever be — artists by temperament no less than by ceaseless cultivation; and that in all notable examples objects of supreme value were perfectly adjusted in balance, in color, and in scale.

FURNITURE

The pieces which fit best into average American rooms are the smaller, plainer pieces, such as Figures 59 and 60. The table shown in Figure 61 is not so easy to adjust. But the chest (Fig. 62) would go in almost any Italian style of room. The chair (Fig. 63) is simpler when covered with a dullsurfaced damask, but against a plain wall old leather is always good. Cupboards, chests and credenzas that are not too large and not too ornate are excellent in rooms of size, against rather wide wall-spaces.

3. THE FRENCH HOUSE

Periods, as already said, are always the natural expression of need and mood and situation. If our own need or mood or situation duplicates that of an earlier people, then the findings of their experience are of importance to us. We are concerned with the palaces of the Italian Renaissance because their colorful interiors were the first to express the quality of comfort, which is characteristic of our interior furnishings to-day. The Gothic style of France, on the other hand, is not an expression of the American house. But it is impossible to mention the French house and entirely leave out this fundamental Period style which the French of the twelfth century created.

In countries other than France — including our own —

THE PERIOD STYLES

the pinnacles and spires mounting heavenward are symbols of Christian spirituality, and even in France the supreme Gothic examples are those of the cathedrals of Rheims, Chartres, Bourges, Amiens, Rouen and others. But there are also many fine examples in French domestic architecture. The ones known to every tourist include the house of Jacques Coeur at Bourges and many houses at Rouen and elsewhere. These are replete with suggestions for those who are intending to build a house wrought lavishly in stone. Such lacework in stone can very well be done! But not for many of us. Nor have many — if any — of us old Gothic tapestries, without which a Gothic room loses the object of its being!

Nor are many of us more than impersonally interested in those vast chateaux of the Renaissance that Francis I and his successors built in the province of Touraine — most of them on the banks of the Loire. We journey to see them because they are of historical importance as well as of architectural beauty. But, barring a few of the private apartments, they are out of scale with the average idea of home-possibility. At Chenonceaux, Chambord, Blois, and all the others and even in the so-called "small ones,” Azay-le-Rideau and Langeais, there is little material that we can translate into houses of moderate size. Many of these chateaux, however, have served as inspiration for American palaces especially Blois, whose marvelous open stairway is distinctly the prototype of one that now embellishes an outstanding estate in

the Carolinas.

ENTER MADAME

One incident of the French Renaissance (which followed the Gothic Period) has a flavor of irony or amusement, depending upon how you take it.

The familiar saying, "Man built the Flouse, but woman made the Home,” which has such a contentedly domestic sound, had several not very domestic interludes in France, at all events. The woman who is said to have been the creator of feminine beauty in the French interior — the woman who

changed the Gothic building of stone into a background suitable to herself, and whose knowledge and taste in palacefurnishing were stamped (as was her emblem) on the Renaissance Period of France — was Diane de Poitiers, the favorite companion of Henry II.

Other favorites more or less adept in 'assembling beautiful rooms, adorning to their personalities, followed throughout the succeeding Periods, but left no definite impression until the Marquise de Pompadour, by favor of Louis XV, repeated — in fact, rather excelled — the feat of la Poitiers. During the nineteen years that la Pompadour was uncrowned queen, she made the French interior famous for all time. Besides some of the finest designs of Sevres porcelain, many of the most beautiful and restrained examples of furniture and engraving are directly due, not merely to her patronage of the applied arts, but to her guiding genius.

It must be noted, of course, that the Little Trianon, that loveliest "house” in France — or anywhere else, perhaps — was built for Marie Antoinette. But to attempt any detailed description of the French Periods is to be carried out on a current of styles that are as replete with confusing motives as a spring flower-bed in a public park, and would necessitate the space of a whole book — more likely a dozen whole books — to itself.

The houses which this short section is intended to describe — houses which are ideal for American adaptation — are the smaller houses of France. These are usually the small hotels of Paris and the manor houses and farms of the Provinces.

PERFECT FEMININE ROOMS

Most beautiful and most suitable for the furnishing of a woman’s room or apartment — or whole house — are all the wood-lined interiors that American decorators are buying as fast as they can be found in France and brought over by steamer. I am calling them wood-lined rather than paneled, because wood-lined is exactly what they are. Walls, ceil-

THE PERIOD STYLES

ings, doors, window trims, mantels and cupboards, all are of wood — and, moreover, all of a piece. Sometimes they are embellished with a moderate amount of carving— very moderate. Sometunes the wood, which may be walnut, chestnut, o^ some other wood of light brownish color, is left unstained except by time and wax; more often it is painted old ivory, soft olive, apricot or ashes of lemon, blue-gray or pinkish gray, or a faded shade of apple-green. Sometimes the molding, whether embossed or grooved (generally both) , is glazed with gold. Sometimes the painted decoration is very elaborate. Most beautiful of all are those painted in miniature scrolls or with floral bands outlining the panels and rosettes on the corners, or with fragile scenes in small over-door panels or lunettes or on the doors of cupboards.

Always the decoration is in minor tone — usually in monochrome classic traceries; in cream or gray on woods painted in the softer gray or olive greens; or green is painted on cream or gray. Many times the color is of one porcelainsurfaced tint — except where the fading of time has thumbed it gently.

Many of the loveliest of these wood-lined roorns, as already noted, are restrained in the elaboration of their detail, no matter which their Period. In the Rococo Period of Louis XIV, their shells and rocks and waves are so modified that the ornamentation is applied in the lightest accent of gayety. The typical rooms, especially those found in the Provinces, are apt to be built in the Period of Louis XV , or occasionally in that of Louis XVI or Directoire. They are most charming with no decoration at all except the curves of the paneling, which also appears in the doors and cupboards and in the

window framing.

FRENCH FURNISHING

The finest French interiors are of endlessly intricate detail. Walls that are a series of decorated panels, and chiseled marble mantels that are as delicate as the work of a jeweler. Floors set in parquetry. Appliques of gilt and crystals. Many

paintings, but of the finely scaled schools of Watteau, Fragonard or Claude. Gobelin and Beauvais tapestries sometimes on the walls, but invariably covering the chairs and sofas. Commodes and vitrines of Buhl, or satinwood veneers, marquetry and porcelain inlays, of varieties far too numerous to list.

THE ROOMS OF THE PROVINCES

No such elaboration, however, is found in rooms of the French countryside. Into the salons of the small chateaux and manor-houses were put simplified assorted articles of furniture. Chairs of Louis XV suggestion are typical. They are sometimes painted, but usually of walnut or chestnut or other unstained wood, upholstered in the material of the curtains; meaning that the room and its hangings are most charming when matched exactly by a certain amount — a third, perhaps as much as a half — of the furniture. To this would be added one-half or two-thirds, perhaps, of other mixture.

In an interior of light gray-green with a little gold, and hangings perhaps of yellow and gray, a small sofa and a chair or two of a scarcely darker greenish tone might be upholstered in the yellow and gray. A brown wood stool might be covered with silver blue. Two chairs, perhaps of light walnut upholstered in blue and faded coral. A gilt chair covered with brocade. A faded red lacquer cabinet or an inlaid writing-table. Occasional tables and ornaments that accentuate the yellow, coral or blue.

When hangings are of taffeta, then cushions of the taffeta are tied to cane-seated chairs, and a small sofa — not often sat on — is upholstered in the taffeta as well.

At this point, a word of warning lest you be led to follow in the footsteps of a New York woman, who, having bought a charming little putty-color-and-gilt room in one of the provinces of France, sent it home with orders to a certain cabinet-maker to fit it, and to fix it where necessary. The cabinet-maker fitted it very well, and "fixed” it thoroughly.

THE PERIOD STYLES

Finding the surface far from new, he planed it off as best he could and sent for a painter to make a good job of it!

As a matter of fact, we can — and do — produce these rooms in America. We can make them quite perfectly. But skill is lacking to duplicate the slight unevenness of surface produced by the softening of time — which is quite different from the mechanical repetition of irregularity.

A perfect key for one who is building a new interior would be a Chippendale mirror. Curved panels, unornamented save for a Chinese motif at the top of an occasional panel and over the cupboards.

To speak of these rooms as "little” may be misleading, since the effect of littleness is really produced by fineness, and many of them are moderately large. The ceilings of some are high, but in the majority of them the proportion is kept low. An eight- or eight-and-a-half-foot ceiling is ample in a little room about twelve feet square; a nine-and-a-half- or even a nine-foot ceiling is not too low for a room that is twenty to twenty-five feet square.

IN THE MODERN APARTMENT

Where the low ceiling is normally a handicap to be struggled with, as in an apartment, these French interiors are ideal. Such backgrounds are most appropriate to the very feminine — no longer so very young — woman who is slightly precious in her taste, and whose friends come in for conversation and a cup of tea. One cannot think of these fragile wood-lined treasure-boxes swarming with a horde of girls and men in golf clothes, who scarcely know the meaning of conversation (so it is said!) , and who are notably indifferent to tea!

The French Period is suitable for a drawing-room into which no one goes until after dinner; it is equally suitable for a boudoir or a bedroom, or a small book-room. But in its more formal aspect it is not the sort of room that average men or children feel at home in.

I

A FRENCH HOUSE THAT MEN LIKE

There is another style of French interior — and exterior as well — that men like exceedingly. This is the Normandy type of house.

NORMANDY COTTAGE - GROUND FLOOR

The name Provencal, by the way, cannot mean Norman cottage. It applies only to a house whose style has come from

Provence, a former province in the Southeast of France, now divided into four departments. The houses of the provinces, on the other hand, are those all over France outside of Paris. At the moment, we are having a Normandy craze, and houses with open-timber frames and pointed roofs are building as thick as mushrooms on the dunes of coast re-

NORMANDY COTTAGE - SECOND FLOOR

sorts and on the hills inland. They are put up chiefly by those who admire the Tudor-Jacobean-Cotswold house, but

LOUIS XVI

who prefer (or must be satisfied with) a house of less importance than that of their admiration.

In their favor it can be said that the Normandy houses are exceedingly adaptable and homelike. The walls are of stucco, and open-timbered. Roofs are made of flat tiles (composition usually) and seem to fit on without discomfort, no matter which way they are pointed. The one requirement of beauty is that ridges and valleys must be skilfully adjusted so as to look soft and weathered. When they are sharp-edged

they are hideous — ^likewise when they are too fatly rollededged.

An architect who is building them by the dozen told me yesterday: "J^st handle them picturesquely and you can do anything with them. You can tag a big room on here and a little one there, put on a balcony or roof-garden or whatever

FRENCH CUPBOARD WITH PAINTED DESIGN

you please. The more irregular they are, the better ^ey look.” The word "picturesque” has, I am afraid, a catch in it. It suggests the canvas upon which an artist has applied a lot of layers of paint and made a valuable picture. But paint put on in layers by just any one who can hold a paintbrush is something else again! Which reminds me of something I was told about these make-believe old-new cottages.

The way of the "old-wood-beamed” interior is amusing.

Big machine-sawed beams are planed — not too well — an excellent job for apprentice carpenters. The wood is then stained. When the stain is thoroughly dried in, a coat of whitewash is applied. When that is dry, a thin coating of asphalt is put on and then rubbed off, taking much of the whitewash with it. Result, “old wood” that is detectiondefying in its effect!

I have not seen this trick performed and cannot guarantee that the practise of it is as easy as it sounds. But if you want an old weathered beam instead of a new plank, this is the recipe of an expert. I have therefore repeated it exactly as it was told to me.

The plan shown in Figures 64 and 65 is that of a summer cottage built upon an Atlantic Ocean beach. An amusing feature is the outdoor entrance to each of the four bedrooms —-for bathers from the beach. Another is that the partition walls of the room marked “Den” swing or lift out of the way, so that the living-room may be made the full width of the building for a small dance.

FURNITURE FOR FRENCH PERIODS

Typical chairs of Louis XV and Louis XVI — such as shown in Fig ures 66 and 67 — would be suitable for wood-lined rooms, described above, or for the paneled manor houses of more formal type. And so would the lovely cabinet painted in light grays and greens and creams shown in Figure 69.

For the Normandy houses and — equally suitable — for our own Colonial houses, there is now being brought to America a great deal of most adaptable furniture that is made of various light brown unpainted woods. A little of it is old, but most of it is modern with much of the effect of the old. Sometimes it is quite sophisticated, such as the settee in the hall in Plate 1 6. The quilted-petticoat-covered chairs shown in this same plate and in Plate 50 are now being made by the thousand, and petticoat quilting is far from being unusual — also far from any likelihood of ever having been petticoats!

Figure 73 is a Directoire chair that is almost equally English Adams and American Empire. Its front legs are somewhat pantaletted, but it is otherwise a perfect Colonial chair. (Also see American Empire example in Plate 54.)

4. THE ENGLISH HOUSE

The English Period, as many of us think of it, is cut into distinctly separated halves. The one which we think of as "English” is the early Tudor, Elizabethan, Jacobean,. Cotswold, English Cottage, or whatever we call it. The other, which might be classified as the house that Jones and Wren built, we simply label Georgian, whether it was built by Jones in the days of Charles I or by an Adam brother in the days of George III.

THE PERIOD STYLES

The first type of house is built of stone, or of open timber filled in with stucco or with nogging of brick. Other characteristics are unpainted materials, multiple roofs covered with stone or slate surmounted by tall chimneys, usually ending in a row of ornamental chimney-pots. Gables are a continuation of the side walls, and there are always casement windows and dark wood trim within.

The second type of house is evenly balanced — therefore formal. It is built of smooth-faced stone or brick, with quoins of marble or of heavier stone at the corners, and with a hip or double gabled roof, or one that is hidden behind a balustrade. The chimneys are plain and the dormers rise from the roof without relation to the side walls.

Serious delving into history is far beyond the scope of this book, but as the family story of the English Periods is that of'our own, it seems to me that a family tree of some sort must be included. Instead of adding a thumb-nail chart at the end of the chapter, as I had intended, I might better write it now, so that it can show at a glance exactly where the styles belong that we unceasingly refer to in buying furniture for our houses to-day:

Manners, morals and magnificence all brought from the court of Louis XIV of France. Extravagance of Charles has one valuable result: abetted by the great fire of London, Sir Christopher Wren is given his chance to build St. Paul’s Cathedral, Temple Bar, and other buildings and churches of permanent beautification to England.

James IPs daughter Mary and Dutch son-in-law William take his place, 1688.

Home influence. Domestic virtues. Furniture brought from Holland is very refined. Vaseshaped splat and cabriole leg and other Dutch curved outlines later tagged with Anne’s name.

Anne unprotestingly accepts the style of William. Needlework seems to be her absorbing hobby.

To these are attributed "Georgian” architecture, with the creation of which they had almost as little to do as Anne with her furniture. In the reign of George II and George III, furniture to suit the style of houses created by Jones and Wren am made by the greatest of English cabinetmakers — Chippendale, Hepplewhite and Sheraton — supplemented by the architects Adam. Under George III we sever our family relationship with England.

Victoria included merely to complete the outline.

THE PERIOD STYLES

Just why we draw a mental dividing-line somewhere between William and Mary, and George the Second, is not very clear. And yet for more than two hundred years we have continued to look upon the classic Georgian Period as the sole ancestress of our New England and Southern Colonial twins, and to call every Period from the days of the early Saxons to the reign of Anne — or even the first George — "English.”

And yet our earliest Colonial houses and furniture come straight from the Periods of James I, Charles I, and Cromwell — in short, they are pure Jacobean!

Our early Colonial chairs — those with ladder backs and rush seats, which we think of as native American, are really Elizabethan.

At the moment, quite apart from tracing our Colonial lineage, our interest in English houses has been fanned into flame. Starting in Pennsylvania, where beautiful native stone naturally encouraged the use of it, modern Tudor and Jacobean houses are now building from one end of the country to the other, because there is no type of house which so thoroughly appeals to a man. (See Plate 24.)

It is impossible, therefore, to begin the outline of the English house at a later period than that in which the father of Elizabeth was King, and whichever of his wives we might choose was rather temporary Queen, of England.

At the beginning of the Tudor Period, the great dwelling was still the Keep. This was a feudal fortress in which the lord of the castle lived — surrounded by innumerable retainers and servants — in a stronghold protected by a moat and drawbridge and all the other defensive devices of the times — and in barbarous discomfort.

The central feature of the interior was the huge highceilinged, church-vaulted room called the Hall. In this the family, the retainers and servants all took their meals together; the family sitting at a long table on the dais and the servants at benches ranged beyond and below. At night the lord and his lady withdrew to their private apartments.

where they slept in a great bed curtained on all four sides; but their household, both men and women, as well as the dogs, slept on the rush-covered floor of the hall.

When Henry VIII heard of the comfortable furnishings of the Italians — Anne Boleyn is supposed to have carried the news — he not only imported hangings and furniture, but invited Italian architects and painters and craftsmen to England. Unfortunately, this turned out rather badly. The English workmen resented the rivalry of foreigners so strenuously that the highly trained and cultivated Italians were all sent home. Utterly ignorant and unskilled English labor botched along through the reigns of Henry, Edward, and Mary, to the day when Elizabeth and the dawn of culture made their simultaneous appearance on England’s hitherto unenlightened throne.

WHEN ELIZABETH WAS QUEEN

Strongholds being no longer necessary, the English country house that still serves as a model of homelike hospitality, was one of the first objects to appear in the broad daylight of the Elizabethan age.

The coming of trade routes which brought commodities of all varieties — as well as wealth — to England gave an incentive to the more comfortable furnishing of English houses. Many walls were still of bare stone, with coarsely woven arras hung over the door openings; but gradually finer tapestries were imported from Flanders and used as embellishments. The rushes with which the floors had been strewn were swept out, and woven floor- coverings laid down, and the decencies of living gradually developed.

Paneling became the universal fashion. High wainscots were built of dark oak, either in small squares or flat carving, or in linen-fold design. Half-circle arches supported on uprights were carved in almost every space big enough to hold one or more of them. Doorways were as ornate as the chimney-pieces. The open vaulting was replaced by the more homelike Italian ceilings, which were flat and finished in

THE PERIOD STYLES

hard stucco with low-relief geometrical designs of squares and circles.

The furniture of the period was enormously large in scale, and completely covered with carving that was crude in detail, but effective. Furniture for the best rooms, as already noted, was made of oak, though sometimes the kitchens and houses of the farmers were furnished with "deal” (fir or pine). And partitions were put up between the family and the animals. Beds and chests were the outstanding articles of furniture.

The beds were ponderous and as ornamented as carving could make them. The headboard was much like that of the chimney-pieces and supported the head of the canopy. The foot was held upon two ornate posts that ballooned in the center. Chests were used as seats as well as for storage space. Other articles were limited to "bread and cheese” cupboards, chairs, and huge refectory tables with bulbous legs and plain underframing close to the floor. (Fig. 74.) A few of the chairs brought from Italy had upholstered seats and backs.

ELIZABETHAN TABLE LEG

ELIZABETHAN SEAT

JACOBEAN CHEST

and a few replicas of these were made in England. The typical chair or bench of the period, however, was entirely of oak. (Fig. 75.)

AS THE ELIZABETHAN PERIOD APPLIES TO US

The point that we should take note of is that Elizabethan furniture, for instance, is suitable only in houses or rooms of great size. It is large in scale and gives a general effect of magnificence, but it is often strikingly crude in detail. Therefore, if your problem is the furnishing of a barnlike room — quite possibly the actual interior of a barn turned into a living-room — Elizabethan furniture would be as good a choice as you could make; but in rooms of medium or small space, beware of its overpowering exaggeration.

The colors of the period included quantities of browns, the deeper shades of red, greens, and blues, all of which gave an effect that was richly sombre; it was not gloomy, however, because window-glass was becoming more plentiful, and though the panes were very small, they were used in increasing quantities, held in place with strips of lead. Windows grew larger and larger, until in some rooms they filled the entire side of a wall. In fact, such buildings as Hardwick Hall were said to have "more glass than wall.”

The intention of the Elizabethan house-design was hos-

THE PERIOD STYLES

pitality. A beautiful example of this spirit is the entrance to Montacute, over which is inscribed "And your’s my friends.”

THE JACOBEAN PERIOD

When James of Scotland came to the throne, he brought about no radical changes in the style of Elizabeth, excepting that ornament was checked and all needless waste of material discouraged. The gables on the houses grew higher and stiffer, ornamental parapets disappeared, furniture became increasingly rigid, and there was a gradual elimination of the balloon designs that had been admired by Elizabeth.

James I, however, was greatly in favor of armchairs of Italian design with crossed legs and cushion. An interesting feature is found in the turned legs, and in the stretchers of chairs and tables, with blocks set in the joining — precisely like that of the gate-leg and butterfly table of Colonial days. (Fig. 77.) The settle or armchair with back that folds over the arm and makes a table is sold by all makers of Colonial reproductions. (See round-backed settle, Plate 43.)

The important architectural event of the early Jacobean Period, which includes the reign of Charles I, was the return of Inigo Jones from Italy and his creation of the Classic English house that was later to be known as Georgian — though Jones had lain in his grave for more than half a century before the first George was crowned.

THE COMMONWEALTH

The influence of Cromwell is of especial importance to us because it is from this Period of dour austerity that the point of view of our Puritan settlers, no less than

JACOBEAN PROTOTYPE OF COLONIAL GATE-LEG TABLE

our early New England architecture and furniture, came. Our early colonists were imbued with the asceticism of the Cromwellian creed; mortification of the flesh in the form of privation and discomfort was looked upon as virtue, and every form of ease and comfort was sin. The narrow rushseated ladder-backed chairs, the hard board beds, the wooden settles and benches and wooden-seated chairs were quite possibly chosen not so much from necessity as from aspiration to virtue. Our earliest Colonial houses, though often defined as "Tudor,” were completely Cromwellian-Jacobean. There was nothing of the Tudor family’s self-indulgence in the scrupulousness of their intention.

MODERN USE OF THE OLD ENGLISH STYLE

Before leaving the subject of the Tudor and Jacobean Periods, it may be well to say something about their descendants in America to-day. For a moment, therefore, let us turn from the days of Cromwell to those of the present, and consider briefly the characteristics that are essential to an interpretation of old English style in a modern house.

The great beauty of the "English” house is its sincerity and its effect of spacious and friendly comfort. Its honest use of material is for the purpose of durability. Wherever possible, its choice is for local stone and slate, and its color is supplied by means of natural unpainted material which time increasingly beautifies. Its form, which is of direct Tudor ancestry, includes what might be defined as the "home-making quality,” and it accepts every type of interior decoration and furnishing that is simple and also direct.

In other words, the ballyhoo house is diametrically opposed to the first principle of charm, which is repose. On the other hand, the too formal house is opposed to the quality of inviting comfort. To define the elements that produce the effect of restfulness and of relaxing ease, there is no better example than the perfect old English house. Therefore, unless

THE PERIOD STYLES

abominably designed with too steep a roof, too narrow and too few windows and other architectural exaggerations, or unless the assembling of its materials is harshly handled, it quite naturally gives the impression of permanence, stability, and comfort that the cultivated man dreams of when he thinks of "home.” In its distorted form, it expresses too sharp angles, eccentric what-is-its, and no-reason-wherefores! Its outside is chopped into, and its inside is cut up into a

CHARLES I CHAIR

QUEEN ANNE CHAIR

lot of little stalls or compartments in which there is neither grace nor ease.

If your house is to be huge, you can have as many divisions as you please; but if the space of your house is small, you only make it smaller by cutting it into samples. Screened partitions around the stairs in a little hall, an ingle-nook or other stuttering obstruction is equally destructive to comfort and repose.

And now let us turn back again to the end of Cromwell, the rigors of whose control ended in anarchy and the consequent Restoration of the Stuarts. Charles the Second be-

came King. He and his brother James, who followed him, exchanged Cromwell’s stiff discomfort for French gildings and luxuries — to say nothing of profligacies. Many French objects of furniture in the overgilded and ornate Period known as Baroque were brought over from the Court of Louis XIV by the King and his favorites. The end of the Period developed the Flemish chair with turned frame, scrolled stretcher, and tall ornamental back elaborately framing an inset of cane. (Fig. 79.)

Other furniture of the Period included chairs and tables and chests supported on trestles with curved stretchers and legs inset with turned trumpets and inverted cups. Drawers had long drop-handles. There was much square or octagonal or variously outlined paneling without carving. Ovals and rounds and all imaginable patterns of spindles and drops were split and glued on as the decorations of otherwise plain surfaces.

WILLIAM AND MARY ARRIVE

When Charles II had died, and James his brother had given up his succession in order to enjoy the pleasures of life

WILLIAM AND MARY HIGHBOY

CABINET

THE PERIOD STYLES

in France, the daughter of James and her Dutch husband, William III, Prince of Orange, removed themselves and all their belongings to England. According to accounts, they must have left little that was transportable behind them. This moving was a most notable event in the history of English — and American — house furnishing.

The French importations by Charles and James had made not a ripple beyond the gates of the royal palaces. The typical English houses continued to be supplied with squareframed furniture made of the solid oak of England, all of it — change its trimming as one might! But no sooner was the furniture of William and Mary unloaded than the news of its curved shape, its smooth-polished wood, spread like wildfire! Every lady in the countryside, no less than in London, longed for a sitting-room furnished with bandylegged elegance. The chairs and cabinets of William and Mary — which we know by the name of "Queen Anne” — ^were light and fine and entirely different in outline and in surface texture from any English furniture which had gone before. (Fig. 80.)

The style, however, that bears the name of William and Mary is illustrated in Figure 81. Another very Jacobean William-and-Mary cabinet is shown in Figure 82.

The next, and most important, Period of furniture was that of the three great cabinet-makers of the eighteenth century. And since the designs of these three men (to which must be added the designs of Adam and our own Duncan Phyfe) are the models of excellence, it seems rather necessary to consider each of them, and their styles, at some length.

CHIPPENDALE

To Thomas Chippendale must be given the same eminence as a furniture-maker that is held by Inigo Jones as an architect — in that he was the creator of a new interpretation in close accord with Classic style, and in the beautification of a new medium — mahogany. Son of a woodcarver, and him-

self a wood-carver, it was natural that his furniture should display especial skill of this art.

While none of the experts proclaim him a copyist, they do agree that he took ideas wherever he found them, but developed them into a style that was his own. His outstanding model was the style of the two curving Louis of France — XIV and XV — but upon this inspiration he constructed every motif that chance brought his way.

Amusingly, perhaps, it was Chippendale’s mastery of Chinese design that brought him his greatest popularity — especially in mirrors. And yet that every Chinese design which was epidemic in the middle of the eighteenth century should be without exception tagged "Chippendale,” seems hardly fair to the many notable designers of the day. Edwards and Darley, Thomas Johnson, the Halfpennys, Matthias Lock, Ince and Mayhew, and others, had been turning London into a veritable Chinatown before Chippendale made his famous discovery of Chinese motif.

But none of these are ever mentioned as having given Chippendale a single idea. The inspiration credited by all his biographers is that of Sir William Chambers, the favorite architect of George 1. The rickety bamboo furniture which the architect persisted in having constructed for whichever of his clients would let him, gave the cabinet-maker the inspiration for the fragile, perforated fretwork introduced into his chair-backs and table-rims.

A certain few of Chippendale’s biggest pieces, especially the state beds, are overcanopied to the point of annoyance; but his fretwork-rimmed tables and China cabinets, and the long-beaked bird amidst the scrolled "C’s” of his name ornamenting his gilt mirror frames, are dreams of Celestial loveliness.

The Chippendale furniture that we all know best, perhaps, includes his ball and claw-foot chairs with broad-shouldered backs, his center-columned piecrust tables, and his secretary book-cases. His book. The Gentleman’s and Cabinet-Maker’s 'Directory, was published in 1754. A

THE PERIOD STYLES

2J3

modern reproduction of it is to be found in nearly every public library.

MESSRS. A. HEPPLEWHITE AND COMPANY

Chippendale had long been at the top of the ladder of fame when two younger men, whose names are invariably bracketed with his, mounted the rungs beside him. About thirty-five years after Chippendale’s book appeared, Hepplewhite — or, rather, Messrs. A. Hepplewhite and Company — published The Cabinet-Maker and Upholsterer’s Guide, or Repository of Designs for Every Article of Household Furniture in the Newest and Most Approved Taste.

It is rather the popular belief that Chippendale stands head and shoulders above all other eighteenth-century cabinet-makers — but many students and collectors declare Hepplewhite a truer artist than Chippendale — and Sheraton the truest of the three.

Chippendale must often have worked with his tongue in his cheek and with commercial advantage in his mind. He was no stranger to the desire to create novelties in the hope that they might sell quickly. Hepplewhite also strove for novelty, but not at the risk of departing from that motto at the beginning of his book: *'To unite elegance and utility, and blend the useful with the agreeable.” Hepplewhite was a hundred and fifty years in advance of the modernists of to-day, one of whose multiple aims is precisely — in almost the same words — the beautification of utility.

The actual motto of the firm was "Delicacy and Elegance.” These two words expressed the intention of every Hepplewhite design. Delicacy was interpreted in refinement of scale and symmetry. Elegance was interpreted in grace and exquisite detail of workmanship. The scale of some of hlepplewhite’s furniture is so small as to be almost miniature — and always it is extremely feminine.

Chippendale’s furniture, while fragile compared with Tudor and Jacobean massiveness, is nevertheless stable enough

CHIPPENDALE ARMCHAIR, CABRIOLE LEG

HEPPLEWHITE CHAIR, VASE SPLAT

to be reassuring to the most masculine. (Fig. 83.) But the average satinwood, slender-legged Flepplewhite chair is the typical man’s anathema! (Fig. 84.)

SHERATON LADDER TABLE

THE PERIOD STYLES

Hepplewhite centered his ornamentation on the backs of chairs, which were usually shield-shaped hollow frames, fitted with an elaborate skeleton design of loops and urns or Prince of Wales feathers. He strove to bring lightness and color into rooms that had hitherto been somber, by the use of light-colored satinwood and painted ornament.

SHERATON

The Story of Sheraton is that of many of the other great characters of the world. He belonged to the tribe of characteristically simple, self-effacing men, absorbed in their visions, and rather fanatically hoping to endow humanity with their gift of knowledge or beauty.

As poor and unknown as Messrs. A. Hepplewhite and Company were rich and recognized; utterly incapable of understanding the commercial showmanship of Chippendale, Sheraton worked all his life in poverty. The fame which was given him by posterity was not dreamed of by the customers of his day. In 1791 he published also a book. The Cabinet-Makers’ and Upholsterers’ Drawing-Book, com-

CHIPPENDALE CABRIOLE CHAIR

CHINESE CHIPPENDALE CHAIR

CHINESE CHIPPENDALE, FRETTED STYLE

pleted in four volumes. The first quarter of this work is a discourse on geometry, perspective drawing, and the Classic Orders — a dissertation on the necessity for a thorough knowledge of these principles by all who aspired to his profession.

And yet his designs give but small idea of the beauty of the actual furniture. Even in his own drawings, many

of them look overtrimmed and fussy, whereas in the actual rendering the effect was quite the contrary. He delighted in dark-brown- toned mahogany inlaid with every variety of light-wood marquetry, and often embellished with painting. But most of all he delighted in the ingenious contrivances of "harlequin” furniture that, seeming to be a simple table, turned into a library ladder (Fig. 85) , a dressing-stand or writing-desk when the table-top was lifted. Sheraton’s dexterity in camouflaging an object of homely utility should be more than ever valuable in this day of crowded space, when the flat-in-one-room idea is rapidly drawing near. In the Cabinet-Makers^ and Upholsterers^ Dratving-Book are endless suggestions for roll-back tambour screens that disclose you know not what.

Personally I like best Sheraton’s beautiful mahogany secretary-bookcases with tambour doors, and his sideboards with little, if any, inlay. Tambour slides, by the way, were made of very small split reeds of wood glued on material. Closed they look like reeded doors, open they slide out of the way into the recesses made to hold them.

In contour, Sheraton’s furniture is straight-lined and simple, relying for its beauty on perfect proportion.

THE STYLES OF THE GREAT THREE

A few of the characteristics by which we classify the styles of these three men are as follows:

The wood associated with Chippendale was Spanish mahogany of darkest color. It was dense and heavy, and aged beautifully. It was always embellished with carving. His chairs are often cabriole-legged with balland-claw feet. The arms are out-curving, and the supporting post is back of the front legs of the chair, while the rails of the arms terminate in a spreadout and rolled-under rest for the hand, as seen in Figure 86.

The "Chinese” chairs and many later models had straight legs. (Figs. 87, 88, 89.) He never used inlays of other woods. He often used gilding — on his mirrors, for instance — but he never used paint. He was first and foremost a wood-carver, and a wood-carver he remained. For the fine fretwork of his Chinese designs he used wafer-thin woods glued three-ply, with grain running in opposite directions.

Chinese Chippendale chairseats were often of wood as flat as a table-top. The upholstery of the other chairs and sofas was sometimes finished on the outside of the seat-frame with nail-heads (Figs. 90 and 91) , but usually the seat fitted into a frame (as in Figures 83, 86, and 89).

In his gilt mirrors and girandoles, as well as in many commodes and other mahogany pieces,

Chippendale followed the curved

CHIPPENDALE WING CHAIR

lines of Louis XIV — especially the short curve that suggested the C of his name.

Sheraton followed the straight lines of Louis XVL

A supposed test whereby one may recognize the Hepplewhite chair-back is that its curve is unbroken at the top. The Sheraton chair always has a shoulder set in, or a bar across the center. (Figs. 92 and 93.) The Hepplewhite arms are usually set back, as are those of the Queen Anne and Chippendale chair. The arms said to be characteristic of the Shera-

HEPPLEWHITE

ton chairs are recognizable as being a continuation of the front legs of the chairs. This feature — when present — ^marks him quite easily. But Sheraton often made a set-back arm. (Fig. 94.)

liepplewhite had a great fancy for satinwood and for painted decoration. Sheraton embellished his furniture with inlay and sometimes with painting. Some Fiepplewhite chairs had cane seats with cushions tied by tasseled cords to the frame.

Sheraton’s chair-legs were turned and often reeded, but he also made occasional straight legs with or without the spade foot. Fiepplewhite preferred the straight leg with a spade foot (Fig. 95) , but often made turned ones, and on oc-

2S9

casions a modified cabriole. Hepplewhite very much liked upholstery fastened on with plain bossed nail-heads. But Chippendale used this upholstery also. The "pie-crust” cabriole-columned tip-table is typically Chippendale, as is also Chinese and Gothic. Fret-carving is typically his.

Glass in the doors of Hepplewhite book-cases was often set in brass; the ends of his sideboards were typically concave, while Sheraton’s were usually convex. (Fig. 96.)
