SHERATON HEPPLEWHITE

borrowed from the other; but the list indicates the characteristics by which we classify various pieces of furniture, though the chances are slight of their ever having seen the work-benches of any of them. You never can tell, however, whether one of the pieces that have come down to you or that you buy here or there — may not be one of theirs, or at least made by one of their contemporaries.

Innumerable pieces of Hepplewhite’s work came to America (Fig. 97), and a certain number of Sheraton’s. The best of Chippendale’s are said to have been held in England — but who knows where or how many they are!

And to go back to the subject of Labels: An authentic piece, if it is at all a good one, is a treasure of value. That

is undeniable! But, on the other hand, if a piece be the work of a nameless artist who worked with the same love of his craft that brought the greater names to fame, it is not less beautiful as an object to treasure. A beautiful thing is a beautiful thing, even though its label be lost. Form, balance, proportion, fineness — if you live with beautiful pieces, you know. If you are not sure of this, and want to know what is meant by perfect color and texture, go to a museum and look at the best examples of whichever type of furniture you are interested in. Go and look at them again and again. Make drawings of them — even though you can’t draw. Because, by putting their outlines on paper, you fix them in your mind. If no museum is near you, there are many notable books.’^ A huge one. The furniture Treasury, by Wallace Nutting (two volumes) , is a collection of five thousand photographic reproductions of the best furniture in the United States. No one could look through these volumes carefully and attentively without having a very definite idea of the meaning of beauty and personality in the furniture of an American house.

♦ One who is interested in any of the great Period styles should make a special study of that epoch. Books on each Period in each country are to be found in all art museums and public libraries. Or they may be bought of all the larger booksellers, or of William Helburn, Inc., of New York, publisher of books of Art, Architecture and Decoration, who will gladly advise on special books or a course of study.

SHERATON SIDEBOARD

THE PERIOD STYLES

THE ADAM BROTHERS

The Adam brothers were architects, not furniture-makers, but they designed much furniture to fit their interior architecture, which was classically extreme.

Of the four brothers — John,

Robert, James, and William —

Robert Adam was the one of outstanding importance. An ardent lover of classic architecture, he was inspired with the idea of preserving what examples might remain of Greek and Roman dwellings. He therefore went with several assistant draftsmen to Dalmatia to study the ruins of the Palace of Diocletian, and although arrested as a spy, he managed to leave the country with enough detailed information to reconstruct the building completely in a series of drawings that were published in book form.

After the publication of his book he was appointed Architect to the King and took his brother James into partnership. Their work is easily recognized because of the extreme refinement and delicacy of the classic motives, rendered most typically in stucco. In effect an Adam room suggests a blend of the styles of Louis XVI and Pompeii, garnished with Wedgwood in the manner of Della Robbia.

Their most elaborate rooms look like the hall of Greek sculpture in every museum: Niches holding full-sized marble copies of the statues of the Pantheon and inset samples of Greek and Roman motifs. Ceilings coffered, and a pure Greek cornice. Smaller rooms had ceilings completely covered with

HEPPLEWHITE

secretary-bookcase

arabesques and inset with medallions of Wedgwood pottery, or paintings, or the inevitable plaster bas-reliefs of vases, griffins, and sheep skulls, trimmed round about with endless chains of garlands known as "husks.”

Furniture which they designed to match their interiors — but did not themselves manufacture — was very chaste in outline and very small in scale. It is invariably ornamented with acanthus-leaf designs, long and short-length flutes, and Greek motifs. Typical mantels are of white marble, with a rectagonal panel set in the center of its face, and with perpendicular grooves, called flutes, on either side. (Fig. 98.) Very often four or five of the flutes are stopped with inlays of contrasting marble — an effect that suggests the triglyphs of a Greek Doric frieze.

Adam design is always very precious and delicate. Every classic motive known is attenuated to the extreme of refinement. It was tremendously popular in America shortly before the World War — and for that matter it still is. In its entirety it is too fragilely intricate, too freezingly formal, for the typical American house; but a little of it is embellishing to any type of semi-formal room. An Adam mantel, or Adam doors with octagon panels, or arches or niches, or any of the details of their ornamentation added wherever taste suggests, are suitable.

Much of the Adam style is beautiful, dignified and distinguished. And yet, in its unskilled interpretation, it can become affectedly effeminate to a point where you feel that, like the heroines of Jane Austen, it is inclined to the "vapours!”

5. THE COLONIAL HOUSE

You know, and I know, and we all know perfectly

ADAM MANTEL

THE PERIOD STYLES

well that the Colonial house is — speaking as the dictionary — a house built prior to the American Revolution. But to most of us it is an affectation to call the thousands of whitepainted, green-shuttered frame houses of our villages and farms anything else.

A house of the Early — or Late — Republic means nothing to me whatsoever! Does it to you? To me every house built in the likeness of the Colonial houses of the late — or early — eighteenth century is ‘'Colonial.” Even the American Empire houses, and their furniture, built when we were doting on France and nursing our break with England, are Colonial — unless they are Georgian, which is even more sol Mansard, Neo-Gothic, mid-Victorian, General Grant, and Richardson Romanesque are various American styles that we went through in the interim between the Jacobean-Colonial-Georgian period of our early days and the triform completeness of its present renaissance.

There is so much about the Colonial house throughout this book that the few pages following are included merely to emphasize some differences of character in the typical styles that we call Colonial, no matter which their period or what their inspiration.

As noted elsewhere, our earliest truly Colonial houses of the North Atlantic seaboard were Puritan-plain, both inside and out. They were, moreover, pure Jacobean. On the outside they more or less duplicated the Scotch-Boardman house (Fig. 24). Inside, the walls were finished in rough plaster with all beams exposed. The big spinal beam (called the summer beam) crossed the ceiling from end to end, and the smaller rib-beams or joists were carried from the spine-beam to the frame of the walls. (Spine and ribs are not architectural terms, but I think they make the theory of the construction clear.) Often the underside of the second-story floor-boarding was left as it was, to make the ceiling of the first floor. Sometimes the spaces between the joists were plastered. The chimney-breast and the wall on either side were usually boarded over. There were always wide plank floors

and enormous fireplaces, with a heavy log set in the form of a lintel over the opening. Sometimes this beam was set flush with the wall, sometimes a portion of it protruded and served as a mantel-shelf.

The fireplaces in the kitchens had ovens built in at the side. Otherwise cooking was done over the open wood-fire. Sometimes one room, sometimes another was completely sealed in with wood. The old Capen house at Topsfield, Massachusetts, which is one of the historical and best-preserved examples of a seventeenth century house (it was built in 1683), has plastered walls in the parlor, and the open-beamed ceiling is whitewashed. The kitchen was sealed in with horizontally laid planks and windows with thirty panes.

Such houses as the John Ward house at Salem, the old part of the Whipple house at Ipswich, the Capen house and the Boardman house at Saugus, had full second stories; but in thousands of early American houses the bedrooms were little cubicles opening off the kitchen, or else they were unfinished attics, in which beds were set up among hangings of stored clothing and drying vegetables. There were, of course, no domestic conveniences in any of them. In short, the early American period house must be rather liberally adapted by any one intending to reproduce one now with an effect of charm — meaning that a modern range and electric ice-box as well as plenty of plumbing and closets must be tucked away behind doors conveniently near.

By the middle of the eighteenth century there were plenty of New England houses of size and architectural importance, such as the Hancock house in Boston; Cliveden in Germantown, Pennsylvania; the house of Jeremiah Lee at Marblehead, Massachusetts; and others which proclaim that their owners were provided with all the "elegance” known at that time to London. But since the character of the owner is inevitably expressed in his home, the typical New England houses remained (and still remain) rather Puritanical.

THE PERIOD STYLES

HOMES IN THE SOUTH WERE DIFFERENT

Meanwhile, in Maryland, Virginia, and especially in South Carolina, the planters of means — and there were many of them — ^lived on imposing estates in spacious houses built either of brick or of frame, with classic porticos or balconies with classic colonnades. Perhaps it was the friendliness of this balcony life, and of windows that opened full length, in contrast to the austerity of the closed shutters or pulled- down blinds of New England, which produced the impression of a wide difference between the spirit of the South and that of the North.

At all events, in the notable houses of the South, the rooms were large and high-studded, and the embellishment with Palladian, Jones, Wren, and Adam motives was lavishly applied. Classic cornices, pilasters, and pediments appeared in every principal room. Halls were often notable for the celebrated flying stairs. Beautiful mantels were either imported or copied by Colonial workmen, and furniture was — much of it — made by the notable cabinet-makers of London. The Southern planters also imported all varieties of stuffs, wall-papers and carpets. In fact, it was rather held against them by captious critics — among whom were many of their relatives in London — that they imported much that might quite easily have been made at home.

The object of this description is merely to point out the difference between the mode of life of the South and that of the North, because this difference still divides the personality of Northern and Southern houses to-day.

In New England the wives and daughters of the owners of impressive houses, quite likely, did much of the housework. In houses of average size, as often as not, they did all of it, and the most luxurious article of ease was a woodbacked armchair. The kitchen was the center of family life; the parlor was as uncomfortable as stiffness could make it and never used by the family except to sit in for, an hour or two on Sunday or for funerals or weddings. The parlor

of the Southern house was where the family spent their days when not on the veranda. Not only was the housework done by the slaves, but all the members of the family were waited on, hand and foot.

All of which means that in choosing a house of Northern or Southern type, you should consider the character that each connotes. The Southern Colonial house was as foreign to an expression of energy or thrift as the summer palace of a Sultan. Houses that are luxurious in style can not be run parsimoniously. Houses that are essentially formal can not depart from the exactions of that style. It is unsuitable, therefore, to choose a classic Georgian house, which is the expression of what the old writers always described as elegance, for the background of one who lives very simply — meaning without regard to form.

Whether in the North or the South or on the Mason and Dixon line precisely, the owner of classic marble floors neither scrubs nor polishes them. The George III tea service laid upon a Chinese Chippendale table is not carried in by the mistress of the house — nor by a servant who is lacking in the exactions of smartness or skill.

In the less pretentious Southern house, hospitality must be abundant, but the requirements of etiquette are not exacting if the service be adequate and courteous in intention. The simple type of Dutch or Puritan Colonial house, however, has no exactions of service at all — beyond the stipulation that if there be any, then let it be conducted with neatness and dispatch.

The conclusions are obvious: If you run your house to perfection in its every detail, then no background is more suitable than that of a formal example of Georgian. But if you are likely to fall short of perfection, then don’t choose the one background that is more critical than any other in the world.

To have a perfect Georgian house is impossible on a small scale, because rooms of Georgian perfection should have a certain effect of space.

THE PERIOD styles

In its uttermost austerity, the Georgian house lacks homelike appeal, but in its chintz-and-flower-filled rendering, no other style can ever, in my own heart, take its place. But no compromise will do. It must be perfection — or it must be something else. Bad Georgian is like a misshapen Parthenon! On the other hand, a sweet, ordinary, every-day semi-Colonial or semi-French, semi-Italian, or any other type can be made into utterly contenting surroundings.

OUR PERSONAL LIKES AND DISLIKES

Some one said the other day that one must be born to the Georgian style and to the Russian language in order to be acclimated to either. Perhaps! Perhaps this is also true of all periods. There must be an inherent affinity between ourselves and the homes we are impelled to select.

It is inevitable that we should each of us feel a sympathy for a certain period inherited from American, English, French

TYPICAL HIGHBOY OF QUEEN ANNE ANCESTRY

OLD AMERICAN PINE HIGHBOY - QUEEN ANNE STYLE

AMERICAN MAHOGANY HIGHBOY, CHIPPENDALE STYLE

Italian, Dutch, Spanish, German — or whatever other ancestors — that we can never feel for any other periods, unless we are so racelessly Modern that we care for nothing that antecedes today.

And in accumulating this innate preference (or its modernistic lack) we achieve a background that is truly and personally ours. Furniture manufacturers probably think that they launched a return of Colonial style! But it is quite as likely that the wide appeal and suitability of Colonial furnishing is due to the fact that hundreds of thousands of Americans are of Colonial ancestry. Colonial houses, therefore, comfort our sense of well-being as though bestowing upon us the blessings of our great-great-grandparents.

The examples given on these pages (Figs. 99, 100, and 101) are direct copies of Queen Anne. The mahogany highboy (Fig. 102) is in the best style of Chippendale, as is also the mahogany desk (Fig. 103). Louis XV is distinctly traceable in the rosewood and horsehair armchair that was so characteristic of our later Victorian — or General Grant — Period. (Fig. 104.)

WINDSOR ARMCHAIR, FAN BACK

THE PERIOD STYLES

THE FURNITURE OF OUR OWN DUNCAN PHYFE

Having somewhat elaborated on the English cabinetmakers, it will hardly do to leave out our own preeminent Duncan Phyfe, who is rated very little behind — if not on a par with — the greatest of the Englishmen. The country of his birth was Scotland. His family migrated to America and settled among the Dutch of Albany when Phyfe was sixteen.

TYPICAL COLONIAL DESK

VICTORIAN CHAIR EVOLVED FROM LOUIS XV

DUNCAN PHYFE CHAIR WITH LYRE BACK

He learned the trade of cabinet-maker and wood-carver in Albany. When he was still in his early twenties, he moved to New York and opened a shop of his own. He achieved immediate success — much as Chippendale had in London. Like Chippendale he took infinite pains in the selection of his mahogany and in the stability of his construction. His designs are more like those of Sheraton than of Chippendale, but he too perfected a Chippendale type of pedestal table with dog’s paws in place of the Chippendale ball-and-claw feet. And just as Chippendale and Sheraton had gone to

DUNCAN PHYFE SOFA

Louis XIV and Louis XVI for much of their inspiration, Phyfe interpreted the Directoire Period of France almost exclusively.

Certain characteristics of his are very easily recognized, notably his use of the lyre (with strings of brass or whalebone) set into the backs of chairs, the arms of sofas (Figs. 105 and 106) , or the pedestals of consoles. The acanthus leaf is on nearly every piece of furniture he made — not curled forward, as in the Corinthian Order, but laid flat and in basrelief. The legs of his pedestal tables were embellished with the acanthus leaf laid along the upper surface tapering toward the foot (Fig. 108) , the dog’s paw of which was made of brass. On other pieces of furniture he substituted a carved eagle in place of the lyre.

THE PERIOD STYLES

Another characteristic of many of the chair and sofa frames is the reeded edge. He also made a most interesting form of trestletable, with the top resting on four posts which rise from a platform supported on concave dog-footed legs. (Fig. 108.)

While Phyfe’s furniture is extremely refined, it is strongly built. There is an insatiable demand for anything that could be attributed to him and his hundred or more assistants. Perhaps this is because his name is the rage. Perhaps, again, his name is the rage because his furniture fits perfectly into every imaginable type of American house which, rightly or wrongly, is known to us all as Colonial.

Of less concern in such a book as this, but of equal importance to students of American craftsmanship, are the beautiful examples of such masters as John Goddard of Newport, William Savery, the Philadelphia Quaker, and their associates and contemporaries.

Many critics consider William Savery the most worthy successor to Chippendale that America produced. And Goddard’s block-front secretaries, highboys, and other pieces served as models for generations of conscientious craftsmen. At the moment we seem to care little for carving and nothing for pieces of substantial weight, which is, perhaps, the reason why the eighteenth-century cabinet maker in highest favor at the moment is Duncan Phyfe.

XIV

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING

T'^HE definition of decoration is somewhat like the age of Anne and the size of a piece of cheese. On occasions interior decoration assumes the structural function of interior architecture; at other times it is limited to the making of curtains, the choosing of chintzes, and the color of paint.

Having in a previous chapter rather criticized the pretentions of a certain type of decorator, I would like to make it clear that criticism of the majority of decorators is in no way intended. There are uncountable numbers of them who have knowledge, originality, and taste; but it must also be repeated that those who are bad can be very bad indeed.

If the professional decorator were obliged to qualify for a diploma, as a graduate of the schools of law or medicine is required to do, or if he (or she) had to serve a long apprenticeship as student and draftsman, as an architect must do, that would give us a definite standard. But since every one is as free to open a decorating establishment as any other type of shop, the name "decorator” does not of itself furnish any assurance of qualification. This statement is easily confirmed by the veritable epidemic of young women who, without training or study or the least evidence of talent, seem to believe that, by gift of sex alone, they are qualified to print "Interior Decorator” on their cards, and conduct under their hats what sample-matching business they may.

Their ambitions are perhaps quite all right, but their deficiencies explain why you should put your house into the

hands of one in whose knowledge you have confidence. There is no point in choosing any other kind. Moreover, it may be as well to avoid the type of decorator whose talent is limited to the persistent repetition of a single idea that expresses only herself (or — but not so often — ^himself) .

THE PERSONALITY OF THE OWNER IS OF FIRST IMPORTANCE

It must never be forgotten that in every house of appeal the owner’s personality is the one of importance. If the house expresses the architect or the decorator and not the owner, then its personality is a song out of tune. John Singer Sargent, the greatest portrait-painter of his day, always interpreted the character and personality of his sitter. In the same way a decorator worthy of the name interprets the personality and character of the individual houseowner for whom he (or she) creates the setting.

A short time ago a notable architect and an equally notable decorator finished a house for a newly-rich man and his wife. As an example of the coldest, most restrained period of Georgian architecture, it was supremely beautiful. And had its owners been people of cold, intellectual formality, the work would have been flawless. But the wife was a plump, red-cheeked, enthusiastic, busy little woman, who liked to bake her own pies and churn her own butter, and who was really happiest with a dust-cap over her hair and a dustcloth in her hands. The husband was also fat and jolly, and his idea of ease was sitting in shirt-sleeves and carpet-slippers. Consequently, they were unbecoming to the house, and the house was unbecoming to them.

Houses that exact formality, not merely of manner but of outlook, are the palatial types of the French Louis, the houses of Jones and Wren and Adam in England, the formal type of Italian "Villa,” and the Georgian or severely classical Colonial.

None could, feel at home in the formal restraint of these periods who are not innately formal and temperamentally

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 275

reserved. For instance, if you are uncomfortable unless you put on evening clothes for dinner; if you habitually sit straight in your chair, with hands folded in your lap; if you have naturally a certain unbendingness of spine, a Calvin Coolidge type of reserve, no matter how deep your sympathies may be; if, in other words, the exacting, fastidious refinements or the Puritanical principles of your ancestors are ingrained in your nature, then the wood-lined rooms of France or the spacious coldness of Georgian or the Classic type of Italian will doubtless be becoming to your personality, and you to theirs.

But if you and your family’s traditions have come from the unhampering life of the "wide open spaces,” and if you like to sit down at dinner without preparations other than washing your face and hands and smoothing your hair, your house should properly have an effect of straightforward, uncluttered, but homelike hospitality; plenty of broad windows, heavy-timbered beams, oak wainscots, or rough-plastered walls, and generally comfortable and unspoilable furniture of Spain or Italy or Old England. You could equally well choose Normandy French, Peasant Spanish, Farmhouse Italian, or Primitive Colonial, using these names merely for descriptive convenience (in no way intending them to be meticulously exact) . Or you could choose a house of any nonperiod of "adapted” American. It could be of important size, and filled with every object of greatest luxury, but it should not suggest the use-me-not-roughly quality of the styles listed in the classic category.

MANY WAYS OF APPROACHING THE DECORATION AND FURNISHING OF A HOUSE

To follow a Period is the least personal and the least difficult way to insure a safe result — granted sufficient wherewithal. In its most commonplace phase this method consists in straight reproduction. Select this room or that house and copy it — ^if not exactly, at least approximately. Furnish it

with examples — either real or copied — of its Period, and carry this idea throughout. The process is as mechanical as following a recipe in a cookery book, and the result is, at worst, a pale, sad failure, or at best a success much like that of a well-baked pan of biscuits.

The haphazard way, while not likely to result in beauty, may quite well have personality. This way, as its name suggests, is simply putting whatever you happen to have into whichever rooms you happen to occupy, and letting them all agree or disagree, as may happen to suit them.

The personal method of furnishing is to build each room on those objects that belong to each of us; things that would subtract from the sum of our personality were they taken away. What is really essential — granting that we have a fair amount of taste — is that we shall put together the things we like; the things that we are used to; the things that express us; so that our homes shall be a true expression of our own taste, character, and personality.

And yet, if beauty is our object, it is necessary — ^usually — that we select those of our possessions which will be becoming to the unity of a decorative scheme. A certain few persons who have for years been eliminating and collecting may be able to move, or to do over the house, and make no change in their possessions; but for them a book such as this is of no use, anyway!

THE BURLAP OF SENTIMENT

Most of US who are moving or refurnishing, however, are surrounded with old familiar objects that are completely wrapped in the burlap of sentiment, so that we do not see them as they are at all. For example, I am told by no less an authority than Mr. Frank Alvah Parsons that mahogany doors set in a white Georgian wall are very bad. And I feel that my expression is very like that of a terrier that has been told "No!” As a matter of fact, I have no idea of putting mahogany doors in a house of my own — at the mo-

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 277

ment, I happen to like doors that match their trim much better.

But the memory of a house of long ago is still — notwithstanding my faith in Mr. Parsons — utterly beautiful. It had two drawing-rooms architecturally alike, with creampainted walls and Ionic pilasters supporting a perfectly proportioned cornice. And it had hugely tall mahogany doors — with matched grain down their centers. The furniture was Sheraton — I have some of it still — and over two consoles were lovely gilt mirrors about six feet high by thirty inches wide, topped with fish-tailed horns of plenty. They are in my study now.

But in contrast to these few objects which I know are beautiful, are other things that I have difficulty in dissociating from sentiment. Perhaps they are good — perhaps they are not good at all. But I like them. And home would not be home to me without them — nor would others recognize my rooms as really mine, were I to put newly picked-up acquaintances in their places (which I have no intention of doing) .

Other possessions required no effort of renunciation to send to auction, or leave in attic or stable loft. Perhaps paintings of cows and old grizzled-faced men with pipes, and fullsized marble copies of Vatican statuary might fit somewhere, some day — but I doubt it!

Statuary is extreme, I admit. Nevertheless, it is necessary to look at your own equivalent statuary possessions with a reasonable degree of criticism. If the house is big enough to allow you a treasure museum in which you can put grandmother’s marble-topped walnut furniture, and grandfather’s portrait — which no member of the family would have, because it was such a bad one — and the desk you had as a child, and the nursery mantel ornaments of cherubs in a field of forget-me-nots, and all the Christmas and birthday this-and-thats given to you by — you can’t even remember whom — that is one thing. But to put them all into a room you would make beautiful, is another — and rarely feasible.

Therefore, you must select — not necessarily the things that a professional appraiser would put the highest value on, but the things that would grieve you to part with. Take these things as the nucleus of each room into which they are to go, and then build a background that is becoming to them. This is the perfect way; in fact, the only way to make the atmosphere of your house express YOU.

Meaning that if you have a small Sheraton desk and two Hepplewhite chairs, a pair of Sevres vases, a little Tanagra figure, a Ming Bowl, a Chantilly inkstand, and an Elizabethan table, you could fit all these things — barring the table — into a house that is Georgian, or French, or Colonial — or that is just a comfortable house. But the heavy table would be out of harmony. Not because its name is Elizabethan, but because it is out of scale. A small Elizabethan piece might fit perfectly, as we shall see when we come to the problems of Interior Furnishings. (See Index.)

THE PROCESS OF DECORATION

It is high time, however, to consider the process of decoration. Whether an interior is the work of an architect, of a decorator, of the owner alone, or of all three, there are four distinct steps in the completion of each room in every house :

First — ^The architectural embellishment or preparation for the decoration.

Second — Completion of all walls, ceilings and floors that have been left unfinished by the builder or architect, and all actual decoration of interior surfaces with color; also selection of lighting fixtures, hardware and mantels, when these have not been supplied by the architect.

Third — ^The selection and supplying of movable objects of furniture.

Fourth — ^Placing furniture in an inviting and convenient arrangement, and supplying the accessories of ornament and comfort.

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 279

Architectural embellishment of the interior was the subject of Chapter IX. The subject of this chapter is the completion and decoration of the immovable interior. Let us begin, therefore, with the walls.

THE WALLS

Leaving all thought of Periods out of the question, the texture and detail as well as color of the walls must be in harmony with the furnishings that are to be used. For instance, rough-surface walls must not be chosen for fine-surface furniture, although smooth (plaster) walls can be used as a background for crude furniture.

An overlarge and overhigh room as plain as the inside of a cardboard box would be unendurable. In a room of great size, there should be an ornamented ceiling or vaulting supported on brackets or columns, or at least an important cornice as well as hangings of some sort in the biggest wall spaces. Moreover, the necessity for decoration increases in proportion to size — especially to height. Roughness of wall surfaces may increase also in proportion to size of wall space.

The one warning to those having rough-finished walls is to beware of gobby surfaces or smeary mixtures of color. A sandy and rough- troweled finish is good as a background for very heavy, somewhat crude furniture, and for coarse textures of hangings. But the smaller the rooms, the smoother the walls must be. Those intended as background for eighteenth-century furniture mmt be smooth. Moreover, unpainted plaster, either rough or smooth, does not look well as a background for such furniture as that of Hepplewhite, Sheraton, or Duncan Phyfe. Smooth plaster that is painted is entirely proper, whether paneled or left plain.

The perfect background for furniture of fineness is wood paneling, such as we find in typical French rooms, or in rooms that are Georgian or Colonial (described in Chapter XIII). Wood-lined rooms are either waxed and left light, or acid-stained and filled. Or else they are painted in a solid

color — perhaps with moldings accentuated with a relieving shade. Bedrooms or small rooms designed in lighter vein have panels, the frames of which often are striped in several colors — a fashion that is rather more decorator-like than homelike. But if you like it, there is no objection, unless perhaps it looks somewhat tricked out and therefore self-conscious.

Beautiful wall-paneling can be made of plaster that is painted as though it were wood. It is fine in texture and can be rendered in every type of molding and carved ornament at much less cost than wood. There is an interesting Portland cement composition that imitates various woods — notably old black oak beams, and paneling of what looks like age-old pine. This last has an effect of worn-off paint that has real charm. The cost, set up, paneled and with surfaces completely finished is about $1.50 a square foot.

IF YOU CAN PAINT

Comparatively few houses of average size and cost have any decorated rooms, but if you can paint — really paint — then the variety of decorations that you can do are limited only by the amount of wall space and the versatility of your imagination. If you can’t, and you very much want a specially decorated room, you can of course give the commission to an artist — which is never quite the same as being able to do it yourself. Artists are temperamental. They paint (naturally) what they feel, which may or may not be what you want.

However, given a wood-embellished room with smooth plaster panels or insets (the kind of wall that must be decorated) , and a theme such as a tapestry, that is to occupy one wall, or a picture or panel that serves as a decorative suggestion, the possibilities are that a painter who has any flair for decoration will produce a room of charm.

An enchanting room can be made by enlarging old prints of whaling scenes, or prints of New York in its village days, or any other old or modern, American or foreign pictures

THE DRAWING-ROOM-LIVING-ROOM IN THE AUTHOR’S NEW YORK APARTMENT

ENGLISH OR PROVENCAL HOUSE

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 281

that can be enlarged to four, six, or eight times their size. A not impossible thing to have done well, because wall decoration is much less exacting than pictures hung in frames.

An architect I know well ornamented his study with two long wall panels (somewhat in the Botticelli manner) showing the daily life of his family. He called them "scenes of reality and fancy.” The first caricatured all the members of his family at their exaggerated worst; the second showed beautifully angelic images of them at their own fancied best. As a contrast, the first background was autumn, and the second spring. It was this painting that I had in mind when I put the "if” into "if you can paint.”

Even if you can’t really paint at all, you can do much with transfer designs by choosing them wisely and using them sparingly. Wall-paper motives cut out — possibly from several papers — then combined and pasted, contain many possibilities. The whole wall may then be shellacked.

At all events, let your walls — if they are to be different from the ordinary, every-day, plain-painted, papered or pinelined ones — express not merely your taste but your idiosyncrasies. I said this once to a member of my family who was having maps put all over the walls of her husband’s study. Her husband, as it happens, cares as little for geography as the boy at the foot of the class in this subject at school. She replied that he cared only for engines, and that she couldn’t have the walls painted with engines! Of course she could have — and beautifully! Only, again, she would have had to find not merely a blue-print draftsman, but an artist who could translate engines and their complications into half-clouded-over dreams.

A room of personality is ideally an interpretation of its owner’s characteristics and taste. To fill your rooms with books that you know you will never open, rather than with those you like, or to block a small room with a piano if you care nothing for music, or to make a collection of boat models if you know nothing about boats and have no love for the sea, or to surround yourself with objects that in

nothing suggest your habits or your tastes, is to produce the same impression of misfit as if you wore a mask and wig.

To decorate the walls of a personal room with pictures of sports or gardens or scenes that you love — even to do small wall-spaces in a theme of lambs and bulls and bears and ticker-tape, the city sky-line (a silhouette, by the way, that is not too difficult) or a theme suggested by whatever you really take an interest in — is far more personable than to depend on conventional decorations. And the possibilities are endless. There is a theme in almost everything you do, or are, or take an interest in, that is decoratively interpretable if you have ingenuity enough to devise the way. Naturally, such self-expression carried to extremes is not meant for general use — unless the theme is a general one, and really well done. The suggestion is inserted here because dressing-rooms, studies, upstairs halls, and small rooms — ^particularly those in unexacting houses — are so often blanks of lost opportunity.

AVERAGE WALL DECORATION

To return, however, to the more impersonal and average decoration of our walls. Appealingly characteristic of our early American Period is the room that is paneled across the width of the wall occupied by the chimney, and the rest of the room papered above the chair-rail. Below the chairrail, either there is paneling or the wall is painted. Paneling is often made of wide boarding, with or without doors leading to chimney-cupboards. But it is also made of narrow sections like those in shutters and doors. In fact, old inside shutters (that were solid) and old doors that may be bought of the house-wrecking companies and fitted together by sawing off the front section of the frame with the knob in it, make delightful interiors at fairly reasonable cost.

An inexpensive effect of wainscot is made by merely papering above the chair-rail, and painting the plaster below to match the trim. (See Plate 54.) The reverse of this, which

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 283

is much in favor in Georgian houses, is painting the wall a plain color above the chair-rail, and marbling or putting a marble paper below. In the second instance, the chair-rail is marbled as well as the base-board ; in the first, the chair-rail is painted to match the trim. In the Colonial house the trim is either white or cream or whitish' blue. In modern decoration the trim is painted to match the background of the wall-paper — a fashion that is very attractive; so attractive, in fact, that it is to be recommended for all simple, not too strictly period interiors, as well as for all rooms — elaborate and otherwise — that are painted.

A KITCHEN WALL

Before leaving the subject of wall -trimming, there may be a suggestive idea in the stencils in the kitchen on Plate 42. This wall was finished with the shiniest white enamel paint to be had, with the result that it looked beautifully clean, but cold and bare as the inside of an empty ice-box. So I sent for a stencil and painted through the design with bright blue lacquer, as can be seen in the picture. To space the stencils evenly I used a cardboard box-lid — using two corners and the center of the opposite side as a guide to measure by — and painted it myself. It took less than an hour’s time to do the whole room. Any wall that must be washable can easily be done the same way. (For similar suggestions see Children’s Rooms.)

Besides the unlimited ways of decorating walls, or paneling or trim, there are of course hundreds of methods of embellishment that can be added to a room; embellishment of ceiling, of floor, of doors; addition of niches and lunettes, of cupboards and bookcases, of recessed or framed-in windows, or over-doors, of wainscot or frieze, of mirrors or paintings, or of wall-paper set in panels over the mantel or over doors. Modern insets of bas-relief, painting under the glass of mirrors, inserting glass-faced trenches for electric lights, and scattered fragments of shelving, see Chapter XXII.

WALL-PAPERS

The question of wall-papers depends entirely upon whether you are one of the many who like them, or one of the few who do not. Personally, I think nothing takes their place in certain country rooms and in many types of others. In fact, the homelike decorative value of wall-papers is almost immeasurable.

Paper wall-hangings were originally made to take the place of tapestries and stuffs that were beyond the medium purse. The "Domino” papers which are associated with Italy — where papers of the same variety are still for sale — were actually first made by a group of men in Rouen, though the idea originated with the small diaper-patterned and marbled papers that were made in Italy for book-linings. These papers came in small sheets that were sold by the ream — or the quire. They were somewhat disjointed in effect because the design of each square made no attempt to fit the pattern of its neighbors.

Gradually improvements were made; Papillon in France devised the modern continuous design, and his successor invented blocks for printing the separate colors instead of stenciling them by hand. The next step was the making of picture-papers. The first of these were made by Reveillon in Paris, and were intended to simulate the painted panels that were in vogue. Later the Zuber, Dufour and Leroy manufacturers began making the scenic papers. Most of these are out of print, but a few are still to be had. The Zuber factory is still printing "Scenic America,” "Eldorado,” and "Isola Bella” from the old blocks, and there are also a number of others, both old and new, on the market. They are necessarily expensive, because hundreds of blocks are used, each of which must be stamped by hand, a tedious and timetaking process. One wishes that many of the others — especially those of Leroy — might be reproduced.

The only criticism of scenic papers is that they are apt to attract attention to themselves rather than to serve their

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 285

purpose as a background. The answer to which is to feature them instead. In halls, reception-rooms, dining-rooms, and even in bedrooms, they are utterly enchanting when they are made the center of interest and nothing is put against them.

It must, of course, be remembered, because it is important, never to hang objects on a scenic paper; neither sidefixtures nor mirrors — nor anything else. A scenic paper must be treated as though it were a painting, which, from a decorative standpoint, it is.

WALL-PAPER SET INTO PANELS

This looks very well if the background of the paper and the color of the paneling is the same. But too great a contrast is apt to be disturbing — and very few designs are important enough to warrant disturbance. Scenic wall-paper panels such as those printed by Leroy, being shellacked and treated, have all the decorative quality of paintings. But even so they look best when framed in a neutral color of brown grays or sea- weed green. (This applies to paneling only, not to panoramic walls, which are best in a room of cream or puttywhite.) Paper panels in sepia look well in wood surroundings because they have the same tone. Gray and white panels look best in a room painted French gray.

There are v/all-papers for all rooms in all colors and for all moods. For Italian rooms, there are the flock papers that look exactly like materials. For English and French and American houses of importance, there are hand-painted Chinese papers of the seventeenth century, which are, needless to say, worth a fortune — as are also some of the scenic papers that have long been out of print. But there are thousands of modern papers and rooms to be made with them that are unlimited in possibilities.

For about twenty-five years or so, papers were little used. The best houses were paneled and white-painted. Then apartment buildings and hotels habitually imitated paneling by

2S6

fastening squares of picture molding on plaster walls, that were then painted taupe or cream or sand color. Lately has come the mania for painting every room light green or robin’s-egg blue.

A very pale gray-blue, by the way, was quite as typical of the old Colonial interior as the white which we unfailingly associate with it. Whether paint was mixed this color deliberately, as in the Woodbury Room in the American Wing at the Metropolitan Museum in New York, I do not happen to know. But I remember very well that whitewash used in Maryland when I was a child was more blue than white. At all events, the present fashion for blue walls is merely a return of an old custom and not a new taste.

The reverse of the usual order of colored paper with white trim, is to paper the wall above the chair rail with a flowered paper on a white ground, and then paint the doors and trim and dado in a dominant color of the paper’s design. Leaf -green is perhaps most usual, but delphinium-blue, mallow-pink, or any of the high light tints are chosen frequently.

An effective decoration for a small dressing-room, or for a woman’s bedroom, is to panel the walls with paper molding. Many moldings and other designs suitable for bandings are made by all wall-paper manufacturers.

AT PRESENT IT IS PINE

At the moment, every room is either beautifully or badly sealed in with pine. It does not matter so much about details or the quality of the wood — pine walls we must have. And, because they are in fashion, they look well, of course. Some of them are really lovely. In this book are many illustrations of them — ^lovely ones, all. Wood lining can be entirely attractive, made of plain planks (flat boards) . But do not use matchboards on the side that shows beading! In a room that is a reconstructed barn interior, let the studs and braces show, and plaster roughly in between.

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 287

THE MOST VULGAR WALLS POSSIBLE

There are various plastic materials that, properly used.

give excellent results; but improperly used, they are - !

In fact, the way to make the most vulgar interior possible to imagine, would be to take plastic paints of two or three colors, stir them around until they streak, smear the mixture all over a wall, and brush it into a rough surf effect; or else press a mortar-board against it and pull off at right angles, making a series of volcanoes and craters. A third horror, which might be called the confectioner’s nightmare, is to put soft plaster in a bag and squeeze it through a fancy tin nozzle, as confectioners squeeze icing! I have not made this up, I promise you; I was actually shown a room decorated in wobbling spirals all around the doors and window-frames, with rosettes applied at intervals! In a confectioner’s store, cake-icing rickrack walls might be suitably amusing, but as the decoration of a house such gobby trimming is, needless to say, equal to ipecac.

Let it be added that the vulgarity of such results is the fault not of the materials but of their misuse. Perhaps, in a certain crude type of room, plaster or plastic paint skilfully applied might simulate old wood-carving. It is, after all, not so much the what we use, as the how we handle it, that makes the difference between good and bad.

COLORED PLASTERS AND CEMENTS

There is no possible objection to plaster that is colored before it is applied and thereby performs the operation of plastering and painting at the same time. Plaster that is colored all the way through is, moreover, far more durable than that which is white and then painted, since a nick in the surface will not be proclaimed by a fleck of white. Many tinted cements make ideal surfaces. One that is of cream or fawn color can be made to look like stone that has been rainwashed to ivory smoothness. A huge dining-room in Penn-

I

sylvania has such walls as these, with casement windows, on either side of which hang Elizabethan crewel work on sandcolored linen — slightly darker than the walls. The ceiling is timbered, the floor set with tiles in a russet monochrome. The furniture is of Elizabethan oak, with cushions of worsted damask in a tone of burnt orange.

In this same sort of room, on a smaller scale, 'William and Mary or Spanish peasant or primitive Italian furniture would look equally well with a wooden floor of varied- width planks, a Scotch worsted rug, and cotton-print hangings and cushions in color and design to suit the furniture.

The coarse linens printed in what are called Tudor designs — or more expensively those which simulate the needlework of early England — go equally well with plaster walls or with oak or other wood paneling.

FLOORS IN GENERAL

But before considering hangings, which properly belong in the next chapter, it is essential that we first finish the floors. Proper preparation for whichever type of floor is to be used is a necessary item that the architect attends to when the floor joists are laid and the distance to the floor level calculated.

For a stone or brick paving, for instance, the subfloor must be not only sturdy but low-set, so that the finished surface shall not rise above those of the surrounding rooms. A thin flooring, such as parquetry or what might be called a hardwood veneer, on the contrary, takes very little space. Certain varieties of flooring are thick, and others are thin; neither those which stand up too high nor those which have to be laid on lathing to make them high enough, are to be compared with those laid on a subfloor properly prepared.

In other words, the type of material to be used for the floors must be decided before the house is built or remodeled. Details as to the variety of planking or the pattern of parquetry or tile to be used, can, of course, be decided later.

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 289

It is merely necessary that heavy floors be thoroughly supported and that space be left for thick or thin ones, accordingly.

Harking back to Periods (as is our fixed habit) , we instinctively pigeonhole tiles as set apart for Spanish houses, marble for Georgian or Italian ones, stone flagging for Gothic, brick for certain primitive varieties of the above, and wood of one kind or another for all the rest. As a matter of fact, each material may have great latitude of use if properly chosen. Moreover, there are endless composition floorings, many of them suitable for equally endless uses.

THE WOOD FLOOR

Since it is the material that is most often used, let us begin our detailed description with the wood floor, which is actually banned from no house — not even the Spanish. It is more suitable to every other type of house than to this particular one, of course; but I have seen wooden floors in Spanish houses — many of them. Wood floors are, of course, typical of every house in England — except those that antedate the period of Elizabeth — and of every house in France later than the Gothic; also of all the houses that we call American.

The ideal Colonial floor is of planks — of pine, usually — that vary from eighteen to twenty-four inches in width. It is diflEcult sometimes to find a local lumber-yard that supplies wide boarding, but every builder knows where to send for it. A contractor got some yellow-pine floor-boards a few days ago that are twenty-four inches wide.

It is not necessary to get more than a few very wide boards, for these can be mixed with those of twelve or ten inches. Odd widths of wood make an ideal flooring for all primitive or unsophisticated styles of houses.

The floor of an English house should be of oak — preferably cut straight with the grain — in as wide boards as can be procured. The filler should be made of powdered quartz

and transparent varnish mixed to a paste. This is used on all woods that have any porous openings in their grain, and is necessary to produce a porcelain smooth-finish. Colored filler is proper on occasions, but it should never be strong in

contrast on a wood such as oak.

Perhaps if it were treated differently a quartered-oak floor might be admitted to a house in good taste. But in its vulgarized display of dark-brown hatchings on a green-yellow background, and the whole made glass-shiny with varnish, it has few equals as a despoiler of distinction. Cut with the grain and with a colorless filler, oak is not only the most durable of American woods, but is beautiful as well.

There is no objection to having it stained, but it must avoid the greenish mud-and-mustard tones. Boarding of second and third grade is apt to vary in color. In very primitive houses this variation — if not too pronounced — is rather pleasing — especially in a floor that has arrows or pegs of wood set in in purposely contrasting colors. (Lighter boards pegged with darker pegs and the darker boards pegged with lighter ones.) But usually the floors are stained to an even match.

Cypress, maple, ash, elm, poplar, redwood, spruce, and most of the pines are all beautiful woods for floors, as well as for trim and for wood walls. Chestnut, birch, cedar and all the fruit woods are much used for walls.

Usually the wood is acid-stained — unless you want to keep it light. After this it is filled, and the surface is waxed. A less expensive method, with a quicker result, is to varnish — and perhaps finish with wax — and perhaps not. The most beautiful finish possible is obtained by using nothing but wax — and elbow-grease. The perfect floor — of any wood is waxed and polished, and waxed and polished for days, weeks, months, years — until, at the end of a lifetime or two, the surface that we call patined is achieved! This is really more for furniture than for floors, which look very beautiful when filled and waxed in moderation. A less expensive floor finish — and one that is much in use, is a thin coat of varnish and then a few polishings with wax.

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 291

In a house of perfect finish, such as a French one, for instance, or a beautifully detailed Colonial one, the floors are made of narrow boards of oak {not quartered) , maple, butternut, walnut, chestnut, or yellow pine, laid herringbone or in one of the simpler patterns of parquetry.

THE PAINTED FLOOR

The painted floor is most suitable and becoming to all the simpler houses of Colonial suggestion — ^let us not restrict ourselves definitely to pure Colonial!

From personal experience, I have found that the most durable floors are those painted light in color. Dark ones painted night-blue or bottle-green or brown, or even those which are in the brighter colors of primary and binary hues that are scarcely grayed, show every dusty footprint and are marred by every heel-mark. Moreover, the floor-nails of old floors have a habit of gradually declaring their whereabouts with small light-colored polka-dots. Light sand — about the color of an average manila envelope — is one of the most durable and unmarkable of colors. And as the woodwork is apt to be white or cream, a sand-colored floor is quite as dark as necessary for a feeling of security. (The tendency of a too-light floor is to rise like an elevator.) Other very good — possibly even better — colors are very light gray-green, leaf -green, and green-blue.

The painted floor is usually finished with a thin coating of waterproof varnish, but the most beautiful ones are finished with enamel paint, and then thoroughly dried, waxed, and polished. A floor that is black or night-blue must be very highly polished. Very light floors look very well flat.

THE PATTERN-PAINTED FLOOR

An amusing way to paint a wood floor is in imitation of marble blocks. The floor is laid in even planks about ten inches wide. Each board is marked off in ten-inch squares and

then painted in two colors like a checker-board. Rather more effective is the drawing of diagonal lines instead of straight ones, and making diamond-shaped lozenges. For a hall, paint them white and black, or white and night-blue or bottlegreen. Black usually looks best. Another pattern is made by using a ten-inch board and a two-inch board alternately. Paint the narrow boards one color and then two two-inch bands crossways, completing the squares, and painting the ten-inch blocks left in the center another color. In any case, these block-painted floors must be polished to the highest degree. Other old Colonial floors were painted dark red or brown, and then a very small device, such as a bell or a lozenge or a star, was stenciled on at intervals. A spattered floor is even more typical.

Floors of rooms other than halls are best painted in quiet wood tones, such as the varying shades of light and dark mahogany, red-wood, walnut, all in solid color. Sometimes checker-boards are painted in slight variegations not evenly spaced. A floor of somewhat pinkish-orangeish brown, evolved from not very colorful autumn leaves, can make an intriguing floor of a pleasant, mellow quality hard to describe.

A floor that matches the furniture — or at least the majority of it — is always safe. In a bedroom that has much maple or birch or mahogany furniture, a floor of maple or birch or mahogany looks wonderfully well. A floor set like a wood pavement has possibilities in a house of such primitive type that it might have its floors set with bricks or cobblestones.

MARBLE FLOORS

Floors of marble are supremely beautiful in all types of formal houses. Those laid in special designs are very expensive — but so are formal houses!

This reminds me that no word in the American language is more often misapplied than "formal.” The meaning of the word, in the sense of etiquette, is ceremonious; in architec-

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 293

ture and decoration, it means classic. Therefore, let us repeat that a Georgian hall — if it be formal — is paved with marble. There is no substitute for marble.

Linoleum laid on a floor where marble is suitable is unthinkable. Far better — and, in fact, quite properly — change the word "formal” to "house of beauty or dignity,” and put down a floor of wood. By which is not meant that formal characteristics are necessary to perfection. Far from it! It means merely that the word defines certain limitations. If it is formal then it must be kept in character. If it is not — well, then it is not! Perhaps you like red, or perhaps you like blue. But there is little sense in painting a sample of red and labeling it blue.

TILE FLOORS

Tile floors are suitable to many houses. Many have beautifying qualities. Many others are too beauty-destroying for words. The smooth-glazed ones in the terra-cotta, russet, or wood-brown shades make perfect floors in all varieties of plaster-walled rooms and loggias. The high-glazed ones that come in gorgeously brilliant peacock, emerald, amethyst, turquoise, amber, and so on, can be delightful on bathroom or loggia floors, and possibly in other rooms; they suggest innumerable possibilities, and yet — how seldom do they fulfil them!

Mosaic tiles that suggest Bulgarian embroidery are difficult to handle because of their disconnected restlessness and insistence upon attracting attention to themselves. There are exceptions to nearly all rules, but on general principles the floor should be considered as the earth on which we construct or into which we plant — a mere foundation upon which we build.

In small spaces, such as the facing of a fireplace or the risers of certain types of stairs, Spanish, Majolica or Dutch tiles are suitable and eflective — if you like them and can adjust their surroundings to their colors. (See Index.) Those

in monochrome design, such as the Dutch, are suitable as facings for colonial fireplaces, but are never used on floors. Persian tiles do not go very well in average rooms, and the kaleidoscopic tiles which so passionately trim so many Hispanic -American houses and patios can certainly not be admitted to a house that is French, English or American- Georgian. But plain, smooth tiles that are softly dark in color can be used — and have been used — in utterly beautiful English and almost any other type of house.

An attractive substitute for tiles can be made by painting an ordinary cement floor in a pattern, each tile outlined with a narrow band of gray (to look like cement) and each tile painted with rather thin oil paint in whichever colors you please. When finished cover with several coats of valspar or other waterproof varnish, and then wax. The only flaw in such decoration is that it will not stand hard footwear, but where there are rugs or runners to walk on it will do perfectly for the not-usually-walked-on edges of a hall or a room.

Stone paving or brick flooring looks well in Gothic or other very old period houses. But these surfaces are torturing to most women to walk over. Highly waxed floors covered with small rugs are slippery unless you use the anti-skid material that comes for the purpose of holding small rugs steady. It can be bought in any furniture or dry-goods store and looks like a dull-faced thin oilcloth. You cut a piece the size of each rug, lay it on the floor, put the rug on top of it, and it sticks to the floor as tightly as though it were glued.

THE KITCHEN FLOOR

You may imagine, if you are a man — or if you are a woman who has not happened to think about it — that a tile floor in a kitchen and pantry and laundry is perfection. In the laundry it is not so bad, because a thick rug can be laid in front of the tubs and in front of the ironing table, and a laundress need not walk about very much —

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 295

unless she heats her irons on a stove instead of using electric ones. But in the kitchen and pantry, never! It would be no more inhuman to ask your servants to sleep on stone beds than to make them walk and stand all day on stone or tile floors. Not only are they torturing because of their hardness, but their coldness is actually endangering to health.

CORK AND LINOLEUM

The best kitchen and pantry floor in the world is linoleum — the thicker the better. Painted oilcloth over a wood floor is perfectly satisfactory, except that its surface wears through; the linoleum that is inlaid is really worth the difference in the cost.

Cork is a perfect floor to stand on and when laid in a parquetry pattern, scraped and waxed, it makes a beautiful floor for any room in place of wood. It must, however, be very smooth and highly polished. The color of cork is not half as pretty in a kitchen or pantry as linoleum. In a kitchen of my own (not illustrated in this book) , the floor is so pretty that I cannot stop looking at it whenever I see it. It is of inset tile in a monochrome of bright and light blues, and is resiliently soft to walk over.

Some day perhaps the barrier of fastidious taste against linoleum for the front part of the house may be lifted, because it is a colorful and serviceable floor covering. But in the fashionable world it belongs in the kitchen, the laundry, the pantry and the servants’ hall. With a rug on top of it, it goes in a simple country bathroom. It is excellent also in the children’s play-room. Those who have no prejudice and whose friends have no prejudice can of course use it as and where they see fit.

Once long ago, in Spain, leather was stretched on the floor. I have often thought how marvelous linoleum made like tree-calf or morocco could be with tiny patterns (like tooling) stamped in gold. Or linoleum of dark blue with occasional silver stars. It would be perfect in a library or

a man’s room — if its quality suggested that of tooled levant book-bindings. In other words, why do the linoleum makers not use it for a material it resembles (as the cork manufacturers have done) instead of as a substitute for tile and marble and rugs which it in nothing resembles?

HARDWARE

Another detail of the house that should be finished before the furniture is assembled, is the hardware. Thousands of people who pay the greatest amount of attention to the details of walls and floors and the Period of their- furniture, think of the hardware not at all. Which is very like putting on beautiful clothes and their accessories and forgetting to brush one’s hair.

Door-knobs of bad design and cheap finish cheapen the whole room. Window-catches are not important unless they are handles and rod-bolts on casement windows.

It is the easiest thing in the world to find certain Period styles of good hardware. One can find lovely Colonial knobs and knockers, box-locks and old Colonial hinges, and handwrought steel and iron latches with little egg-shaped brass knobs. Delectable ones are made by several specialists in Colonial iron work. Chiseled French door-fastenings are another story! Simple fastenings suitable for the provincial French house can be compromised, since many manufacturers make excellent English casement-window bolts and every sort of door-knob.

For my little Massachusetts house I used the most ordinary box-lock that is made, and had small good brass knobs fitted. They make all the difference. It is not possible to put a huge satin-finished greenish brass knob on a plate on a door and expect the room to suggest the dwelling of a person of cultivation — because it won’t. The rhinocerostoed shoe of a few years back was in no worse taste than green satin finish or oxidized bronze door-knobs and keyholes.

DECORATION IN PREPARATION EOR FURNISHING 297

THE FIRELESS FIREPLACE

Another barrier of social prejudice — but one which is rapidly lifting — is the one against the fireless fireplace. Because flues — among many other things — are impossible to include in the small space of the average apartment, thousands of us are becoming gradually accustomed to the lack of the open fireplaces which we used to consider essential to all principal rooms.

There is such cheerlessness without a fire to animate an otherwise lifeless winter room, that actual need is driving most of us to accept what best we may. An ornamental mantel surrounding a mirror or a blank wall may serve indifferently as a wall trimming, but as a substitute for a fireplace it has about as welcoming an appeal as a shut barn-door.

And yet, since in the average apartment of highest class there is seldom more than one open fire, and in one of more reasonable price there is seldom even that, what is one to do? Does one prefer a blank wall in front of which to draw one’s easy chair? Or does one use a substitute? And if so, which? Gas-logs were never much in favor. Those of the past were obviously artificial at best — and inclined to smell of gas. Electric fires are gradually improving, and the coal ones look quite nice — though in these days of steam heat no one burns hard coal in the grate! Nothing, however, as yet, takes the place of logs.

It is all a matter of personal feeling — a question which one must decide for oneself. A woman of notable taste has just put into the dining-room of her apartment a mantel and chimney-breast and fireplace complete — all but a chimney! At the moment it is arranged with logs with kindling and paper and even ashes carried from the real fireplace in another room. It can, of course, never be lighted, but it does complete the room. Especially since many more times are dining-room fires left unlighted, than lighted. A gas or electric fire would add the decorative effect of light, which

is becoming, certainly; but again it proclaims itself a substitute. The question to ask is, why should one mind?

The fact that the electric fire gives out no heat is a great advantage in a dining-room, where the backs of the persons sitting next to a genuine fire are usually cooked. But in a living-room the heat is missed. Perhaps they make electric ones with heat as well as merely with light. Perhaps they now make gas ones that are less obviously false than they used to be. At all events, if you have a fireless room, there are a number of manufactured substitutes which it would be well for you to consider — unless you can dissociate the connotation of home and hearth.

HANGING THE CURTAINS

As a last detail of preparation for furnishing, let us consider the structural preparation for the hanging of the curtains. If the windows are arched, there should be a space behind the arch across which to fit a straight rod. The curtains are hung on this as on any other rod, and the arch frames them in circular form. A round rod with curtains fitted on its sloping sides is difficult to fit and a makeshift when finished. If the arches were made before the house was bought, and you must fit them, the best way is to tack them to an arch-shaped frame of wood which then screws into the arched opening of the trim. But if you are building arched windows, don’t forget to arrange for the hanging of the curtains before the walls are finished. (See Fig. 152, at end of Chapter XX.)

Windows that are deep-set have rather more distinction than those that are flush with the walls. Flat windows are very suitable in a simple house that is modern in intention, or in a frame Colonial one whose walls are typically thin. A very easy and attractive method of window treatment in a library, living-room or book-room, is to frame the entire window side of the wall-space with book-shelves. This will set the windows into an embrasure. But do not forget to

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 299

leave enough width beyond the trim to allow the curtains plenty of room to hang in.

The heavy curtains hang just back of the face of the bookcases, with lambrequins close to the bottom of the shelf, crossing the tops of the windows; or with a shelfpaper trimming of wood set over each window as a lambrequin.

BOOK-SHELVES

Book-shelves need not be as deep as most people make them. The best thing to do is to measure the books that you intend to put in them. It is of vital importance to waste no space in a room that is small. If the room is very big, an inch or two added to shelf-width does not matter. When bookcases are too deep and the books have a tendency to slide back out of line, making the shelves look like mouths full of crooked teeth, get some strips of wood — anything will do, though very small quarter-round is best — and tack them across the back of each shelf at the depth of the books that are on it.

Built-in bookcases in a pine room are naturally of pine. Built-in bookcases in a room with white or painted trim are equally proper in a wood finish that matches the furniture, or painted to match the trim. I think it depends perhaps upon the lightness of the wood. Pine bookcases are usually left waxed in a room with pine furniture. But in a room with mahogany furniture it is usually best to paint them to match the trim. The obvious place to build bookcases is on either side of the mantel, thus leveling the wall — unless the chimney-breast projects too far into the room. In that case it is wasting room to build the bookcase the same depth.

Twenty years and more ago, bookcases were built about five feet high all around a room, so that pictures could be hung over them, and ornaments and a regiment of photograph-frames stood on top of them. But now they start at the ceiling and come down to the floor, or to whichever

point is best for the framing of a piece of furniture. (See Plate 34.)

BUILT-IN ORNAMENTATION OF FURNITURE

Such bookcases or inset cupboards or corner cupboards, the bases and cornices of which are continued by the baseboard or cornice or other moldings of the walls, should, of course, be completed before the movable furniture is placed. When this is not possible, and the furniture is all piled in the middle of the room and covered over with old sheets and curtains and paper — we all know what that is like, and make the best of it!

CANDLES, LAMPS, AND OTHER LIGHTS

If you are building, it is essential that you study the electric outlets carefully. Live in each room in imagination and be sure that every light and electric appliance has been arranged for. If you build the type of drawing-room that looks best with candle-light, then have candles by all means. But whether you are likely to use side-wall outlets or all the base-plugs you put in or not, do not omit plenty of electric wiring in a steel and concrete apartment building. It is much better to stop up the prepared outlets than to find out later, when your walls are finished and your room furnished, that you must either chisel the walls down or sit in darkness, or burn two or three dozen candles nightly that have to be replaced with fresh ones every morning. Nor is this the worst of it! If you are thinking of candles, the chances are that you have old appliques or candelabra in which you intend to burn them. The chances are also that such constant changing of candles will at some time be done thoughtlessly, without supporting each branch from underneath as the candle is put in, with the result of a broken arm or an applique pulled off the wall. It is convenient, therefore, to use candles for occasions — or use occa-

DECORATION IN PREPARATION FOR FURNISHING 301

sional candles; but for the essential lighting of your house, have plenty of outlets available, should you need them. (See Index.)

In the evening the drawing-room is quite properly lighted for becomingness to itself, but in the livfng-room there must be plenty of good lights, with shades that tip or that overhang the chair or sofa far enough to throw a bright light on book, or sewing, or writing-table.

An ordinary goose-necked lamp of green-painted iron with a reflector shade is unequaled for eye-comfort in reading small print or to do fine sewing by. If it is unsuitable or unbecoming to the room you like to use it in, it can easily be kept elsewhere and attached to a socket on a cord prepared for it. An excellent light for ordinary use is that of a "bridge” lamp, with an arm. That you can’t get its glare below your line of vision is the only reason why the opaque shade of goose-neck or lowered drop-cord lamp is better. Nothing adds more to the charm of a room than lighting that is perfectly adjusted to need.

One of the fundamental faults (among many) of the modernistic interiors is found in their uncovered side-lights and their little squat lamps, which either stare you out of countenance or otherwise are about as comfortable to see by as tethered glowworms.

XV

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING

/"N attempting to create a house of personality and charm, we must remember that while we have all the objects of the world to choose from, having once made our choice of color and prevailing form or style, the details which we then assemble must be in harmony with each other, with the climate where we live, with our manner of living, and with ourselves. We may — if we have the skill and knowledge — put utterly unrelated styles or forms together, if they but follow the laws of suitability to situation, to purpose, and to taste.

WHAT IS MEANT BY GOOD TASTE

It is easy enough for any of us to say: “Mrs. This has the most perfect taste, and Mrs. That the worst!** The important thing is to prove that this verdict is something more than untrained prejudice or haphazard preference.

Good taste might properly be defined as nice perception of the standards of suitability, form, proportion, scale and color, and the harmonious relation of each to the other. Knowledge of these standards must be acquired either consciously by means of study, or unconsciously by means of special opportunity.

For instance, if all your life you have lived in beautifully appointed houses and among people of discriminating taste, your instinct is naturally warned against the blatant, the vulgar and the sham. On the other hand, if you have happened to live your life in quite different surroundings, certain definite standards of taste must be learned through

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 303

Study and application, exactly as you learn a foreign language. It is the harder way, but it may have even better results.

It is sometimes argued that taste is merely a standard established by the opinion of the majority, which is much the same as assuming that the manners of the majority are, ipso facto, correct! Consider the uncouth millions pouring yearly into this great melting-pot, our United States. Imagine the manners and tastes of the majority, and consider what the charm of living would be, should the majority be the criterion of culture.

IMPORTANCE OF YOUR PERSONAL CHOICE

It is quite true, however, that taste is to a great degree a matter of personal choice. It is this difference in choice that makes your house different from my house, and both of our houses different from those of our neighbors. And yet there are certain fundamental rules which your house and mine, and every other house of good taste, follows. In other words, no room that has any claim to beauty can be out of scale, ill balanced, or vulgar in color or ornament. Neither can a room have charm without an inviting arrangement of furniture. The one seeming contradiction is that there must be unity, or the room is mongrel; and at the same time there must be diversity, or there can be no interest. A paradox, to say the least, but one to which we shall later have the key.

Except color, which has already been described, let us take each of the requirements of beauty and good taste in detail, beginning with scale.

SCALE

If you have an innate sense of scale you instinctively recoil from gigantic ornament on a miniature object, or the reverse. You may not measure the human figure to discover

that its classic proportion is seven heads high, but you think a person runty who is between four and five heads high. On the other hand, a figure that is eight heads high is pleasing, since it is the idealized classic measurement. But carried to an exaggerated extreme, too long a body becomes equally unpleasant — in fact, headless.

Precisely, therefore, as you find unpleasing the human figure that is out of scale, you find unpleasing — though you may not reason why — a room that is out of scale; because a person or an object that is out of scale is neither more nor less than a deformity. And yet, although to choke a tiny room with too big furniture gives an effect of strangling, furniture that is small in scale gives to an average room an effect of ease, and breathing space. On the other hand, a very large, high-ceilinged room should not look as though filled with doll’s furniture.

Apart from size in relation to its surroundings, an object of furniture may be a deformity because it is weak-legged, or because its top is too heavy, or because it is otherwise out of scale. Or it may appear ugly because its ornamentation is out of scale.

BALANCE

Everyone knows that two objects of equal weight, at equal distances from whatever may be the center, balance each other. A room is perfectly balanced when it appears to be completely at rest. Of course, some people do not mind — or even notice — whether a room is at rest or not. Some people do not notice that a ship is pitching, either. But one who is sensitive to balance (whether esthetically or as a sailor) is made keenly uncomfortable in either case.

If your sense of balance is at all acute, your impulse is to steady a room that has too much weight in one spot, exactly as your impulse is to steady a canoe that is unevenly loaded.

The furniture in a room is balanced, for instance, when
