a very tall secretary is safely centered on the wall opposite two windows, at a point between them, making the lower point of a V.

(Fig. 109.) Or, when there is only one window, and that not in the center of the wall, placing the secretary at the same distance diagonally off center — on the wall opposite — will restore its balance. Sometimes a pediment added to a door on a wall opposite heavy furniture will give the room balance; this identical pediment, however, added to a door on the same side as the furniture weight, would produce shipwreck.

Bisymmetric balance, of course, means identical objects placed at precisely the same distance on either side of the center of a wall. Two windows exactly the same distance from the center, or duplicate chairs standing on either side of a table, or any two objects evenly placed, are bisymmetrical.

OCCULT BALANCE

Occult balance is much more difficult to explain, because it is a balance that you feel rather than one that you can measure. It is an adjustment that satisfies your own sensibility, and is often very subtle. Nearly every house of charm is an illustration of occult balance — an instinctively adjusted proportion, or picture sense. If you lack this sense, you can still achieve occult balance; you need merely arrange each wall as though it stood upon a see-saw and you wanted to keep it level. Better still, perhaps, is to imagine the entire floor balanced on a center pin, and to furnish it so that one side or corner of the room will not go down and its opposite one go up.

Sometimes, especially in an apartment building where, in order to have a large room, it may be necessary to push walls out of line, symmetrical balance is out of the question.

OBVIOUS BALANCE

And yet a perfectly restful room can result. Figure 110 illustrates lack of architectural balance in the drawingroom of my own apartment corrected by occult balance. The vestibule at the lower left is the entrance. It is included as part of a series of arches that frame two bookcases and another recess, and that extend the length of the wall. The double door off center on the left wall is balanced by a broad secretary bookcase. The difference in width of the walls, on either side of the three windows at the right, is balanced by a dark mahogany drop-front desk on the narrow wall and a small sofa covered in light-colored material on the other. On the upper wall the chimney projects as shown in Plate 26, and is set between bisymmetric windows that match the bookcases. (See Plate 16.) (Since writing the above, I have put a big chair in place of the light sofa, but the balance is maintained.)

The other furniture in the room is not indicated because the diagram merely illustrates wall-balance. But two items might be noted, though they have nothing whatever to do with balance: The vestibule was a trick by which an effect of size was added to the room and taken away from the over-length of the gallery leading to it. The other item is that the dining-room doors open into the living-room, instead of into the dining-room, as is customary. Whether you like this plan or not depends upon whether you habitually keep these doors open or shut. But if your habit is to keep them shut except when you are eating, and then leave them open, the plan is very practical, since neither room is ever encumbered with jutting doors. If you leave them open, the usual way is better. If you never have them stand open, it does not matter.

EXAMPLE OF OCCULT BALANCE

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 307

PLACING THE PIANO

A difficult object to place in a room — without capsizing it — is a grand piano, since it can rarely if ever be put in the middle of the floor! The usual way to balance it is to hang very high and rather dark curtains on the opposite side of the room; or else a unified group of furniture. For instance, a whole wall-covering of tapestry, with a sofa under it, is not too much counterweight for a concertgrand piano. A commode or long table, with a picture or mirror hung low enough to be included with it as a single unit of furnishing, will balance a baby-grand. A piano is, of course, made still heavier if it has an unusually large cover over it weighted down by ornaments or a ponderous lamp.

Usually the best way to place an upright piano is to stand it against one wall and put a bookcase — or secretary bookcase — on the opposite side to balance it. In rooms that are long in shape or that have wide doorless door frames, an upright piano is sometimes used successfully as a screen — keys to the room or back to the room — with a sofa or a table against it. But flat against the wall is safest. The worst possible way to put it is across a corner.

The extreme height in the average room is furnished by the windows, and it is balanced either by the weight of furniture or by the overmantel, or by other wall-hangings. The weight of a sofa is easily balanced by a mantel on the opposite wall, but a sofa — or possibly two sofas — next to the mantel and therefore an addition to its weight, must have an important counterbalance.

In Figure 114, page 345, for example, the mantel, two cabinets, and two small sofas are counterbalanced by the large sofa, two large tables with important lamps and ornaments, and a large picture, decorative panel, or tapestry hung over the sofa. If it be a picture, it must be hung low enough to come well below the top of the table lamp-shades, in order to produce the effect of a single heavy unit instead

of one low, widespread unit and a separate picture unit far above. The mirrors over the two consoles on the left wall are very high and balance the windows. They are not nearly as high as the windows, but they are in occult balance because you feel that they weigh more. In fact, if they hang from the picture molding, the curtains should be moderately dark. But if they rest on the consoles and are therefore weightless, the curtains may very well be light. If there be a mirror over the mantel, the picture or panel over the big sofa might better be fairly dark, especially if the two cabinets against the opposite wall are of mahogany. Should they be gilt or light, the over-sofa decoration might quite well be light, depending upon the general coloring of the room.

The furniture arrangement in Figure 112, page 322, has a tendency to dip down at the upper right-hand corner, where the big sofa, the three windows and the piano are more than dangerously close together. Balance is, however, restored by a large dark picture over the mantel and a hanging of dark brocade over the heavy refectory table centered on the left wall. In effect this hanging should appear more solid than the window curtain material, which is perhaps of taffeta.

LINE AND FORM

Line means merely the edge of anything. Form is the complete shape of a finished article. The lines of a book are straight, and at right angles to one another. The form of a book is a flat rectangular block. A single object is often composed of innumerable lines that are parallel and rightangled — such as doors and all straight-lined furniture. Other objects are composed of innumerable, beautifully related, or antagonistically unrelated curves.

Curved lines, unless they have purpose in the general design, are apt to be disturbing as well as futile. Your eye, for instance, follows the contour of a Chinese vase; it starts at a given point and follows a pleasing outline to completion at

GENERAL ERINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 309

the point it started from; or it follows the flowing lines of the Italian Renaissance arabesque. But applied ornament that has no relationship with that which it ornaments, and therefore starts with a jerk and stops with a bump, is disturbing. Like the ill-considered wriggles typical of cheapest ornate furniture, wriggles that start nowhere, and go nowhere, and belong nowhere, because serving no definite purpose, are usually bad.

Apart from the lines which perhaps beautify a single object, one must consider these same lines in relation to the room. A room is usually longer than wide and less high than wide; its ceiling and walls are flat. Of course, the ceiling may be vaulted, barrel-rounded, coved, or open-beamed with a pitch roof. Sometimes a room is octagonal; sometimes it is round, or oval. But, taking as typical the room that is a flat-ceilinged rectangle, its lines are all horizontal or vertical. Its windows are rectangular, and so are its doors and its fireplace opening. If we panel the walls, we add more straight lines. A carpet on the floor is another rectangle. Then we put the furniture — more rectangles — against the wall. A few chairs or occasional tables stand obliquely, but the effect of the room is one of parallel and perpendicular lines, which is the most dignified, restful, conservatively correct — and therefore the safest — method of furnishing possible.

But let us suppose that you like the embellishing grace of the curve; and let us say, then, that you join the wall and ceiling with a coved cornice, and that you panel the room with Louis XV curved-topped paneling; that you put in a few straight pieces of furniture to match the structure of the room, and some curved pieces to match the curved paneling. In this way the lines of the room are brought into harmony.

Or you could — more simply — have put a figured paper on the walls, that would have added all the lines of which the design was composed. From these you would instinctively interpret the general character of the design in the detail of the furnishing.

AVOID MIXING PRONOUNCED PATTERNS

' There is one rule of interior decoration that most of us follow blindly — that of avoiding mixed patterns. In other words, if you put a figured paper on your walls, you should not put up figured curtains! You are supposed to stick to plain hangings and furniture-covering or use striped material; unless, of course, paper and chintz are made to match — which is very pretty if the room be small and sparsely furnished, and the pattern not too large or striking. As there are exceptions to most rules, a room can be much prettier and have greater personality than any matched one, if it be done in two or even more figured materials; but they must supplement each other.

The triads noted in Chapter X are used by all manufacturers. It is easy, therefore, to find variety in the same color scheme. Moreover, patterns that are in accord are not as hard to find as might be supposed.

For example, the present popularity of robin’s-egg blue and terra-cotta pink with foliage of a very dark blue-green, means that the market is filled with papers and chintzes in these colors. A chintz therefore that has trees in deep bluegreen and distant scenes in flesh terra-cotta on a robin’s-egg background goes beautifully with a wall of pale flesh-colored paper with scenes and figures accented with blue-green. It is the opinion of certain authorities that one may use two figured materials that are different in scale. It can be done, but it is a dangerous rule for the novice, unless the little material is practically a diaper or small all-over pattern that has the value of a plain material.

A bisymmetric conventionalized floral design on a wall, and natural outlines of realistic flowers in the chintz, are, of course, out of harmony.

Straight lines, curved lines, and oblique lines, each unrelated to the other, are disturbing. For instance, catacornered rugs have always been considered bad — likewise picture wires that hang from a single hook in the shape of an A

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 311

— because they are out of harmony with the vertical lines of the room. But in Modern rooms where triangles flourish and zigzags reign supreme, pointed picture-wires and crosscornered furniture and small rugs laid herringbone will be perfectly in accord.

HANGING PICTURES

To repeat: the typical wire that runs from the upper corners of a picture to the single hook on the molding, like an A, is the pet tabu of all decorators.

The way all the best decorators hang pictures is to run a wire through two picture-hooks screwed into the back of the frame on either side near the top, and then to have each separate end of the wire hang vertically from a hook in the molding. The picture naturally adjusts itself to the wire — nothing could be simpler. Moreover, the wires are parallel with the perpendicular lines of the room. The ends of the wire loop around the picture hooks and the picture or mirror is merely pulled straight on the wire, which slides through the screw eyes.

If there must be wires, then this is the proper way to adjust them. But why wires? True, the first principle of architecture exacts that every object be supported, and a picture put on a wall without any visible means of support is structurally wrong. It must be. But, on the other hand, we all know that there is a wire behind the picture and a good strong picture-nail on which the wire is hung. Perhaps this is occult picture hanging? Why not?

Expert selection and application of ornament is a test of good taste. The best rule for the decoration of a wall or a piece of furniture is the same as the rule which every well-dressed woman applies to the trimming of her clothes. Add so much as is necessary to complete the definite intention of the design, and then stop. The room in worst taste is invariably one that is overtrimmed, since vulgarity and overtrimming are one and the same.

A MUSEUM OF SENTIMENT

Another unpleasing room — not vulgar, but frumpy is one that is turned into a museum of sentiment. In such a room the owner will eliminate nothing that any member of her family ever fancied. All the table-tops and twice as many cabinets as the room should hold are packed solidly with treasures that range from Sevres, Royal Worcester and cameo vases to dozens of picture-frames, baby’s rattles and small china dogs and kittens associated with the users of the rattles. Or perhaps mistaken ideas of house beautification induce the preservation of Rogers groups, embroidered plush hassocks, or conch shells. Perhaps the owner of one such room believes that a miniature Alpine hat with a feather labeled "Gruss aus Innsbruck!” is an embellishing ornament.

The Japanese practise of keeping most of their ornaments put away, and bringing them out one by one, is a little severe for our ideas of cozy comfort; but the suggestion is one that most of us would do well to take to heart, and in going through our rooms we might at least ask ourselves if we must really have nineteen different photographs of the baby. Would not two, or at most three, be enough? Why do we continue putting the same ornaments year after year — with slight accumulations — on certain tables or on the mantelshelf? Would the table or the mantel not perhaps look much better if the objects of genuine beauty were left and the objects of sentiment relegated to a little museum-mausoleum elsewhere?

Of course, there are rooms and rooms! Living-rooms, libraries or studies that have individuality and charm to the highest degree are rather especially those which have every inch of wall space hung with personal mementos, such as sketches, framed letters, documents, photographs; each an evidence that the owner is one whose contacts with life have been both interesting and personal. In other words, suitability should be considered in one’s selection for a mu-

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 313

seum of sentiment that is installed in one of the principal rooms.

MIXTURES vs. MATCHINGS

A mixture of furniture, if it be assembled with taste and skill, is far more likely to produce a room of personality than is one which is precisely matched. It is obvious — if you think about it — that nothing is quite so personality-destroying as an entire room filled with furniture, each piece of which is precisely the same as every other. Boredom is the inevitable result — unless this handicap be overcome by very unusual counteracting interest. But the handicap is heavy, even so. In fact, the mid-Victorian-General-Grant period of bad taste might be characterized by city streets of identical brownstone-front houses and “suites” of furniture. The brownstone fronts are rapidly demolishing, but in the furniture trade, suites still flourish, and — except in little cottage bedrooms, and in occasional dining-rooms that have painted furniture — personality suffers accordingly.

Rooms that have painted furniture should be considered differently, because, no matter what the style of the individual pieces may have been, the painting usually makes them match. And yet they would in many cases have more charm if they were somewhat different — at least in the effect of decoration. What could be more uninteresting than a collection of painted furniture, each piece garnished with the same decalcomania on the identical spot? A spotty wallpaper that induces your eye to run up and down on the diagonal stepping-stones of the pattern is not half as disturbing as jumping from object to object to find the inevitable rose-bud or Chinese pagoda! The annoyance of this repetition is easily avoided by changing somewhat the design and its location, and by omitting it on some of the pieces, or by adding one or two objects painted in a contrasting color.

THE PARADOXICAL REQUIREMENTS, UNITY AND DIVERSITY

The demands of unity and diversity are quite easily fulfilled, since unity means harmony and diversity imagination. For example, let us paint a dining-table a yellowish graygreen, and paint the sideboard the same green with panels outlined in rubbed-off gold. Then we will decorate the chairbacks elaborately with painted fruits in neutral color and gold. Or, if we like much color, let us paint the chairs Chinese red and decorate them in silhouettes of green and gold.

A matched dining-room of no pretensions whatever may have plenty of interest in its table-setting and its color. But a room of importance with so-called Period furniture, and with the details of all the pieces alike, is not merely monotonous but palpably false, because Period furniture was never made in complete sets — except dining-room chairs and occasional sofas with two or four matching side-chairs. Otherwise the only pieces made alike were pairs.

Beautiful furniture may have been alike in general effect, but it was created piece by piece or pair by pair. Even in a dining-room, the room in which the furniture is more properly alike than in any other, there were perhaps a dozen chairs made, let us say, by Chippendale. The sideboard — possibly a pair of them — had perhaps been made by Sheraton. A side-table might be Chippendale’s — but not a repetition of the chair-back design. That furniture by notable men, or belonging to a definite Period, is sometimes seemingly a set, because of a preference for certain forms or ornaments which they constantly made use of, is true, of course; but, even so, they never resorted to mechanical duplication.

Whole rooms full of furniture turned out in quantity, and all to match, are quite suitable in hotels, since personality is impossible there anyway. Rooms that are occupied by three hundred and sixty-five persons in a year can scarcely express three hundred and sixty-five different per-

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 315

sons. The chief objection to matching sets is that such sets destroy personality. What possible personality can be expressed in Pattern No. 987,654,321 in sixteen pieces? The same details sixteen times repeated! After all, would you choose sixteen identical hats?

THE NECESSITY FOR VARIABILITY

The variability of our own personal choice in assembling our homes is the true expression of our personal taste and is the factor of interest. No one could ever be interested in unvarying repetition — except as a cure for insomnia.

Moreover, just as it is advisable to avoid the pattern No. 987,654,321 effect, it is often better that Periods be not too strictly adhered to, either. It does not make the least difference — except in a museum, or in rooms of museum character — whether a piece of lacquer is old English, Chinese, or made-yesterday reproduction; or whether a chair is English, German, Dutch, Swedish, Spanish, or American. The only matching-law that matters is that you cannot put perfect quality directly next to no quality at all, without making the latter unendurable. The new-cut crudeness of raw surface screams in pain if put next to the subtle softness of age-old patina. Were it not for this one inimitable quality of texture and surface that age alone can produce, there would be no point in buying old furniture. In factj when it is made of well seasoned, carefully chosen wood, and is conscientiously designed; and when its surface is finished as it ought to be, modern furniture can be — and sometimes is — as beautiful as any antique that ever was.

SLAVERY TO THE WORD "ANTIQUE”

Why good modern finish is so often less than beautiful is hard to understand, especially in this day of unlimited flexibility of machinery, when we should be able to reproduce, at least passably, that tedious-to-make but undeniably

supreme finish known as French polish. Why certain commercial varieties of finish are often worse than bad is another side of the story, which will be dealt with in a later chapter. (See Index.) Nevertheless, there is little sense in what might be called slavery to the word "antique.

The fact that a piece of furniture or an object of ornament or utility happens merely to be old is not the slightest reason for buying it, unless, apart from its antiquity, one thinks it beautiful, and unless one has — or is likely to have — a suitable use for it.

At the sound of the words, "We guarantee it to be genuine,” countless otherwise sensible people are hypnotized into buying every sort of useless junk. Shorn of the glamorous tag, "Antique,” could anything be more unsuitable to a drawing-room than a potter’s implements, kitchen crocks, washstands and warming pans?

Of course, if one have a New-England-kitchen type of room, or if one be making a collection of Americana, that is one thing; but assembling a modern house of suitability is perhaps another. As for limiting the comfort of one’s home for the sake of slavish adherence to the past — nothing could be more idiotic!

Even if one does happen to live in a house a hundred years old, that is little reason for going without deep-springed beds or chairs or sofas. One who is such a purist as to refuse to have modern furniture in an early Colonial house, should be consistent and not have plumbing, or a modern range, or an electric icebox, or electric lights, or a furnace, or a radio, or a telephone, or any other comforts of modern life.

ADJUSTMENTS IN FURNISHING A VERY OLD HOUSE

The main object in furnishing an old house is to keep the quaintness and the spirit of its character as much as you can without sacrificing a single item of comfort or luxury that you like and can afford.

What is meant by keeping the spirit of a room or a

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 317

house is simply adhering as closely as possible to an impression of suitability. Modern improvements are admissible because their absence would not merely mean discomfort to yourself, but would make your manner of living a nuisance to all your friends. It is within the picture, however, to choose your modern equipment in suitable form. Beds can be old-fashioned in appearance; the covers laid over them in the daytime can be buckram-lined and boxed to look as quaintly stiff and hard as you please. But when the blankets are turned down for the night, let the mattresses and springs be such that you or your family or your guests can find rest on them unmarred by corn-husk lumps and plank hardness.

CAMOUFLAGING RADIATORS, RADIOS, PHONOGRAPHS AND

TELEPHONES

A room that is to have an old atmosphere need have no evidence of radiators (nor of their window-box cages) , because steam-pipes are easily stowed away in the prepared bottom of a corner cupboard, or put into an Italian, English or Spanish chest. Or they can be set into a section of a bookcase; book-back trimming can be fastened on a door, with narrow slits above each book-row, and the whole door can be opened when necessary. In a bedroom the pipes can be hidden beautifully under the petticoat of a dressingtable. If your living-room is finished in Jacobean oak or other dark-colored wood, it will not be hard to find a radio and phonograph that will fit in suitably as they are; sometimes they look better when they are cut down in height. In certain rooms where they are too much out of key, they can be fitted into any type of commode-chest or highboy. The drawer-fronts are made into a door, and, if necessary, the top lifts up.

But, whatever we do with our new inventions — our radios and radiators — let us not petticoat our telephones! Telephones hidden under doll’s hoop-skirts or other frip-

peries are more typical of the movies than of a house in good taste. An ordinary black telephone is an inoffensively plain object of utility; it can usually be stood on a desk or on the lower shelf of a table, where it is out of the way yet easily reached. Or the new one-piece instrument can be painted to go with the room, and look very well almost anywhere.^

LIGHT OUTLETS AND FURNITURE SPACES

Before you build, remodel or buy, it is essential that all furniture of importance be carefully considered and marked on the floor-plan. Even in a very large house, with plenty of wall-space, it is necessary to have an idea where the furniture is to go in order to get an impression of the finished room, and to know where the light outlets and bells are to go. In very little rooms, it is essential to fit the furniture exactly, since the moving of a wall or door an inch or two makes the difference between a room that is completely comfortable and one that is completely the reverse.

The best way to study the practicability of each room is to live in it — on paper. And the only way to do this is to measure the exact size of the furniture that you have — or, if you haven’t bought it, measure that in the rooms you are living in — and decide whether you want this or that piece larger or smaller.

Nearly all plans are drawn to a scale of a quarter of an inch to the foot (the scale is printed on each) ; so you merely measure the outside width of a sofa by its outside length and cut a little piece of paper in exact reduction of its size. A sofa lYz feet wide by 6 feet long would be represented by a rectangular fragment of paper % of an inch wide by 114 inches long. The average single bed 3 feet wide by 7 feet long is represented on the plan by a piece of paper ^ of an inch wide by 1 ^ inches long. Many people try to furnish a room by guessing at the size of the

* It is against the Telephone Company’s rules to paint your own telephone, but the compapy will paint it for you to match any sample you send for $17, plus $3 exchange charge.

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 319

room and of the furniture as well, a procedure that is so inaccurate (except for a professional draftsman) as to be useless. Most people draw outlines- on the plans. But if you cut paper patterns of the accurate size of the necessary pieces of furniture, it is easy to move them about on the accurate plan of each room until you see which way they fit best. The arrangement is clearer if you cut your furniture patterns in paper that is of a different color from that of the plan.

Many rooms can be furnished automatically, but others are difficult, and often some of the larger objects are impossible to fit in without adjustment of the wall space. Small rooms with uneven or insufficient wall length for an essential piece of furniture, must perhaps be replanned. The smaller the room, the more necessary it is that the furniture and room measurements be minutely accurate, since the whole point is in being able to make difficult adjustments to best advantage.

When the furniture is placed, -then mark your electriclight openings. Nothing is more important than convenient and becoming light. This matter is worth a lot of pains, especially as nothing is more easily done wrong. In fact, about 25 per cent, of light fixtures must later be moved, or one must endure wires lying all over the floor.

A FEW LIGHTING REMINDERS

Decide where all pictures or tapestry or other wall ornaments are to go. Measure them and mark them, and then see where you want your side-lights.

The builder’s delight is to panel a room and then center a side-fixture in each panel. Nowhere to hang a thing! When a room is paneled it is fairly safe to put side-lights between them, or possibly in narrow ones. It is taken for granted that side-lights go over the mantel. Yes, but where? The answer depends entirely upon what else is going over the mantel. Measure this out on paper.

Side-lights in the wrong place can interfere with form and balance quite as much as a stepladder or a child’s wagon, or any other left-out-of-place object. Base-plugs are not a difficult problem if you put in plenty of them — especially on both sides of every door. The one snag is to find a plug on the right of a door for a chair that must stand on the left. This sounds too foolish to write; yet we have all of us at some time had to cut into walls or floors or endure electric wires running around a door-frame.

For the service end, be sure to remember outlets for whichever electric appliances you are likely to instal. You are not likely to forget one for the icebox, but you might forget to put a plug in every room for the vacuum-cleaner, so that you can use it without pulling out a lamp plug; or you might forget the plug for the iron in the sewing-room, or that for some other essential object which you are sure to need.

And do not forget a double plug at the head of every bed. One is for a night light and one for emergency requirement — for a heating pad, say, or a milk-heater, or anything of which the occupant, well or ill, might have need.

Bells are noted for a persistent tendency to be in the wrong place. You might think of this — though it won’t do much good, because, no matter where you habitually sit, the bell will be elsewhere.

ROOMS THAT ARE BAD-MANNERED

In placing the furniture in whichever room you receive your friends, it is well to remember that an essential of charm is hospitality. A room that expresses rudeness has no more appeal than a rude person. Moreover, a room can be every bit as rude as a person. A grouping that is notoriously bad-mannered is a fireplace blockade, such as the one shown in Figure 111.

In this most ill-bred arrangement, a huge upholstered sofa is jammed up in front of the fireplace, with a colossal

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 321

library table back of it, two blockading armchairs flanking the sofa, and a cow-path left to walk around.

If you imagine yourself a guest who has just entered the door, you will see that you are blocked on all sides. You can sit in the corner on your right by yourself, but you are barricaded by the long table, sofa, and chair-backs from

Fig. Ill

THE INHOSPITABLE WAY W — Writing Desk. T — Table. X — Lamp.

the principal and only comfortable grouping around the fireplace. You are also barred by the screen and piano from discovering any welcome at the left.

Even if the room be large enough to leave plenty of space around it, a central cluttering of furniture, barricading the hearth — the symbol of hospitality — results obviously in back-turned rudeness. Just why persons very new to luxury invariably select this awkward arrangement, in which the occupants of the sofa and of the two flanking

chairs are literally cooked, while the others are segregated in the cold and loneliness of the outlying districts, has never been explained! Of course, if such furniture-placing be in the frozen North, in an unheated shack, or in any coldweather camp, the greater the barricade the better for the elect. Moreover, any newcomers beyond the capacity of

the hearthside seats can sit on the floor in front of the fire. But in a modern luxurious house, such furnishing is awkward, inhospitable, and charm-destroying — unless the room is occupied by a man alone and no more than three other persons are under any circumstances admitted.

The same room, with furniture differently placed, is shown in Figure 112. Here two or three or any number of people are equally welcomed by facing chairs and ample floor space for easy circulation. (In both drawings the piano

GENERAL PRINCIPLES OF INTERIOR FURNISHING 323

by accident was turned curved side to wall. Actually it should be turned around or placed elsewhere.

Rooms that are skilfully and invitingly arranged have ease, poise and welcoming grace of manner. Others that are inexpertly assembled are often as ill-mannered as the unknowing provincial hostess who has the dishes at table presented to herself first, and her broken-into leavings passed on to her guests.

XVI

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

T’^HE intention of this chapter is to begin at the front door of a composite American house and go through it, room by room, in order to define its character and purpose, and perhaps thereby discover just why the come-hither of certain rooms is irresistible, and why the get-thee-hence of others is equally pronounced.

First of all, therefore, let us consider the character and the purpose of the entrance hall.

THE ENTRANCE HALL

Since this is primarily a passageway, and its object is to lead you to the rooms of the house, it should not be cluttered with furniture; neither should it invite you to sit down and stay indefinitely. In short, an open fire with a low easy-chair drawn up before it should not be put in a hall — unless it is also a living-room (in which case it will be considered as such elsewhere) . A fireplace is suitable, of course, always and everywhere; there should be merely no encouragement to sit beside it.

An important item of beauty in a hall is appealing color. This may be in the painting or papering of the walls, or, greatest beautification of all, in a lovely stairway. Wallpaper gives an especially friendly — and therefore welcoming and homelike — effect. Walls of pine or other wood, suitably painted, also make a homelike hall. Marble halls can be completely beautiful, but they are apt to be cold and are sometimes overpretentious. The white-paneled wall and black-and-white-marble floor, which are still epidemic

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

among the decorators, are always good style for a small hall or the foyer of an apartment.

On general principles a hall should be light — unless it is oak-paneled in an English house. But, whatever you do, avoid highly varnished wood-trim, embossed or oatmeal papers, and rough plaster protuberances, if you would have a house of charm.

FLOORS AND STAIR-TREADS

Loveliness of stairway that is suitable in type and size for the house is of first importance. A stair of intrinsic worth is of second importance.

To the stair details already noted in Chapter IX we might add that carpeted marble or tile stairs are apt to look overgrand-hotelish. A carpet — or even rugs — on a stair such as the one in Plate 23 would destroy its charm. Wood stairs, on the other hand, are usually carpeted; but if you like walking up and down on bare treads, a runner is unnecessary. (If you also like high wax polish, it might be no more than prudent to take out an accident insurance policy covering every one who comes into the house.)

Bare hall floors are often preferred when the flooring is not of wood. Those that are plain or that have an all-over pattern are covered much or little, or not at all — as one prefers. But a marble floor that has a design of formal importance should be neither extinguished by a big rug nor patched with little ones. Wood floors, whether plain, painted, or inlaid, should be at least partially covered with carpet or rugs. Personally, I like wood stairs carpeted, but all marble and most other hall floors that are not of wood, bare. (See Plates 16 and 23.)

HALL FURNITURE

For a hall of small size, a chest or bench, or a chair or two, on which men visitors can lay coats and hats; a table with a writing-pad and pencil, so that a friend may leave a note; a mirror for women visitors to glance in, as

well as for embellishment, are all the furnishing necessary. A hall too small for even this amount of furniture would better have a chair or bench than a table, since something to lay a coat or wrap on is the first essential.

If the hall be formal, the items of furniture would be better in pairs. But a large hall of irregular type, especially one in a country house, is quite as suitably furnished with unmatching pieces — -a bench against one wall-space, a chest against another, an odd number of chairs, and a small writing-table or a secretary, perhaps.

But no matter how small or how big the hall, its effect upon the eye should be one of emptiness rather than of being too fully furnished; and the more formal its type, the more empty it should be. (See Plate 30.)

What little furniture there is should be effectively placed, and good of its kind. The first is easy to achieve, since to put a small object all alone by itself against a bare wall cannot fail of effectiveness. The second is not difl&cult, either, since the simplest and fewest objects are all that are necessary — one chair, one small table, one little mirror, one little print.

HALL LIGHTING

Side-lights are suitable in every hall, unless there is a scenic paper. In this case, the side-fixtures should be set on pilasters, or else the lamps should be put on tables or floorlamps should be used.

Sometimes specially-made light-fixtures can be adjusted to the design of a scenic paper, but it is very difficult. On a certain paper that shows a street opening into a square, a side-fixture made like a lantern hangs on the front of a building in the foreground. And in the office of a well-known decorator lights are applied to the buildings on the banks of the Seine. At all events, as small lights must be made part of the picture and their size adjusted accordingly, it is easier for most of us to use floor-lamps. (See Plate 16.)

In all types of cottages or in halls that are at all broad,

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

lamps on tables — if you prefer them — are quite as suitable as side-lights, and are more homelike.

Few details lend themselves to greater vulgarity than lighting fixtures — and, in fact, all hardware and metal-work. Shading — the manufacturers call it "high-lighting” — is in equally bad taste, whether on wood surface or on metal. The rubbing off and painting on which is commercially labeled an "Art finish” is also to be shunned — unless judgment be reliable enough to choose accurately between glazing that can be good and smudging that is an abomination. Silver that is oxidized is nothing more nor less than dirty finger-pailed. Satin-faced silver is perhaps practical, and therefore admissible for those who lack silver-cleaning facilities; but in a well-appointed house it is not in best taste.®^ And avoid as you would the plague the satin-finished brass known as "brushed/’ But tole (a French yariety of enameled and decorated tin) is nearly always good. Colonial fixtures of plain polished brass, wrought iron, gilt, crystal, and even tin, are almost invariably good. If you avoid brushed brass, art-shaded bronze, and all oxidized metal in door-knobs ,as well as light fixtures, lamps and all other equipment, you cannot go far wrong, since metal objects in bad taste are almost invariably confined to these tabu materials.

Neither hall-globe nor door-knob-shaped ceiling lights are in good taste, because they are almost certain to be of tabu material and bad in outline. If there must be a fixture in a ceiling that is too low for a lantern, then it would best be set flat in the shape of a star or in one of the modernistic geometrical designs.

The hall is a very suitable place to put decorated doors — if you happen to like them. Those with small panes of glass (builders call them French doors) were — and to a great extent are still — ^very popular in small houses and apartments. One reason for their popularity is that they light dark halls and apartment foyers. Another reason is that many

* Brides— or others— who may have had satin-faced silver given to them, can have it changed to a polished surface in a moment’s time by any silversmith.

people find them more cheerful and informal than the totally barricading wooden doors. If you have them and do not like the typical silk curtains stretched behind them — which do more or less suggest a dressmaking establishment — they can be decorated in any number of ways. Prints or other decorations are easily set behind (or in front of) each pane, or a modern effect can be achieved by fitting silver tea-paper into each frame. The frame is then silvered or lacquered.

A FEW HALL DETAILS

Since the umbrella-stand is rarely an object of beauty, the simpler and less obtrusive it is, the better. One of the painted metal, tole, in the shape of a topless octagon cage, looks best, perhaps — unless the typical Chinese umbrella-jar is a good one, or some sort of Italian jar is more suitable.

The vestibule — if there be one — is the proper place for the umbrella-stand, with a mirror over it, so that one coming in from the rain can leave the umbrella and look in the glass to make what facial reparations may be necessary.

Hall-racks should be avoided — unless you have a plain, old-fashioned, "homey” house, in which case an old one might best remain. (See Chapter XIX.)

In every house where many guests are invited for weekends there should be two closets in the hall; one for the coats, racquets, and golf -bags of the visitors, and the other for the use of the family alone. This is necessary to the comfort of the family quite as much as of the visitors.

A telephone closet is also a great convenience in a house that is apt to be filled with people who are likely to be called to the telephone.

BOOKS - SOMETIMES !

Where space is limited, bookcases do not belong in a hall, since the hall is not a place in which to read. But in a wide hall through the center of a house, or in the long gallery of an apartment, the owner of an important library

Dwight James Baum, Architect

AN OUTSTANDING EXAMPLE OF INTERIOR ARCHITECTURE

William Lawrence Bottomley , Architect

A PERFECT EXAMPLE OF DRESSING-TABLE HIDDEN BEHIND PANELING

WHEN NOT IN USE

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

might of course line the entire wall-space with bookcases. In the house of a genuinely literary person, book-lined walls are everywhere suitable, because they and the owner’s personality are inseparable. But in an average house occupied by persons who are neither scholars nor bibliophiles, nor the inheritors of a notable library, books are better bestowed elsewhere than in the entrance hall.

HALLS AND CHILDREN

We all know that, in general, the first requirement of a hall is orderliness; but we also know that a certain sort of disorder in a living-room is not out of place; a desk rather crowded with papers, a book or a work-basket left open on a chair or sofa to which we intended to return, a puzzle on its board, are merely punctuations in the story of our day.

We know perfectly well that in a hall — a front hall — no evidence of occupation belongs. And yet, a house overrun with children should not be old-maidishly prim even in its front hall — unless the children are to have no part of its expression. Giving the children their part, however, does not mean that there should be rubbers heaped in the middle of the floor, sweaters hanging over the door-knobs, a train of cars balanced over the stair-rail, hats capsized on a table or flung under a chair; the meaning is merely that a hall too austerely formal for children’s use publishes that fact and affects the personality of the house.

But whether your children have free run of your house and put their personal marks — in the unmistakable way that they have — all over it, or whether they come into the front of the house only at definite times and when especially presentable, there should if possible be a set-apart entrance in every country house into which they can tramp in muddy boots and with their muddy-pawed pets, as muddily as they please.

Such an entrance might ideally lead into a vestibule that has washable walls and a floor with a drain at one side of it.

and possibly a small spigot that turns with a wrench (the wrench to be hung on an out-of-reach hook so that the youngest members of the family cannot turn the water on!) In an important house this vestibule might be lined with tiles — a perfect place for gaily patterned ones! Otherwise washable walls and a concrete floor are excellent. Stormcoats live here, and rubbers too, and if it is large enough, the children’s carts and garden tools.

In an entry that I once devised, there was a platform four inches above the drain floor; on this platform two doors opened — one into the front hall and the other into the children’s playroom.

A side-entry is almost an essential in every country house, for the use of all those who come in mud-splashed or rainsoaked, whether they be children or grown people.

On the ground floor of house Number 1 (Fig. 45, page 97) is a rear entrance that opens on a small covered-over brick terrace that has turned out to be a perfect place in which to leave rubbers to dry and rain-coats to drip, and in which juvenile express carts can be parked in an orderly row, as vehicles are parked in any street.

THE BABY CARRIAGE PARKING PLACE

Where to put the baby carriage is nearly always a problem in a small house, since it graces neither the front porch nor the front hall. But if there be no other where to put it, there is nothing to do but put it where you can — unless you build a little lean-to garage for it somewhere at the back, where you can later substitute a go-cart stable or a little house for other personal properties of the children.

THE HALL LIVING-ROOM

Doing away with the hall and letting the stairs ascend from the living-room is practical in every house of small size, because it is space-saving. Moreover, the staircase is often a

SCREENED ENTRY

very attractive feature — especially in a Spanish house, or in any doubled-storied or openraftered, gable-ceilinged room.

Perhaps a gallery runs the length of one wall or more.

Perhaps, on the other hand, the house is Colonial or Italian — or any style of Cottage- American — and the ceiling is low and plastered flat.

The primary objection to putting the stairway in the living-room or making a livingroom of the hall, is that there is no privacy whatever in a room that the front door opens into, and through which servants or members of the family are continually passing. Another objection is that in cold weather the heat goes up to the roof or is drawn up the stairway to the second floor. The latter situation is easily overcome by putting a door at the head of the stairs, but a two-storied type of room in cold weather is freezing except next to the fire, where one is all too unevenly cooked. In a hot climate, however, a highstudded room that can be shuttered or opened on whichever side one chooses, and where an overhigh ceiling encourages the heat to mount as far as it can and stay there, is ideal.

The real point to consider in having the front door open directly into your living-room is whether you are so constituted that you like to leave the latch-string out and are delighted to welcome whoever walks in, or whether you are one who prefers seclusion. A hall living-room is usually defenseless against unwelcome interruptions — if interruptions are unwelcome.

The effect of a small entry can, however, be very well achieved by means of a screen, as shown in Figure 113. It might be necessary to have a five-leaf screen in order to reach the point where the stair hides the room. Or privacy can otherwise be contrived by putting paneling for a cer-

tain distance, in place of spindles, under the hand-rail of the stairs.

Many decorators suggest fixed screens, because they insist that movable ones blow down. A fixed screen is a wood partition built from floor to ceiling. One objection to a fixed screen is that it is neither wall nor furniture; a second objection is that it reduces the size of the room and, moreover, spoils its contour. An ordinary screen, on the other hand, is about six feet high, and the fact that it is distinctly furniture, and that you see the full width of ceiling and the upper part of the wall beyond it, gives you the impression of the room’s full size. Danger of its blowing down is easily overcome with angle-irons attached to the frame of each leaf and screwed to the floor. It is simple to unscrew it at any time.

In the foregoing diagram the entrance side of the screen is furnished with a small half-round table against the screen; there is an umbrella-stand at the left of the door, and a chest or a settee — on which a visitor can lay his coat and hat as he enters — opposite the edge of the screen. On the room side, the screen makes an excellent background for a sofa or for a long table.

DRESSING-ROOMS FOR VISITORS

As the living-room’s turn is not yet, let us go back to the entrance-hall in a house of sufficient size to include visitors’ dressing-rooms. In most houses of great importance, two dressing-rooms, one for women and one for men, are put as near the door of entrance as possible. Usually these rooms are normally reception- or sitting-rooms; sometimes they are definitely equipped as coat-rooms or dressing-rooms and are used for no other purpose.

The decoration and furnishing of a special coat-room for men consists merely of a long table, one or two chairs, a mirror, perhaps, and a door leading into a lavatory. In a house where entertaining on a large scale is habitual, there are perhaps two or three long broad shelves. Tables or shelves

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

are more typical in New York houses than coat-hangers; but there is not the slightest objection to a hanging-rod with one or two hat-shelves over it, especially if space be limited.

In the majority of houses — palatial as well as tiny — men’s coats and hats are hung in a closet by a servant or folded and left on chests or chairs in the hall. Perhaps there is a mirror and a cleared table, perhaps not.

The walls of an especial coat-room and lavatory are usually painted a medium-light tint, or perhaps they are papered with a marble paper — or any other plain finish.

If you like the word "powder-room” better than dressing-room, there is no reason why you should not so call the women’s room, especially since the women who use it usually powder their noses and never dress. And yet, it has a fussy suggestion of an overfrilled, hoop-skirted and garlanded dressing-table, gilt-edged mirrors thronged with cupids — and painting on satin!

On the other hand — and more likely — it might have walls of night-blue glass punctured with stars, a silver dressing-table recessed in a cabinet, the cabinet embellished with baguettes, and other furniture fringed with mirrors and curtains — in fact, anything at all! For this is one room, at least, that can be as elaborate or as bizarre as imagination can devise. Or it can be a perfectly plain little box of a room, papered in a small diaper pattern. It would be prettiest, perhaps, with a small sprigged design on silver ground, with nothing in it except some hangers on a clothes-pole, a mirror, and a door leading to a lavatory.

A most lovely dressing-table that shuts — when not in use — into the paneling of a reception room is illustrated in Plate 33.

DRESSING-ROOM EQUIPMENT

Apart from its extreme or moderate decoration, the equipment of a dressing-room should include a mirror with a good light over it, and a dressing-table — or console, or shelf — under it. The top of the dressing-table should be glass

— a mirror, if preferred — or marble. It should never be upholstered in unremovable material that can become grimed with ground-in powder or smudges of lip-stick. On it should be a comb and brush and hand-mirror; new powder-puffs in glass-paper envelopes; a container with dark powder and another with light; a pin-cushion with safety-pins, black and white and in assorted sizes. Also — in spite of bobbed fashion — assorted varieties of hairpins.

In the house of people who rarely have much company, dressing rooms are rather superfluous, since in this day of closed motors most people arrive in a perfectly tidy condition. Those who come from a distance are shown to a bedroom, otherwise women leave their wraps — and men their coats and hats — in the hall.

It is essential, however, that there be lavatories — or at least one for the men — somewhere on the entrance floor. A lavatory is the place of all places in which colorful plumbing fixtures most suitably belong. The deeper colors — cobalt or navy-blue, jade-green, lacquer-red, or even black — are especially appropriate in any downstairs lavatory, whether it open off a dressing-room, off the hall, or off the owner’s study.

THE RECEPTION-ROOM

A reception-room is rather more characteristic of a hotel, office, or institution than of a private house. In short, a reception-room is merely a small or medium-sized room with small-sized stiff furniture in it; no personal objects, and rarely more than the minimum of ornaments. A receptionroom in a house is used exactly aS it is elsewhere, as a sort of office in which to receive not too warmly welcomed strangers. Occasionally, rather rarely, it is attractively decorated with old woodwork or painted panels. At the moment, it is apt to be subjected to modernistic experiment ; but, even so, it has no personality whatever, since, except to show some one its new decoration or to see a stranger on business, members of the family rarely cross its threshold.

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

THE '“'“parlour”

One is tempted to spell parlor in the early Victorian way, with a "u,” because that is in keeping with the spirit of an institution that belongs characteristically in the Victorian era. Our grandparents had parlours in their houses; very often they had two, occasionally more. New York houses of the brownstone front period had almost invariably a front parlour and a back parlour, the one in front being rather more of a sitting-room, and the one in the back (usually a middle room and dark as ink) being reserved for evening use.

The New England parlour was — and still is — very stiffly furnished, with a prim white-ground wall-paper and mahogany furniture, upholstered in rep or hair-cloth. It was and still is — sat in only on formal company occasions or on Sunday afternoons.

The parlors of the old Southern houses that remained English Georgian were identical in every detail with the drawing-rooms of to-day, and may be spelled in the modern way. To these must, of course, be added the finer houses of Massachusetts, Connecticut, and all other Atlantic States, whose interiors never succumbed to the marble and walnut craze, the black era of ebony, or the mud-and-mustard epidemic of the bad-taste era. Beautiful parlors that were, are drawing-rooms of distinction in the vernacular of to-day, and Georgian woodwork — either painted or embellished with scenic or other Colonial paper — has again come into its own.

The withdrawing-room is merely a second drawing-room set apart for evening use or for candle-lighted occasions. It corresponds to what New Yorkers used to call the back parlor, and New Englanders called the front parlor and Southerners the best parlor; we now set it apart as the music-room, or any other room that is never used except when we g^ive a rather formal party. The party-room, however, is altogether different and will be mentioned later.

THE DRAWING-ROOM

If we were to divide the rooms of a house temperamentally, we would classify as impersonal: entrance-halls, reception-rooms, ballrooms, and music-rooms. The reasonwhy of the first two has been explained. The last two are essentially backgrounds. The object of a ballroom is to display an aggregation of people. The object of a music-room is to withdraw from attention — or at least it should be.

The room of intermediate temperament is the drawingroom, which should suggest the old-fashioned term of gracious but somewhat formal hospitality. It is also more than any other room dependent upon perfection of detail.

The term drawing-room is the fashionable equivalent of best parlor or the French salon, and its exactions, except in rather conservative social circles, are not in sympathy with typical modern American life. At all events, men and drawing-rooms seldom meet until after dinner, and children and drawing-rooms show each other enforced neutrality at best.

The difference between a living-room and a drawingroom is that the drawing-room demands a certain degree of formality alike in our occupations, our behavior, and our clothes, whereas the living-room is equally welcoming to us no matter what we happen to be doing, how relaxed our spines, how casual our dress, or how easy-going our character.

Whether a drawing-room or a living-room will better suit your house, depends entirely upon your own personality. If you are innately formal, if you cannot endure to have anything out of place, if you like the conventional rather than the unconventional, if you have no inclination to sit otherwise than as at a dinner party or in a box at the opera, if you have neither dogs (outdoor dogs) nor children; if, in other words, no one ever disturbs the perfect order of your surroundings, a drawing-room would be as suitable a livingroom as you could wish.

If, on the other hand, the room belongs to your husband

WHERE ENGLAND, FRANCE AND COLONIAL AMERICAN COMBINE IN HARMONY

A DESK THAT IS INVITING TO USE HAS EVERY NECESSITY SUPPLIED, BUT IS CLUTTERED WITH NOTHING ELSE

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

and to your children quite as much as to you; if you have sons and daughters who are inclined to sprawl at length rather than sit up straight, and who like to put their heels up on the sofa; if dogs as well as the younger children stand all over the cushions in preference to standing on the floor; or if part of the room is in inevitable confusion because of the momentary occupation of this one or that, nothing more unsuitable than a drawing-room, either in name or in fact, can be imagined.

However, let us suppose that you like a room of formality. Whether it is to be your only sitting-room or merely one of many, it will follow more or less faithfully a rather set formula in the arrangement of its contents.

THE PLACING OF DRAWING-ROOM FURNITURE

The first requisite of a drawing-room is grace of arrangement, with perfection of detail. Its comfort must in no way suggest relaxation. The seats, chairs, and sofas may perhaps have springs — or perhaps only cushions — but they are rarely, if ever, deeply upholstered. The typical drawingroom chairs and sofas have carved-wood frames, and little bolster-pads on the centers of the wood arms. Furniture that is particularly suitable for a drawing-room includes all the more simple Louis XV, Louis XVI, and Directoire French examples, as well as the work of the eighteenth-century cabinet-makers, whether English or American.

The arrangement of a drawing-room is as symmetrically balanced as possible, and the room should quite distinctly suggest the tranquil serenity of a great lady prepared to receive her friends. Furnishings and ornaments are displayed to their best advantage, and the arrangement of chairs and sofas is planned carefully for pleasantly convenient conversational grouping. There must always be a general circle of one or two sofas, with chairs and perhaps stools drawn near, in which as large a group as one is likely to have for lunch or dinner can be seated. In a room that is large enough, there

should be various other groups of chairs, so that twos and twos can sit apart in the usual after-dinner convention.

A diagram of a typical drawing-room is shown in Figure 114, page 345. One could not look at its plan and suppose it anything else. The essential group includes two settees by the fire and the long sofa opposite, each with its surrounding chairs and stools or benches. There are plenty of tables, and there is a lamp on each as well as side-lights. The individual lamps need not be brilliant, but there should be many, because of their decorative effect as well as for the pleasantness of light.

The furniture is all small in scale. There is not a really big sofa or chair in the room. Stools and small benches play a very important part, because they face in every direction. Drawing-room stools and benches have upholstered tops of velvet, brocade or needle-point. Wood-topped stools or benches are convenient in almost any room, since they can be used either as tables or to sit on. The upholstered drawingroom stool or bench, however, is used for nothing but to sit on, especially when talking to some one close by.

THE COLOR OF A DRAWING-ROOM

A drawing-room is usually light in color. Most typically the walls are lacquered white or cream or very pale green, possibly accented with gold or gray-blue, or grayed orchid, or light yellow or chartreuse — or stronger color if one chooses. The walls are most often paneled. Perhaps the field of each panel is decorated, or inset with paintings; or perhaps it is left plain and a picture or mirror is hung against it. Typical also of drawing-rooms are cabinets with shelves for the display of porcelains or jades or ivories.

Since the character of a drawing-room is inflexibly formal and conservative, these qualities should not be overlooked. Each object of furniture should look innocent of work. Papers — filed or otherwise — have no place on a drawing-room writing table; a work-basket is not admitted. An

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

embroidery frame is considered quite in key, however, since it connotes spare-time occupation rather than absorbed attention! (The fiction of this explanation is manifest to any one who watches modern petit-point enthusiasts in almost any drawing-room.) At all events, no one sits in a drawingroom and does the family mending! Nor does one glue, or cut out pictures, or hammer things. Those of us who love energetic occupations must stay in utilitarian living-rooms or build ourselves a workshop.

In average houses of many rooms, the drawing-room is used at certain times in the day — to take afternoon tea in, perhaps, or to meet in before dinner and to sit in for a while afterwards. But the typical American family leaves it for company and lives in the library, the living-room, the bookroom, the study (or den) , or the sitting-room. The drawingroom is essentially a room of beauty, and sometimes — if it be lived in — it is a room of enchantment.

A DRAWING-ROOM-LIVING-ROOM

We need a name to express the hybrid room that is equally a drawing-room and a living-room. It is a room that, while having many formal characteristics, is utterly a room of charm because it is lived in. It has not quite the personality of a living-room, because it has no easy-going characteristics.

The principal difference between the true drawing-room that is set aside for company, and the one that is lived in, is that the latter has easy-chairs and comfortable sofas, as well as others that are somewhat more reserved in character.

The big chair illustrated in Plate 26 is not at all in keeping with a formal drawing-room, and neither are the cigarette boxes and match-box on the near-by table. The desk, moreover, is a big one, and the opposite wall of the room has set-in bookcases; otherwise the taffeta curtains, the lightgreen brocade of the upholstery, and the jade and crystal ornaments are suggestive of a drawing-room.

In a drawing-room-living-room, the writing-table should

always be serviceable — either a secretary or a drop-front desk or a knee-hole one of size, with implements of utility on its blotter and many drawers in which the inevitable accumulations on every much-used writing-table are kept safely out of sight. In other words, the writing-table, though it is fitted with practical writing implements, is still an article of embellishment as well as one of utility.

Slip-covers, of course, have no place in drawing-rooms except as summer-coverings. But slip-covers and all other devices of beauty combined with utility and comfort are suitable to the room which is the very heart of the American home — the living-room!

THE LIVING-ROOM

One dreams of the living-room as a room filled with sunshine and appealing color; with comfortable furnishings and many books and flowers or plants; a room in which a big fire burns on cold days and evenings, and in which many lamps are lighted at night; a room — if it be in the country — that during the warm months of the year looks out upon a garden.

You know how well-worn, beautifully-polished hunting boots look? The perfect living-room should look the same: in no way worn through, but thoroughly easy and comfortable in fit — and with every evidence of having been long used and cared for.

Nothing is more out of keeping with the spirit of a living-room than to have it filled with brand-new furniture and upholstery, brand-new books in uniform bindings, curtains and over-curtains that keep out all the light and air; a fireplace that has no mark of fire-smoke and no trace of ashes on its hearth; a room with not a single potted plant or flower, not a sign of well-worn usefulness anywhere about it. Be chary, therefore, of reupholstering or recovering the living-room furniture simply because it shows signs of having been used — ^^and enjoyed. If there are too many evidences

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

of really worn-through shabbiness, then perhaps do over the worst pieces; but rather than conspicuous newness, even a patch or two will do no harm — until the time comes when an article really has to be made over somewhat new.

The living-room may be utterly beautiful ; but if it looks self-consciously fine, or if you feel that it is unsuitable for hard use and that its charm would be lost were its color to fade or its furnishings to become worn, then let us call it a parlor or a drawing-room, since a genuine living-room it is not.

The most ideal living-room that can be imagined — the sort that we love to go into and hate to leave — is a room of comfortable size, with windows on the long walls, flooded with sunshine, and — in the country — filled with flowers. In fact, if it be in the country, let us hope for windows on three sides, and have at least one of them looking out upon, or opening into, a garden. And let us hope that there is a welcoming log fire on every day that the weather is cold enough. Books there must be — lots of them; and a table spread like a small news-stand with magazines. There must be a much-used desk and constantly sat-in chairs, and for those of us who love animals, a dog or cat lying on the rug before the fire. Or, best of all, there may be a baby building blockhouses on the rug beside the dog; or a bigger child curled up in an armchair reading.

Some of the qualifications listed above are not always possible — especially in a city apartment. And what we cannot have, we must do as best we can without.

A living-room can be inviting without a fire; it can be appealing without a near-by garden; in the city it may have charm without a flower. It might even have appeal without a book, but it must have light and pleasing color and comfortable furnishings conveniently and becomingly placed. It must also express the combined needs and tastes of the family that lives in it. In other words, it may be without books if no one in the family especially loves them. It must perhaps be without flowers in the winter, unless some one

342 THE PERSONALITY OF A HOUSE _

in the family cares enough to grow them in pots. It would be difficult to make a living-room inviting without a flower in the summertime.

ESSENTIAL LIVING-ROOM DETAILS

Essential to every perfect living-room is light to read or work or sew by, both in the daytime and at night. There may be a number of lights that are for becomingness to the j-oom — like those in the drawing-room — but there must be others for utility. There must be one on the desk or writingtable that brightly illumines the paper you write on without casting the shadow of your hand or blinding you with a glare; and, no matter which chair or which corner of the sofa you choose, there must be a good light that turns on at your shoulder.

The color of the room should be soothing rather than exciting in tone, since it must be restful and sympathetic whether you are glad or sad, well or ill. If you paper the walls, be sure that the design is not one so decided in pattern that it unceasingly interrupts. Attention- attracting papers are quite all right in halls, dining-rooms, bathrooms and even spare rooms, but are altogether bad for a room that you must live in, rest in, and think in.

For comfort, chairs and sofas must be low and deep and completely restful. If the sofa tempts you to lie down on it, then it is just the sort of sofa that the living-room ought to have! (Not that you are likely to receive your friends flat on your back, or that visitors will throw themselves full length!) If its chairs be so low and deep that getting out of them again is difficult, no matter! Going away is the last thing a perfect living-room should make you think of.

One might describe a comfortable chair as one that completely supports you in a position of ease. The most uncomfortable chair ever imagined is the typical parlor-car chair that slaps you across the shoulders and then hollows away from your back the rest of the way down. Moreover, it

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

has an overwide carpet-stiff convex seat on which you bounce about continuously. For reading nothing is more comfortable than to sit in a chair that fits you comfortably, with a cushion on your knees and a book on the cushion. Personally, I like very soft springs; but this is a matter of preference. At all events, a chair-seat should be slightly lower at the back than at the front. One that slides downward from back to front edge — is desperately uncomfortable for anyone who sits normally straight.

If you are one who likes to read lying down, the rightsized small square-edged hair-cushion on your chest, with book-edge on this, is extremely practical. An attached bookrack for use on your own pet sofa or chair is rarely a becoming adjunct to a piece of furniture; but if you like it and use it, this is in some way expressed and makes it perfectly acceptable. In other words, the living-room expresses beauty when it may, but its foremost requirement is comfort and suitability to practical use.

THE WRITING-DESK

A perfect desk is one that invites you to write on it. A desk ornamented with irrelevant objects that leave scarcely any space on which to lay a sheet of paper, and no elbowroom — and probably neither pens, sharpened pencils, nor fluid ink to write with — is not inviting. A desk or writingtable should have a big sheet of blotting-paper. A flat sheet is much better than a small pad with high interfering corners that get in the way of your wrist and, if in any way ornamented, catch a thread in your sleeve.

There should be fresh ink, new pens, sharpened pencils, stamps, mucilage, shears, and paper-cutter, as well as plenty of paper and envelopes, ready at hand. (See Plate 33.) My own favorite writing-table (Plate 57) is an architect’s drafting-table with a top six feet by a little over three feet wide, and at standing height. Few people would care for this, nor would many be likely to choose a drop-leaf kitchen-table.

which is my next favorite. But the point is that a writingtable — or whichever other piece of furniture you use most — should be adjusted to your own personal requirements of comfort.

The fact that I happen to prefer to sit on a high office stool hour after hour, and day after day, is no reason that any one else should do the same. Nor could any piece of furniture be more impossible to fit into a room than a full-sized drafting-table — except perhaps an electric bathcabinet!

FOR CARD PLAYERS

There must be two bridge-lamps that can stand at opposite corners of every card-table — unless there be indirect lighting or a chandelier that affords genuinely bright light. For a card-playing family that has much company, nothing is more convenient, if there be room enough, than a permanently set bridge-table, so that if one cares to play one need not wait to have a table set up and cards and score-pads brought out. This is not practical except in the corner of a very large room, or in a separate room that is used only for cards. A bridge-table that is always in readiness is shown in Plate 63.

On the other hand, a tea-table perpetually set with an empty array is not an object of suitability, since outside of Alice in Wonderland the tea hour is not perpetual. Nor can the tea-tray stand always waiting with water boiling, cream in the pitcher, lemon in the saucer, cakes, sandwiches, and so forth. This is noted merely because modern taste in every way eliminates that which is useless.

Every object in every room should be necessary to comfort, to utility, or to beauty. Certain rooms accentuate these three requirements more than others. The living-room accents comfort; the kitchen, utility; the drawing-room, beauty ; which must not be thought to mean that the accenting of one requirement eliminates the need of the other two.

Bruce Price, Architect

LIVING-ROOM AND DRAWING-ROOM CONTRASTED

The two diagrams on this and the following pages are identical in floor plan, but entirely different in the character of their furnishings. Figure 114 is a drawing-room; Figure 115 is a living-room.

The drawing-room, as already noted, is furnished with as many small-sized chairs as possible, arranged for the sole

object of conversation. The consoles. A, and the cabinets, B, serve no purpose but that of embellishment. Over the consoles are tall mirrors, and the shelves of the cabinets are used for ornaments. The tables on either side of the wall sofa have tall lamps on them and a few conspicuously beautiful ornaments. Every lamp in a drawing room should, of course, be chosen for beauty of ornament rather than for utility. But if the drawing-room were also a living-room, the sofas and chairs would be somewhat larger in scale, the cabinets would probably be replaced by bookcases, and the lights by the fireplace and on either side of the long sofa would necessarily be good to read by.

The living-room is totally different in character. The furniture takes up much more room, the sofa in front of the window is huge, the chairs are wide and deep and low and utterly restful. The furniture is not grouped for conversation with visitors, but solely for the comfort of the family. The secretary is lighted from the left and is adequate in size. On the refectory table is a big lamp in the center and magazines in rows and at least one bowl of flowers. If the

OBVIOUSLY A LIVING-ROOM

owner of the room is musical rather than occupied with writing, the piano is perhaps turned around and put where the secretary stands. A radio would probably stand against the right-hand wall between the book-shelves and the windows.

ROOMS THAT ARE HARD TO FURNISH

A difficult room to endow with charm, or even to overcome the inborn awkwardness of, is one that is long and narrow and has a fireplace across one narrow end. And when

its only windows are across almost impossible to live in, unless it is so little that a sofa can just about stretch the wall between the windows and the fireplace. (Fig. 116.) But a
