AWKWARD PASSAGEWAY LIGHT-EFFACING SCREEN

room of this plan, if it be of hospitable size, is hopeless; because hospitable is just what it can’t be without elbowroom around the fireplace and windows near enough to be of use. A narrow-end fireplace is never as amenable to charm as one that is centered on the long wall.

A big, long room, such as is shown in Figure 117, necessarily divides itself into two units; one group near the fireless windows, and another over in the windowless darkness around the fireplace. Furnished as a living-room and diningroom in one, this is as hard a problem as can be found. If you put the table near the fire, there is nowhere to sit evenings. If you put the table at the window, you sit in the dining-room by day — unless you carry the table back and forth. But, no matter whether it be furnished as two or as one, a long distance between the chimney and the windows has no architectural excuse except that of unavoidable necessity — in an apartment building, for instance, where one room in one apartment may have to be sacrificed to the better plan of others.

A room such as I have sketched in Figure 118 furnishes itself. On the other hand, a room such as Figure 119 — an actual room in a Park Avenue apartment house — is as hard to do anything with as the far-end fireplace. It is utterly ruined by the blockading chimney. If the mantel-breast were flat, or

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

if it were on another wall, the room would be a lovely one. But with a mantel dividing the window space into two closetshaped alcoves, you cannot sit near the windows except behind the chimney. Doors make two long sofas impossible. The best you can do is to put a low table in the window, a sofa on each side beyond, and a sofa in the middle — which everyone going in and out of the door on either side has to walk around. It is not a blockade, because you enter within the fireplace circle; but the room is cut in half.

WHEN LIVING-ROOM, DINING-ROOM AND ENTRANCE-HALL

ARE ONE

Using a screen to make a vestibule has already been shown in Figure 113. In the little diagram. Figure 121a, a screen also makes a vestibule and hides the room from the stranger at the door. A room with windows down both long sides is, of course, perfection to furnish. In fact, it is almost impossible to make such a room unattractive, if the color be good and the furniture comfortable.

THE THREE-IN-ONE ROOM

For the three-in-one room of charm, it is essential to keep to the character of the livingroom. In short, do not put sitting-room furniture at one end and dining-room furniture at the other, but make the whole a living-room. The table on which you dine can

be made into a library table when not set for a meal. This is one occasion when a refectory table or a gate-leg table is not only suitable but essential. In any case, choose a table that will look like a living-room table, with a lamp and a few easily removed objects on it, rather than shout its dining-room character between meals. A much better effect is achieved if you have different chairs instead of the eight or ten replicas that are customary in a dining-room. Four side-chairs might be alike, with an armchair of a different style for the head of the table, an armchair of another style for the foot, and a pair or two of odd side-chairs to be used in another part of the room when not needed for the diningtable. Instead of a sideboard, choose a chest or a commode with so little on it in the way of ornament that it can easily be cleared and used as a serving-table at meal-times.

Dining-room furniture at one end of the room is a mistake, not because of any attempt to pretend that the room serves only as a living-room and that you have a diningroom elsewhere, but because it is as disturbing to unity as though the partition between two different rooms had fallen down. On the other hand, the dining-room end, when set for a meal, does not give the impression of being out of place, because a living-room background is cozy and homelike and altogether appealing to dine in. But a dining-room is not appealing as a room to live in, unless you can have sitting-room furniture in it and thereby cause it to lose the characteristics of a dining-room.

Where one room serves two purposes permanently, the living-room personality is without question the one to choose. Remember also that the dining- table should be unusually low as well as small.

A FEW COLOR SUGGESTIONS

Perhaps we should have considered earlier those two essentials, color and light. No room that is filled with the gloom of twilight by day and little spots of glimmer at night can possibly suggest that those who occupy it, live! And in a

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

living-room, quite as much as otherwhere, one cannot evade the subject of color.

The need of adding some warmth of color to a north room that is to be occupied in a cold winter climate is selfevident. A gray-blue fireless room on a cold day would be about as welcoming as the interior of a foodless ice-box. But a cold blue room lighted by the flames of a generous open fire is at once made friendly — especially if books or cushions or flowers or ornaments add color. It is the color and light of a fire, by the way, that add beauty to a room, not its heat. Were heat the sole object of a fire, it would be far more evenly and comfortably distributed by means of steam pipes than by a fire, in front of which you are too hot, and away from which you freeze.

If the windows face north and are, moreover, rather small, it will be safest, perhaps, to follow the most obvious rule of decoration that exists; which is to paint a sunless room sunlight -yellow. It should be remembered, however, that the yellow which is the color of the sun is as light a tint as possible, and that it should have a polished surface. Dark or pumpkin yellow with a mat finish is light-absorbing to the last degree.

For a room that has too small or too few windows, a pale yellow that has a lemon hue, or that is lacquered slightly creamish white, is the best wall and trim color possible; it will go with slip-covers of the gayest, most colorful pattern that can be found. Glazed chintz with white ground is

charming while it is new, but then - ! They say it can

be reglazed this side of England, but it is very apt to return from the ordinary cleaner’s looking like the kind of cotton material used for the underclothes of inexpensive dolls. On the other hand, endless chintzes can go into the tub and come out looking new, and can for that reason be both light in color and serviceable. But a big sample should be washed before buying. The pattern is best that is very much covered, since the white ground cannot be expected to stand hard use. Cost, by the way, is no criterion of wear. Expensive

materials do sometimes wear wonderfully, but sometimes those of highest price neither wash, clean, nor endure the sun. One of the most expensive chintzes ever put on the market faded out completely in three weeks. It is true that the very cheap chintzes are less apt to last than the best ones, but when they do launder and stand the sun, they do so perfectly. After all, it is not a great waste of time to wash half of a sample and to hang it in the sun for a week. If it will hold its color after a week’s test, so that you cannot tell which half of the sample is which, its color is obviously pretty safe.

Try wall-paper the same way. If it fades at all, beware of it!

For a living-room that has sufficient light, the walls at present preferred are of pine. Walls painted a pinkish-pine color are also very good.

DOORS OR DOORWAYS?

In a northern climate, a living-room should if possible have doors. Not merely because draughts sweeping in from the stair-hall perpetually disturb its temperature, but because it is sometimes pleasant to be able to shut yourself in. If you like open doors, it is always easy enough to let them stand wide; but if there are no doors, there are times-— before the furnace is lighted in the autumn, and after it has gone out in the spring — when the living-room, shut in, is easily made warm. And there are countless other times — if you are not feeling very well, or are having a business conversation — when it is convenient to be able to shut a protecting door. Doors are an essential of privacy; which is, after all, one of the essentials of civilization.

In the far South, doorless doorways are all right if one likes them, but shutter-doors are most practical. There is a "louver” door of commerce that is made with slats that open encouragingly to drafts, and likewise draftlessly close (when they don’t stick!).

THE LIBRARY OR THE BOOK-ROOM

The name Library brings two pictures to mind. The first is of the most comfortable, the most restful, the most inviting room in the house; in other words, it is a living-room filled with books. The second picture suggests nothing so much as a mausoleum built by a newly-rich man for unread books interred in crypts of glass. A human room filled with books is usually called something else — living-room, study, book-room, or "father’s room.”

Just why is not very clear, but the word Library means a room that is importantly big, scholarly, perhaps, but rather pompous — and very, very expensive. On the other

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

hand, we think of a book-room as a nice, comfortable, homey sort of place — or is it rather precious?

At all events, a book-room is apt to be small, though, of course, it need not be. At its very best the book-shelves occupy every inch of room from floor to ceiling. They are open shelves — not a glass front among them. (If the bindings have to be put behind glass, then label the room a library.) Book-room books are in every variety of bindings. A few shelves of sets, other shelves of somewhat matched mixtures, still others of single books of every size; tall ones, little ones, thick ones, thin ones, ranged together according to subjects. Fascinating shelves! Their very variety would turn the most indifferent of us into book-lovers.

An ideal book-room might be no more than big enough to hold an easy-chair, with a book-rest on it, or apart from it. There should be a shelf or two that pull out like the writingboards of office desks, for convenient use when necessary.

The room illustrated in Plate 36 is no bigger than the picture shows. The lantern hangs in the exact center of the ceiling. Opposite the window is the door of entrance, and the walls are solidly lined with books.

The library-study of a notable author, illustrated in Plate 37, is one of the most livable rooms imaginable. The wainscot and bookcases are of dark oak; there is an Elizabethan mantel of great beauty on the wall facing the book shelves shown in the photograph. Other book-shelves are out of the picture. The curtains and several chair-covers are of crewel embroidery in brilliant orange and green on sandcolored linen; the small sofa in the foreground is of sand-colored wool-damask; the long sofa is of morocco leather; the big chair (with another one out of the picture) is covered in a very gay linen on a burnt-orange ground. The desk back of the small sofa with a daughter’s doll on it is of green lacquer. The Eastern rug repeats the brown, orange, and green colors of the room.

It is not necessary that the walls of a library be completely lined with books, but books should really dominate

the room; otherwise it is a living-room or a sitting-room. It, is rather pretentious to call a room a library that is not filled with books — unless their value makes up for their quantity. A room lined with novels might be the fiction room, or the room of romance, or the yellow-back room, or what you choose; but it is really neither a library nor a book-room. All of which makes no difference at all, except as it bears upon the personality suggested by names.

Having criticized glass-front bookcases, I should add that any great amount of glass genuinely handicaps the personality of a room. Very choice books that must be put behind glass would perhaps better be considered as lockedaway treasures than as furnishings. A really beautiful bookcase or a bookcase-secretary is embellishing to a drawingroom or living-room, because furniture is in character there, rather than books. But books, to be inviting and to seem alive, should stand in welcoming rows on friendly open shelves.

A little book-closet can be an entirely satisfactory spot, if it be big enough for a window, three walls of books on open shelves, and an easy-chair. One that I know of has a door covered with book-shelves that swings on a central steel pole like a turnstyle (Fig. 126). What more could one ask, except a fire and room for a dog — and perhaps the latter might squeeze in though the former is doubtful.

SPEAKING OF DOGS

Let us not forget to mention — and this is as good a place as any — that very necessary piece of furniture, a bench for your friend the dog. In hot climates no provision other than the floor is necessary for a big dog; but in cold weather there is a strong draught on every floor. On many floors — ^just put

A WINDOW, A CHAIR, AND books!

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

your hand down and feel it blow! Dogs run out and come in with damp coats, and lie down on a draughty floor; no wonder those without a stick of furniture of their own are often crippled with rheumatism long before they are old!

A proper bench should be at least ten inches above the floor and be surrounded on three sides — like a solid wood armchair. It is made in general accord with the furniture of the room and has a snugly fitting carpet that matches — as well as possible — the rug or carpet of the floor; and it is made suitable in size to the size of the dog.

THE DINING-ROOM

An east exposure for those who go to breakfast — and most Americans do — is very pleasant if it can be contrived. But if one must choose between morning sun in the diningroom and morning sun in the sitting-room, there can be little question whether it be better that husband read the headlines in the paper by sunlight, or whether wife shall have hours of sun in the morning-room or sitting-room — or wherever she spends the greater part of her day and does whatever it is that she has to do.

In building your own house in the country or on a lot that is big enough to give you light on all sides, you can place your house purposely so as to give each room whichever exposure you like best. Ideally, perhaps, a dining-room should face southeast, so that it may have lunch-time light as well as morning sun. At all events, whether it be sun-flooded or partially electric-lighted, the decoration of the dining-room can be as colorful as you please — since it is the one room in the house that should express cheer rather than rest. If it can have sun, so much the better. If it cannot, then it must be made as gay by night-light as possible.

And yet it is essential that a dining-room express certain conventions of form. Dining-room furniture need never be more than moderately comfortable. The diningtable is not the place at which to sit and dream, or to brood,

or to work out problems. Since one goes into the diningroom to eat — conversation being an accompaniment rather than an object — the furnishings should be centered on the table.

One is supposed to eat more tranquilly in an unencumbered room. At the moment it is the fashion to keep one’s silver in a closet or a safe, and to put out only those things which have decorative value. The tea-set on its tray is displayed in many — in fact, most — dining-rooms. Otherwise candlesticks or candelabra, or an especially beautiful tray or tankard, or a pair of coolers, is chosen to ornament the dining-room sideboard or consoles, and the rest of the silver is put away.

Dining-room walls are equally suitable with much decoration or none at all. Especially appropriate to dining-rooms, as well as to halls, are panoramic papers which merely suggest that one is eating amid pleasant scenery. If one eats often alone, the companionship of a view such as that of the "Voyage en Suisse,” or the "Fetes de Versailles,” or the purely scenic "Eldorado,” or the friendliness and gaiety of "Scenic America,” shown in Plate 39, can be attested to by everyone who has long lived with any of these.

A plain-walled dining-room has always been looked upon as the most suitable place to hang old portraits. At one time the color of the typical dining-room was red. Now it is most often cream, or putty, or light gray-green, or blue-green, or yellow-green, or yellow. The dining-room walls, as a matter of fact, might be decorated in a hundred ways — and each one a perfect way for whoever makes it express his or her personality. A plain-walled dining-room is illustrated in Plate 38, which shows a lovely Georgian room with a portrait over the mantel and Queen Anne furniture.

DINING-ROOM FURNITURE

The essentials are the dining-table and the needful number of chairs. This is all the furniture that is necessary; to it,

THE FURNITURE IS EMERALD GREEN

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

of course, you add as much or as little as you choose to find room for. But remember that empty space is far better than furniture that crowds close to the table. Perhaps you have room for a sideboard, or even for a pair of them. Or perhaps you choose consoles instead. You, of course, put a servingtable near the door to the pantry, select as many chairs as you need, and perhaps a screen; but even in the biggest room this is enough.

If you are furnishing a new room with old furniture of your own, you naturally adjust it as best you may. But if you are buying furniture, a much better effect will be achieved by avoidance of duplication.

With chairs of the type of Chippendales, you will perhaps choose a Sheraton sideboard, or a pair of Adam consoles, and quite possibly a dining-table in the style of Phyfe. If you choose Stuart or Elizabethan chairs, you will probably choose a bread-and-butter cupboard. Or if you choose old Colonial, you may choose a plate-cupboard or perhaps .a milk-bench. If you choose painted furniture, it is usually best to vary its design somewhat — unless it be without ornament of any kind.

It is scarcely probable that I was the originator of the present vogue of painted furniture in dining-rooms; but it is true that the first painted dining-room furniture that I ever heard of was some mahogany of Sheraton design that I had painted emerald green. That furniture is now very somber, but then it was almost outrageous. I also had a decidedely unorthodox table made, for which I am now duly penitent. It was — and is — thirty-eight inches wide and six feet long. Its top is (most inconveniently) a solid piece of wood that neither pulls out nor shuts smaller. As its dimensions are at the moment not at all unusual, it might be well to list its faults: In the first place, it is too high (30^ inches from the floor) , and the frame which holds the top comes within 1^2 inches of the edge, and prevents its being cut lower without interfering with knee-room. But its greatest objection is that its length is unalterable. For two at table

the coziness of sitting six feet apart may be imagined, and for more than eight, end-tables must be added or a detachable top put on, making it still higher! As for sitting at it alone, the top of a moving-van would be just about as appealing! (This wrong-sized table is pictured in Plate 39.)

But in a room that is very narrow for its length, a long narrow table is obviously suitable. Moreover, tables that are from twenty-seven to thirty inches wide are rather attractive even for two or three or four persons to sit at, if they face each other across the center in a compact group, instead of using the distant ends (Figs. 127 and 128). But when a table is more than three feet wide it becomes clumsy.

and sitting so as to face each other crossways is simply awkward. The little table shown in Plate 5 5 is perfect in shape and size for a family of two or three. Those who have used square or round tables have discovered that the regulation square or round covers do not fit them. Nor can refectory table-linen be used on any other table.

The refectory table shown in Plate 22 is about eight feet long and thirty inches wide, and is ideal to sit at facing a friend across it. The photograph cannot show the color of the room, which has a glowing quality of warmth that, combined with a substantial as well as hospitable personality, appeals to everyone — especially to a man.

WHY THE BREAKFAST-ROOM?

In a very big house, where the dining-room is used only for very big dinners, and the table for the family alone

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

looks like a peppermint in the center of an empty bureaudrawer, a smaller dining-room is perhaps essential — especially as waste space is of no importance in such a house. But in a

little house - ! Why should space which is more precious

than anything else be so senselessly squandered? Why is not one room to eat in — indoors — enough? If the dining-room is dark, why is it dark, if there was space in the sun to build a breakfast-room? In other words, if the breakfast-room be pleasanter to eat in than the dining-room, then why not eat there always? Why must breakfast be eaten pleasantly, and lunch in gloom?

A summer dining-room is quite another thing. Who would not like to eat breakfast, lunch, and dinner on a screened porch jutting into a garden, on a beautiful summer day? In mild weather, an outdoor dining-room is perfection; then why not build the dining-room — if there be only one — like a sun-porch, with windows let down into the window-frame, after the manner of automobile windows, so that it can be entirely closed or entirely open? And, of course, if you like breakfast-loggias, breakfast-rooms or breakfast-nooks, there is no possible rule against them. Nor, for that matter, is there any rule against having seven diningrooms and eating in a different one each day in the week! Why not? Some one might like the idea very much. A blue room for Blue Monday, a rose-colored one for Saturday, and so on. Again and again let us repeat that each person’s house should be built and decorated and furnished precisely as each person likes best!

A PARTY-ROOM

If your house is big enough for a special party-room — or, perhaps best of all, if you can convert a barn into such a room — it might be amusing to decorate it rather riotously; that is, if your idea of a party in any way connotes riot of sound or rhythm of color. A perfect party-room in a barn might have walls hung with gay posters and tin lighting-fixtures, a circus-tent ceiling, a floor highly waxed and

covered with easily removable druggets; gay sofa-benches around the edges, plenty of chairs and tables, but no ornaments except cigarettes, match-boxes, and ash-trays. There is no excuse for lack of an enormous fireplace, since nothing is easier than building a chimney on the outside of a barn.

At one end of the party-room it would be useful to build a small stage for either professional or amateur actors or singers, or for musicians at a dance. At one side there would undoubtedly be a soda fountain, or a lunch counter, or whatever you wish to call it, and a shuffle-board, hop-scotch, or badminton court or plan of some other game painted on the floor. There might also be ping-pong tables decorated on their under sides and arranged to pull up against the tent-top when not in use. Or the decoration of a party-room that is part of the house might be entirely different: It might be in the modern theme of gray and black and steel and white. On the other hand, it might break every rule of color and outdiscord discord!

THE BEDROOMS

So many questions of individual preference enter into the plan and furnishing of your own bedroom that little is possible in the way of suggestions beyond those already made in an earlier chapter. The important thing is to divide the space as best suits your own ideas of comfort.

Considering a bedroom solely as a room that is set apart for sleep and rest, the most suitable wall might be a misty gray-green with ceiling a little lighter, a browny-green floor, green Venetian blinds to shut out the light from the windows as much or as little as you please. It should have a bed that is ideally comfortable, a chaise longue on which to rest by day. And that is all. Such a room should certainly suggest sleep and rest.

But the typical bedroom has many other requirements, according to the uses to which it is put. A dressing-table is rather essential, since it is an item of bedroom furnishing,

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

whether one use it or not. Clothes containers are necessary in the average room, but whether one chooses such bedroom objects as bureaus, chiffoniers, and bed-stands, or whether one prefers sitting-room furniture instead, is a matter of personal taste.

WHERE TO PLACE THE BED

The most important item in the furnishing of a bedroom is the placing of the bed. Some people like to sleep with their heads on the window-sills; most people — whether they like it or not — sleep with their feet to the light and their heads to the wall, because that is the way every architect and builder delights to place the beds. And, of course, if you do not like air blowing on you; if you do not care about going to sleep looking out at the sky, or at whatever your near-by window looks out on; if you get up the moment you wake and go downstairs for breakfast; if you are never ill — in other words, if the architect’s pet position for the beds suits you perfectly well, no further thought on your part is necessary, because foot-to-the-window is exactly where the bed will be.

But if you have the European habit of taking your coffee in bed, you may as well prepare to read your mail and the morning paper with the light in your face and the page you are trying to see in total darkness — unless you arrange the wall lengths to suit yourself. If you have the American godown-to-breakfast habit, you may think that light to read by in bed is of no interest to you at all; but if you ever are half-way ill, or recovering from an illness, the only thing you can do when you wish to read will be to shift your pillow and lie with your head at the foot of the bed. Otherwise, it means lying all day in a sliced-off piece of room, looking at the wall six feet away.

This, however, does not mean that there is a certain place which every one agrees on as being best for a bed. I am merely saying that you should consider all the possibilities that concern your own personal comfort.

That a bedroom sofa should have its head to the light is even more important than light to read by in bed, since many people rest on a sofa who never read by daylight in bed. And yet a chaise longue, as often as not, is placed with the head to the wall.

The diagram shown in Figure 129 is that of a room furnished by a foremost decorator. It is very attractive in color and in detail, but the placing of the furniture — if the room be that of a woman who is rather an invalid — could hardly be any worse. Figure 130 shows the same furniture rearranged. Now its light is not only comfortable to read by in bed.

but it is back of your head instead of shining in your face whether you read or rest on the sofa. In the rearrangement, the desk is not in a very good place, but as it is one that is rarely if ever used, the electric desk-light answers perfectly well. Were the desk really used, it would be practical to put a very small dressing-table in the window, with a pictureframe mirror on it that could be laid flat at night, and a writing-table at right angles to the other window; and the sofa could be put across the foot of the bed.

BEDROOM FURNITURE

The furniture may be strictly bedroom in character, or may not. In the simplest sort of little cottage, genuine bed-

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

room furniture is suitable and becoming; but in a room of importance, to install a large set consisting of twin beds, chiffonier, bureau, dressing-table, bed-tables, occasional tables and chairs, each piece in the precise style and color of every other, is pretty sure to spoil the chances of achieving a room of personality. If the furniture be unusually attractive, with not too many pieces, and if the walls and hangings and bed-trimmings be lovely and colorful, it may be possible to digest the overdose of monotony — but it is far from likely.

If you have only moderate closet space and it is necessary to include the entire bedroom furniture list, then at least let the different items be somewhat varied. For instance, in the cottage bedroom shown in Plate 49 the furniture was all chosen separately and then painted to agree but not to match. The beds are pale spring-leaf green, with a deer and fawn standing under trees of darker green; the top drawer of a chiffonier has a similar decoration, but in a lower and longer design. The bureau in the picture is painted light greenish yellow, with a silhouette landscape in green— about the color of the green furniture — on the top of it; in front of the setback, two armchairs are green with a srnall scene painted on the splat; two side-chairs are yellow with a very slender green vine around the splat; the desk is plain green with no trimming whatsoever. Two tables are green, one plain, the other with a small forest vista that is more yellow than green In other words, although the designs repeat the same hues of green and yellow, the individual pieces are greatly varied both in design and yalue, and although one might perhaps call the furniture a "set” since it was obviously painted to go together, the room gives no impression of monotony. The contradictory requirements of unity and variety are fair y

well illustrated in this particular room.

In a bedroom— either large or small— where there are not many pieces of furniture, a bed, chest, table, and two chairs that are alike, look very well. But even so, interest is greatly increased by changing outlines somewhat, or adding or taking

away details of ornament. In a room that has five pieces of painted furniture, it is safe to decorate one piece rather elaborately and another slightly, and leave the rest plain except for a slight accenting of the paneling.

A pet method of my own is to have one drawer of a chest or bureau or desk decorated, or possibly to use a design that is painted across the space of two drawers, but rarely to spot the front of a piece of furniture with repeated design. By varying the amount of decoration, by reversing the color, even by adding supplementary color here and there, and by slightly changing the values, you give the whole room interest and beauty.

In Plate 10, the desk in the corner, which is of lemonyellow with maple interior, has across the lowest drawer a scenic design in yellow-green and brown. The rest of the furniture in this room is of maple — some of it antique, some of it new. The chintz is flame-color with dogwood flowers in white and lemon. The organdie bed-hangings are flamecolor. The mirror candlesticks and shades are orange-red lacquer, the toilet articles are amber. The floor is maple, the rug sand-color, and the wall-paper a tiny light red design on an ivory-white ground.

For nearly every type of room it is well to remember that variety which vccilly tn-cutis intcvcst — can be provided with a little thought. We all know that color will do most to make a room attractive, but that interest, plus attractiveness, plus comfort, can alone produce the sort of perfection which the helpless look at in bewilderment and say: "I don’t know how you do it!” And yet to provide these three sample requirements is the whole secret.

If the furniture of a room be of unpainted wood, there is no possible excuse for monotony, since pieces that are in accord without being replicas are easy enough to find. Rosewood and mahogany ; maple, birch, and fruit woods ; walnut, butternut and dark oak; even painted pieces and wood pieces all go together perfectly, if one but pay attention to texture and to scale.
