A BEDROOM OF COMFORT IN A HOUSE OF DISTINCTION AND BEAUTY

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

WHAT IS MEANT BY COMFORT

You naturally know what you need for your own comfort better than anyone can possibly suggest. And yet habit is so strong that many of us unthinkingly accept discomforts that we are used to, such as inconveniently placed furniture and electric -light tortures, without stopping to consider whether they might not be quite easily changed.

Therefore, to come back to the subject of placing the bed, which we started this section with, and then wandered away from — let us repeat the advice to be sure to place your bed where you like to have it.

A bedroom that is my personal idea of a fairy-tale come true is illustrated in Plate 40 ; and yet to countless others the position of the beds may have no allure at all. To one who is afraid of sleeping in a current of air, or to one who has never through illness realized the dullness of the average bed’s view, or to one who does not like to go to sleep looking out at the stars, or to one who would evade the bright eye of the early morning sun, such closeness to the windows may not appeal. In that case nothing is easier than furnishing a bedroom precisely as the millions upon millions of other bedrooms are furnished!

Such bed-in-the-window arrangement is not entirely new — one is even illustrated in Plate 45 — but the beauty with which it is done is outstanding. Usually such a windowseat bed is chosen for a single room, since few rooms have windows on opposite sides, and at right angles the beds do not balance so well. Moreover, were the dressing-table windows in the illustration missing, half of the charm and comfort of the room would be lost. Such arrangement is, of course, impossible for a double bed, since the bed could not be made up, and its alcove would be too deep.

A sleeping-porch room with nothing in it but the beds, two chairs, and two night-tables, and then a dressing-room and bath for the wife and a dressing-room-bath for the husband, is an arrangement that has already been suggested.

GUEST-ROOMS, SPARE ROOMS, THE CONVERTIBLE SITTING-ROOM

Guest-room is the word that one writes in a book such as this, but spare room is the term that one uses when speaking. Thus the two may mean the same thing. But for the purpose of making a distinction between them, let us say that a guest-room should be rather formally exacting, and that spare rooms should merely be as comfortable and as inviting as the capacity of an average house and the possibilities of average taste can make them.

The convertible sitting-room is of especial use in the small house that has very occasional overnight visitors. It will therefore be fully considered in Chapter XX.

The best guest-room arrangement for a man and wife in a house of importance provides two rooms alike in size — one with a wide double bed, the other with twin beds, and each room with a bath of its own. This arrangement allows the guests to sleep where and as they select. In a house of great luxury a sitting-room is also included. The sitting-room is not necessary, but two rooms and one bath, or at least a room, a bath, and a dressing-room, are the minimum requirement of an important house.

This does not mean that those who live simply cannot ask whoever they please to stay with them, but merely that if a house makes great pretense of luxury because of its style and its size, its guest-rooms must naturally be in keeping.

THE GUEST-ROOM OF PERFECTION

The house we love to stay in is not necessarily one of greatest importance — not by any means. But certain comforts it must have, unless we are numbered among the rare few who care nothing — or perhaps know nothing — about the details of comfort which those who are fastidious find it hard to do without.

At all events, it makes not the least difference in the world

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

whether the interior decoration of a spare room be elaborate and its furniture of fabulous value, or whether its walls be whitewashed and the entire contents of the room have no intrinsic value at all. But there is one requirement that cannot be overlooked, and that is its comfort, its convenience, judged by the standards of its occupants.

This brings us back to the constantly repeated question: How do you live? How do your friends live? The importance or simplicity of your house may mean much or nothing. People who live beautifully live just as beautifully whether their surroundings are those of the simplest farmhouse or those of a palace. On the other hand, people who have no knowledge of the appointments of a beautifully run house live just as badly in a palace as in a shack. The difference is merely that in a shack perfect appointments are not expected, while in a palace their absence is inexcusable.

The essential requirements of comfort for every guestroom of perfection, whether it be in a palace or in a little but well appointed house, include:

1. A bathroom of its own.

2. A delightful bed.

3. Plenty of light and air.

4. Choice of pillows.

5. A perfect dressing-table.

6. A good light to read by in bed.

7. Heat in cold weather.

8. Cross-ventilation in hot weather.

9. A sofa to rest on.

10. Situation as free from noise as possible.

In a house of perfect appointment, there must be a bathroom that belongs to and adjoins each bedroom, or that is at least situated between two bedrooms and shared by a husband and wife, or members of a family. If you have to go out into the hall to your bathroom you might as well go any distance. Stationary wash-stands, by the way, belong only in the servants’ wing, where a bath for each room is not possible; or in a small hotel or boarding-house which

is not equipped with a bath for each room. But in a house of quality, ^'running hot and cold water in a master s bedroom is as unthinkable as a laundry tub!

If for some reason of your own you must install a washstand in a bedroom, then set it into the paneling of the wall (like the dressing-table in Plate 31). For that matter, any or all bathroom fixtures could be set behind paneling. But, except in an apartment where additional space is impossible, to build on a small bathroom would be a great deal better — and cheaper.

Equally important is a restful and well-equipped bed. No matter how unpretentious a house may be, no visitor should be invited to spend the night on a thin, hard, or lumpy mattress, nor on springs that sag like a hammock and are fit only for one who has had training as a sailor. The only bed that can with truth be described as good, is one that has a thick mattress of spongy consistency — either reasonably new or newly made over — on coil springs. In a primitive house, the springs can perfectly well be open instead of boxed-in with ticking."* Hammock springs can be made fairly flat by putting an apple-dumpling folded comforter or at worst from one to three pillows between two mattresses, to take up the slack.

THE EQUIPMENT OF A GOOD BED

In cold weather and for those who like a soft bed, an old silk-covered wool-filled quilt is more comfortable than the usual cotton-mattress cover. The under sheet should be at the very least two feet bigger on all four sides than the mattress, so that it will stay smooth and tucked in. If your sheets are somewhat short, add a strip of sheeting (or muslin will do perfectly) across the bottom, so that the sheets need go no further down than the bottom edge of the mattress, with the muslin used as a tuck-under. If sheets are narrow

* open springs are especially suitable in houses at the seashore, where unpaInted iron crumbles with rust. Open coil springs are easily repainted where the paint chips off. Those under ticking give no warning of their disintegration until they rust through!

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

as well as short, sew muslin at the sides and keep these sheets for under ones. There is really no excuse for a bed that cannot be properly made. Long sheets are especially necessary for a tall man. Not one man in a thousand knows the difference between linen and cotton sheets, but every man is made miserable by bed-covers that are not long enough to tuck thoroughly down.

Blankets should, of course, be warm and light in weight. A wool-filled quilt folded over the foot of the bed is better than one filled with eider-down (which is too hot for most climates) , unless the eider-down quilt is especially long and used in place of blankets, with a thin quilt or blanket as an extra.

An item that most hostesses forget is that for every single bed there must be two pillows, one of ticking or coutille, well stuffed with feathers, and another of very soft sateen filled with down, so that one may sleep on the sort of pillow one is used to.

SHEETS OF SILK, LINEN OR COTTON

It is far from necessary to provide silk sheets. It is not even necessary, in an unpretentious house, to have linen. In fact, fine cotton sheets, though usually with linen pillowcases, are preferred by many. But remember there is cotton and cotton. One very well-known New England mill makes cotton of a texture that is glass-smooth, handkerchief-soft, and ideal for those few who do not like the coldness of linen. In the ultra-modern house, silk sheets and pillow-cases to match the room are not unusual. If you like silk, therefore, flat silk is best. But if it is crepe-de-Chine, be sure that it is not inclined to be pebbly and rough. After all, if you choose to sleep on silk you are apt to expect it to be smooth.

Those of us who prefer linen to silk, but who are attracted by color, can easily have as much linen as we choose, dyed. Pink, apricot, and peach dye beautifully. Blue and

green are not apt to turn out so well; but neither are we likely to choose blue or green for bed-furnishing. Whatever bed-coverings we choose let them be smooth and fine. Cotton blankets as heavy and as hard as a layer of books, with fuzzy cotton sheets, do not make a bed that one delights to sleep in.

A COMFORTABLE DRESSING-TABLE

A dressing-table of comfort means one that has a good light by day and by night. The best possible light by day is one that is set directly in a window. Plate 40 illustrates a lovely dressing-table perfectly placed. The one shown in Plate 10 is very simple, but very practical. Any two small tables with a few drawers in them can be set at a convenient distance apart and be covered with a glass top. The top is a plain piece of fairly thick glass the size of the tables and the space between.

But at night, lamps or candles standing on the dressingtable are of no use whatever! A bright light on your combs and brushes and whatever else is laid out on top of the dressingtable is little compensation for a head that is in total darkness.

(Fig. 131.)

Even the lights in Figure 132, which are quite high, are impossible to see by. High lights would be fairly

IMPOSSIBLE

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

good if made so that they could be brought forward on slides which would bring them on a line with your shoulders, as indicated in Figure 133; but they must stand as high as your head and have tip-shades to form reflectors on both sides. The only dressing-table light that is genuinely serviceable is a drop-light that hangs directly over the chair on which you sit at table, with a wide drum or umbrella shade. A good bright light can be hung high enough to be well above your head when you stand. (Fig. 134.)

In a bedroom of my own there is a dressing-table five feet wide by only fourteen inches deep. (I have been cutting thinner nearly all the furniture I own.) It has a mirror-top, a full-sized mantel-mirror back of it, and two very tall lamps with three-sided shades — meaning that one side is left open. I thought that I had evolved a very bright idea, and that by turning the shades around and directing the light as I pleased, they would be perfect. But they are no better than any other failures. They either shine in my eyes or devote their illumination to the combs and brushes!

OTHER REQUIREMENTS OF A GUEST-ROOM

Do not clutter the dressing-table of your guest-room with an array of brushes and combs and manicure sets

POSSIBLE WITH SLIDES TO STAND TALL LAMPS ON

which take all the space that the visitor needs for her own things. A whisk-broom or clothes-brush is sometimes useful, as is also a pincushion with assorted safety-pins, as well as a small work-box with needles and threads, scissors, and thimbles of two sizes. The average thimble, about No. 10, is utterly unusable by one who has thin fingers. One No. 9 and one No. 7 would provide the proper size for almost anyone — if flower-pot rather than cuff -shaped.

An outstanding requirement of a spare room is a good light at the head of the bed. Let some member of the family — one who has a habit of reading in bed — try the guest-room bed-lights and report on them. The little squat lamp with screwed-on snuffing shade and a 2 5 -watt bulb is only too well known to all of us who at one time or another have tried sitting propped up in bed, with book in one hand and light held over it in the other! Comfortable? What do you think?

On the writing-table should be a blotter, fresh ink, variously assorted pens, a sharpened pencil, and a scratch-pad, as well as good house-stamped paper and envelops. There should be a few stamps, a pair of paper-scissors, and a small bottle of mucilage.

A sofa should have a few good pillows (instead of dozens of pincushion samples) and a quilt folded across the end of it.

Venetian blinds are the very best shades, since they shut out as much light as you choose, and let in air. But they must be fitted properly into grooves on either side of the windowcasing, or they rattle and slap all night through.

If the windows are loose, have thin wedges made and attach these with cords to the window-casings, so that they can be easily pushed in beside a rattling window sash. Also have the shutter fastenings kept in repair.

An eye-bandage is an indispensable item for one who sleeps with every window opened wide. They sell them at the sporting-goods stores, but a four-inch cuff-shaped bandage of smooth dark silk, with two pleats at the top to keep

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

it from sliding over the face, cannot be too highly recommended to those who would have air without light.

The books for a spare room might include one or two best-sellers, a go-to-sleep book of essays on topics that are not too absorbing, a book of short stories — perhaps one of the yearly collections — and half a dozen magazines of greatest diversity. But there is not much use in putting books in your spare room if there is no light by which to read or even to guess at the subjects of the pictures!

FURNITURE TO SUIT EACH VISITOR

In all hotels there are furniture-storage rooms from which suitable pieces are taken for the comfort of certain guests, and to which other pieces are returned. A private house might very successfully do the same.

A dressing-table in the center of the biggest window in the room will meet the approval of every woman who takes any interest in her looks. But it is a blockading nuisance to a man. Plenty of space in front of the windows will be much more acceptable to him.

A tall man is made utterly wretched in a short single bed. Remember, therefore, that if you have two spare rooms — one with a double bed and one with a single bed — and two men visitors, it is not a question of giving an older man the better room but of giving the taller one the double bed.

IF YOU WOULD HAVE A ROOM IN BEST TASTE

Do not throw cushions about on the floor. And do not pile your bed with an unrestrained assortment of pillows on top of the bedspread. Remember that suitability and sincerity are the fundamentals of good taste, and good taste scarcely recommends — except on a beach or in a beach cabin — that people of distinction lie around on cushions on the floor. Therefore, if not to lie upon on the floor — why cushions? Soft, squashy ones are of little use as footstools. Neither

is it good for the counterpane to lie down on top of it, which is what a collection of pillows invites you to do. One or two pillows of unusual beauty might be considered decorative, but dozens of overtrimmed and thoroughly uninteresting as well as useless baby pillows — decked in lace and ribbons — belong with the doll-petticoated telephone and all the other footless baby-talk trimmings that proclaim arrested development of taste.

THE DESTRUCTIVE DRESSING-STAND GESTURE

The following is not a bit important, but it might as well be included as a warning to guests. On every Colonial bureau in the average guest-room is a little Sheraton, Hepplewhite, or Cottage Colonial dressing-stand, with a drawer in it. In front of this there is plenty of bureau ledge, on which, many a time and oft, most of us have stood a clock or a bottle of perfume or other object, and then pulled the drawer out and sent clock or bottle crashing! Why do we? And why will we continue to put the clock out of order and spill our best perfume on the carpet, which probably turns yellow? Who knows?

BATHROOMS

A short while ago a magazine article by a well-known writer on interior decoration urged that one bathroom in a house with four or five master’s bedrooms is quite enough, if it be of large size. Which was very much like declaring that one sponge for the use of the family is all that is necessary if the sponge be a big one!

The one essential of comfort in a bathroom is that we may each have one to ourselves. Size and decoration are very secondary requirements. A most comfortable and pretty bathroom can be put into a space that is five feet by six — even five feet square is possible. A bathroom for the nursery, in which a baby must be bathed and children dressed — and which must, moreover, have room for the endless parapher-

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

nalia that seem necessary for small children — should, if possible, be bigger; but five feet six by seven feet six is ample for anyone or for anything. Comfort and attractiveness depend, of course, upon how the fixtures are placed.

The bathroom with the tub across its width, leaving a square space, makes an infinitely more attractive room than the ugly and space-wasting setting of the tub longways.

The most unattractive bathroom possible to think of is one with a slit of a window, and with the tub and fixtures in a row, leaving a bowling-alley passage along the wall. A bathroom that is square in effect — with a full-sized window — is almost inevitably attractive. If there must be a needlebath or other varieties of fixtures, size is naturally essential. And if you like a six-foot tub, then the least width possible for the room is six feet six inches. But there are many of us who prefer a five-foot tub, unless there can be one big enough to swim in — which is something else again! In a country cottage, where the force of water is not overstrong, the smallest tub that you find comfortable is the best, because it fills most quickly.

In a luxurious type of house, tubs are necessarily larger and of better quality than those in an unpretentious cottage. In the former, width of tub produces a greater effect of luxury than length of tub. A large tub should be double walled and either faced in one piece with its interior or else set in place with tiles.

In a little house, however, the cheapest tub that comes — the ordinary one that sits up on feet — is a thousand times to be preferred to those which imitate the better styles, but, being only half sealed in, are veritable dirt-pockets. The ordinary tub can be cleaned on every side with ease, and the floor wiped below, while the semi-set-in tub, which is sealed around the rim with an opening between the wall and the back, might be cleaned by a trained python, but by nothing else that I have been able to think of.

The size of the wash-basin depends, of course, upon the space available. Let us hope that you need not put up with

the discomfort of a little basin with a chain that pulls the stopper out prematurely unless you are very careful; that is so narrow that you bump your forehead if you wash your face with water held in your cupped hands, and that has no rim on which to stand anything.

A stopper and chain — a chain that looks like strung beads — is, on the other hand, the best bathtub outlet so far devised. The thought of a plunger is not very likely to appeal to you if you have often noticed the return of soap from previous baths — especially in hotels. There may be plenty of tight plungers, and perhaps this is a personal antipathy; but it is a suggestion that might bear investigation — unless you don’t mind yesterday’s soap in to-day’s bath.

Personally, also, I cannot endure the mustache-cup effect of the anti-splash hand-basin which the plumbers are at the moment most likely to install if you do not watch out. Of course, if you like this contrivance, then it is quite all right. In a bathroom that is big enough, a hand-basin slab three feet wide makes all the difference in the world — to me. In fact, my personal bathroom recipe might be: A short broad tub, and a big basin set in a wide fixture; a shower over the tub, a square floor-space, big medicine-closet, mirrors for decoration; a big window (this should be underscored) , a slipper chair, bathroom scales, and a velvet carpet covering the whole floor when the tiles are not pretty ones. There must be attractive color, plenty of towels, and plenty of heat in cold weather. This recipe is given merely so that you can accept or reject as much of it as you happen to choose.

Colored plumbing fixtures are utterly fascinating. I would like very much indeed to rip all white ones out and burst into color throughout. Who amongst us would not? But colored plumbing in a bathroom set with colored tiles loses all attraction, unless by miracle of chance the plumbing and the tiles go better together than those which I have happened to see.

In one room Spanish tiles of turquoise blue made a beau-

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

tiful background for black-and-white marble fixtures; but tiles and fixtures of supposedly matching colors should usually — one or the other of them — be indicted for esthetic murder. There are endless other backgrounds, however, against which colored fixtures are irresistible.

Walls set with engraved mirrors are beautiful; and so are walls that are made entirely of glass mirrors on the backs of which an artist has painted decorative designs before the quicksilver was put on. And both, I may add, are fabulously expensive.

Lacquered silver makes a lovely background for almost all colored plumbing, either light or dark, and is not necessarily expensive. The plaster and all the woodwork is silverpainted and then shellacked. In an elaborate house the floor would be of marble or dark glass or covered with a black velvet carpet.

BEAUTY AT LITTLE EXPENSE

For a simple house, nothing is prettier than trim and dado painted with enamel paint either a little lighter than the plumbing or of a contrasting shade, and papered above either with silver tea-paper or with a wall-paper that accentuates the color of the plumbing. In an inexpensive cottage there is nothing better for the floor than a black-and-white linoleum. Be sure to pick a color that is pure black-and-white and that has not a yellow cast — unless you deliberately choose yellow to go with your color. You can, of course, have plain linoleum cut into whatever geometrical pattern you choose, and set like a mosaic in little pieces.

If you choose a marble floor for the bathrooms or anywhere else, be sure that the black will polish jet-black and that the white is a clean white. Beware of the dirty-colored marble with a dull surface that is often used for the very small blocks habitually set in bathroom floors. You can have it cleaned hour after hour, day after day, forever, and it will never look anything but dull and dirty. (It is this sort of a floor that I always cover with a black velvet carpet.)

BATH-DRESSING-ROOMS

The bath-dressing-rooms that one sees advertised are in a few instances beautiful; but many of them suggest perfume-makers’ display-rooms, or beauty-parlors, or almost anything rather than the bathroom-dressing-room of a woman of distinction. Such rooms are far from appealing to the woman who really knows much about luxury.

A bathroom, whether it be also a dressing-room or not, is necessarily a room of utility into which you go to bathe, to brush your teeth, and so on. If there is one place in which you are not likely to want to stay, it is in a room that is misty with steam from a hot bath. A sofa in a dressing-room is another matter; but in this case the plumbing fixtures belong elsewhere.

In fact, speaking from very personal experience, a combination dressing-room-bathroom has many disadvantages that you would do well to consider before building one. If you have a personal maid, where would she be — and stay? And where would you go for privacy? You could say, "Celeste, go out!” and call "Celeste, come back!” But sending her in and out is scarcely a situation of luxurious convenience. Or, on the other hand, if you get out of your clothes and put them on by yourself, the almost dramatic luxury of the background does not very well fit the wait-on-yourself dramatic action. In either case an inconveniently large bathroom-dressing-room with nowhere to go but up and down the different floor levels, and with not a door to close, may do very well in a picture, but does very uncomfortably in fact.

But for those who like bath-dressing-rooms, the plan shown in Figure 13 5 is fairly good. The recessed bath is curtained twice. It has an inside shower curtain hung close to the tub, and outside curtains hung so as to make a small bath compartment. The cupboards over the tub are spacesaving in a small house. A hand-basin might be in the recess at left of bath, if shelf closet could be spared.

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

My own idea of dressing convenience includes a bathroom that may be very large, or that may be so small that you can just about get into it and shut the door. Its size is no matter at all. But its door is essential. If you can’t be alone in your bathroom, where can you? And again, what is your idea of luxury? A movingpicture scene in which four or five attendants help the heroine take her bath, is undoubtedly supposed to suggest luxury. Perhaps it does. Perhaps we might as well leave it at that.

PLAN FOR BATH-DRESSING-ROOM

THE SERVICE END OF THE HOUSE

Each housekeeper knows how she runs her house, how she chooses to live, and how much her mode of living exacts in luxury, convenience or space. The question of taste is affected only in this: There must be agreement between the character of the house and the manner of its upkeep.

To live beautifully in the most unpretentious little house in the world is in very best taste — if "beautifully” includes no suggestion of pretentious scale. It is difficult to fit numerous men servants into a primitive cottage in a simple environment, because primitive simplicity and numerous men servants are out of harmony. Men servants can perfectly well be fitted into the littlest house of a bachelor, and a dignified butler is quite adjustable to a simple little house. But a footman beside a chauffeur on a country motor-car is as unsuitable as evening clothes worn down-town to business — unless the occupant of the car is an invalid who has to be lifted by two men. Even so it would be in rather better taste were the footman in plain clothes, like the nurse or valet whose services he performs.

THE PERSONAI-TTY OF A HOUSE

In even worse taste, on the other hand, is the house of size and apparent magnificence in which the occupants haven’t the most elemental conception of proper equipment. Within a certain distance of New York there is a certain new house of arresting magnificence. It contains a gallery, a loggia, a drawing-room, a den, a living-room, a diningroom, a breakfast-room, a billiard-room, a library, as well as half a dozen master’s bedrooms. And then — room for two servants!

Of course, there is no reason in the world why a family should not live in a big house and at the same time do much — or even all — of its own housework if it is able and willing. But the house must not suggest an important English country seat or a French chateau, or any style that can be called pretentious. With perfect propriety it may suggest a farmhouse, a ranch-house or any type of house that lends itself to primitive simplicity of living.

THE KITCHEN

Taking an average well-run house, let us put at the top of the list of the service department, a light and convenient kitchen. Its size depends entirely upon the family’s manner of living. In a house that has few, if any, big dinners or other entertainments requiring much kitchen and pantry space, a small kitchen with plenty of light and air is more convenient than a big one. The word "small” is, of course, relative. For a cook alone, a kitchen ten feet by twelve is much better than one that is too large. If you have a kitchenmaid, the kitchen must be somewhat larger than for a cook alone. A kitchen sixteen feet by sixteen is almost too big for a cook alone — unless it is so arranged that she need seldom leave the working corner of it.

The arrangement of a kitchen is important, since unnecessary steps waste not only time but energy as well. The sink, dresser or supply closet, range, and zinc-topped table should be as close together as possible, and in a good light

A PLEASANT AND AIRY KITCHEN IS AN ESSENTIAL OF EVERY COMFORTABLE HOUSE

A MAID’S ROOM THAT IS BOTH PRETTY AND COMFORTABLE

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

both by day and by night. The only disadvantages of a small kitchen in which the servants do not eat — are heat, and lack of space when preparing for a big party.

Every kitchen must, however, have proper equipment, including a good range and a clean and adequate icebox. Whether the icebox be electric or filled by the iceman is of little moment; its white-lined cleanness is of chief concern. Another thing to remember in a kitchen is a sink big enough to hold a life-sized dishpan. The tendency of the builders of average houses is to put a uselessly small sink both in the kitchen and in the pantry.

Kitchen decoration is at the moment rivaled in glory only by that of the bathroom-dressing-room. If you like a kitchen painted every color of the rainbow, the paint-box is open for you to choose what you will, and in the shops are utensils and floor-coverings to match or contrast with whichever colors you may fancy.

An ugly, dingy back of the house is really inexcusable, since very little thought and even less expense can change it into beauty. The difference between the old kitchen in Plate 8 and the kitchen in Plate 42 is produced by white paint, a new sink, and oilcloth. Paint alone would have made the old kitchen attractive to look at. The floor would have been quite as pretty brightly painted blue and waxed.

THE servants’ HALL

In a house of importance there is usually a combination sitting-room-dining-room or both a sitting-room and a dining-room. In an average house there is perhaps a small room furnished with a table and the necessary number of chairs; or possibly there is an alcove fitted with a long table between wall-benches — commercially called a breakfast nook.

The size of the servants’ hall naturally depends upon the number of servants. In the age of brown utility it was apt to be rather drab and dreary. Now its walls are usually painted with washable paint, or it is papered with a colorful

THE PERSONALITY OF A HOUSE _

paper and then shellacked; its furniture is either varnished or painted, and the effect is attractive and homelike. One maid alone naturally eats in the kitchen.

THE PANTRY

The size of the pantry should, like that of the kitchen, be in proportion to the family’s mode of life. In an average small house a pantry five feet by seven — or even six — is entirely adequate. (See Fig. 46, page 98, and Fig. 48, page 100.)

The pantry in a house of size, in which many parties are given, must obviously be larger in proportion. A slide between the kitchen and the pantry has one objection: It encourages the smell of cooking to come through into the pantry and thence into the dining-room. The difficulty of keeping the smell of cooking out of the front part of the house is too well known to most of us to need comment further than that every range should have a hood over it, and that there should be two doors between the kitchen and dining-room; indeed, a third door between pantry and dining-room is not too many. The kitchen that opens directly into the dining-room or into the front hall is a detail that every house plan should avoid.

FOR PASTING, SORTING AND PACKAGE- WRAPPING

An item of great convenience, in the back entry, or flower room, or shed, or coat closet, or wherever space for it can be found, is a drop-shelf table, about two feet six inches by five feet six inches, with plain wood or wood covered with oilcloth (cemented or closely thumb-tacked around the vertical edge) , for pasting, writing, sorting, or package-wrapping. A large, unencumbered table of this kind, instantly available when needed, is unendingly useful.

On the wall at one side, if possible (otherwise somewhere near) , one or two commercial wrapping-paper rollers should be installed. You can buy them of any stationery supply

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

company. A small one and a big one may be necessary, if you send many packages; otherwise a big one will do, of medium weight but strong manila paper. Narrow shelves above the drop-table are essential. On the lowest one, fasten three or four small perpendicular iron rods by setting them into holes made with a small gimlet through the shelf. On these put three or four balls of different weight twine.

Divide the rest of the shelf spaces into compartments for various tags and rubber stamps and labels — one with your name and address, and any addresses to which boxes or hampers are habitually sent. Also have labeled spaces for mucilage, glue, stamps, scales, clips, rubber bands, and so on.

Fasten the shears and scissors by slender chains or strong string, so that they cannot be taken away by the absentminded or the careless.

Empty boxes, and especially big sheets of wrappingpaper, quires of tissue paper, and so on, can perhaps be kept in a cupboard or on a high shelf.

At all events, well arranged equipment for sending off packages is essential to the convenience of every house.

THE REQUIREMENTS OF WELL-APPOINTED SERVANTS* ROOMS

A room can be the minimum in size, so minimum that only by the greatest skill can the bed, bureau, table, and a chair or two be fitted into it. In the plan shown in Figure 46 the room marked "2” is the smallest maid’s room possible to furnish. It is so small that in order to fit the bed and bureau in, the side-bars of the bed had to be shortened. And yet no one could ask for a prettier or more comfortable room, now that the furniture is packed into it. It has two windows and a good-sized closet. The wall-paper has a white glazed ground, with sprays of flowers in lovely colors. The furniture, the simplest possible to buy, is painted light leafgreen. The floor is painted green, and the clearflax rugs also are green. There are crossed-over dotted Swiss curtains. A dolphin glass lamp with a parchment shade stands on a table.

There is a wall-light at the head of the bed, and an unbleached muslin candlewick spread.

Maid’s room No. 1 on the same plan and in Plate 43 is occupied by a personal maid. The floor and rugs are the same as in Room 2. The furniture is painted corn-yellow with narrow black lines, and the paper has small bouquets of flowers on a white ground with tiny blank dots. This room also has a candlewick spread. The settle has a plain pine top which, when not in use, is turned toward the wall. When it is in use it is an excellently strong table to stand the sewingmachine on, or to cut or to press on. The other rooms, 3 and 4, have a pink-flowered paper, light-blue painted furniture, and the trim painted blue to match the furniture.

This description is given because, no matter whom they might be furnished for, simple cottage rooms could not be prettier; nor, on the other hand, could they have been much less expensive. The entire difference between these rooms and others of complete hideosity is color. And color is one thing, at all events, that costs no more when lovely than when villainous. The only detail that anyone could list as extravagant are the bed-springs, mattresses, and wallpapers.

As said elsewhere, cheap wall-papers are bad economy. In this day of easily applied, unstreakable paints, all rooms of mud-and-mustard ugliness are indefensible — unless one have not the price of a brush and a can or two of paint.

The day of serviceable color — meaning one that does not show dirt — is past. To-day light colors that show dirt very much are chosen by all good housekeepers for that very reason. Serviceable color, moreover, is not really practical from the standpoint of a good housekeeper, because there is no incentive for any human being to take an interest in dinginess that is just as dirty-looking after being scrubbed as it was before.

But a room of beautiful color, with the beautifying accessories of pretty curtains, a pretty lamp and shade, a pretty bedspread, a neutral rug, and ordinary attention to normal

EACH ROOM IN DETAIL

requirements of comfort, has long proved — to my own satisfaction, at all events — that those who show their indifference to beauty by failing to keep it as beautiful as they can, are in the uncountably small minority. Most of us now realize that a house which is attractive only in the front part of it can scarcely be classed as one to which the word "perfect” may, more than half-way, be applied.

XVII

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

/"N this present day of interest in child psychology, there are few who fail to realize that children, instead of being unknowing, uncaring little animals, needing merely to be housed, clothed, and fed, are supersensitive to beauty in its almost every phase.

To a grown person, beauty of surroundings may be a necessity or it may be of no consequence at all. In other words, the need for beauty may be compensated for by something that has gradually taken its place. But for every child, light, and air, and color are factors of vital importance to its happiness, as well as to its normal growth.

That children cannot judge between excellence and abomination in esthetic taste is quite true, but they are easily affected by their surroundings as measured by degrees of light or darkness, freedom or restraint: light produced by bright pleasing color and the responsive cheerfulness of the grown person who takes care of them; darkness produced by ugliness, the gloom of a cross or moody person, or the hampering restrictions of ceaseless "Don’ts.”

A child must have freedom as well as beauty, or else the price of beauty is too great. Things can be so high-priced that our pleasure in them is spoiled. We all know that! We know, too, if we stop to think about it, that the price of continuous "don’ts” is the highest price a child can be asked to pay.

The first requirement, therefore, for the contentment of a child is a space somewhere — no matter whether it be big or small — where he can be free to do what he pleases; where he can make just about as much of a mess as he wants to; and where the word "don’t” is never heard.

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

To children who live in the country — or in the suburbs — outdoors means liberty, even though liberty be enclosed in a small front or back yard. In a big city, "going out” means — for the small child — walking on the street, held by the hand; or at most, roller-skating up and down the block under the constant surveillance of a nurse, or governess, until almost grown. In the city, therefore, an indoor playroom of pleasing and practicable appeal is of greatest importance.

From the mud-and-mustard wall-paper, and the old banged odd-lot yellow-brown furniture that provided the playroom furnishing of long ago, to the last room created for the children of fortune by specialists in child’s-room decoration, is a far cry! Some of the new designs devised for children are interesting, and a few are very beautiful; it must be added, however, that many others are disturbingly distorted, and that, as incentives to the love of beauty, their value is difficult to perceive. A small room with a life-sized animal completely filling each wall space; furniture of grotesque outline; no single foothold anywhere that is not crowded with toy favors like the counter of a confectioner’s — such a room is very like an overcrowded flower-bed in which you expect a little plant to thrive and grow. Light, air, and uncrowded space are needed for a child as well as for a plant.

The ugliness of the drab and dingy room noted above is one extreme; the other extreme is the hampering spoilability of the too-fine turquoise, shell-pink, or peach-colored taffeta upholstery in the sleeping-room-play-room of the child who is housed like a doll, dressed like a doll, and supposed never to behave otherwise than as a doll. Of the two extremes, the former is far to be preferred, as it does at least tend to make character and quite possibly promote the ingenuity of a child.

This sounds like a flat contradiction of the first paragraph in the present chapter. But what is meant is that a child’s room should be as pretty as possible, but must not

be hampering to freedom. In other words — should a choice be necessary — freedom is of first importance, and beauty of second. Normal development is checked — sometimes even deformed — ^by the continuous pressure of restraint. The dollchild is almost certain to turn into an exotic or a prig.

THE IDEAL PLAYROOM

But there is really no reason why a child may not be surrounded by beauty and be free at the same time. The principal requirements of a playroom are that it have plenty of air and light and that it be pleasing but unspoilable. The requirement of air and light would dispose of too-heavy window-hangings; the requirement that it be pleasing would select useful furniture, made cheerful and pretty by the color of its paint or of its slip-covers. A lovely wall-paper, properly varnished, is washable. White or colored organdie curtains for a girl’s room, or straight net ones that can pull back for a boy’s room, are window-trimming enough, unless the windows be very wide and sunny, and one chooses to hang a plain washable fabric with a wall-paper — or chintz with a plain wall. But whatever the decoration or the furnishing, let the essential requirement be utility. Let the walls and trim and curtains be washable, and the furniture be painted with a standard color, so that it can be easily touched up; a hard-surfaced linoleum floor of attractive color is most serviceable — with small rugs that can be taken up when the children want to play with paint or clay or any other rug-spoiling materials. Or if the floor be covered with a carpet, two or two-and-a-half yards of oilcloth can be laid down on the floor during various occupations, and then can easily be rolled on a stick and kept in a closet or on a shelf when not in use. A double set of slip-covers, so that one set can be on while the other is in the wash, is practical in an especially sooty city. In the country, a well- covered design of chintz will last indefinitely — even with hardest usage.

Among the essential details of furnishing — unless there

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

be an ample closet — are built-in shelves. Their width and number will depend upon the space required for toys and games as well as books. In the country, the shelves can perfectly well be left open, but in most cities, dirt protection is a necessity. Doors are perhaps most practical, but if curtains are to be used, short ones — ^like ruffles — hanging from rings on rods across the face of each shelf, are rather more decorative than long curtains. The rods and rings of the lower curtains are hidden by the bottom of the curtains above; the rod of the top one is covered by a projecting wood molding or by a narrow valance. Suitable curtain material might be colored oilcloth with a pinked bottom edge; or it might be chintz or cotton print bound with a contrasting color, or hemmed and left plain.

FOR BIGGER CHILDREN

A settle is an excellent playroom item for the larger children, since it turns into a bench when not in use as a table. Otherwise a big drop-leaf kitchen table painted to match the room is as serviceable as it is imspoilable. If the treatment of it be very marring, the table can have a cover put over it when not in use. A thoroughly practical light to play or study by, is a two- or three-bulb droplight, with a drum-shade, hung over the table. Straight chairs will do perfectly for sitting at the table, but a comfortable old upholstered sofa and a chair or two should be included, if possible.

FOR YOUNGER CHILDREN

A platform is the most useful article of the playroom. A very good size is about three feet wide by five feet long, and is made of ordinary planed boards nailed on two-byfour-inch cross-blocks, bringing the top about five inches above the floor. It is the delight of all younger children, especially boys. On this they can hammer and paint and build blocks, and set out their soldiers, and run their trains — and

leave their armies, or their villages, or their train-tracks in position on it, since a platform is not the floor!

Where there is plenty of room, a small platform and a big train-track platform spell perfection for the child who likes his trains better than anything else. With no place but the floor on which to set out his tracks, the child must spend most of his playtime hooking the tracks together and putting them away again, and gradually getting them so bent that laying them flat and keeping them steady becomes impossible.

Very long ago a collection of tin tracks, growing more and more wobbly, and more and more tiresome to put together and take apart, induced me one Christmas to have a track platform built. It was about four feet by seven, and was a foot high; and on it the beginning of a railway system was laid out and tacked down. From time to time, more tracks, turntables, stations, bridges, and so on, were added, until the platform had eventually to be supplemented by a second one. In fact, it ended by occupying nearly all of the playroom floor. It was of unceasing interest not only to the children to whom it belonged, but to all their friends. The tacks were pulled out and much or little of the track arrangement changed about from time to time, so that it never became monotonous, as is apt to be the way with stationary toys.

One way to make room for a train platform when space is limited, is to have it hinged to the baseboard so that it can be lifted and hooked up against the wall when not in use. Or if you want it out of sight, you can easily have a board top fitted in place of bed-springs on the frame of a disappearing bed, which stands up on end and shuts into a closet.

A doll’s house, even if it be small, is one of those toys which are of prohibitive cost if bought. And even when it is bought, it is likely to be completely out of scale, and never very beautiful if judged by the standards applied to an actual house. To make one that is really beautiful requires an architect — or a potential one — who has manual dexterity to a

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

marked degree. But making a fairly attractive doll’s house can be a long-continued occupation for boys as well as girls. The boys — with some help from their father or from a carpenter — can build a well-proportioned house from drawings made on cross-bar paper, and build it to scale.

When the house is finished, the girls furnish it. Furnishings in the Modernistic style are the easiest possible. Nearly all the furniture can be built with a saw and chisel and a small box of blocks. For mantels and overdoors and ornaments of every sort, plastic paint applied to surfaces of not too even detail will smooth them out beautifully. Clay is invaluable as a material, and so is paper-pulp molded when wet and left to dry and harden.

Children are strongly inclined to invent and construct, and although it is true that they rarely have more than the most primitive skill if solely dependent upon themselves, a little enthusiastic cooperation by a grown person — who must, of course, have tact as well as skill — will induce most children to become both inventive and adept.

WHEN THE SLEEPING-ROOM MUST ALSO BE THE PLAYROOM

Beds are great destroyers of floor space. A child’s bed alone, when set sideways to the wall, is not very hampering; but when — as usual — the nurse sleeps in the room with a child, or two children occupy the same room, floor space is a problem. The afore-mentioned disappearing bed, which shuts up against a door and then closes into the wall, is not especially appealing from the point of view of beauty; but in a small room it has one undeniable advantage — it can be put out of the way when not needed.

Beds built in tiers, like ships’ berths, are another possibility. If you have two such beds, one above the other, the springs of the lower bed can be on the floor, and the top of the mattress not much over a foot high; this allows the upper one to be about four and a half feet from the floor, which is fairly practical, and at least frees the floor space

39^

of one bed. About the only other possibility (barring folding beds) is to choose two daybeds and push them flat against opposite walls, leaving as much floor space as possible between them.

THE COUNTRY PLAYROOM

Wherever possible, a room on the ground floor with its own entry (described in the Hall section of the last chapter) is, of course, ideal. An outdoor playhouse is perhaps most ideal of all. But in an average country house, where no special playroom was included on the architect’s plan, a part of the attic partitioned ofl for the children’s use is a perfect substitute. If it be dark and low, a dormer window can give height as well as light. Or any room, no matter which or where, will do for the children’s playroom. But, wherever it is, let it be their own. Let them decorate it and furnish it, or possibly build it (with help, if necessary) to suit themselves.

Play-scenery perennially dear to children is that of an imaginary robbers’ cave; say, an abandoned doghouse surrounded, like a grotto, with brushwood and stones, and possessing an entrance too small for grown persons. A tree-house with a rope-ladder which the children can mount and then draw up after them is a delight to every boy; and so is a wicket-gate in their playroom door, which they can slide open to see if the one who knocks is a friend to be admitted, or a foe to attack. A front-door bell is an acceptable feature of a girls’ playhouse, which no grown people may enter except when invited — or unless they behave as visitors. Such are a few fragmentary suggestions regarding the personality of a true playroom.

In a playhouse, a cooking-stove and other appurtenances of a kitchen have always been items of great importance; but whether the children are to be allowed to have a kitchen under the home roof is something that each mother must decide. If kitchen-furnishings are to be added to a play-

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

room, the corner where the stove is put must at least be scrubbable and all the utensils kept in a segregated enclosure. Taffy-pulling over by the sofa, biscuit-rolling on the writing desk, and butter brought into close proximity to a freshly papered or painted wall — these are not likely to result in a room that will long keep its freshness.

A little outbuilding kitchen is another matter.

FURNITURE FOR A CHILD’s ROOM

Much furniture is and has always been made for children. Some of it is in the form of undersized replicas of real

ALL LITTLE CHILDREN LOVE TO CLIMB

furniture, and some of it is specially designed for the children. Many of the nursery examples turned out by the modernists have little to recommend them, as they are heavy.

awkward, inclined to be grotesque, uncomfortable, and very expensive. Certain pieces of climbing furniture — ^like an irregular tower mounting against a wall, and built of blocks that are either shelves or boxes — ^might be substituted for the ordinary toy shelves, to give a modernistic accent and to delight the young child whose chief impulse is to climb. (See Fig. 136.)

Picture-book furniture ornamented with the animals or characters of the child’s favorite books can have much charm. The idea lends itself, moreover, to an infinite variety of interpretations. Headboards or footboards can be cut with a jig-saw into whatever outlines a child may like — animals, flowers, trees, gardens, toy villages, fairy scenes, or various toys; they need not be difficult for any carpenter and house painter — or almost anyone else — to do.

Or, if the furniture and the walls be plain, a few curtains or slip-covers of nursery fabrics such as those designed by Tony Sarg, in themes of toys and fairy-tales, are full of charm.

THE DECORATIONS OF THE WALLS

Of all the rooms for children that I have ever seen, either actually or pictured, the decoration that stands out beyond every other is that which Helen Benton Minnich painted for her own child. It was illustrated in The House Beautiful. One of the walls was covered with pictures of the most famous domes and towers of the world. They mounted one above the other with the ethereal lightness of dream buildings, and yet they were equally real. The Capitol at Washington, the Brooklyn Bridge, and the Wool worth Building — as I remember — were at the bottom just above the chairrail. St. Peter’s, the leaning tower of Pisa, and the Taj Mahal were at the top. Another wall had a decorative treatment of gas and oil tanks; still another was a country landscape.

The imagination, beauty, and unending interest in these paintings are something that few could duplicate as they should be duplicated — to fit the imagination of a certain

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

child. And unless the decoration did just that, the whole intention of it would be lost. There should be walls with pictures of ships for a ship-lover, with machinery for one whose bent is mechanics, with scenes from the tales best beloved by a child who lives in a land of fairy-tales. But, alas! such decoration is possible only through the hand of an artist — a very imaginative and gifted artist, who sees and feels and can therefore transcribe the imagination of a child.

But cruder varieties of decoration are possible to those of us who can attempt to draw at all, and we can work them out as any imaginative child, if encouraged, will direct. And the unimaginative child is as rare as the dog that does not leap and bark, or the lamb that does not gambol, or the flowering plant that does not bloom. If we cannot draw or paint, we can at least cut out with scissors and wield a mucilage brush.

The only objection to many of the most effective wallpapers is that their pattern so monotonously repeats. Unless one buy a scenic paper, what else could it do? Scenic papers are very expensive — much too expensive for the average mother to put in the children’s room — very expensive for most of us to put in any room. But the cutting out and rearranging of the designs of repeating wall-papers is a diversion that has great possibilities. Moreover, the trick of using such cut-out designs as patterns for silhouettes drawn lightly with a pencil and then filled in with lacquer paint is not difficult at all, and it is one capable of many variations. For instance, choosing a wall-paper with trees, and another with foliage on it and birds or more treebranches, we can cut out the motives and draw around them and fill in with color. For the shading of the design, we follow the original, but in our own color. On a foliage background children can continue to paste birds. For instance, as they find them in magazines, much as birds were pasted on the Chinese tree-branch papers in the eighteenth century.

Of course, if we draw at all — or even if we have a decorative sense, which can sometimes take the place of the far

more exacting talent — wall decorations are not difficult. In making them, however, it is well to remember that four walls overspread with conspicuous repetitions are as deadly boring as overtrimmed furniture. This is why paper cut out and rearranged can be very pleasing, especially when the wall spaces are not too high or too wide. Or perhaps the children might cut out other pictures they like and compose a design of a scattered pattern, with the biggest pictures at the bottom and the tiny ones at the top; and when the wall or the panel is completed, they can shellac it. Or we can buy stencils of whatever we choose and let the children paint — or at least help them to paint — their own walls.

Whether we can help with actual decoration or not, let us at least be sympathetic, and do what we can. When Sallie begs for a sky-blue sprigged-with-silver wall-paper for her room, or for a pink dress, it is not sensible but cruel to paper her room in tan and dress her in brown instead. The child’s spirit wilts amid the constant surroundings of tan and brown and other colors repellent to her, until, becoming accustomed to the ugliness, she ceases to care; which is the worst that can happen, since it means that her sense of beauty is perishing. If there is a good reason why she may not have the sky-blue paper — for instance, because it is expensive, and rolls of tan are already in the house — at least explain this to the child, so that she will take her disappointment as something to be endured with cheerfulness, knowing that she will be given a pretty paper when and if it becomes possible. Children are perfectly reasonable beings ; a child of three will make herself a consistent ally if you take her into partnership and explain. The mother whom I am criticising is one who, simply out of imwillingness (or inability) to think from the child’s point of view, says to herself: "Blue and silver? Nonsense! The child does not know what she wants.”

The very little child, as I have said, distinguishes chiefly between brightness and gloom. An average baby of six months will cry if approached too closely by a woman wearing a crepe veil, and will hold out its arms to one in a dress

FOR THE CHILDREN OF THE HOUSE

that is gay and shining. As the child grows older, its dependence upon mere brightness decreases, and its sense of beauty- values — if this is being trained — naturally develops.

Children invariably become interested in what they take a part in making. A room done for them and given to them is not as developing to their initiative, or to their taste, as is one that they themselves design. If they make a mess of it, what_ matter? It will teach them to do better next time.

Perhaps this opinion is a personal one. Perhaps the reactions of other children would be different. An experience of my own was this: When I was ten we moved into a new house. I wanted so very much to furnish — and decorate — my own room that my father decided that I should be allowed to try. It was a north room of good size, with plenty of light but not a glimmer of sun. Whether my idea was suggested by wedding decorations, I don’t know, but my choice of color for the entire room was pure white and dark leaf -green! I chose a bluish- white paper with a faint silver stipple on it that could be seen only at certain angles. The furniture was white; the bed-hangings and curtains were of white dotted muslin, tied back with garlands of imitation smilax! The carpet was dark green. The single armchair and heavy curtains were of dark green velveteen. There was no relieving color anywhere. The room had no pictures, and its only ornaments were white. Anything more cold and empty and uninviting and lacking in quality could hardly be imagined.

I still remember my bewildered disappointment that the result was not a bit as I had pictured it. I also remember that I thought it fair that nothing be done about it; it had been my own taste and my own choice, and that was that! But I really think that interest in houses was born of this experiment. Its failure made me notice other rooms; it taught me the unforgetable lesson that texture and detail must be supreme to make up for lack of color. The vital importance of color was emphasized by my mother, who, I found on our return from the country the following year, had trans-

formed that same repellent room into a dream of delight, with pale flesh-colored walls and a pink-ground rose-flowered chintz.

Apart from self-expression — which is a quality of a different sort and with quite a different object — the quality of real pictures chosen for the walls should always be good. Good pictures may be prints of value, or they may equally well be home-framed illustrations cut from good magazines. Ornament the walls otherwise, or paper them, or leave them plain; but don’t, if you can help it, let children grow up under the daily influence of bad chromos that express no idea whatever, or of other discarded examples of ugliness. Pretty ornaments in glass or china need not be expensive; dirt-colored paint is no cheaper than jade or Chinese blue; ugly wood can be painted or slip-covered; ugly floors as well as trim can be painted, rugs can be dyed, and beautiful chintz of good design can be found at moderate cost if one but care enough to take a little trouble to find it.

In fact, the recipe for making not only the children’s room but the whole house charming, is the same as the definition of genius; "Infinite capacity for taking pains.”

XVIII

A HOUSE— OR A ROOM— FOR A MAN

T'^HE type of room the average man likes, and feels at home in, should not be easily spoilable. Chairs that look easily breakable, coverings light and perishable in color and texture, all the things that go to the making of feminine rooms, in short, are more or less unfitted to convey the feeling of "home” to men, no matter how attractive the men may think them.

On the other hand, there is neither sense nor beauty in the popular belief that manliness can be expressed only in the sort of solidity suitable for caging a grizzly bear; that a small room set aside for his personal use must be known as a "den,” and be furnished with an overstuflfed sofa that would support an elephant and with chairs obviously made for baby hippopotamuses. It is not necessary that the office desk and other objects of wood furniture be either of rawbeef -colored mahogany or of fumed oak, or that the entire color scheme be maroon combined with wet-mud brown.

The perfect room for a man has most typically a neutralcolored wall-paper; or perhaps the walls are painted or woodpaneled, or hung with material. But a plain background of solid color against which he can hang whatever he likes — or nothing — is always good. The room should, of course, have a fireplace if possible, a deep, low substantial sofa, and deep, low substantial chairs. Even so, they need not be elephantine.

All-over upholstery in a man’s room is apt to be better looking than that which is framed in wood, because wood framing to be beautiful should be fragile. Planks and beams or box-shaped contrivances of exaggerated size, made into settings for swollen upholstery, are equally clumsy, whether

the chairs or sofas be labeled "Modern,” "Mission,” or "Circus Ring.” The requirements of an ideal room for a man are very much the same as those of a living room: First of all, the room must look as though it were used. It must, moreover, be obviously comfortable, restful, quietly pleasing or at least negative in color, with plenty of light by day and by night.

WHAT A man’s room SHOULD CONTAIN

A man’s room should have a big desk or broad writingtable kept properly equipped with a clean blotter, a big well of fresh ink, and a drawer filled with a supply of whatever sort of paper and envelops this particular man is likely to use. In a special drawer there should be mucilage, glue, string, rubber bands, clips, labels, shears, and plenty of stamps — and the children should be forbidden to use any of them!

If possible, there should be provision for a favorite occupation. Books and special magazines if he reads; pipes and whatever he smokes; or, if the room be big enough, a workbench or drafting-table or other special object of utility for whatever his hobby may be. As suggested for a room for children, a big settle (if it be suitable to the type of the room) is very useful. The drafting- table (see Plate 57) can be turned into a bench. For ordinary use the table would better be supported on lower side-arms, bringing it to average table height. The one in the picture was built for an architect and is at standing height. Its size is not apparent, except by noticing the Hitchcock chair— a rather highbacked one — which comes several inches below the table.

A settle can be made in Jacobean style most suitably, as it was in this period that it was invented. In a mahogany room or in any room of sophisticated style a settle is unsuitable. A gate-leg table is equally good for a man’s room any table, in fact, that can be quickly cleared and have whatever a man may choose spread out on it: maps, papers, photograph prints, stamp collections, clippings — the list is.

A HOUSE— OR A ROOM— FOR A MAN

40$

of course, unlimited. An unencumbered table is of first necessity for a man who may want to cut, to paste, to draw, to construct almost anything.

Perhaps a man’s room is a workshop, perhaps it is principally a place for him and his friends to smoke in after dinner, perhaps it is an office, perhaps it is a room where its owner can go off by himself to rest or to think. The appeal of its furnishing depends entirely upon its suitability to use. An important item for comfort is a well-planned closet — not just a sentry-box with a few hangers around the sides and a shelf toward the top, but one that has its space especially fitted for the things that its owner wants to keep in it.

A HOUSE FOR A MAN

That any normal man should be repelled by the least suggestion of effeminacy is only natural. But even so, there is no reason why the house of a bachelor should be furnished like the display rooms of a cement company. A man’s house is apt to be Elizabethan or Spanish, or to seek some other bare-walled, dark wooded effect; but many bachelors’ houses of distinction are, with equal suitability, Georgian.

An Elizabethan type of house finishes and furnishes itself, since it cannot be other than masculine, no matter how it is completed. Every open-beamed bare-walled house is essentially masculine, as is the dark, rich coloring of the typical Italian house. A French house of elaboration, on the other hand, is far too feminine to be suitable for a man. Yet a man’s Georgian house can be fairly elaborate, if its decoration be strictly classic and the ceilings high.

A very high ceiling makes a room masculine to begin with. So much so that if the ceiling be as much as eleven feet in a room about fifteen by twenty feet, an entirely suitable man’s dining-room can be painted an otherwise too feminine color. To paneled walls of pale gray-green, for instance, masculine strength of color can be added by means of window-hangings of crimson grosgrain or even damask

brocade. Such a room would most appropriately have Chippendale furniture or any other not-too-fragile design of mahogany, and either family portraits or steel engravings, or sepia prints in plain dark frames. If the engravings or prints have margins that are white, they should be painted over with a very thin wash of India ink, since staring white margins divert attention from the pictures and disturb the harmony of their surroundings.

The trim and mantel should be severely conventional and dignified. If the ceiling be only moderately high, the lambrequin should be set high over the windows, and the curtains cut long enough to train half a yard or more on the floor in order to add the utmost dignity of length.

The decorations of any room in a man’s house can also — and very suitably — be modernistic (see Chapter XXII) .

FEMININE TENDENCIES OF LOW CEILINGS

Low-ceilinged rooms may not risk feminine color or materials. The best low-ceilinged rooms in the house of a man are those which are crudely primitive, or those which characterize his personal tastes. For a sea-lover, plain woodpaneling suggestive of a ship’s cabin would be suitable. All plain wood-lined rooms are good, as are also walls painted or papered in neutral tones of putty, or sand, or olive-gray, or wrapping-paper brown, made colorful by many books (red ones!) .

For a man’s study — unless he never reads — the most furnishing decorations are well-filled book-shelves, not too closely matched. Odd lots of books, reference books in calf or leather, or at most, sets of favorite authors in cloth bindings, are far more suggestive of the man who reads than are even rows of splendidly-tooled bindings — unless bindings are his hobby.

For the upholstery of a man’s living-room, wool damask or rep or velveteen is quite as suitable as leather. True, one always associates a man’s room with morocco or pigskin or

A HOUSE— OR A ROOM— FOR A MAN

Other leather, even though one rather wonders whether it is lack of vanity, lack of self-assertion, or something else that induces certain men — especially those who are fastidious about their clothes — to take meekly the polish of coatbacks and elbows, that leather upholstery is inclined to give. Tufted leather was not so bad, but it is now out of fashion, which is rather too bad, since a flat-cushioned leather chair is inclined to be slithering to sit on, as well as a veritable cloth-polishing buffer.

In the illustration of the very distinguished man’s room in Plate 44, the set-in of damask on the seat and back of the big leather chair is an excellent anti-polish device, and is notably well contrived. Other good men’s rooms are illustrated in Plates 37 and 45. The living-room in Plate 29 is a low-ceilinged room that would be equally appropriate for the house of a man alone or for a family house. This is also true of the dining-room in Plate 22, the hall in Plate 30, the dining-room in Plate 38, and the living-room in Plate 27.

SUITABLE AND UNSUITABLE

In other words, all rooms of dignity and untrimmed simplicity are suitable for a man. Curtains may be of beautiful stuffs, but they must be plain and severe in effect. Colors should be either deep or neutral. A bachelor’s house may have great elegance without becoming feminine, if it be restrained. Frilly curtains, too many ornaments, fragile furniture, attenuated decorations — especially those in a high key of color — are effeminately unsuitable to the use of a man. Emptiness is an asset of suitability — especially in a man’s drawing-room or any room that is set apart for his guests. Emptiness is always an essential of classic beauty and of dignity.

A man’s study can properly be cluttered with books. Possessions of personal use can overflow in a room that expresses use rather than beauty. In a man’s house, ornaments solely for ornament should be very carefully chosen; a mantel should not be a parade-ground for trifling objects. A clock

and two plain vases or tankards or any other pair of objects — whether their value be great or trifling — is quite enough. A lamp, a few well-bound books, a bowl of flowers, a few ornaments of suitable size may be necessary to furnish the larger tables; but on smaller ones, an ash-tray, matchbox, and lighter are sufficient.

A very good living-room for a man is one with perfectly plain sealed-in walls of pine or cypress, lightly stained and waxed, and with olivish-green corduroy upholstery. If he is not one who reads, his walls might be hung with etchings, or sporting prints, or ship pictures, or enlarged photographs, or drawings of whatever subjects interest him. One room like this has shelves filled with big and little boat-models — which at the moment seem to have routed the stuffed birds and lacquered fish of thirty years ago.

One material of decoration that must neither be misunderstood nor overlooked is chintz. Popular "den” builders to the contrary notwithstanding, chintz is entirely suitable to the room or the house of a man. Not chintz of the pinkroses -and -blue -ribbon -bow -knot variety — scarcely! But nothing could be easier than to pick out an ideal chintz for every room in every thoroughly masculine house imaginable. No material ever designed so perfectly combines the qualities of use, of beauty, and of homelike appeal.

One of the most beautiful — and suitable— bedrooms or sitting-rooms possible to design for a man might have Chippendale furniture, plain walls in any very light grayed tint, curtains, bedspread, and upholstery (or slip-covers) in a chintz of well covered and moderately large design in colors that are soft but glowing. The ground-color of the chintz, if neutral, could be matched by the color of the walls — and possibly by the trim.

Or an Elizabethan, Jacobean, or any other dark-woodlined room, with dark furniture, and with its color supplied by the curtains and bed-covers and the upholstery of chair or sofa, is always excellent.
