XIX

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

chapter is written especially for two types of house-owners. The first is one who, having a limited amount of money for a new house and its furnishing, would like to know on what to spend and how to save. The second is the owner of a conspicuously unattractive house who helplessly exclaims: "Ugly old house! Ugly old furniture! Nothing can be done about it; we can’t afford to buy all new.”

So let us answer the second house-owner’s problem first, and see what can be done — about some of it, at all events. To begin with, we must consider what is meant by "ugly.” Is the house ugly outside, or is it ugly inside, or both? Is it comfortable or uncomfortable? If the latter, is it because it is too small, or is it uncomfortable because badly arranged? Are its furnishings hopeless? How hopeless? Before taking up the detailed questions of comfort and beautification of the interior, let us begin with the outside.

THE UGLY OUTSIDE

To the chapters on Building, Remodeling and Garden Decoration, and to the many other exterior suggestions (see Index) , there are only a few exterior considerations to add. If the sum to be spent is very small, it is best under most circumstances to spend the whole of it on the interior — except the renovating of such parts of the outside as may be shabby or out of repair.

A well-cared-for appearance is of first importance; color, second; inviting door and windows, third; garden decoration, fourth. After these four essentials — put them in which-

ever order you choose — comes the remodeling of the roof and facade.

No really shabby down-at-heel house can have charm, any more than clothes can have charm if they are out at elbows. But to remake an average exterior into a semblance of architectural convention is not half as important as to keep — or produce — an effect of inviting comfort, for this, above all other attributes, expresses the quality of home.

We all know houses — old-fashioned, mid-Victorian ones, especially — that may perhaps have little if any architectural merit, and yet, because of their air of well-cared-for comfort, have an appeal very much like that of certain people whose loveliness of character shining in their faces makes them seemingly beautiful.

The reason why certain brand-new — often very expensive — chouses are antipathetic to taste and to sentiment, is because their builders have attempted to apply charm as though it were a cosmetic. The charm of a house, like the charm of a person, is an outward manifestation of inward grace. Artificially applied, it is like nothing so much as rouge on an old woman’s cheeks.

This last is perhaps not a very happy simile, since the color of a house is of utmost importance, and ugly or disturbing color is impossible to beautify. Neutral, unobtruding color is best for commonplace architecture, because it camouflages structural shortcomings; it might be defined as the color nearest to that of its neighbors. (For definite color suggestions, see Index.)

Third in order of importance to the charm of a house, are the windows. Casement windows, oriel windows, little copper-topped shop windows, big modern studio windows, all are equally embellishing to the style of architecture they best suit. Perhaps windows are my monomania! A house without full-sized windows in the bathrooms and windows all over the place holds no charm for me. Perhaps you like gloom and darkness; perhaps you can even endure slits in the walls of bathrooms; in such case, my own insatiability

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

for windows may be left at that! But to me windows — wide ones, and lots of them — are smiling and friendly on the outside of a house as well as cheerful on the inside.

I have always believed — and practised — the theory that comfort, which includes light and air and cheerfulness on the inside of a house, is interpreted by the outside. This is my own idea of why certain houses less architecturally perfect than others have greater charm. I do grant that a house of importance may not be built like a factory; but there is a very wide range between rooms with whole glass walls and rooms with dormers like nothing so much as long narrow horse-stalls.

I might as well say frankly that I do not like steep roofs; I do not like their looks on the outside, and I hate their cut-into spaces on the inside. I’d rather have a little square room with big windows than have the biggest L-shaped room ever made with no matter how many windows away off at one end or the other. After all, few and little windows are no more becoming to a house than squint-eyes and pinched lips are becoming to a face.

The two remaining items of importance, remodeling and garden decoration, will be found in their own chapters. Other details of the exterior can be found through the Index. And now let us consider the real subject of this chapter, which is economical beautification of the interior.

BEAUTIFYING THE INTERIOR

If genius is the capacity for taking infinite pains, talent for creating a home of completely satisfying personality might be called the capacity for observing infinite detail. And if we except establishments that deliberately assume perfection, the ability to endow an average house with beauty and charm depends not so much on expenditure as upon your ability to recognize the potential possibilities, not merely in what you buy, but in what you already possess. At all events, ugliness is quite as often the result of unper-

ceived opportunity as of a slim purse. That an entirely empty purse can buy nothing is true, but it is equally true that the contents of a very moderately filled one spent knowingly can produce far greater beauty than a big bank account squandered with little sense and no sensibility.

Under average conditions, the outstanding qualification of beauty in every interior is color.

The greatest aid to charm is comfort, which includes convenience.

The theory of color-harmony and also certain phases of the behavior of color have been described in their own chapters, but the temperament of color, meaning its amiability, its indifference, or its cruelty to mixed varieties of furnishings, was not included.

The house-owner who can build or buy or completely remodel — who can start out with everything new and throw out every item that displeases — has no color problem but that of her personal decision and taste. The problem that is a far greater test of skill is ability to combine a heterogeneous collection of objects in a series of rooms of no especial beauty — and then to select a color that will be kind to all these objects and induce them to be kind to each other, and not stand off each by itself and shout, "Oh, look! Look how ugly!”

CRUEL COLORS

Certain walls behave exactly in this latter way. Those which have smooth-finished paneling in cream, oyster-gray, or turquoise blue, are kind to beauty, but cruel to ugliness. Turquoise-blue has the tendency, more than any other color, to make every object placed in front of it as conspicuous as though in the glare of a calcium light — which is why decorators invariably choose it as a background against which to display their wares. A plain plastered wall accentuates color and defines outline — and yet not too critically. But a cream or pale-gray paneled wall of beautiful finish ex-

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

hibits the shortcomings of each object placed against it with cold discrimination.

COLORS THAT ARE KIND

Colors that are kindest to ordinary objects include sand, light pinkish taupe, creamish faun — or any of the pastel or twice "grayed” colors (at high light, if you choose) indicated in the spokes of the color chart. (See Chapter X.)

Walls that are somewhat primitive in effect, such as those of rough finished plaster, or those that are papered with any of the unpretentious diaper or Colonial designs, all make sympathetic backgrounds for odd assortments of furniture and possessions.

Especially friendly to furniture that is somewhat overheavy are the colors that range from browns, coppers, russets, and old reds, to grayed yellow-greens, egg-plant purple, and sand. Combinations of these colors are to be had in many semiglazed percales that are entirely lovely, and that sell for a dollar or less a yard. There are also gay cotton prints of very small designs that are especially beguiling in primitive or cottage or bungalow surroundings, many of which can be bought for as little as twenty-five cents a yard. Unfadable chintz can be found for a dollar, or a little over, a yard, and sun-fast materials are to be had at from fifty cents a yard up.

The thing to do, of course, is to find material that is becoming to the majority of the things that you have, and then either remove the few that are jarring or change their color by painting, dyeing, and slip-covering.

KEEPING DOWN THE COST

Before attempting to say how much can be done for how little money, it is necessary to ask not merely what sort of house or apartment you have to begin with, and what you have to put in it, but first of all, how much can

you design, devise, or make yourself? If you must turn a room over to a decorator, it is necessarily much the same as going to an importer for a dress. In both cases, the chances are that the result will be good but the bill will be long.

On the other hand, if you have taste and knowledge enough to see opportunities — above all, if you are blessed with what is known as manual dexterity — ^you can have rooms at the cost of materials and at the price of the lost opportunities of others, as will be explained presently. At all events, whether you are having the actual work done for you, or whether you are going to do much of it yourself, color is the miracle-worker in all beautification of ugliness. Paint, dye, and slip-covers are literally the three magic gifts of the fairy tales.

Although the problem naturally varies with each example, the procedure is the same. The first thing to do is to collect the furnishings that must be used, and then, as best you can, bring them into harmony. For golden oak and unpainted brick, see pages 191-193. If the trim is mahogany or dark-stained wood, and you like it or may not change it, putty-colored walls are about the best — either painted or papered in plain three-inch stripes — and a colorful, rather big-patterned chintz.

A room impossible to refine without scraping and refinishing is one whose walls are gobbed with plastic paint. It will look like a tawdry grotto on a scenic railway and make all its surroundings look common. A whole room of wood is one thing, but crude dark trim is unreasonably exacting. It must be perfect in design, it must look hand-made, it must seem to be simple, but be in reality handled with supreme skill. It must, moreover, have just the right furnishings or it shouts "Common, common, see how common!”

HOW TO GO ABOUT IT

However, to take a specific example, let us say that you have an old-fashioned room that you want to do over. Let us

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

say that you have an old rosewood sofa and four chairs covered in somewhat faded but nice olive-grayed green rep, or wool damask; that you have a rather clumsy but comfortable mahogany secretary desk; a Morris chair in golden oak; a jigsaw-walnut spring-rocker upholstered in peacock-blue plush; a gate-legged table, good in design but "high-lighted” in finish; a Turkish rug that is genuine, but crude in color. Let us say that the room is high studded, and has a Victorian but good mantel, and tiles set around the fireplace.

Let us begin with the tiles. Do you like them? Are they good or horrible? If they are good Dutch or Italian, then you would best attune your room to them, which might allow you to keep the olive upholstery, or might not. If they are Majolica, perhaps by using the olive of your present covering as a background you could work out a very good color scheme in olives, browns, and yellows, with an accent of russet-red. If the tiles are blue Delft, the olive-green would be pretty bad — even though you add blue-violet and redorange, which completes the triad. Rose and cream are the right colors; or blue, blue-green, and green. If the tiles are without value or beauty, it would be best to have their surface hammered off and let them be cemented over — unless you fear spoiling that which is beyond spoilability, which is an often-encountered complex.

A woman of taste will sometimes allow bad tiles or bad woodwork to destroy a room utterly rather than to take the initiative herself and destroy the tiles or rip off the trim. She will also let a piece of furniture spoil a room rather than paint over a perfectly ordinary surface of wood! Curious arithmetic!

But because I personally prefer plain cement and painted surface to wrong-colored tiles or an unbecoming variety of wood, this is no reason why you should agree with me.

Let us say, however, that the tiles in this room are Majolica and very nice, and that the olive-green upholstery makes a very good background. It would be suitable to paper the walls a light gray-greenish color or an equally neutral but

light tint of taupe. You might paint the Morris chair olive green, sandpaper the shadings off the gate-leg table, refinish the floor with paint — or apply an even stain — and wax. Have the rug dyed as soft a brownish olive as it will take. Hide the jig-saw rocker under a slip-cover of heavy material, chintz or percale. Ideal coloring for the latter would be a lightgrayed yellow-green background with roses and tulips in gray-orange or orange-salmon, and an occasional big creamcolored rose or peony. All department stores as well as regular decorators have quantities of materials that will bring almost any unassorted furnishings into harmony. (See Chapter X.)

Having suggested, a few pages back, that beauty at little cost depends somewhat upon your own facility, let me explain what I mean by a rather personal example: Most families have words of their own, no less than characteristic habits — or whatever one chooses to call them. In our family, we long ago coined the definitions, a "pay-for,” a "make,” and a "fake.” A pay-for was a perfection that has to be bought, a make was a perfection that could be made by ourselves; a fake was a contrived effect. Whenever we thought of something or perhaps saw something that we wanted to put in a house, we always put the question to each other, "Can we make it, or fake it? Or is it a pay-for?” Whether it was a pay-for or not depended equally upon the exactions of the house and upon our own natural or acquired expertness.

PAY-FOR ITEMS

Items that have to be bought include plumbing fixtures, floor coverings, interior painting and papering, furniture, lamps, ornaments, pictures, mirrors, books, table appointments, kitchen utensils. And yet, even in this list, silver, glass, china, and kitchen utensils are alone unmakable and unfakable. I would not pretend that anyone can make plumbing fixtures; but in a simple house that makes no pretense to fineness of detail, partial decoration of color can be added to

WINDOWS THAT LOOK OUT INTO THE MIDST OF A FLOWER GARDEN

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

inexpensive fixtures by enameling — or lacquering — the outside of an ordinary porcelain-lined iron tub, and all other paintable surfaces.

MAKES

Makes include all the things which any member of the family is able to do professionally well. Floor-coverings, for instance, may figure in this list in the form of home-made rag, braided, hook, felt, and other rugs. Anyone can make a rug, such as the one in front of the fire in Plate 50. This is not a hook-rug but a cross-stitch rug made with especially heavy wool and canvas. The ship was home-designed, but endless patterns for cross-stitch can be bought. Hook-rugs are also made by many people. And embroidered petit-point for chairs is almost as much of a vogue to-day as it used to be among the ladies of the Middle Ages.

Mural painting is the most valuable "make” there is, but for this there must be a genuine artist. Ordinary room-painting, however, is within the ability of many owners of houses that are unexacting, and decoration is limited only by imagination.

Anyone should be able to make curtains and slip-covers. Lamp-shades made of chiffon, which have come back into fashion with the modernistic lamps, are merely a question of sewing neatly. Each wire in the frame is covered first with strips of folded china silk wound in a tight spiral (like a fire-lighter) and sewed tight where it ends. This gives you a foundation on which to sew the covering smoothly and tautly. Paper lamp-shades — unless left raw-edged at top and bottom, and merely held on a frame — are so difficult as to be almost impossible for an amateur to attempt. Undecorated ones, however, are for sale at all the art counters of the department stores. Those of white paper bound with a narrow gilt-paper passe-partout are hard to find, but manila-paper shades bound at top and bottom with gold gimp are for sale everywhere. And decoration with set-in prints or cut-out and glued-on pictures can be done by anyone.

FAKES

Fakes, according to our home-made definition, are things that we can do well enough ourselves for stop-gap effects — sometimes well enough for permanency. Many fakes, in this sense, include things that anyone 'of normal dexterity should be able to do easily — if necessary.

Time was when painting a whole room or even small pieces of furniture could be done passably well only by an expert, since the inexpertly handled brush left streaks with stubborn persistence. But to-day dozens of varieties of paints are being manufactured especially for the amateur. One might call them paints that cover their own brush-tracks, and they can be applied by anyone who will put a moderate amount of paint or lacquer on a brush, and carefully cover a surface with an even quantity of it. Painting a large wallsurface or a door is a different story; or maybe I only think it is because once, twenty-odd years ago, I attempted to paint an entire room with brush-tracking paint and made a thoroughly bad job of it.

But if you can do your own painting, the cost of paint by the gallon can is quite different (and so, too, perhaps is the result) ! from that of the finished room handed over to you by the painter.

One word of warning: In case you mind the smell of oil and turpentine — or, still worse, of banana oil — it is only fair to tell you that it can be very sickening. In fact, to be shut in a room with the smell of lacquers can be as bad as two days out on a rough sea to a worst sailor! But if you can paint outdoors, lacquering ugly pieces of furniture is not difficult. And decoration with decalcomania can be done by anyone. Decalcomania for furniture must, of course, be the transfers made for decorative purposes, and not the little ordinary ones sold by toy stores for children to play with. You have to use an adhesive preparation that is bought with them, and finish with several coats of varnish or shellac, rubbed off with pumice. (Directions are given by the manufacturers.)

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

The hardest painting detail for an amateur is painting a fine straight line. The only way to attempt it is to draw both edges of the line with a pencil and ruler, and then fill in with a very fine brush. A painter or a draftsman simply draws it free-hand. Pure magic!

All sorts of ugly furniture, battered furniture, crude furniture, can be painted. If it is a half-way good piece of wood surface, and is varnished, begin by removing the varnish with turpentine, banana oil, or denatured alcohol, first trying a spot to see which takes it off most easily. Some paints, it may be added, can be put on top of varnish without any trouble at all. Often as good as decalcomania, and sometimes better, are cut-out pictures, pasted on and shellacked. But don’t use too many.

All makeshift furniture that is used until better can be found or afforded, belongs in the list of fakes. For that matter, greatly to be preferred is a plain pine drop-leaf kitchentable with cheapest wooden chairs of no cost, painted attractively, as compared with some expensive furniture that is not what you have in mind, and whose price therefore represents a waste.

For a house that I was doing for a client, we faked many makeshifts, because objects of real beauty were beyond price. Amidst the horrors of an extremely bad furniture department I picked out the cheapest chairs and tables that were possible in outline. They were villainous in finish, but we put on several coats of varnish to make a smooth surface, sandpapered it, and then painted on top of the varnish, thus disguising the porous wood. Copying an eighteenthcentury chair, we painted them all a black-brown, with very little striping of wiped-off gold, and then sketched a Chinese pagoda design on the chair splats in gray and greenish-brown and black. Thus, with the aid of a few odd hours of time, a perfectly good-looking dining-room set of furniture was faked for less than twenty dollars.

Many varieties of decorations are also possible with very little ingenuity. An ordinary six- or eight-paneled door can

be made lovely by having a piece of looking-glass (not beveled) fitted into each panel, and then painting or applying a decalcomania border around the panel-frames. Or a fake would be to cut out a running pattern in silver paper — very carefully, of course — glue it around the panels, and either leave it in flat silhouette or shade it with sepia or with neutral tint. When thoroughly dry, everything but the lookingglasses could be coated with shellac. If you can’t draw, you can get a pattern design at an art-embroidery department, cut it out, and trace around it. In fact, the things that can be done with scissors, a glue-pot, and shellac would fill a book. In the make-shop of our family my own share was everything that could be done with scissors and mucilage, or needle and thread.

I am told that few children of to-day are taught to cut and sew — that there is no time in modern life for such an old-fashioned occupation. But when the child grows up and has a house of her own, ability to cut and sew whatever she may think of is decidedly an advantage, since the cost of having every item bought — or made to order — runs into hundreds or even thousands of dollars. About 80 per cent, of these dollars can be saved by those who have skill to make things, and the money spent instead on pay-fors. Of course, workmanship that is less than good is merely the definition of a botch, and botches are becoming neither to furniture nor to house.

A dressing-table is an article that can be made out of almost anything — a sawed-off bureau or a shabby table with a broad top nailed on it. The top can be painted and trimmed round with a petticoat and cover of what you like, and any kind of mirror can be added, either standing on the table or hung behind it.

Bed-springs and mattresses are absolutely pay-fors. But a bed-frame is unnecessary — beyond a platform on which to stand the springs. In case of no bed-frame, there must be a valance from the top of the box-spring to the floor, and curtains hung at the head.

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

Sofa cushions, slip-covers, heavy curtains, lambrequins — the many things which cost much when bought or made to order, and which, made by yourself, cost little — bring down the total more than most people realize.

A FEW ASPECTS OF ECONOMY

Except those of us who are so rich as to have no need to count the cost of anything, or so poor as to have nothing to spend, we all have our pet extravagances or pet economies. My own pet economy is to refuse flatly to buy for five dollars what I can make for fifty cents. To me it seems plain lack of intelligence to pay a high price for nothing. I believe in paying whatever you must — and can — for something that really contributes quality to a house or a room; but I also believe in spending nothing for what is worth nothing. Buy a truly beautiful object — no matter what price its value — if you can pay for it; but don’t buy something half-way expensive that is really worthless.

To translate the proposition into clothes: Get a rabbit coat for seventy-five dollars or get a higher-grade better coat for two or three hundred dollars, or whatever it may be. But don’t buy a paper-stiff light-yellow mink coat for twelve hundred dollars, because that is twelve hundred dollars thrown away! In the same way, pay what you must for an eighteenth- century secretary that is a beautiful example, but don’t throw away three hundred dollars on a piece of furniture that — to one who knows anything — looks exactly like stiff yellow mink!

WHAT TO SPEND ON

A few nice things instantly lift the value of an interior. A conspicuously good secretary, or break- front cabinet; a good chair or two, or a pair of console tables with mirrors; a foundation of genuine beauty, upon which to build gradually, later on; a few ornaments of value; and, above all, beautiful lamps and shades. One might even put lamp-shades

at the top of the list, since they are accents of greatest value to the beauty of every evening room.

It is consoling to remember that negative, unnoticeable things are easily put into the background, while the more conspicuous situations receive the things of worth. But objects of screaming vulgarity are no more to be subdued than a screaming child that keeps on screaming throughout a railroad journey, or wherever escape from its disturbance is impossible. Nothing, for instance, can be done to quiet a violent rug or to shut out other abominations of color or form in furnishings that are placed where the eye cannot escape them. In short, only a major operation can refine a vulgar room. Major operations, of course, include reconstruction with carpenter’s tools, painting and upholstering, or even discarding and buying new.

ILLITERACIES IN HOUSE-FURNISHING

Illiteracies of language are defined by grammar, illiteracies of clothes are recognized by nine women out of ten on sight — because American women are more smartly turned out than any other women in the world. But illiteracies in housefurnishing must be far from unusual, if certain objects found in catalogues, house-furnishing departments, and advertising pages find any buyers. Obviously they do, or those who offer them for sale would be out of business.

In the beautification of ugliness, put down these villainies: Every object whatever made of shaded wood, oxidized metal, art bronze (except in green), brushed brass, stained

glass set in metal lamp-shades, and all usdess wriggles and shadings.

A certain amount of color whisked on and wiped off _

such as smooth, even bands of half-worn-off gold or other coloring on painted furniture— can be perfect. But remember, antiquing furniture is very like making up a face. How often does it look well! How often false? You say it is all in the eye that sees? Exactly!

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

UPHOLSTERED MATERIALS

If you find a material that really makes the room, buy it. But be sure — if it is very expensive — that nothing else will take its place at half or quarter the price. In saying this I am taking it for granted that you are not one of the herd of price- tag sheep, who think highest cost evidence of value! For such as these, this chapter is not written. No chapter ever can be written for them. How could it! But whether high-priced or low-priced, do not recklessly buy materials that will stand neither wear nor sun. Many materials that are quite cheap are color-fast; others that are very expensive fade at the sight of the sun. I bought one little print that cost fifty cents a yard, and its fourth seashore season finds it looking as colorfully gay as it did at first. On the other hand, a beautiful linen chintz at sixteen dollars a yard faded so completely that it had to be replaced at the end of six weeks. This does not mean that cheap things are good and expensive things bad, but merely that it is not always a question of cost. Guaranteed chintzes or other materials that will be replaced by their manufacturers if they fade, are genuine economy — especially those whose guarantee includes the cost of reupholstering. A certain well-known manufacturer replaced for me both upholstery and curtains of an entire room because a guaranteed chintz faded.

Best upholstery silks cannot be equaled — not completely. But a certain synthetic taffeta at less than three dollars a yard wears splendidly and has much of the luster and beauty of the regular upholstery taffeta at ten and a half dollars. Dress taffeta — at least the several dozen pieces that I have on various occasions tried — stands neither the sun nor even the shade.

Excellent materials in manufactured silk, in wool, jute, and cotton, are to be had in almost every dry-goods store in every city in the United States; or samples can be procured from the great stores of the greater cities, if you will write them what you want.

FURNITURE ADAPTATIONS

Perhaps the greatest economy possible in the house of little cost can be made in furniture — both by adapting it to different uses and by subjecting it to plastic operations. By adaptation is meant merely that objects which are unsympathetic to the position for which they are intended can quite possibly be embellishing as well as useful elsewhere. A secretary-desk, intended for a library, can equally well be put in a man’s bedroom or in his study. A typical chahe longue will go perfectly well into any informal down-stairs sittingroom that IS at all feminine. A highboy in a living-room, or even in a drawing-room, is no novelty; and neither is a bureau (called commode) if mirror-supports be taken off. Or if an enormous Provengal wardrobe be completely clogging to the bedroom it is intended for, it may have its back taken off and its depth cut shallow and be adjusted as a doorway between the hall and the pantry, or between the hall and the children’s playroom. For the former use, the apron at the bottom would have to be cut through in the center and each half fastened to the bottom of each door; but as a playroom entrance the step-over into the inside of the wardrobe makes just the sort of personable doorway that children find delectable.

An extension dining- table that does not go with the dining-room furniture can be divided, and half of it used as a console in the hall, while the other half will serve as a magazine-table in the living-room or elsewhere. In other words, if half of it may be put where all of it is impossible, divide it, even though half must go into the attic!

The very good bookcase table that is given with the 1930 edition of the Encyclopcedia Britannica is useful in any house where it can be stood in the middle of a room or with one end to the wall; but in many houses (including my own) where this arrangement is impossible, it can be cut lengthwise through the center and a pair of bookcase consoles made instead. At least that is what I did! It must, of course, be

A BEDROOM WITH EVERY DETAIL OF INVITING COMFORT

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

emphasized that cutting furniture — or what might very truthfully be called destroying it — should not be done recklessly. It is suggested merely as an alternative. If a misfit threatens to spoil a whole room, it is allowable to risk spoiling the misfit.

If an object be of great value, it should be rather prayerfully considered. Even so, in the small, low-ceilinged rooms of modern houses or apartments, the most beautiful piece of furniture imaginable would better be reduced to a size fitting the proportions of the room, than be allowed to spoil the scale. If it be too perfect to alter, and too out-of-scale to use, then sell it or give it away, or put it in storage. But don’t hope that it embellishes the room it does not fit, any more than you would hope that a hat five sizes too small or too big could possibly be other than grotesque. Many pieces of furniture that are now unsightly encumbrances could be made beautifully becoming, were their depth reduced.

An average room can perfectly digest such a piece of furniture, for instance, as an eighteenth-century break-front bookcase, because, in the first place, its fragile design counteracts the amount of wall space it occupies; and in the second, its shallowness further diminishes the effect of size. The pieces which obtrude are these which project far into the room. Empire pieces, both French and American, are among the worst offenders, because they are heavy in design as well as thick through, so that it becomes a choice between making them shallow or letting them gobble up the room — or sending them to auction, where, for the sole reason of their depth, they sell for nothing.

PLASTIC OPERATIONS ON FURNITURE

Let us hope that such a piece of furniture is bought by one who has a diagnostician’s eye for plastic operations, because a greater asset for one who would furnish a house at least expense is hard to think of. It is a pet asset of my own — which I have so far kept to myself. But if it should prove of

any use to you, you are more than welcome! The value of the idea is this: All big pieces of furniture sell at auction for half or quarter the price of small-sized furniture — sometimes for still less. If you have an eye for adjusting furniture — which is precisely the same as an eye for adjusting clothes — bargains surround you on every side. In other words, you buy the big or the thick pieces at no price at all, and make them into thin and small pieces of value.

Last winter two fifteenth-century refectory tables were sold at auction. They were identical in quality and in age. One was seven feet long by thirty inches wide, the other was twelve feet long by forty inches wide. The small one sold for sixteen hundred dollars and the big one sold for ninety. Nothing was easier than cutting down the top of the big one, and finishing it with wax; the under frame was very narrow, and the long stretchers were simply cut down in length, rejoined, and waxed. The result was a genuinely old table embellishing to any average house.

The reconstruction of a big roll-top ofl&ce-desk can sometimes be accomplished by cutting off the top and leaving the pigeonholes across the back in the way Sheraton so often did. Scraping and refinishing the surface and putting on good brasses will usually complete the job; but, if necessary, you can also cut down the height or depth.

Perhaps this sounds like spending a fearful amount of money, but it does not necessarily mean that. A carpenter under my own direction reshaped a colossal walnut desk in one day. This particular desk was not cut through the back; had it been, the rebacking of each drawer would have taken another day. In this instance, the wood was good. If it has to be scraped and refinished, the painter’s bill can come to more than that of the carpenter — unless you finish it yourself.

Where one has a workshop, meaning that some member of the family happens to like such work as a pastime, one of the small electric polishing machines make refinishing very easy. Remove any excrescences that you dislike with a

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

chisel. Scrape off varnish with a paint-remover and a scraper, then wrap the polisher with sandpaper or canvas dipped in ground pumice and let the machine do the work. When the surface is prepared, stain and varnish lightly, and then use the wax polisher until the finish is satisfactory. Last of all, put on new brasses.

Apart from cutting down size, furniture operations have many other economical advantages, since ugliness most often is due either to vulgar ornamentation or to vulgar finish. Therefore, if you can look at an object as something to be reconstructed — exactly as you look for possibilities in a house you think of buying to remodel — you will find it quite easy to see that an old bureau can be made into just what you are looking for, by taking off the mirror and putting on new handles; that an old library table can become a treasure, if you chisel off the ornamentation and refinish the surface; or that the marvelous mahogany of a shabby, lumbering desk can, with little or no expense, be changed into a shape that is inoffensive — or even pleasing — to the eye.

As knowledge always develops with practise, your critical faculty soon learns when to accept and when to reject old furniture. Many people say it brings discontent to develop critical standards, because it makes us more often displeased than pleased. Perhaps! But, on the other hand, a critical sense can teach us to construct quite as well as it can encourage us to destroy. We have no right to tear down, of course, unless we do our best also to build; but those who acquire this remaking facility will find their reward in using it, for plastic surgery is an art that can be quite as successful on furniture as on faces. Moreover, it is within the skill of anyone with observation enough to see what can be done.

One might set up a furniture hospital in which deformities could be made into objects of beauty — a fascinating hobby for a man who loves carpentry and good furniture. It would be a godsend in any neighborhood. Without some means of reconstructing furniture, we may as well admit right here that the insurmountable obstacle to a house of

beauty at little cost is the impossibility of buying acceptable wood furniture. In fact, so serious is this handicap that I cannot leave the subject without entering a plea to those who can remove it.

A PLEA TO MANUFACTURERS OF COMMERCIAL FURNITURE AND TO THE STORES WHICH DISPLAY IT

In no country in the world can those whose purses are moderately slim choose more beautiful china, crockery, glass, wall-paper, every variety of textile, than in the United States. We Americans cover our walls, hang our windows, set our tables, make our beds, furnish our bathrooms, and complete our kitchens with objects of beauty and utility at reasonable

expense. But, furniture - ! Those of us who are able to pay

Fifty-seventh Street prices can buy here in New York the most beautiful furniture in the world. But those of us with fastidious tastes and medium-sized bank-accounts have a long, long search to find furniture that is not unspeakably common. In fact, we usually have to go without it — barring what we can find at auctions and perhaps remake!

On special floors of certain department stores, there is a limited selection of lovely things, but, alas! at from two (in one especial New York Store) to four times (in other stores) the cost of the horde of hideosities filling every inch of their own regular furniture department.

Why do they not finish simply and beautifully one single article that is for sale on the "regular floor” for purses which are only moderately filled? As for the objects depicted in the catalogues of mail-order houses, I wonder if their manufacturers think it fair that those who happen to have been denied an education in critical taste should be induced to squander their hard-earned money on monsters? If they saw a woman with a baby that had the head of a toad and the body of an armadillo, they would be filled with pity as well as repulsion. True, a deformed article of furniture is scarcely to be compared to the tragedy of a deformed child; yet it is hard to

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

understand how a reputable manufacturer or merchant can be so callous as to sell deformities to helpless buyers who have had no chance to know what the normal form of beauty should be.

It is, of course, not fair to accuse all manufacturers of wantonly producing ugliness; but, apart from the ubiquitous butterfly table, the Windsor or Hitchcock chair, and a few other Cottage Colonial pieces, it is difficult to feel that the manufacturers are doing their part.

If you, who are a person of taste and cultivation, think I unfairly exaggerate, let me ask to buy a secretary, a sideboard, a dining-room table, a library table — or even a woodframed drawing-room chair — that is neither vulgar in design nor high-lighted in finish, and to make a note of where you found it, and what you had to pay. I think you will end by agreeing with the foregoing criticism, which applies to wood furniture only. You can get good upholstered furniture at reasonable as well as high prices.

When I wrote the original edition of Etiquette in 1922, it was impossible to find ready-to-wear men’s clothes of American manufacture that were not freaks. When I rewrote the book in 1928, my arraignment of the clothing manufacturers was deleted, because it was no longer true. Furniture is to-day in exactly the situation that men’s clothes were in 1922. One must go abroad to buy, or pay a price far beyond the reach of the average purse. And if you cannot yourself tell good from bad, you can pay every bit as much for bad as for good. The fact that the expensive examples of bad furniture are beautifully made, so that drawers pull out with a finger-nail and doors close with perfect precision, is just an additional item of discouragement, because it tells of expert workmanship gone wrong.

That every manufacturer is eager to supply what the public demands is an excuse too trite for comment. Testing the public’s taste by what it must accept or go without, is no proof that we, who are the public, might not prefer something very much better, were we given our choice.

Meanwhile, my earnest advice is to remember that until you can find articles that beautify, articles that uglify can be done without. Far better paint an object that you have, or that you can buy for a few dollars at auction, and save until you can find or afford a table or a desk that will contribute distinction, rather than to endure mediocrity because you can find nothing else.

COMFORT ALWAYS AN ASSET

Remember also that the outstanding asset of homelike and inviting charm is comfort. Comfort is, moreover, produced far more often by thoughtful attention to detail than by money-value. Even a rather homely object that is really comfortable is apt to have a certain becomingness — unless it be jarringly vulgar or unsuitable. Other things being equal, comfort is the most important attribute of charm.

Since comfort and convenience are both discussed at length in other chapters, let us at this time merely note that objects of comfort should not be discarded until other objects of greater beauty and equal comfort take their place. Keep an old desk that has plenty of useful space; its obvious utility will gloss over its shortcomings. Slip-cover the old chair that has worn into hollows, if the hollows fit you, so that you find comfort in them. In other words, don’t change old for new unless the comfort of the old will be equaled by that of the new.

AGAIN THE ASSET OF SUITABILITY

My own little house in Massachusetts is especially suitable as an illustration for this (also the next) chapter, because a house of charm at least expense was my objective, precisely. I wanted a house that would be no responsibility, a house in a friendly neighborhood where a lock on the front door would be unnecessary, because even if it were to stand wide open, its tranquillity would be undisturbed. I wanted exactly that type of house in the very spot where it is; it couldn’t

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

be more perfect for my own requirements, and I always hate to leave it and long to get back to it. And yet there is nothing expensive about any part of it. It has windows and much sunlight, and it is always filled with flowers. It has one discomfort — its hot- water system is an unending vexation; but, barring the difficulty (and cost) of hot water and an occasional atmosphere of cellarless coldness, the house has no flaws! It has brass plumbing-pipes and plenty of small but pretty and comfortable bathrooms. Its only bits of extravagance — ^meaning details that could have been much cheaper — are the stairs and the front door.

The painting throughout was not much more than an effect and a promise, but the papers are sun-fast; the most expensive ones, as it happened, are those in the servants’ rooms, and the only enamel paint is in the kitchen and the pantry. The floors are all painted and waxed, the rugs (except in the hall and living-room) are clearflax, and a lot of small hook-rugs. The hall-carpet and stair-runner are velvet, which is all wrong; but I could get the right color in no other make, and color is to me always of first importance.

In most of the rooms a few nice or genuinely old things are mixed with things of no value at all. In the room shown in Plate 50, the mantel is a genuine earliest-nineteenth-century example, but the mirror over it is one which, when I bought it, was new and shiny; I left it out in the sun and rain and snow a whole winter — and faked its antiquity. The ship prints are mixed; three are good, two are quaint, and one is poor, but they all are much the same in color and look well enough together. The model of a New Bedford whaler in the window, Plate 46, had an accident just before the photograph was taken. I caught my sleeve in the rigging and it crashed to the floor, broke off the bowsprit (which may be seen hanging), broke the tops of both masts, and just about wrecked it!

The desk in Plate 33 is an old one of pear- wood, and the ship picture over it one of the good ones, and the mirror very nice. The glass bowl for flowers was fifty cents. The candle-

sticks of brass with bells were bought at auction. The lamp cost very few dollars, the inkstand fifty cents, the pen-tray a like amount — at auction — and the shot to hold pens is in a glass of no value. New pens and clips are on a small octagonal egg-plant glass dish, meant to be an ash-tray; the paper cutter is amber, and the stamp-box silver. The curtain hold-backs are modern reproductions in egg-plantpurple glass. The chair is a modern copy of a Hitchcock armchair.

The French chairs, covered with genuine Norman petticoat material, are nice ones, and the bowl for flowers on the end of the sofa (Plate 50) , and another at the end of a long table are Lowestoft and about the best things in the room. The rug is a plain sand-colored Scotch wool; the ship-rug in front of the mantel is a "make” by myself.

The big armchair is of most comfortable deep-spring upholstery, but it was covered with such a dingy imitation tapestry, and was so enormous in size, that I bought a pair of them at auction for seven dollars apiece. I cut off the legs and hid the ugliness of the tapestry under slip-covers. The sofa was bought for fifty-odd dollars at a sale in a New York department store noted (and justly) for the value it gives. It was marked down because its covering had faded. Its covering was of no importance, since I should have slipcovered it anyway to match the room.

Almost any upholstered furniture that is comfortable is satisfactory, because it can be slip-covered. Wood furniture must be good — or at least not offensive in outline. There are a thousand things that can be faked until such time as you can buy or find — worth-while pay-fors. The lamp-shade over in the corner at the wall end of the sofa is merely one of those sold at every art material counter — of plain manila paper bound top and bottom with gold braid. Colored prints are cut out and pasted on, and shellacked; the gold braid is covered with taffeta ribbon.

The lamp-shade on the desk, Plate 46, is the same thing, bound with ribbon and with a little print pasted on. The

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

chest of drawers over in the corner behind the sofa was made by a local carpenter and lacquered red, with lines around the drawers and a decorated Chinese design in gold across the top drawer and on the sides. The gold outlining is very much rubbed off and does not show half as much actually as in the photograph, which accentuates the contrast. It was made solely as an object of convenience — with sixteen drawers, each intended to hold two stacks of typewriting paper or manuscript.

Having said, earlier in this chapter, that painting, dyeing, and slip-covering were of greatest value in producing beauty at least expense, I offered a few suggestions for paintings but not a word about dyeing or slip-covering. Therefore, some practical suggestions here.

HOW TO DYE THINGS AT HOME

Dyeing in the ordinary sense is dipping material into dye — which usually means sending it to the dyers. In many cases, however, it is possible to dye things at home, with a brush instead of going through the usual laundry process of wetting, and drying, and manipulating to prevent streaking, and then ironing. Personally, I cannot qualify as a laundress, and my own part in any dipping-into-dye process goes no further than the mixing of the color and trying out a sample. But the brush process I have done myself, time and again.

You simply mix dye as you would mix paint. Take a very wide paint-brush or a sponge (in this case wear rubber gloves) , and then put the dye on as though it were paint. You can do rugs, carpets such as Brussels, and clearflax floorcoverings. You can tone down violent colors with dye, or with coffee. You can pin material out on long planks — first putting blotting-paper or old sheets or something under the goods to absorb any excess of liquid — and simply paint the dye on. This is especially good for any material that should not — or can not — be wrung out.

Straw porch-rugs can be painted. So can awnings. Painted awnings do not pull up and down very well, but for those that are left down, painting is perfectly satisfactory. I have had them done time and again. Dye would .not do except for loss of color.

HOW TO MAKE SLIP-COVERS

Slip-covers, which are so unreasonably expensive when made by certain decorators or by certain stores, are so easy to make at home that anyone who can fit the shoulders of a child’s dress or put in a pair of sleeves could not possibly have any difficulty.

There are three ways of making slip-covers. The professional and hardest way is to sew the seams on the wrong side and set a cord-filled piping between. The second way is to fit them on the wrong side, then sew the seams about threeeighths of an inch nearer the edge than the basting, and turn right side out and sew a French seam. The French seam goes right side out to give the effect of cording.

But the third, and much the easiest way, is to fit the seams on the right side and sew them as fitted on the right side. After they are sewn on the machine, cut the seams evenly close — about three-eighths of an inch — and bind with tape or organdie binding — the sort that is folded and pressed and for sale in every village or city dry-goods store. The latter is easier to put on smoothly, better looking and likely to wear perfectly if the slip-covers are not constantly laundered. Begin by cutting your material in short lengths — the fight lengths with pattern centered on chair or sofa-back and seat. For a sofa, seam the material in sufficient width before fitting. Pin to the furniture and cut wide edges roughly (Fig. 137), then pin each seam exactly over the upholstery ones underneath. Where there is a pleat or a set-in piece in the upholstery, make the same pleats or set-in pieces in the slip-cover. Pin all the seams exactly as you would fit a shoulder seam. The object is to fit close enough to be smooth,

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE
