but not tight. Make little pleats where the edge of the material stands loose, or snip in the angles that draw tight, exactly as you snip armholes for sleeve-joining. Be sure to tuck plenty of material in around the edges of the seat at back and sides of arms to allow for the play

of the springs. The trick of good slip-covers is to have them as loose as possible without wrinkling.

If you want to pipe the seams, the way that is easiest — for me — is to pin the material securely to the chair (or sofa) about an inch inside of the seams, and then to baste the piping to one side of the material precisely on the line of the seam, and then pin the joining material exactly along the basting.

Good slip-covers have a box-pleating for a finish at the bottom. But those in a very simple house can be left plain and finished with gimp or narrow fringe.

SEW ON THE DOTTED LINE

LINED CURTAINS

Ready-made glass curtains are so inexpensive that unless you want some that are very unusual, they are really not worth the time spent in making them at home. Making heavy curtains that are unlined is too easy even to describe. But lined ones have a mysterious tendency to bag. Only the professionally expert can manipulate the top hem and adjust the stretchability of material and lining. But, there is a trick: Simply leave the curtains disconnected at the bottom by hemming material and lining separately, and they will hang beautifully.

INEXPENSIVE FLOOR-COVERINGS

The one advantage — economically — of Eastern rugs is that they wear for ever and ever, and never tell anything. A plain velvet carpet attracts your attention to every footmark, and never recovers from anything spilled on it. Other surfaces, such as the Scotch wool rugs, wear very well. There is a carpeting that resembles a very coarse Brussels: it is cheap and good-looking for a country house.

Clearflax rugs come in excellent colors and are entirely suitable in simple country cottages. For houses of greater importance, plain neutral-colored velvets are best. Worsted velvet is most durable — and most expensive.

Oriental rugs of exquisite softness are within buying power of none but deep purses; Oriental rugs of medium price, or domestic rugs of Oriental design, are usually kaleidoscopic horrors of blatant restlessness — unless you dye them to keep them quiet.

THE QUALITY OF APPEAL

If a house lack appeal, it may be because the furniture suggests discomfort. A chair standing by itself, over toward a corner, without daylight, lamp-light, or a table near it, is uncomfortable to sit in, and looks it! Furniture with its back turned to the entering guest is never inviting. How can it be? Neither is any arrangement that is inconvenient.

But never let yourself believe that your house is unappealing because you are not rich! Such a reason is nonsense — unless you are not only poor as poverty, but helplessly incompetent as well. Without a cent to spend on materials, you can do nothing; that is true. But the waste of dollars upon dollars by those who spend ignorantly is the outstanding cause of an ugly home.

In fact, how many rooms can you count, in the houses of the very rich, that you envy? Personally, I can remember only one house that ever made me think, "Yes, it might be

the house of charm at least expense

wonderful to be very, very rich and own such beauty as this!” On many occasions I have thought it might be pleasant to choose certain things — oh, yes! The woodwork of a room, perhaps; a picture here, an ornament there, a piece of furniture — but the whole possession complete — never but once.

WHY HAVE VERY RICH MEN’s HOUSES SO LITTLE CHARM?

My own answer to this question is: Perhaps because color is of paramount importance, and the typical palatial rooms are characteristically submerged in monotones of gold and brown and raw sienna! I often wonder whose pulses are really stirred by yellow- veined marble walls embellished with gold, and hung with huge tapestries depicting colossal nymphs and satyrs in deeper greens and tans, or with heavy gold-framed paintings of religious subjects that impel one to speak in whispers as though in church. How personal is the appeal of great urn-shaped vases on ponderously ornamented consoles, or on a caryatid-supported mantel? How much can anyone enjoy sitting on a titanic throne chair with heavily nubbined back that is bruising to spine and skull to lean against? How can one choose to dwell in a room with threetimes-curtained windows, or in the dim twilight of enshrouding amber lights barely strong enough to reveal a priceless porcelain lamp-base, or a heavily ornate applique? If the room be a library, the chances are that there is not a book in all the parading rows of gold-tooled bindings that individually invites you to take it down, no gleam of light to read it by if you did.

Of course, if you like the gloom of grandeur — or the grandeur of gloom — that is all right. But give me instead, any day, a room with casement windows — attic ones will do — with a view of trees, or even of chimney-pots, with flowers growing on the window-sill, a big plain unencumbered table to write on, a sofa to sit or lie down on, a piece of furniture with plenty of drawers to hold papers, a few comfortable

43 S

chairs for friends, an open fire, plenty of light, flowers for ornament, and lots of books. What more could one ask?

FAKED BINDINGS FOR DECORATION

Speaking of books in an attic reminds me of a pet fake that might be useful to some one. A long while ago we built a huge new library with yards upon yards of yawning shelves to fill. Up in the attic were countless discarded, odd-backed books. The room was much too good for mixed-back junk, and shabby at that. But we had a fake idea: We went to a manufacturer of book-binding materials, and bought a lot of different surfaces in red, of many shades, a considerable amount of blue, a lot of calf -skin and leatheret, and covered almost a thousand books. Covering was easy. The snag was writing labels! Some of them we wrote on a typewriter with a black ribbon on variously tinted papers that come in little packages at stationery stores; but for most of the labels we used gold paper, and printed the titles by hand in sepia ink.

The label-printing is a snag because, unless the shelves are high or distant, it is very difficult to hide the fact that they are neither stamped nor printed. The one of us who could really letter did a hundred or so, but the rest were pretty bad. Typewriting was the next best, and would be very good if you had — or could borrow — several machines, so as to vary the size of the lettering. Or if you have very many books to do, it might be worth buying a toy printingpress. To have a printer make them is apt to swell the cost out of all proportion, since the type has to be reset for each book.

All this, of course, is not a treatise on how to cover old books that are half-way presentable, but merely a suggestion whereby a lot of old ragamuffin novels can help to furnish shelves and even add the ever-essential becomingness of color.

If you choose to take pains, you can do really attractive

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

books in passable bindings. The best way is to cut the cover as shown in Figure 138. Glue the cover all around the edge, and paste it to the inside covers of the book. Push the glued flaps A-A over the ends of the back between the back and the binding.

Then paste a thin lining-paper over the inside cover, and the book is practically rebound. But in doing a great number of books that are to be used temporarily, an ordinary jacket like that on every new book will answer sufficiently well.

When we covered our books, we bound the ones we really liked in the way described, with gold labels and sometimes small cut-out gold designs, and put red jackets, blue jackets, and tan jackets on the others, which were temporary fill-shelves only.

EXTRAVAGANT ECONOMIES IN BUILDING

To the not-rich builders of a new house, the following warnings are offered:

No extravagance is more reckless than skimping on the structural details of your house. This does not mean that you must build a fireproof building at seventy-five cents or a dollar a cubic foot, but merely that a house should be sound at core — ^unless you are prepared to pay for it time and time over in repairs and replacements.

On the other hand, to be told by the builder that a steel and stone and copper building is the wisest construction to choose, is very much the same as being told that it is wiser to buy diamonds instead of glass, because diamonds wear better. Most of us cannot pay for such construction except by sacrificing other things — space, for instance, and other essentials to comfort.

If this be the case — if you cannot build a stone or brick

HOW TO CUT A BOOK- COVER

house without sacrificing space and comfort and money left for furnishing — it could hardly be as satisfactory in result as a house of stucco, or possibly of wood. But whatever type of house you do build, build it structurally well.

Skimped building is not an outlay of least expense; it is money thrown away on something that is worthless. By skimped building is meant the using of unseasoned lumber, badly mixed and badly formed cement, iron piping, tin flashings, vile-looking roofing, no insulation, and inconvenience of plan. Do not overlook or give up, as extravagances, any details of comfort. The place to economize, if you must economize, is on trimming. If necessary, leave parts of the house to be finished little by little. Depend upon comfort and color for beautification, and then perhaps gradually, as you are able, add embellishment that counts. Of, if the house is to be finished at once, choose the things that are to you essential, and see that they are not among the things absentmindedly omitted.

Where I would economize, you will perhaps not. Details that I care nothing about may be of consequence to you. For example, tiles in the kitchen, tiles in the bathrooms, or hard-wood floors and trim, mean less than nothing to me. In a little house such as my own in Massachusetts, they are not even suitable. In a town house or a certain style of important country house, bathrooms — kitchens, too — are tiled. Personally, I like other walls better than tiles. A velvet carpet entirely hiding the floor is the only antidote I know of to cure the hospital effect of the typical white-tiled bathroom. Tile walls for kitchens are very practical, but it is absolutely necessary to lay cork or other flooring over marble or tile in kitchen and pantry. (See Index.)

Hard-wood trim throughout the house is best for quality, but unpainted it is often the stumbling-block of charm in furnishing.

There are many items that are senseless extravagance for one who must economize. If you have plenty of money — and like that sort of house — then go ahead and choose all the

TPIE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

gables and dormers, and bays and irregularities that spread all over the lot. But if money is important, it is merest common sense to remember that every irregularity, and every object that is not of stock size and pattern, helps the expense to mount like a taximeter and quite likely adds not one iota of comfort.

You may not like a square-box wooden house, and there is no reason why you should build what you do not like — except that, with a given amount of money, you can have bigger rooms, more of them, and many other compensating details of plan and furnishing, at the cost of a small starkempty irregularity of steel and stone.

WORTH-WHILE EXPENDITURES

For a frame house, asbestos shingles of best quality are better than wood ones, if they can be afforded. But badquality composition roofing is not only cheap -looking, but cheapening to the value of your property. A beautiful madeto-order front door and a beautiful stairway are two things that are worth having if possible. Brass piping for plumbing is essential for most climates and for water that contains iron. Copper valleys in the roof are wise if the price be possible, otherwise zinc will do. But tin means that sooner or later leaks will come through, and the repairing expense will be twice the cost of the original difference between tin and zinc, or even copper.

Have your chimneys waterproofed when built — especially those that run up on the outside of the house. It is not an expensive process, and it will prevent damp walls and even save you from standing dishpans in the fireplace every time it rains!

ONE LAST WORD

Remember that the house we love to go into — and the house we love to stay in — is not necessarily the house of greatest splendor. It is, however, the house of a hostess who

knows how to make her guests comfortable and how to make their sorroundings pleasing in color, and friendly in arrangement.

When furnishing your own house, therefore, be sure, first of all, that the colors are harmonious, that the chairs are restful to sit on, that writing-tables are well equipped, that lighting is perfection for whatever purpose it is intended. Remember, too — if you live in the country — that no ornaments are so beautifying as flowers; garden flowers, wild flowers — any flowers!

If you have house-guests, be sure that every detail for their comfort has been thought of. Be sure that dressingmirrors are perfect for a woman to see herself full length and to do her hair by, or for a man to shave by. Be sure that the shaving-mirror is of adjustable height. Remember that a guest may be less than five feet two or over six feet four — and that adjustment for one will mean standing on a chair or kneeling on the floor for the other! Also remember a hook for a razor-strop.

Be sure that beds are really good, and that the sheets are long even if lengthened at the foot with unbleached muslin so that they can be tucked a full half yard under the matress and will not pull up the moment your guest lies down in bed. If you would have a house of charm, you must think of every possible detail. Put yourself in the place of a guest or a visitor, and look at your house and its equipment from the point of view of every type of friend you can imagine, and with every faculty you can muster.

\^alk up to your front door, and look at it critically; Is it inviting? Is it neat? Is everything about it shining? Is its brass or nickel polished like a looking-glass? Does the door open properly? Is the hall neat? Does the furniture in the sitting-room suggest that you sit down, or does it turn its back? Does the room look cheerful, does it seem friendly? Are there lots of flowers? If not, is it because no one took the trouble to pick them? Or is it because flowers are out of season? In this case, could no plants have been saved from

THE HOUSE OF CHARM AT LEAST EXPENSE

the garden? Could nothing green or flowering have been bought or grown?

Stand on the threshold of the room in which you receive your friends and imagine yourself one of them: In which chair would you be inclined to sit? Why? Are other chairs uninviting? Why? Couldn’t you make them look comfortable? Have you ever tried?

If you play bridge, can all four players see their cards, and those laid on the table, without blinding reflection of light?

Questions of "Why is this detail effective?” and "Why is that one somehow wrong?” are asked again and again by every woman who has learned the art of perfect arrangement — the art, in short, of exercising thought, ingenuity, and imagination.

A house of outstanding beauty — meaning value — plus the qualities enumerated, is perfection, of course. But a house without very much intrinsic value, which yet has the quality of comfort that impels you to make yourself at home in it, possesses the one quality that is the very essence of inviting charm.

XX

THE LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE

LITTLE simple house can much more easily have ^ personality and charm than a great one — for many

reasons. One reason is that the bigger and more imposing the house, the more surely does it challenge criticism. You pounce instinctively upon whatever shortcomings you discover, because anything that pretends to conspicuous perfection is not only exacting but puts you on your mettle to prove your own qualification as a connoisseur, if only to yourself! Your impulse, in other words, is to go flaw-hunting; whereas, in the little house that pretends to nothing, you go merit-collecting instead.

“How sweet! How charming! How comfortable!^* (This last with emphasis.) “How you must love it!”

You beam at the owner, and the owner enthuses, “Oh, we do!”

And then she shows you all the details that might escape your notice — especially the tricks of comfort of her own devising. You are surprised at unexpected doors that lead to unexpected spaces; the blind-sliding panel, for instance, that is deep enough for a rack of linen poles that pull out from the dining-room wall at the back of the circular stairway; and another panel opening that is square and flat and just big enough to hold a bridge-table; the yawning door that takes in the whole lower half of a corner cupboard, including the normal doors and an apparent shelf of books that is merely a trimming of book-racks, arranged to make space enough for a radio and loud-speaker below it.

You are enchanted as a child with the unsuspected stairs that let down magically between the open rafters of an

NINETEENTH-CENTURY MANTEL

A FLAGGED AND FLOWER-BORDERED PATHWAY GIVES CHARM TO THE

LITTLEST HOUSE

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 445

out-building study ceiling leading to a bachelor guest-room and bath above. You enthuse and the owner enthuses and the little house loses nothing by this double enthusiasm.

Perhaps, moreover, you are thinking of building, and have therefore been measuring various big and little rooms. You know that the Jones’s living-room is as big as the ground-floor of your whole house. You know that the Smiths’ living-room is so small that you have to thread your way between the meager furnishing. You have perhaps been feeling discouraged at the inevitable discomfort of rooms as small of those of the Smiths. In fact, you wonder whether attempting to build is wise. This house of delectability is just what you want. It has the unexacting character of a little house, but it is big enough for comfort. You get out your tape-measure and your note-book (which every prospective house-builder inevitably carries) , and find to your amazement that the room you think so comfortable has the same floor-space exactly as the impossible small Smith one! "The measures must be wrong!” You look at the owner blankly. The owner laughs: "An effect of size is often as not just a trick — easy enough when you know how the trick is done!”

HOW LITTLE ROOMS ARE MADE TO SEEM BIG

In this chapter, therefore, let us not only explain a few tricks of illusion, but let us also make a list of space-saving methods which are perfectly simple examples of primarygrade arithmetic, but to which countless owners of little houses pay no attention.

In the first place, the plan — upon which depends the arrangement of the furniture, is of utmost importance. Many doors — especially double ones — make a small room smaller, because the floor space is turned into cross-roads. Hence Rule One: The smaller the room, the fewer and smaller the doors; but, on the other hand, the more and wider the windows the better. Windows on three sides — casement windows at that

give a room the appearance of being a third larger than a room with windows at the narrow end.

In the plan shown in Figure 124, which is that of the living-room illustrated in Plate 50, the room without any tricks of decoration has the comfort as well as the effect of a considerably larger room. Why? Because the many windows give the illusion of breaking the walls out into the views of the garden, or to the width of the awning. Also very space-saving is the fact that the doors are both at the windowless end of the room — and at the same end. If the window beyond the sofa were a door, the room would be a passageway and its present furnishing unfeasible. As it is, there is an effect of much floor space, which greatly increases the impression of comfortable size. And the fact that the windows are opposite the door permits the sofa and principal chairs to face the entrance and welcome whoever enters.

Every arrangement that blockades one’s entrance into a room is bad. Moreover, furniture so placed that you have to thread your way between it, gives an impression of cramped discomfort. The ill-mannered fireplace blockade (Fig. Ill) on page 321 is probably the most space-destroying arrangement of furniture possible.

^ Essential to the beauty of a small room is furniture that IS small in scale. This does not mean that chairs or sofas must be so small that they are uncomfortable, but that they must not be larger than necessary to comfort. Huge widths of backs and arms, and, as already noted, pieces of wooden urniture that are as thick through as packing crates, would clog a moderate space completely.

TRICKS OF SCALE

rimi *’*8 furniture can sometimes be made to fit easily in small rooms. The first is cuttina

cut^”; -.’1 preceding chapter; the second is

cutting It low. For example, the big chair at the right of the

fire m Plate 50 would have been impossible to fif Into the

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 447

room were it not for the trick of cutting off its legs. Big pieces of upholstery lose their effect of size very surprisingly if cut low to the floor.

In this same room are a number of tricks of scale made necessary by the lowness of the ceiling. The ceiling is only seven feet three, and the room is three inches less than fourteen feet wide by twenty-three feet long. Unless the furniture were very low, it would look much too big for the size as well as the height of the room.

On the other hand, in this same room the windows are set uncomfortably low, and unless disguise‘d would bring the ceiling still lower. The windows are, therefore, heightened by the trick of putting a five-inch board above each windowframe, with a lambrequin fastened to a shelf -board that is attached to the top of the five-inch board. The shades and glass-curtains are fastened several inches above the actual top of the window-trim. The shades are always pulled down far enough to seem to cover a row of upper panes of glass. Why this make-believe upper row is not a dark space, I don’t know; but light from the window is reflected on the shade, and the lack of panes is unnoticeable.

The extreme fineness of the mantel detail reduces the effect of weight, which counteracts the effect of size. Moreover, in old Colonial houses, the mantels used to be enormous, so that a very big fireplace opening is suitable to the type of house. The chimney was built on the outside of the house to avoid cutting into the room; but this was actual space-saving, and not a trick. A trick, in the present sense, is something that appears to add — or to take away from — height or width. Height is added to a room of more elaborate type by a cove-cornice painted the color of the wall (Plate 3 5 ), or by any cornice that encroaches on the ceiling and continues the color of the walls. (Plate 26.) The reverse trick of carrying the ceiling down to the picture-molding is used to lower height.

The best illusion of size is, of course, produced by mirrors. The wider they are, and the more like windows or

doors or whole wall-panels, the better. Be careful, however, of lining foyers or small rooms with solid mirror-walls, because the result will be a bewildering — and far from appealing — ^maze.

An effective and inexpensive use of mirror-doors is to cover the ordinary wooden doors with small panes of looking-glass held in place with mitered horizontal and vertical strips of half -inch, half-round molding. Be sure that the glass covers the entire door to within a quarter of an inch of the edge. In a cottage type of house, a poor quality of glass gives a much quainter and prettier effect than glass which is good — one of the many instances where cost does not mean esthetic value. (Never use machine-cut beveled glass if you would produce a decorative effect of quality or charm.) The cost of a door of this sort varies according to cost of labor, but averages between fifteen and thirty dollars. Engraved glass, and glass decorated before it is silvered, are examples of cost that can also be examples of beauty — depending, of course, on what the design is, and how it if used.

Another deception of size is the panoramic one of a scenic paper such as is shown in Plate 39, or any similar decoration that has a deep perspective in its drawing. A flat design, such as the one on the hall in Plate 16, which is without perspective, neither increases nor decreases the effect of space. All types of perspective decoration, including painted niches, are tricks, which create an illusion of space that does not exist. Real niches, embrasures, or set-in bookcases add space that is actual.

A certain effect of space is added by color. Very light blue or green walls make a room seem much bigger than walls that are red or dark orange. A room painted light yellow of a slightly greenish cast looks larger than one which is light yellow with an orange cast. The lighter the color, the larger the effect, of course. A pink room is supposed to look smaller than a blue room, but this has never been apparent to me probably because the pink is apt to be of very high

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 449

value — ^flesh color, most likely. Pink wall-papers are often deep, but pink paint of depth is unendurable; and blue that is not much deeper than blue-white is equally unendurable. The pink of the moment is pale apricot or peach.

So much for the effects of space produced by color. Apart from these, the nature of the decorations, the location of the windows, the providing of a central unit of floor-space by proper arrangement of the furniture, are all genuine adjustments and utilities of space, upon which the beauty of a little house depends.

Each type of house has its advantages and its failings. The advantages of every little house include the enthusiasm with which its comfort is acclaimed and the facility with which it may be made attractive because of its lack of exactions. Its only disadvantage is its lack of room and its accompanying discomfort, because there is no place to put anything. Moreover, a single object out of place puts the whole room out of order. One can be comfortable in very small quarters only if there be storage space for everything that has to be put away. In other words, a srqall room with big closet-space is comfortable, whereas a bigger room with nowhere but on the floor to put anything is thoroughly uncomfortable.

WASTE SPACE IS EVIDENCE OF A BAD ARCHITECT

Although in a house of size economy of space is of less importance than in a small one, waste space never proclaims a good plan. In a small house— and still more emphatically in a small apartment — the economical use of every inch of space is an exacting mathematical problem; a problem not only for the architect to solve on general principles, but for an owner of ingenuity to study personally and particularly. And the smaller the house the more personal and particular

must the study and ingenuity be.

In apartment houses, especially those having several apartments on a floor, the smallest or "remnant” apartment is

often fitted in between the plans for the larger ones. Or in remodeling, where least alteration of walls is of first consideration, a certain amount of makeshift space is often unavoidable. But in the plan for a house there is no possible excuse for space- wasting elbow- jointed passageways and poke-aways. No half-skilled person would pack a trunk leaving a lot of waste holes! Then neither would an architect who could possibly be considered good, do this very thing in packing rooms into a floor-plan.

On the other hand, if a little house is to be an example of perfection, the endless fitting of belongings into spaces, or the contriving of spaces for belongings, is necessarily accomplished or at least suggested by the owner. Even the most painstaking architect in the world can scarcely be supposed to measure each belonging of each member of the family, and fit it on a certain spot on such-and-such a shelf in a specified closet. But this is hardly an exaggeration of what an owner should do. To get maximum comfort out of minimum space, it is essential not only to measure the size of each closet, but to notice where the space of each is taken from, and to take pains to decide whether greater comfort would be achieved by cutting a few inches here, or adding a few inches there. A closet that won’t hold what it is intended to, is useless. A closet that holds all you intend to put into it, with inches to spare, represents just that number of inches wasted if these few inches can be used otherwise.

A clothes-hanger closet must be twenty-two inches deep for men s coats with average width of shoulder. A depth of twenty inches is plenty for a woman’s dress-closet. A closet twenty-six inches deep is a useless waste of four inches in a man’s closet, or of six inches in a woman’s. Of course, if this extra width fits into a jog in the plan in such a way that it cannot be put to other use, then the unnecessary depth is of no importance. In nine cases out of ten, however, adjustment

of space makes all the difference between comfort and discomfort.

Here is an instance in point: The plan shown in Figure

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 451

139 was the bathroom dress-closet arrangement of a New York apartment. I asked the architect to explain why he had left all that long tail of elbow-hitting floor-space beside the tub in the bathroom — always an awkward arrangement, and of no use whatever except to buy a rug or carpet for — and why he had to commit grand larceny on the only closet space in order to do it! He said that he thought a tub six feet long by three feet width — inside measurement — was "de luxe.” What I said is of no importance. But I lost no time in drawing the space as shown in Figure 140, with the result that most of the owners who saw the rearrangement insisted upon havjt3 ^

This is given merely as an instance of the importance of really studying a plan and working out its best adjustment for yourself. Each of the owners who disliked the arrangement of a scrimped closet and a worthless tail-end bathroom had looked at the plans without noticing that three and one make four just as easily as two and two.

People who have very few clothes and very few possessions — people, in other words, who by their own expert eliminating methods avoid the accumulation of possessions that gradually submerge most of us — need but a fraction of the closet-space necessary to chronic accumulators. So perhaps^ it might be best to omit an attic, and omit those put-away closets which for the most part are crypts for never-to-belooked-at treasures and never-to-be-used finery ^unless the children use them to play dress-up in! But a little house with-

out enough put-away space for every-day essentials is quite another thing. So let us look to our waste inches and see which ones to save.

CLOSET MEASUREMENT IN A REALLY LITTLE HOUSE

A valuable space of seven feet by three feet by at least a foot high is thrown away under every bed, even in the house so small that closet and bureau space is lacking for the slimmest wardrobe. Yacht beds — ^meaning built-in beds with drawers under them — give invaluable bureau space. In a man’s room, for instance, if he does not mind having his bed unusually high, space equal to that of two or three chiffoniers can easily be added without subtracting a single inch from the floor. In the tiny room shown in Plate 53, the drawers under the built-in bed are much bigger than they look, because they are thirty-five inches deep. Other available space, used by very few, is represented by the very wide and deep cupboard that can be built over and perhaps at the sides of a cross- ways bathtub. (Fig. 141.)

SPACE ROBBERY BY MOST BOOK-SHELVES

Book-shelves that are needlessly deep are inexcusable in a small-sized room, in which every quarter of an inch must

be counted as precious. Wanton waste of almost a foot of room is carelessly tolerated by the unthinking owner who has the book-case space finished with lath and plaster — baseboard and all — and then has book-shelves built on the outside of that, ten or even twelve inches deep!

Whether bookcases can be set into the wall or not

WHERE TO FIND CUPBOARD SPACE IN A BATHROOM

A COTTAGE COLONIAL BEDROOM IN GREEN AND CREAM

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 453

depends upon what is on the other side of the wall, and what may be within the wall. One cannot cut through to the outdoors, or to the kitchen; nor can one eliminate plumbing-pipes. But in many instances, such as in the walls between rooms that need not be sound-proof, the side boards of the bookcases can perfectly well be set in the wall instead of studs. In an ordinary small frame house, this saves four inches. Four inches plus lath and plaster mean that shelves for ordinary five-and-a-half-inch books can be backed with thinnest wood or even with tin, and set flush with the wall; or that wide books need take up no more than three extra inches.

Heavier construction usually provides somewhat more width, with steel beams faced with wood. But even with

steel and hollow- tile construction, to set big books flush with the walls in modernistic fashion means that the back of the shelf-space must jut out into the room on the other side — unless used in a space that is furred. Otherwise big books would need to be cut in half — or books for modernistic shelves would have to be printed specially on half -width pages.

To stand back of any sofa that juts out into the room, a long wood-box that serves as a table is an excellent economy. (See Fig. 142.) The box behind the sofa and on which two lamps stand in Plate 50 is built of birch, stained maple. Such a box should be almost the length of the sofa it stands behind, and be made of wood that matches the room furniture. If the back of the sofa is toward the end of the

room at which you enter, an open wood-box with logs showing is not very ornamental, though it could — if no other wood space be available — be made inconspicuous and even presentable with two cupboard-doors that hide a boxed-in wood space. (Fig. 143.)

CLOSET SPACE OR ROOM SPACE

My personal method in drawing plans for a little house or for an apartment, or for any living quarters in which space is limited, is first to plan the essential closets carefully, and then to put whatever space is left over into the rooms. The smaller the house, of course, the greater the necessity for mathematical precision in the spending of inches. You should make exact calculations by measuring the closets wherever you live. Put down the space you would like to have, and beside it put down the space you mmt have, and then see which "like” and which "must” fits into your plan.

A typical list of closets is given below, merely for your convenience — to be used like a laundry list. On applying this list to your own needs, you naturally cross out items that are unnecessary, and add any others that may be indispensable to you. Beginning, therefore, at the back of the house:

KITCHEN

Stores closet.

Ice-box.

Kitchen cabinet.

Screened larder.

Pot- closet.

Dresser for china and glass, with drawers for kitchen-cloths, rollertowels, and servants’ dining-room table-linen.

Broom closet.

Ironing-board shut into wall, if no laundry.

PANTRY

Dressers for dining-room china and glass.

Safe (or closet) for silver.

Shelves or closet for dining-room linen.

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 45 5

Space for dish- and glass-towels — and a place in which to dry them.

A drawer for all items necessary for silver and brass cleaning. Brush and broom closet.

Place for a house-ladder and for breakfast-trays. The ladder can perhaps hang high on the wall somewhere; the trays fit into a slot made at the side of the dresser or at the side of the drawers under the sink.

CELLAR

Bins for coal, kindling-wood, and log-wood.

Preserve closet.

Cold but non-freezing root compartment, for storage of vegetables and garden bulbs.

SERVICE EQUIPMENT

Housemaid’s closets on each floor for brooms, carpet-sweepers, vacuum-cleaners, brushes, dust-cloths, etc.

In a house of any size, a housemaid’s sink on each upstairs floor, and a drain-floor closet at back entrance for servants’ rain-coats, rubbers, and umbrellas.

A closet in each servants’ room, at least thirty inches wide by twenty inches deep; or, if fitted at end of bed, thirty to thirty-six inches deep.

CLOTHES CLOSETS

Storage space for the family’s summer clothes in winter and for winter clothes in summer.

A separate space for men’s fishing and shooting equipment.

Coat-closet in the entrance hall. Separate closet for guests.

Space devised somewhere for card-tables, and for firewood and a baby- carriage.

Linen-closet on bedroom floor, necessarily measured to fit the amount of bed and bathroom linen used by each household.

Bedroom-closet space for each occupant of each room.

Wherever there is plenty of room, a big square linencloset with shelves and cupboards on three sides is ideal. But where there is very little room, wide shallow space is usually easier to contrive than square space. In both plans on pages 98 and 100, the linen-closet is wide and shallow, and is entirely adequate. Measure your stacks of sheets and towels and be sure that the shelves will take them; sheets and bathtowels can be stacked sideways if necessary.

One detail to attend to yourself is to see that no unneces-

OTHER CLOSET DETAILS

Another linen-closet detail to be remembered is storage for extra blankets, comforters, and pillows. Usually all three are stuffed on high upper shelves where they gather dust as well as creases from June to September — sometimes the whole year through. In a big linen closet there should be regulation pillow and blanket drawers. But a shallow closet can be fitted with high instead of deep — uniform boxes on the lowest shelf, such as those described for a box-closet. They are both tidy and dust-proof.

Bedroom closets must be provided for each occupant of each room. Such closets vary in size from one that barely holds half-a-dozen dresses, a hat or two, and two or three pairs of shoes, to the 10,000 cubic feet of space occupied by a clothes-room in a certain house at Palm Beach.

While on the subject, it might as well be noted that, in the enormous closet just mentioned, every dress or coat is hung in a numbered compartment, which also contains the hat, shoes, slippers, parasol, wrist-bag, scarf, special jewelry or other accessories worn with it. Evening dresses that are beaded or otherwise likely to be marred if hung on their s oulder-straps are laid at full length in dress-trays, and the trays are sometimes tagged with a second number which is that of a matching evening wrap. Furs are hung in a special

THE CIRCULAR STAIR IN A COLONIAL COTTAGE

A SUCCESSFUL MIXTURE OF CRUDE MILK-BENCH, FINE TABLE, PROVENCAL SIDE CHAIRS, HITCHCOCK ARM-CHAIRS, AND WALL-PAPER OF GAYEST YELLOW AND BLUE

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 457

cold-storage compartment of their own that is kept automatically at proper temperature. Dozens of shelves, drawers, and trays hold underclothes, stockings, handkerchiefs and all imaginable paraphernalia of dress. The entire collection is indexed in a clothes-book, and instead of telling her maid that she will wear her red and white foularde, her red morocco slippers, and her white straw hat, Mrs. X looks in her book and says, "Bring me No. 129.”

No such elaborate equipment is needed in the ordinary house, of course. The clothes-space necessary for the average woman is somewhere between that of the cupboard for six hangers and that of a small-sized room. The only way to determine the size of your closet or clothes-room is to measure your possessions. Don’t imagine, for instance, that you have about ten pairs of shoes and slippers, and then find you have forty pairs and nowhere to put them! Equally foolish is it to waste space for forty — in a small house where every inch is valuable — when you need space for ten or less.

MAKING THE MOST OF CLOSET INCHES

The average fitting of the average closet, whether it be sixty inches deep or the regulation twenty-two, consists of a pole five feet three inches from the floor, and a shelf three inches above that. If the closet is of coat-hanger depth, it is sometimes wise to vary the usual arrangement of clothespole and shelves over it, by hanging the pole three inches from the ceiling, using long-handled coat-hangers, and filling all the space beneath your dresses (or your children’s clothes or your husband’s coats) with shelves. Lower shelves are much more practical and space-saving than the unreachable space above the door-frame.

There are also numerous door devices suggestive of a fitted bag. Bathroom towel-bars on the inside of a man’s closet-door are perfect for ties. Shoe-racks go fairly well on a woman’s closet-door, if they are not too deep, and if there are little fences at the edges to keep shoes from falling

is deep enough to walk into (Fig. 146.)

In a woman’s closet that is deep enough to go into, a pole seven feet from the floor, with space under it for four long flat trays to hold evening dresses, is very comfortable. For a woman (or maid) who cannot reach as high as seven feet, a broad steppingstool with slightly spraddled legs, so that it can’t possibly tip, and a hand-hole in the top of it, is practical. Up over the pole is a very good place for put-away cupboards; and the same is true of the space over the door, which most people leave unused.

sideways when the door is opened or shut. Or if the closet is wide, so that the pole is longer than necessary, shoeracks are excellent against the wall of one side.

In a man’s closet, there is always room under his ordinary suits for a number of trays or shelves for shirts, shoes, and underclothes. Shelves can also be built in a section, with hanging-space left at the side for tail-coat, dressing-gowns, and ulsters. When the closet to be fitted it, its possibilities are endless.

ADVANTAGES OF A HIGH CLOTHES-POLE

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 459

SHOES CAN BE PROVIDED FOR AL¬ MOST ANYWHERE

Shoe-racks or shelves can be fitted a dozen different ways, depending upon the shape of the closet. (Fig. 148.) Where space is deep, stand them up straight. Where space is narrow, tip them on racks. Where space is only wide enough for a ledge, stand single shoes sideways with rails, made of curtainrods, to keep them from falling. (Fig. 149.) The cheapest spring rods, painted, look as well as any.

For a beautifully furnished closet, thin strips of painted wood molding are prettiest. But very thin wood breaks easily, and thick strips are ugly.

To use the entire coat-hanger width for a row of hats or shoes is bad planning where space is of value, because the space is wide enough for two rows. Thus a single row of hats results in a waste of space, while a double row necessitates taking ofi the hats at the front to find one at the back. Shoes can, of course, be set single file, one at the front and its mate behind (though in taking the back one down a number of its front neighbors usually come tumbling after) . In summer.

when a woman is apt to wear more hats and fewer evening dresses — of the sort best not to hang — hat-shelves over a low pole may be preferable to long evening-dress trays under it. Or the trays can be double-depth and used for hats. Shallow ones can, of course, be used for underclothes, gloves, fans — anything you choose. In fact, the sole object in closetfilling is to achieve personal comfort.

In a man’s closet, shelves or trays should be measured to the exact size necessary to hold stacks of shirts, shoe-racks measured to fit the length and width of shoes. A man who

is very tall, naturally needs greater lengths of space for his clothes than one who is very short.

Because of their stable smell, riding-clothes, even ANOTHER KIND OF SHOE-RACK after being aired, should be

hung by themselves. The same is necessary for fishing clothes that are not put into the tub.

SPECIAL CLOSETS FOR SPECIAL USES

An essential for a house that has a refectory dining-table is a closet in which to hang table-linen. In an average small house a few small centerpieces are easily rolled around cylinders of cardboard and kept in a drawer or on a shelf. But the various embroidered and cut- work table-cloths for refectory tables should be hung if possible on poles (or slats) fitted so that they can be easily lifted out of U-shaped wooden blocks at either end. The poles must be as long as your widest refectory cloth, but for damask cloths, half their width will do, because tradition accepts a fold down the center of the table’s length. Embroidered refectory cloths, on the other hand, should have no crease.

A good-sized hanging-closet is a double-doored cupboard about a foot deep and five feet wide by five feet high. In a space of such measurement, the lowest pole (or slat) is set twenty inches from the floor, and an inch from the front of the closet. This will take half a dozen table-centers a yard square or round. A second pole a foot higher, and two inches farther back than the first, will take tea-cloths a yard and a half square. The third, a foot higher and two inches farther back, will take lunch-cloths and runners. The highest, using another foot of height and two more inches of depth, will

take table-cloths creased down the middle and gently folded over at half their length. The top of the cupboard must be at least six inches above the highest pole to allow of its being lifted out. Best, of course, is a cupboard with a top that hinges up. The general idea is illustrated in Figure 150.

CUPBOABX) FOR HANGING LINEN

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 461

In a house of small equipment a single pole of whatever length necessary or available will quite possibly fill all requirements. *

At all events, the only way to make each closet comfortable is to measure what you want to put in it, and then fit it in to best advantage — exactly as you would pack a bag. For the endless articles that most of us put away, a small storage-closet fitted with boxes is excellent. This is a good use for the turned-wrong closet that is narrow and deep instead of broad and shallow; its entire space is fitted from floor to top of door with shelves, and then boxes are made by any box-maker to fit them. Such boxes, covered with wall-paper, shellacked, and furnished with handles and labels, can be made as good-looking as they are convenient. In fact, a box-closet of my own that is three feet six inches deep and two and half feet wide is a display feature of the house. The door-frame is purposely set flush with the side walls, so that boxes the full width of the shelves can be pulled forward like drawers, without interference by the jamb. Six boxes are made the full shelf-size — three feet six inches by two feet five inches, and nine inches deep. The front half of each lid hinges up, so that the contents can be removed without lifting the whole box down. The two upper shelves are fitted with small boxes twenty-one by fourteen and a half inches by nine inches. The ceiling of the closet is at the height of the door. The space above the door is reached by a blind door hidden in the paneling.

A sewing-room convenience is a waist-high electric-iron outlet; also an outlet for an electric sewing-machine, and a drop-light at the window side where the maid, seamstress, or owner habitually sits. The light must be bright. The best possible light to sew by has a wide, flaring, opaque, reflectorlined shade, and is fastened with a pulley so that it can be lowered at will. A light hung high is never good, if one need to see well; and a shade that lets the light through is not as good to sew by as one that can be lowered close over one’s work and adjusted so as to cast no light into one’s eyes.

BLIND DOORS

* Convenient in any house, but especially so in a little one, is an occasional blind door. The entrance hall, or the foyer of an apartment, for instance, is sometimes little more than a collection of doors that leave not a foot of wall space. In this event it is very practical to have one, or possibly two, made blind so that they have the value of plain wall space. In fact, a console with a picture or mirror over it or any other small piece of wall furniture can be fastened to it perfectly.

In Etiquette, page 420, a blind door is on the wall near the window, and a tall cabinet is fastened to it. The door opens quite as easily with the cabinet — including the ornaments on the shelves — as it would without. A blind door means merely a door that is set without trim, and whose hinges have no projections that show. On a plain wall, such as the one in the illustration just referred to, you can see its outline if you look; but on a figured wall it is almost undetectable. There are three in the hall shown in Plate 16, leading into closets. The only way they can be seen is by looking at the baseboard where V’s are necessary in order to open the doors with the baseboard projection that naturally continues across the doors.

THE ROOM THAT PLAYS TWO PARTS

Where space is very valuable, it is not practical to devote a vital amount of it to a room that is less than generally useful. In thousands of small-sized houses and apartments the living-room and dining-room are, for this reason, in one. And in many otherwise perfectly appointed houses certain rooms are convertible to double use.

Most typical is the sitting-room-spare-room in a small country house or in an apartment. In such a room there is no bedroom furniture. Ideally it is a room furnished as a sitting-room with a dressing-room-bathroom off it.

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 463

If drawer space is necessary, it is easy to replace a bureau by a knee-hole desk, which is much prettier, anyway. In a house that is perfect in detail, a dressing-table is perhaps set in a panel like the one in Plate 3 1 . But a perfectly good temporary dressing-table can be made by hanging a mirror over any sort of small stand or table put in front of the window.

A day-bed of my own invention is shown in Plate 34. It is completely comfortable both as a sofa' (its normal use) or as a bed (very occasionally) . It differs from the ordinary day-bed not only in that it is upholstered at the head and foot, but also in the method of its bed-making. In other words, it is not as it looks. The pleating of the slip-cover does not hide legs and empty space. Behind the pleating and flat on the floor is a deep box-spring. The mattress, which looks like sofa-springs, is shown immediately on top of the pleating. If the bed is to be used, it is made like every other bed in the morning and tightly covered with the chintz spread, after which three huge cushions are put on top of it. Feather-filled, thickly stuffed, rectangular cushions go across the back and ends, which reduce the apparent size of the seven-by-three-foot mattress. No amount of sitting on the sofa disturbs the smoothness of the bed under the cushions. At night the cushions and pillows are lifted off, the chintz spread is removed like any other day-counterpane, and bedpillows are taken out of their boxes in the linen-closet. This room has a bathroom-dressing-room, so that it is used as a bedroom only at night.

THE ONE-ROOM APARTMENT

The one-room apartment is not what cost of space is taking us to. It has taken us. We are here! When space in New York, for instance, is literally many dollars an inch, what is one to do whose dollars are limited? One answer is, obviously, "Don’t live in New York.” But since people — hundreds of thousands of them, in fact — are moving into

these one-room apartments, this chapter would be incomplete if nothing were said about them.

The plan on this page (Fig. 151) shows you the idea of putting all the space available into one room of good size instead of chopping it up into cubicles. The beds are of the flop-down variety that in the daytime are stood up on end against a door and shut into a closet. The closet room is ample, light is abundant, and every convenience possible has been thought of.

CURTAINS

It was my intention to say much about curtains, but the magazines are so filled with suggestions for them, that there is little to add further than to warn against enshrouding drapery that obscures all light, and against overtrimming

A LITTLE HOUSE OF INVITING CHARM

THE DRAFTING-TABLE ON WHICH THIS BOOK WAS WRITTEN

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 465

inclined to be messy or suggestive of a textile manufacturer’s display.

Glass-curtains on rods fitted with pull-cords are very good if they are of china silk or of georgette or of other material that is fairly heavy; they are good also in any room where better light than that for conversation is sometimes needed. To ask whether or not you like harlequin curtains — meaning that the material on one side of each window is of one color or design, and that on the other of another — is like asking whether or not you like the inevitable effect of remnants.

The one thing to beware of — unless you are very sure of your judgment — is the fancy curtain-pole. Ninety-nine per cent, of spear-end curtain-poles are illiterately vulgar, and the decorative value of even the one per cent, is usually questionable. There are long gilt-arrow curtain-poles in a room in the American wing in the Metropolitan Museum; they are beautifully made, with an absolutely plain shaft, and might be studied by those who are thinking of this type of curtain support. At all events the arty poles of tabu finished metal with large still artier brooch effects in the centers can not have other than the same effect on a room that ipecac is said to have on the human digestion!

One detail of curtain-hanging is perhaps important. Curtains hung in an arch must be prepared for by the architect and hung as shown in Fig. 152.

Wood lambrequins, whether applied independently or as a breakfront continuation of molding or cornice, are usually good. Brass ones are very apt to be bad. Exposed curtainpoles should be avoided by any one putting up new ones. The only excuse for curtain-poles that show is that the upholsterer has not finished his work, or that the curtains were hung

CURTAIN-ROD SHOULD BE SET BEHIND ARCH

twenty years ago when decoration was at a rather out-at-ends ebb. A pretty way to hang lambrequins or curtains is to hang them on glass or brass knobs — the sort generally used for the tie-backs (Fig. 153), but smaller. Paper-covered lambrequin-boards are suitable only in a little cottage or bungalow, but the varieties of cut-out-edged or moldingtrimmed wooden ones are unlimited. Painted ones are suitable in every feminine type of house. Wood window-frames, like the bed frame in Plate 53, but with fewer scrolls, are very charming for a single short, wide window in a cottage room. The curtains are hung inside the frame, of course.

Lace curtains — except of lace with a fine all-over motive or dot — belong in the category with curtain-poles. Fancy curtains, bound with colored ribbons and patterned in color, are in dangerous taste. Net, scrim, filet, chiffon, silk gauze, organdie, are all good. Chiffon curtains are prettiest, perhaps, finished with picoted points or scallops, though they are usually hemmed — either two or more inches wide or rolled to an eighth of an inch.

For a very primitive little house, straight curtains of tarletan, either white or colored, finished with a very narrow basted hem, can be lovely. In a country room, organdie curtains with deep-pointed or scalloped ruffles finished with cutthrough hemstitching, are hard to excel.

For a rather important room of Georgian, French or Colonial type, very fine Brussels net, either hanging very full and straight, and finished with the narrowest Cluny edge, or else crossed over and tied back and edged with wide ruffles that^are rolled instead of hemmed, are perfection. "Crossed over” means, crossed at top — as in Plate 46.

If you do a modern room in the Austrian fashion of color chiffon or silk gauze, curtains are suitable either in one color or in several colors one over the other, the thickness increas-

LITTLE HOUSE OF COMFORT AND CONVENIENCE 467

ing as the curtain approaches the window-frame. Several colors are hung in succession like perpendicular Neapolitan ice-cream. But if your modern room be what New York calls smart, curtains of white or silver tissue or very pale gray are your only choice — for reasons which will be told in chapter XXII.

NIGHT CLOTHES FOR CURTAINS

The subject of curtains suggests a long used device of my own for keeping window curtains in a bedroom dry and clean, no matter how perishable the material or how wide the windows are opened on a dusty street or on rainy nights. Buy 54-inch oilcloth, either matte or shiny, in whichever texture you prefer, and in the color that is the nearest match to the color of the room. Cut the 5 4-inch material in half, and for each curtain cut a length that will reach from above the widest opening of the window to the floor. Sew loops of tape at the two corners. Screw a hook on the window trim at the height of the loop from the floor, and at night encircle the curtains, glass and silk ones both, and hang both loops on the hook. (Fig. 15 5.) In the morning the oilcloth is unhooked and hung in a closet.

XXI

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE

would naturally suppose that the garden’s decoraf ^ /tion of a house would be far more appealing to a garden-lover than to one who takes green things for granted, like parsley garnishing on a platter. Yet many of those who dote on gardens as places in which flowers are made to grow — who are deeply concerned with profusion or perfection of bloom — are often the very ones who think little or nothing about gardening in its strictly decorative phase. If the roses are free from bugs and black-spot, if the irises have added to their families, why should any one bother to notice that a stark, bare building, with a garden at a distance, looks like an undressed person whose clothes are on a far-off chair; or that a haphazard drapery of vines, strung on with neither skill nor attention, is no more likely to prove an adornment than are old garments flung on a person who cares neither for style nor for becomingness.

On the other hand, a truly embellishing garden, even for a formal Period house, is much more a question of choosing the sort of garden we like than the unimaginatively conventional landscape designers would have us believe. Like the automatic interior decorator who adds two chairs with figured covering and one with stripes to every sofa and pair of chairs that are plain, the automatic landscape decorator cannot picture an Italian house without a marble-balustraded, cypress-spired garden for it to overlook; nor a house of French suggestion without a trellis-surrounded parterre. Perhaps a parterre appeals to you; in that case you are right in selecting one, though to another a carpet of close-clipped grass and neatly-patterned flowers is just about as satisfying

tHE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE

as a put-together jig-saw puzzle left out on a billiard table. To the English house alone does the garden expert grant a garden of unlimited appeal, whether to walk in, or to sit in, or to live in!

It is true, perhaps, that an austerely formal house must be embellished with a garden that is formal in general character. But even this convention, taken as an unbreakable edict, is likely to produce commonplace monotony rather than personality and charm.

THE WORD FORMAL

Do not forget, however, that it is a well-known American habit to call all sorts of things formal that are not really formal at all! In fact, when people speak of formal dinners, formal houses, formal gardens, one has no way of knowing whether they mean a house of architectural austerity, a ceremonial dinner, gardens such as those of the palace at Versailles, or whether they mean four neighbors in for dinner, a house that is neatly kept, a garden that has a square of grass with a semblance of balance in its planting. But, using the word in its proper sense, there is little to be said in a book such as this; because a land-owner who is going to have a genuinely formal house set in equally formal gardens is either going to employ an expert architect or is himself far too expert to need the advice of a book such as this!

To those who may be in somewhat of a quandary, however, as to how to decorate a fairly average house, the simplest and best suggestion that I know of is to go out and really look at it! Stand at the entrance gate, or across the street or out on the lawn — or wherever are the important places to see it from — and look at it as though it were a room to furnish, a child to buy clothes for, or a hat to trim. In fact, if you have ever trimmed hats with any degree of success, you will know that a smart result is not dependent upon the quantity or quality of trimming half so much as upon the right amount of the right embellishment put on the right place.

You will also know that although you can somewhat disguise a bad shape it is impossible to beautify a foundation of unbecoming color.

Houses of certain outlines and colors are so easy to trim that they almost trim themselves. Others are so beauty-defying that little or nothing can be done with them short of muffling them completely from ground to rooftop with blanketing vines — and even then, evidences of ugliness will probably show through.

THE PROCESS OF EXTERIOR DECORATION

The list of items which a house-owner is likely to consider is as follows:

1 . Color of House of neutral or pastel tint. This includes all natural stone; the pale pinkish shades of brick; cream or warm gray or sand-color cement; wood that has been weathered or has been lightly stained gray, cream or fawn, or that is painted white or other color in accordance with its style. (See color of exterior. Index.)

2. Color of Planting chosen to harmonize with color of house. (Look at the chart of color families in Chapter X and avoid putting barricaded first cousins together.)

3. Application of Decoration. This must be skilful rather than lavish.

4. A beguiling Approach to an inviting front door.

5. A Loggia or T errace — or both.

6. An Inset of Water, if suitable and practical.

7. A Door-yard Garden, or a patio or other intimate out-of-doors living room.

In addition to this list, there are two simple rules for applying decoration to the exterior of the house:

A. Lick out whatever architectural flaavs there may be, and hide them.

B. Lick out the features of special beauty and accentuate them.

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 471

A blank wall looks best with a flat-spread vine that covers one-third, or at least not more than one-half of the surface. Cut back the ends of ivy or of any clinging vine to keep it thick at the base. (Fig. 156.) To let it cover the whole surface is to destroy its decorative value. (Fig. 157.)

On the other hand, an ugly house should be thickly covered — because camouflaging it is your object.

Let us consider in detail our list of seven essentials:

THE COLOR OF THE HOUSE

Pseudo-Gothic houses perched high off the ground (their worst fault) , with overornamentation of porches and gables, and painted in various shades of maroon or pumpkin or muddy mongrel, are among the discouraging subjects for decoration. Unless a platform terrace can be built around the up-on-stilts foundations to bring them lower to the ground, there is little use in attempting to take off its overheavy nubbins or hack-saw trimming, or in making any change in its architecture except, perhaps, to paint it white or cream or oyster or sand and disguse its shortcomings as much as possible with vines.

Still more difficult (if not impossible) houses to trim without repainting are those that have contrasting horizontal bandagings, especially those banded with two contrasting colors on contrasting-patterned shingles, a third color trim, a fourth color shutters and doors, and a fifth color roof! Less offensive to taste, but equally difficult to beautify, are houses with very dark stained side-walls and glaring white

trim that rushes forward to the eye of the beholder, blotting out every planted object in its way.

The repainting and planting of one such house wrought a transformation that might be well to describe in detail. In a suburb of New York there was a nondescript cottage stained black with a scarlet brick chimney and olive-green lattice. Upon the black side-walls the white trim seemed illumined by a spot-light. It had a golden oak front door, the upper half of which was set with plate glass and had heavy guipure lace behind it. Its garden decoration consisted of a few little bushes and a fragmentary vine, and in the middle of the sidelawn was a sparsely filled bed of geraniums. The property was a replica of a hundred thousand suburban homes. It had for several years belonged to people who cut the grass and planted some geraniums, but who evidently had never thought of looking at their property as though they were looking at the composition of a picture.

Last year an artist bought the place. He painted the whole house cream — trim, chimneys and lattice to match. The gray shingle roof he left alone, but the shutters and door were painted light delphinium blue, and small panes substituted for the single plate-glass of the door and the four panes of the windows. Against the fresh light background, the hitherto unnoticed vine and plants came into focus; in fact, the rose vine, which had sprawled unnoticed on the ground, reached easily to the top of a new arbor surrounding the doorway. In place of two wooden steps, there was now a single one of stone, and below it a small front terrace paved principally with the flagstones of the former entrance walk, now broken, and the pieces set with narrow edges of sod between. Along the wall of the house was a perennial border dominated by delphiniums, hollyhocks, and asters, and at the edge of the terrace were blue tubs filled with salmonpink geraniums, ageratum, ivy, and turquoise morningglories. And where the round geranium- bed had been, was an iris-bordered pool, joined to the terrace with steppingstones set into the grass.
