MAKING THE COLORS HARMONIZE

The color that you select for your house is always a question of your own preference, which may have been fixed by your choice of house or may not. Perhaps the materials of the house are brown, fawn, pinkish brick, sand, oyster or white. Against any of these neutral colors, flowers are naturally harmonious. A yellow house with a bright blue roof and patches of geraniums to supply the primary triad is the most vivid exterior decoration possible, unless it be the yellow house with a red roof and brightest possible blue flowers! Neither of these, however, is especially recommended for general choice; they are merely mentioned as examples of vividness.

The criticism of the over amount of white horizontal banding on a dark house is not intended as criticism of dark houses or even of a reasonable amount of light trim. The house of uniformly plain dark color— especially when chosen for a wooded location, and embellished with little or much unflowered green — sinks into the background in a way that is especially inviting and restful. Moreover, dark-stained houses with a light trim and shutters, if these be applied with careful relation to masses of brilliant-colored flowers, such as nasturtiums, scarlet sage, geraniums, marigolds, and golden-glow, assume an air of gaiety that is both hospitably friendly and homelike. These same Spanish colors are also becoming to all sand-colored houses with red-tiled roofs.

Very easy cottages to decorate are those which are low-tothe-ground, whether they be of stone, brick, stucco, shingles, or clapboards — either weathered or painted white. A low white farmhouse and its adjoining buildings literally smothered in bloom are as lovely as a bride with her bridesmaids. But a formal house (really formal!) must be adorned with skill and dignity or it will look like a lady of a certain age dressed as a flapper.

Therefore, if the would-be builder of an Italian villa or any other semi-palace prefers cottage gardens that are

somewhat tangled, rather than the set beds and box sculptures dear to the hearts and hands of head gardeners, let him build himself an informally rambling house of light-tinted stucco or whitewashed brick or stone, the walls of which will smilingly accept almost anything placed against them.

In the northern half of the United States, houses painted pink, sky-blue, Nile-green, violet or buttercup-yellow, have never been thought (why not?) to go very well. But these same bright colors on walls decorated with dark tropical foliage in southern latitudes are simply ravishing! And perhaps if our present enthusiasm for vivid hues is not completely snuffed out by the counteracting craze for colorless cream and brown, or gray and white and black, these most colorful facades may quite possibly be flourishing in our northern neighborhoods before long.

At all events, whatever the color of your house may be, planting that enhances its personality must be painstakingly chosen and arranged against it.

COLORS OF THE FLOWERS AND FOLIAGE

Flower borders, whether of potting plants or of perennials, can be used to transform the color of your entire garden as often as you please. But when planting shrubs and vines and most especially trees, you must choose their colors and their time of blooming for permanency.

There was a time when people who built houses of wood or stone simply planted the wood or the stone collection of vines against them. On the wooden house, a purple wistaria was inevitably started on one corner of the porch, and honeysuckle was planted against each of its supports. Bay-windows and doors were alternately garnished with crimson ramblers or Virginia creeper. If the house was of stone, it had Boston ivy vines planted a foot apart around every side of it, and Its front entrance was flanked by a banking of rhododendrons. And that was that!

Planting to-day is almost as typically inclined toward

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 475

varicolored light green, dark green, yellow-green, blue-green and olive-green conifers standing out from the foundations in alternating flambeaux, spires, pyramids, balls, mushrooms, and headless terriers. Any of these may be beautiful in a moderate assortment, but all collected together suggest a group of strangers waiting for the parade to go by, or for a conveyance to take them on their way.

Wherever the need for a strong decorative accent may be, slender dark green pyramids of cypress or arbor vit« (Douglasi) or cedar, or less formal plantings of white pine, are always successful for a house that is lived in in winter as well as summer. English ivy is beautifying as ever it was to nearly all houses of brick, stone or cement; but, as previously noted, its decorative quality is greatly enhanced if it is thickly concentrated at definite points; and where ugliness makes thick planting advisable, it is better that alternating varieties be chosen, rather than that too much of it cover the entire surfaces evenly, as though it were rough green material of which the house might be built.

Always keep the decoration of your house — the hat-trimming — in mind. It is important not to let the planting gradually change color, thin out, or grow into a meaningless jungle. It must be clipped and trimmed, perhaps replanted or thinned out, but it must at all events be watched, nursed, and trained.

CONCERNING THE APPROACH

Shall the approach to your front door appeal to the intellect or to the heart? Shall it have fat trees, a velvet lawn, plump bushes, and a serpentine driveway? Or shall it have a straight, formal approach between sentinel trees as orderly and as smartly groomed as King’s guards on parade? Or shall its entrance be a road through the woods, or a lane through the fields? Either of the latter might easily be the result of skill and care, without having the appearance of being so.

It is true that with big places and long approaches we are not really concerned; but these same questions of choice

between impressive cultivation and an apparently natural rock or field or woodland fragment of land, may equally well be applied to a cottage or bouse set very near to the road — or to the street.

If you choose an entrance enclosure, shall it be a forecourt, or do you prefer the less important but quite easily as appealing front yard?

Correctly speaking, a forecourt is an architectural front entrance enclosure, such as is typical of the formal French or English house. Usually it is surrounded on three sides by the walls of the front facade flanked by two projecting wings, and it is walled or balustraded across the side opposite that of the entrance door. The court is usually paved, and its decoration consists of clipped trees in tubs or set into earth openings in the flagging. But whether it is walled or balustraded, whether the driveway turns within it, or whether It serves merely as an outdoor vestibule, its characteristic is extreme restraint and exact balance in decoration.

It IS rather a fad of the decorators to describe as a "forecourt any sort of front-of-the-house enclosure; more often ^an not, however, this is like calling a cottage a palace. Of course, you can, if you choose, call your front yard a forecourt and your back yard a rearcourt, and— if you build and plant it formally — ^its name will be correct, whether your house be a formal one or not.

To some people, the term front yard is not alluring, because it seems too suggestive of uncared-for bareness. That picture is easily repainted, however, by adding the word garden and making the ground illustrate the addition.

An entrance enclosure that is ideally suitable to almost every type of house, large or small, has either a carpet of grass with a straight path from the center of the front steps to the pte or archway in the opposite fence or wall, or else ^ ^ flagstones or bricks, into openings between

which are set a few small trees or balanced flower-beds edged with box. If a path leads directly from the front door to the road or street, remember that any sort of walk set low and

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 477

flower-edged has inevitable charm, whereas a paving that is high and hard and bare has none.

THE QUICKEST PLANTING THAT GROWS

Where a decorative background is urgently needed, nothing so quickly gives an old cypress-grove effect as undipped California privet. Flowers can, moreover, be made to thrive close against its roots if you use the following method: Dig a narrow trench four feet deep, lining the entire depth of it on the front side with sheets of tin or zinc. Pack the bottom of this trench with about tVo feet of dressing, and on top of this a layer of loam deep enough to plant the privet in. On the front of the zinc make the flower-bed two and a half or three feet deep. Perhaps you have sometime wondered how perennial flowers can be made to thrive directly next to greedy shrub or vine roots. This is one way how!

In our southern latitudes the immediate decoration of a brand-new house is easily accomplished. Plant a little twig one day in California, and the next it is — a bush. But in our northern States, the hurry-up gardener’s best (least costly) aids in time of bareness are poplar trees and privet bushes — the latter bought in the tallest individual plants possible, and treated as specimen shrubs. Average poplars bought of nurserymen are neither expensive nor difiicult to transplant, even, when they are twelve or fourteen feet high; and they contribute an immediate effect. The little ones shown in Plate 1 1 were only six feet high and cost fifty cents each. We had to do with these, as the season was too far advanced (June 30) to buy bigger ones; but by October they were over eight feet high and quite thick. Two rose vines have grown as high as the pilasters on either side of the door.

I would not know how to contrive the background of the garden or the decoration of a house — if cost must be considered — without privet. Evergreens are more expensive, and take a lifetime to grow. When they are little they are too little, and when they grow they are too big — and they

don’t espalier half as well as privet — and espaliers are my pet trimmings! Privet is within reach of every purse, and it returns kindness with luxuriant growth in any shape and size you have in mind.

Many people think of this easily obtained and thoroughly reliable plant as suitable only to be put into two staggered rows and clipped into a hedge. Wliat I like to do with it is to encourage it to grow into enormous specimen shrubs not unsuggestive of the cypresses of Italy. I know very well that the typical head-gardener looks upon privet as something akin to dandelions on the lawn. But if you have just an ordinary garden, or a little yard, no other shrub or tree will produce so much beauty in so short a time and at so very little cost.

The florists recommend a number of quick-growing vines, but my own best luck has ever been with Clematis paniculata and with the Dr. Van Fleet or Silver-moon rose. Feed the latter a patent pellet called "stim-u-plant,” cultivate it assiduously, nearly drown it with water, and its dark glossy foliage will go over a second-story roof in three years — if it likes the exposure! In Plate 56 the rose at the left-hand corner of the house had been of ordinary seedsman-catalogue size three autumns and two summers before. The bush hidden by the tree is made of one Emily Gray and one Dr. Van Fleet grown on a trellis; these also are two summers old. The Van Fleet near my own front door (Plate 2) was just beginning its second summer.

For the decoration of the doorway of a town house that is flat on the street, and that has a cellar extending under the sidewalk, as it does in most cities, little embellishment is possible except the ubiquitous clipped plants in tubs on either side of the front steps, or two matching trees (or a row of them) set into the curb edge of the sidewalk. But if earth comes close to the foundation, any variety of vine is suitable. Grapevines and fruit-trees are unusually hospitable rather too much so for a public street! But in a sheltered front yard or in a walled-in garden at the back or side of

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 479

the house, a grapevine such as the one in Plate 15 is very decorative. It is hard to imagine a more beautifying decoration than espaliered fruit-trees of dwarf varieties. Plums, pears, cherries — even apple trees — set out when they are young and bendable, either carefully branched in candelabra form, or merely tied flat to the walls in whatever shape the branches best take, provide floral decoration in the spring, foliage decoration throughout the summer, and more than merely decoration in the autumn. The beauty of fruit-trees against a house (though these are neither dwarfed nor espaliered') is shown in full enchantment in Plate 58.

A lean-to arbor of grapes against a side-wall, or a trellis over an end of a garden enclosure, is too well known to suggest. As a last word of advice on the subject of exterior embellishment, it will do no harm to repeat: The house of ugly outline, or with faults of detail that cannot be helped, should be covered to the utmost. But much worse than leaving the faults of a bad house glaringly in view, is smothering an outstanding example of architectural perfection. Failing to mitigate ugliness is merely failing to make something out of nothing, and can be excused; but to obliterate a work of beauty is unforgivable.

BAY-WINDOWS OR CASEMENT-WINDOWS

Decorating any bay-window is effectually accomplished by setting an evergreen at as great a distance on either side as the other windows of the facade allow. If the space is not wide, choose trees that are very narrow. For a summer-house, Lombardy poplar trees or privet tied in pyramidal form would give an excellent effect. Then in the wall space on either side of the window, and between the flanking trees, plant ivy and rose-vines, or a wistaria on one side, and on the other, ivy with a climbing everlasting pea or clematis or honeysuckle — whatever you prefer. These are to be trained up the walls and over the window — eventually. In front of them, plant low shrubs or a perennial border.

A bay-window set in under the overhang of a second story has trimming possibilities that are somewhat greater than one that stands out beyond the house, because the recess on either side of the bay-window makes a stage-setting for

the garden close to the house

planting that has an intimacy of charm not easily attained by a roofless setting.

The exhibit of the Garden Club at Rye at the last New York Flower Show seemed impossible to excel. Pots of spring flowers stood on the window-ledge m-doors and added to the decoration of the out-doors. (Fig. 158.)

IS THERE DANGER IN EYE-CATCHING PRETTINESS?

Because the little house that verges on the picturesque is so easily made colorfully pretty, the thoughtless owner some-

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 481

times overdoes this prettiness. Sometimes only, and even then, only perhaps! But, since the countryside is peppered with Ye Old Kettle Tea-Room and Martha Washington Gift- Shop, too many small tin-awning effects and gaily painted window-boxes filled with gaily blooming flowers. Punch and Judy window- trimmings, and other attention-attracting spots of quaintness, are perhaps quite as apt to suggest commercial enterprise as the inviting atmosphere of home. This is not meant as disapproval of painted tin awnings or brightly striped window-boxes, or china cats on the roof; neither is it meant as a criticism of overabundant wrought-iron ornaments, or window- awnings that are flat on top like the tester of a four-post bed canopy, and that have pots of plants on top of them like a sing-song boat in China; nor yet of turquoise tiling set around the door, or of pot-rings fastened here, there or anywhere; it is meant merely to intimate that these embellishments should be used with regard to their digestibility by the style of the house.

In the courtyard of any simple stucco dwelling, suitable decoration might include a flower bench against the wall with potted plants on it, or potted vines set into rings beside a doorway or along the iron balustrade of stairs or balcony.

Especially becoming to a small Colonial cottage that stands on a low terrace, is a very low picket fence — a foot to a foot and a half high — set on top of a low retaining wall. On the side next the road it is rather better to plant unexacting flowers, such as asters and Shasta daisies, and small-sized hollyhocks, harebells, and native goldenrod. And on the side next the terrace it is well to plant a small annual border. The charm of planting under a low terrace-wall is illustrated in Plate 6.

Wherever rough stone steps go down into a garden, a low pot of flowers on the outer side of each step is always decorative. An old tree that has died, and whose note of decoration is needed, makes a good trellis for climbing hydrangea, ivy, or any other vine.

THE PERENNIAL FRIENDSHIP GARDEN

A friendship picking garden is an unqualified delight, because it can be a mixture if you wish, and because picking bouquets of these or those flowers is a perennially happy reminder of these or those friends. But planting friendship flowers in a border that is part of a definite color scheme is not very practical, unless your friends have an eye for color that is identical with your own; and to have a devoted neighbor rob her own garden to give you plants that shriek at your own plants and devastate your scheme of decoration, is about as unhappy a garden situation as can be found.

AN INSET OF WATER

Whether it be a building as perfect as the Taj Mahal or the latest little picture-cottage, nothing is ever quite so becoming to a building — or a garden — as its reflection mirrored in water. In composing an imaginary picture of an enchanting garden, one instinctively includes beautiful trees overhead, stone paving underfoot, and flower-pot-garnished steps going down to a little flower-bordered pool; and for those who have no fear of a wet cellar, or of babies tumbling into the water, or of mosquitoes being born by the million every hour, the pool that is brought level with and next to the house, so that its border of flowers is literally at one’s feet, lends itself to unending possibilities. If the water be shallow enough, the babies cannot drown; and the embryo mosquitoes can be thoroughly discounted by serving them as meals for goldfish or by turning a hose into every hide-away crevice for a few minutes morning and evening.

A swimming-pool brought up to the north wall of a massive stone house, reflecting its shadowed somberness, has neither the decorative quality nor the allure of a swimmingpool against a light-colored, flower- trimmed house with the sunshine upon it. A fair-sized pool with planting behind it and a walk around it is practical even for the town or subur-

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 483

ban backyard — if the neighborhood water supply is sufficient to give you a pool permit. It is rather disconcerting to put in a pool and then find out that a water permit is limited to a

THE SWIMMING POOL AS A DECORATION OF THE HOUSE

few gallons per family daily, and be forced to fill the hole with earth again! An ideal swimming pool — one as embellishing to the house as it is convenient for the members of the family who swim in it — is shown in Figure 159. In short, a more delectable or inviting close-to-the-house garden would be hard to devise.

THE OLD-FASHIONED FLOWER-GARDEN

For a small, low cottage, especially, but also for almost any type of American country or suburban house, no decoration is more friendly and gay and inviting than perennial flower-borders planted as close as possible to the front door, the side door, or, best of all, the rear door.

Our own very adorable, white-painted, green-shuttered house on the seacoast of Massachusetts, which has had much said about it in other chapters, is literally set in the middle of a flower-bed, being surrounded by perennial flower-borders on all sides — rows and rows of them! They are spread in six-foot widths in front of all the enclosing white-palinged fences; in three-foot or four-foot widths around all the walls of the house; in three-foot width on each side of the brick walk from gate to front door. Nor is this all. On the livingroom side of the house, separated from the six-foot fenceborders by five-foot walks of grass, is a three-sided inner border in which dwarf fruit-trees are spaced at twenty-foot distances with tulips, iris, and gladiolas in bands between.

As soon as the tulips come up and the iris leaves declare their whereabouts, a first lot of gladiola bulbs is planted in the empty spaces. Later, when the tulips begin to wither, they are pulled up and heeled-in elsewhere for bulb ripening, and a second lot of gladiolas in groups of early and lateblooming varieties takes their place in the fruit-tree borders.

In this way, with very little trouble and almost no attention, our little house (not so very little, as it spreads itself out fairly far) is in much the same position as a prompter’s box in the center of a stage-scene set solidly with flowers. The decorative restrictions to a rather definite color scheme keep the somewhat exuberant bloom from looking like a spilledover paint-box. In spring, there are pink and white fruitblossoms, lilacs (both white and purple), and tulips in deep pink, flesh pink, light purple and deep purple; also white and lemon-yellow, but no brick or bronze or scarlet or mixtures. Later, long borders of peonies are white, cream,

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 48 5

and every shade of pink and rose (not a single red) . The tulips in the tree-borders have turned into violet, pale blue, and pale yellow iris. In the fence-borders are foxgloves, lupine, columbines, and pale yellow day lilies, supplemented later on by delphiniums, Newport-pink sweet-williams, deep purple petunias, heliotrope ten-weeks’ stocks, and lemoncolored snapdragon.

Still later, these beds are dominated by phlox, lilies, and dahlias, the last-named planted somewhat like a rainbow in this order: Riverton’s snow, rosebud, Jersey’s beauty; Rodman Wanamaker, Wizard of Oz, Judge Moreau, Mayor Fitz, U. S. A., and Waldheim sunshine — against the end of the yellow garden of a neighbor. Along all the walls of the house facing these borders are yellow hollyhocks, delphinium, platycodon, snapdragon and heliotrope. Roses are by themselves — over the dry wells of the septic tank. (This is the one nobly shared secret in the book!) The front-path beds are massed successively with iris, delphinium, canterbury bells, gladiolas and phlox, bordered by mignonette, heliotrope and snapdragon — replaced later with pastel-colored zinnias. These last are flowers that, having started to bloom in a cold frame, may be chosen with certainty as to color.

Before leaving my own flower-borders, I want to tell brides or other novice gardeners who really care for color that it is not safe to leave last year’s hollyhocks in situations where a definite color is of great importance. Every autumn I dig up all the hollyhocks against the front of the house and replace them with young plants that I have grown from bought seed. Otherwise their double yellow faces are as likely as not to turn maroon or black or red — a situation more easily forestalled than corrected after the first bloom breaks.

Perhaps all this seems too much ado about color for the average house-owner to be bothered with; but it is really not a bit more troublesome to decide on whichever color scheme you happen to like, and to grow those things only which fit into it. In my own garden I have not a single flower that verges on scarlet, sage-red, golden-glow, crimson, or magenta.

One whose taste runs, on the contrary, to the magentas, orchids, and red-purple, or to the brilliant reds, oranges, yellows, chooses seeds or plants accordingly, of course!

PICKET-FENCE CAMOUFLAGE

One problem of this seaside garden I solved after a fashion that might be useful to others who struggle to display spring flowers against the zebra striping of a white picket fence. Each spring the windows of my living-room looked out on a low green dado with dazzling perpendicular strips above it — ■ nothing else! If I went outdoors close enough to the fence, there were lupines, columbines, iris, day lilies in profusion, but a hundred feet away the stripes camouflaged all color of bloom completely. Finally, in desperation, I took a pail of leaf-green paint and a pail of Nile-green paint, two old sponges wired to the ends of flower-stakes, and a pointed brush. Dipping the sponges alternately into the light and the dark, I made big splashes of green behind each clump of bloom. Back of the iris and lilies I painted (very roughly) a thicket of long-pointed leaves. Returning to the house, I looked out upon a garden that had suddenly burst into glorious masses of bloom! (The excitement in the neighborhood was intense, even after the method of my showmanship had been discovered.)

As a background-fence, nothing equals the split chestnut palings imported from France. I put these across the back of the garden of the neighbor’s house described in Chapter VI, and also in other gardens.

In considering the subject of flower-borders one cannot very well omit the garden in monochromes of color throughout each succeeding month. In May, for instance, it is entirely white. In June it changes overnight into pink. In July, It IS entirely blue. In August, it is red, and in Septemberyellow. Any other sequence, of course, may be chosen. To have these sudden transformations of display in perpetually perfect bloom, however, is scarcely to be considered without

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION pF THE HOUSE 487

the aid of a staff of gardeners and unlimited footage of nursery-beds, both glassed-over and open.

THE GARDEN BROUGHT CLOSE TO THE HOUSE

Sometimes this is a door-yard flower-garden, sometimes it is a small decorated terrace, sometimes it is walled in like a patio, sometimes it surrounds a veranda or a small balcony, sometimes it is spread in front of the doors and windows of the house. The garden’s type naturally depends upon the lie of the land — whether it be built on flat, sandy soil or not; whether it be in the woods or overlooking a view; whether the landscape be open or shut in; whether there be water and rocks or not. It also depends somewhat — though not very much — upon the architecture it is placed against.

But the supreme advantage of such a garden is that it^ is an actual part of the house, and neither a subject for its owner’s display nor the objective of an enforced walk, not to say excursion. The most marvelous garden that can be seen only by wandering about in sun or wind, heat or cold, enduring the annoyance of mosquitoes or the perils of snakes, real or imaginary, is scarcely to be compared, in the matter of delight, with the garden that is brought so close to the house as to become a part of it. The real enchantment of the latter is that one may be continuously in the midst of it, whether one be sitting on the veranda or on the terrace, or merely at a window. M^oreover, the beauty that it contributes to one’s daily life is an asset quite apart from any enhancing exterior decoration that it may bestow.

IN CITY BACK YARDS

A little town back yard, twenty-odd feet square or less, can be made beautiful and useful in many ways, both sirnple and otherwise. That even a formal garden is possible in a back yard that is less than eighteen feet wide is proved in Plate 60, The apparent arcade at the rear is actually a flat

fence, upon which an architectural design of moldings, pilasters, and columns has been applied. The actual vines trained against the two small columns, seemingly midway down a long gallery, add astonishingly to the perspective illusion.

•^^nch simpler and more informal garden is made possible by merely painting the typical high wooden fence shadowy green and planting vines or espaliered shrubs or trees against It. Or if the walls be of plain brick or stone, cut down-slanting notches or pockets here and there, pack with earth, and fill with rock plants.

Perhaps it rnight be well to describe another city backyard garden which serves as an outdoor breakfast-room and sitting-room through the spring, and again later in the autumn. The space is twenty-five feet wide by a little more than forty feet long. The dining-room of the house opens on an awnmged terrace twelve feet square. The fence and the walls are painted green and are decorated with California privet trained flat as possible. Across the center of the back boundary is a semicircular wall of cypress, and in front of it is a small pool surrounded with ferns and grasses. Connecting the terrace with the pool is a white-pebbled path. On either side of the path are long narrow flower- borders edged with box and planted with jonquils, cottage tulips, violas snapdragons and petunias, supplemented later by zinnias’ pompon dahlias, anemones, and asters. Between the flowers’ and the privet are two plain panels of grass.

Although patios are properly adapted to Spanish houses

garden-rooms that are enclosed d roofed oyer with glass during the cold months of the year are less of an extravagance, compared with the possibilities of use and decoration, than many country dwellers would seem to be aware of. (For Patios, see Chapter XIII.)

the garden^s adornment of the house

In the country, where beauty of prospect is unlimited garden embellishment ranges from perfection such as that

THE GARDEN’S DECORATION OF THE HOUSE 489

shown in Plates 59 and 61 to the little pathway planting shown in Plate 51. The photograph for Plate 61 was taken from the doorway of the living-room, the view from which is merely one of many seen from other doors and windows of the house. Unfortunately, no photograph — without color — can more than faintly suggest the fairy-tale enchantment of woodland vistas and the colorful harmonies of succeeding flowers.

That a garden such as this demands expert and unremitting care is obvious. And yet one who is unversed in the art of gardening might quite easily suppose that it "just grew like that,” since its seeming naturalness is one of the beautiful things about it. But that expert gardening is indispensable to the building and upkeep of an entrancing garden spot to look out upon and to live in, is not true at all. Any angle of the house will supply the situation, with background and foreground decoration of hardy vines and shrubs, and with moss left undisturbed between the flagstones. Undemanding perennials (there are many such), coming into bloom in clumps, first here and then there, need no care whatever beyond a little food, very little weeding, and only occasional, but very deep, drinks of water.

A small trough, pool, or unpretentiously trickling brook — made by setting a spigot at the top of a rocky runway and even lovely sequences of color in near-by or surrounding borders, are within reach of each of us who will merely care enough to acquire a little knowledge, expend a little thought, and devote a little — but regular — time to their care.

XXII

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

T’^HE Moderns talk and write as though their revolution — their convention, their iconoclasm, their reconstruction, their return to the Stone Age, their claim of a new dimension — were as simple and as consistent as three plus three and two plus four. But to an onlooker — even a most interested onlooker — the contradictions of Modern design are more than confusing.

The gay and amusing impression is of tinsel and silver and gilt, of Christmas-tree ornament, the mirrors of Narcissus, the glass of perfume bottles, the gleaming surface of chromium steel, the flares of a thousand and one lights, toys, jazz color, glittering baguettes, and zebra skins. But then we look again, and it has all become so death-like. We feel the subconscious aftermath of the tragedy of the Great War translated into the rounded edges of highly polished, massively plain rectangles of ebon darkness, suggestive of cofiins— even to the silver handles. Thick slabs of polished stone; draperies of dull white silk or chiffon, pleat on pleat; beams of light directed heavenward from high braziershaped lamps of alabaster repeat the suggestion of cemetery monuments and mortuary chambers.

A third example quite as clearly expresses an asylum for the insane. A theme of bars and thickly padded corners, devised so that the patients can neither escape nor hurt themselves. Chairs and tables, either built-in or so heavy that not even the strength of the demented could lift and swing them.

Fourth we note the building-block theme of every nursery. Tell a baby to build a chair or a table or a bed,

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

and what mmt he build — for want of building material — but the chunks of thickness that are an outstanding claim of Modernistic origin?

Fifth, there are the motives furnished by machine shops and boiler factories. Steel tubing bent in a continuous squared loop to create legless chairs; boilers sliced through lengthways and stood in corners as shields for columns of light; factory whistles projecting from ceiling beams. We also recognize the old familiar outline of the sewing machine, the ice-box, the kitchen cabinet, the butcher’s meat block, the packing crate, as well as the more lately added electric dish-washer.

Sixth, we recognize the bare clean whiteness, the glass and monel metal of a hospital operating room — the patients obviously suggested by the abortive shapes of man, bird or beast.

Of course, we wonder: do any of the interpreters of these different moods believe they have achieved a finished art capable of bringing satisfaction and repose to the mature mind, to the fastidious taste? We wonder also whether the too exaggeratedly solid and rigidly geometrical outlines might not be applied camouflage of masculine strength, of scientific severity, to hide the weakness of a certain effeminate effeteness — although most of it is merely awkward and commonplace. Only in rare examples is it beautiful; and more rarely still can it by widest stretch of imagination be called homelike.

At all events, the estimate of Modern style is rather a hopeless puzzle to solve — unless we might perhaps liken the problem to a dog show at which terriers, spaniels, wolfhounds, Pekingese and Great Danes might be known by no other term than DOG.

One way to separate the much that is bad from the little (as yet) that is good, and to find out moreover what it is all about, might be to put aside these conflicting expressions and begin by separating the motives of design according to intention.

IDEALISTIC MODERNS

In the first and highest class, let us acknowledge the achievements of the idealistic Moderns. These comprise the few who combine sanity with traditional principles in their application of Modernism to practical present-day needs — the few who are accenting the use of new materials and wall treatment, new ornamental accessories, but who are clinging rather closely to established precepts in the matter of furniture outlines. It is these idealists who are producing much of interest and of beauty.

But amongst these are a certain few of those whose quest for newness is fanatical, and who therefore face a problem seemingly on a par with that of the alchemists who sought the elixir of youth — since, according to their creed, "that alone is Modern which has never been expressed before.” Obviously they have far to seek for that which is utterly new. The built-in idea is not new. Cupboards, tiers of drawers, bunks, overmantels, inglenooks, sideboards, are as commonplace in old houses as feed-bins in a stable, or the shelves and counters in a grocery store. We have had many built-in periods that produced a high percentage of ugliness. Georgian, French, and Colonial cupboards and bookcases were beautiful quite apart from the fact that they were built-in.

Indirect lighting is not new. Bands of electric lights were hidden above the cornice and thrown upon the gold-leaf ceilings of hundreds of brown and gold and beige libraries of the newly rich of thirty years ago — long before the Modern mode was born in Austria.

The Egyptians used metal for furniture; so did the Greeks and Romans though it must be acknowledged that gas-pipe as embellishment has no prototype. The brandnewest Modern pilaster without capital or base was typical of Tel-el-Amarna, where Tut-ankh-Amen spent his boyhood, and where the architecture was in fact identical with to-day s block-shaped, roof-balconied houses. There were

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

the same square, wide window openings, though glassless, according to Mr. Seton Lloyd, of the Egypt Exploration Society. Flutes and reeding — also horizontal banding of the shaft — go back to ancient Rome. Even the step-back block forms find their prototypes in the Pyramids.

Mirrors, white, gold or black, engraved or painted, are far from new. The Chinese motif dominates much of the best Modern design — and the art of China is not new!

Even the outstandingly characteristic geometrical cubes, cones, prisms, pyramids, parallelopipeds and cylinders of which all furniture and lighting fixtures are constructed, or the decorations drawn in circles, rectangles or triangles — each and all as impersonally expressed and as bleakly set forth as upon the blackboard of a class-room — are not unlike the furniture of the circus ring and the decoration and pseudo-scientific equipment of the astrologer’s tent of the side show.

All of which is why — except in the hands of those few who have invincible taste — the search for that which is new results in so much that is deformity. It can’t be anything else!

Houses must have floors, ceilings, and walls. One can’t walk on the ceiling or on the side-walls. There is nothing to do but put the floor on the bottom, the ceiling at the top, and stand the walls vertically between. Of course, builders can (and do) bend the walls in and out like screens, leaving hollow (waste) spaces which are punctured with shelves in various staggered, flight-of -stairs, or checker-board designs. They also sink or raise portions of the floor, and practise a hundred varieties of set-back slat work, or cut-through patterns, on the ceilings. There are many more possibilities for new expression in interior architecture than in furniture. New decorative material, for example, is to be found in steel girders and sheets of bent glass. There are also hundreds of strange rare — and most beautiful — woods with equally strange names, new metals, new kinds of composition stone, new textiles, new forms and surfaces of glass. But to attempt

structural novelty is very much the same as attempting to alter the human face and put the eyes beneath the mouth or the nose on the back of the head.

THE DISTORTIONISTS

In a second and quite a separate class, therefore, let us range the distortionists — those who, lacking the skill to create new beauty, or even to recognize it should they happen upon it by accident, simply deform beyond recognition whatever objects come to hand. It is these (unfortunately, they number the great majority) who give to Modern design its examples of illiterate and decadent vulgarity. Everything they make is overstuffed or underspindled, queer in shape — in short, freak.

THE EGO-MODERNISTS

These comprise the third and most diverting class, since they seek only to express themselves — ^nothing else in the world! If you are an Ego-Modernist, you simply interpret yourself either as you are or as you would prefer to be. Moreover, you use whichever theme produced by whichever material in whichever combination strikes your personal fancy, and put them together by whichever type of craftsmanship appeals to you. You are equally free to choose an adaptation of the machine-shop, the barn-yard, the cemetery, the circus or the zoo.

In other words, if a spotted cow chewing her cud at the edge of a pond be the dominating thought in mind when you think of "home,” you simply have your walls decorated m whichever way best interprets a sylvan scene. Perhaps you choose various layers of green and gray and brown hangings. Or perhaps you choose frescoes, or overlapped slices of green or silver wood, or glass, or metal. Perhaps you dig out a hollow in the concrete floor for an inset of water, lay down a deep-pile grass-green carpet, upholster hassocks, couches.

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

or camp stools with calico calf-skin, and set a thousandwatt sunburst in the blue-domed ceiling.

It is not easy to decide whether the Distortionists or the Ego-Modernists are those who have been impelled to play good fairy to the hippopotamus at the zoo, by building furniture just the right size for him to sit on, and embellishing everything possible with the bars that once formed his cage — plated over with chromium steel.

All this, I grant you, may be somewhat of an exaggeration, but it is much less so than you might believe. The pond was dug in a floor in Germany. The menagerie bars and furniture of hippopotamus size are everywhere, and ideas quite as grotesquely scenic are carried out with all enthusiasm.

WHERE MODERN FURNITURE FAILS

Considering Modern design solely as it applies to the home-loving sentiments of most of us, however, it does not fit in with the traditions that many of us are hampered by. One value of old furniture is that quite inevitably it imparts the quality of ancestry; one fault of the Modern interior is that quite as inevitably it flaunts the fact that it has none. In fact, so lacking is the effect of family descent that a thoroughbred cat or dog on the hearth of a Modern room is out of key. Cross a cheeta with a Manx cat and the kitten would belong on the Modern hearth. But never a blue-ribbon winner of registered pedigree.

Perhaps many of us care nothing for inherited traditions. Very well! And yet — without any, what are we? Nameless, familyless — born in the foundling asylum?

Furniture that is uncomfortable even to look at; an arm-chair that imprisons your arms at shoulder height, or one with a back six inches high; a writing-table with receding drawers, those next to the floor being a foot farther back than the top edge of the table; a knee-hole desk in which the hole is an inch or so lower than your knees; steel-framed furniture for which mechanical necessity, such as that of the

two extra seats in a motor car, is its sole excuse — these do not add the charm of comfort either in fact or in appearance.

As for the Modern pet sofa: Was there ever a greater snare devised than a box-couch of the ’80s with loose bookends or what-not stands for arms, and unattached cushions propped against the wall? The other day an athletic man of weight threw himself down upon one of these contraptions. The cushions, pushed by an uncontrolled elbow, slithered sideways against an end shelf, and with all the whatnots went crashing on the floor! Infinitely better the honesty of a wooden bench that makes no pretense to comfort than fake luxury amidst crashable crockery. And yet that a bookend sofa can be ideal is proved in Plate 28. The sofa — a real one — is set between shelves in a distinctly Modern manner that is ideally space-saving, comfortable, and practical. The room is not Modern except in the built-in book-case ends and the checker-board wall covering; but it is an excellent suggestion for Modern arrangement.

To many of us. Home is something more than an experimental laboratory or a parade-ground for the eccentricities of Fashion. One of the interesting problems, therefore, is to devise the adjustment of our personal possessions — those that are an integral part of ourselves— with whatever we are able to select that will harmonize with them. The selected things may be Modern, but first of all they must be comfortable and beautiful.

It is only fair to grant that the Modernists are improving. Any one who saw the Viennese decorations of a decade ago, or who saw the Paris Salon des Artistes Decorateurs in 1925, and again in 1929, is aware that the typically crowded and oversized restlessness of design at the earlier date — suggesting an insane copyist of William Morris who had just taken a dose of Pans green— has given way in many instances to walls of plain surface, or to restrained decoration that is distinctly pleasing. Moreover there are several uses which the modern style admirably serves.

A MODERN ROOM IN BLACK AND WHITE AND SILVER. (EVENING LIGHT)

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

THE POSSIBLE VALUE OF A MODERN ACCENT

Turning to another aspect of the Modern idea, let us ask: How practical is it? For whom, and for what, and for where? The answer, I think, is that its outstanding and unquestionable value at the moment is for advertising. Modern design — especially in its jazz manifestation — is the best possible advertising medium because it attracts attention. A show window, a display room, a new restaurant that makes no pretense to Modern interpretation has little chance to keep its prices at the top. The shop of unrivaled preeminence alone excepted, the very smart shop must be Modern; otherwise its offerings seem out of date.

Modern style also perhaps attaches a stamp of eligibility to the houses of younger persons whose eligibility is rather dependent upon their following the fashions of the moment. The value of Modern style is found somewhat paradoxically at the two extremes. It is a medium of amusement for the very rich, and it is equally a medium of practical value to the fairly poor. Its latter aspect will be explained at some length. But let us first consider it as a means of mere diversion:

If you are so very very rich that decorating an entire house is of such trifling importance that it may be discarded without a second thought should it prove a failure, or should you happen to grow tired of its success, nothing could be perhaps more tempting than to express your own creative fancy, or to commission whichever modernist you may choose, to express his own. Perhaps you might even send to London for Curtis Moffat or Basil lonides, or to Paris for Ruhlmann or Jean Michael Frank. Frank’s interiors come nearer than those of most of the other Moderns, to distinction. But the cost of a room by him is fantastic! You can figure it for yourself: Shagreen or parchment or split straw marquetry, or especially precious marbles, or some other equally valuable substance on the walls; ivory used in quantity; rare tropical woods, fancy slipper leathers, gold, chro-

mium, copper, glass, and fabrics of his own design. The occasional fault of his furnishings — a serious one — is unsuitability to utility. White leather and pale beige suede are not durable chair and sofa coverings — unless they go into hermetically sealed rooms that may be looked at through show windows but never entered. This objection, however, is easily overcome by selecting equally precious but less perishable surfaces. His rooms are worth noting because no Modern designer outranks him, and he never produces freaks. He achieves modernistic novelty by the use of low screens placed as though they were part of the framework at the ends of a conventional and comfortable sofa. His furniture is all comfortable and invitingly placed.

Other types of Modern rooms that have walls decorated with silver, inset with glass, or adorned with bas-reliefs, and that have furniture made of materials of fabulous value, are equally costly. A few are beautiful, many are not. As in every style, persons of taste select beauty; persons of no taste select the contrary. And perhaps possibilities of ugliness rnerely seem greater in the Modern style because our attention is focused upon it. As already noted, its two outstanding expressions are undoubtedly at the two extremes of fantastic cost and no cost at all. The whole middle stratum is just fat and shiny and vulgar — and moreover very expensive.

But the opportunities that the Modern style offers for the simplest type of house are very appealing to the imagination.

LET us SUPPOSE THAT WE BUILD

In short, it seems to me that the Modern style is even more adaptable than Cottage Colonial to the production of an inviting effect and of satisfying comfort at very moderate expense. So let us suppose that we are building a Modern house at reasonable cost from the ground up. Completely Modern house-building is of proportionately higher cost than furnishing, because it is out of key with frame con-

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

struction, but it can be built as cheaply as any concrete or stucco house.

Modern style in America has been confined thus far almost entirely to interior decoration and furnishing. But if we care to build a Modern house, it should not be hard to make it presentable if we design it with a simple fagade of stucco, and with set-back stories at whose edges are balustrades or parapets instead of the inevitable rows of iron piping. On the street side it could be sufficiently conventional with windows of normal size and a very simple door — flat and square, or arched (not an igloo entrance!). Then either behind walls that continue the street fagade, or only toward the garden at the back, we could build whatever window spaces might be necessary to the architectural interior.

The set-back upper floors that provide balconies on the roofs of the lower rooms are very attractive, but it must be noted that they add much to cost because foundations are more expensive to<build than superstructure. Another disadvantage of every flat roof is that every room directly beneath it is unendurably hot in hot weather, and cold in cold.

Building the garage as part of the house is convenient and practical as thousands of American houses have proved. But building the whole house in the likeness of a public garage or gas station, as is the obvious intention of the Modern architects abroad, seems rather overdoing it.

The best features of the Modern style include light and air and space and frankness. Nothing is made to look like anything else; steel is steel, wood is wood, plaster is plaster, and windows — being intended to flood the rooms with sunlight — are literally unlimited in size. Sometimes they run in a continuous band the length of a wall and bend around a corner. Or perhaps they go from ceiling to floor — so that with the curtains pulled back the effect is of a wall of glass, and the ground outdoors becomes a continuation of the floor within.

The room walls are as often as not left absolutely blank and without other trim than a narrow band of quarterround used as a baseboard. Or the walls and ceilings and doors and their frames are embellished in countless ways — in the old fashion, the new fashion, every fashion that permits the effect to be kept flat. Paintings or scenic papers that are in perspective do not belong in any modernistic theme.

Modern wall-papers are especially interesting. Some of them are very fragile in design and quiet in color, so that they look well over large spaces; a few are too strong, except to use as you would use a few widths or a panel of brocade. In this case the paper is cut off at a suitable point, and the rest of the space is painted. Such panels — really just flat spaces — are decorated as fragments, leaving other spaces beyond or in between, painted to match the ground of the decoration. (They are very irritating when broken into strips by use of different-colored grounds.) Gold or silver teapapered walls that are then decorated with flat but colorful motifs, or with fragments cut out of wall-paper or shadow silhouettes — any of these very sparsely applied — are, to say the least, tempting.

When the walls are to be left plain, most beautiful and restful are the lovely indefinite colors of dawn gray, moonlight blue, mist-over-the-sea green, gray twilight purple — colors that are made by glazing two or three depths of grays and greens, blues and purples, one over the other — matching curtains are made by hanging two or three layers of chiffon one over the other. The result of this color over color can be wonderfully soft and quiet. The value of the quietness is lost, however, if there be restless furniture or floor covering.

BLACK, WHITE AND SILVER

At the moment, in New York, most of the best rooms are in black and white and silver, with ornaments of steel, black glass, white glass, and mirrors. This is the coloring of

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

the room illustrated in Plates 62 and 63. The walls are oyster gray, and the mural decorations are in silver.

The problem of this room was to make something of a space created by throwing two ground-floor rooms into one, which resulted in lack of architectural balance and proportion. The theory of the decoration was that it should be done very freely, like a sketch, always preserving the flatness of the wall, thus giving a certain height and sparkle and interest to a room in which the ceiling is too low. Characteristic of the Modern treatment, the irregularity of wallspace was emphasized rather than disguised, but the mathematical placing of the furniture was emphasized. Every chair stands precisely opposite or at right angles to every other. Even so, and although the room is used solely as a party room, to receive in before dinner, and to play cards in (the drawing-room and living-room being elsewhere), this room is especially chosen for the illustration of this chapter because it has a quality of restfulness and of distinction which most Modern rooms lack.

As already noted, the color is kept severely black, silver, and white, so that the people in the room shall give it color and find a becoming background for themselves. The consoles are of chromium steel, as are also the plants and flowerpots standing on them. The lamps on either side of the sofa are of mirror, and all the others are of porcelain or of glass and steel. The bridge chairs are upholstered in zebra skin and the sofa in civet. Two of the big chairs are of silver leather and the others are of black satin. The carpet is black velvet. The mantel, not shown in either of the pictures, is of mirror and black glass. The lambrequins over doors and windows are of mirror. The curtains are of oyster bound with silver.

Another monotone that is more popular in Paris than in New York is from dark brown to cream. (With this, copper or gold is best used with brilliant surface, but sparingly, and steel or silver more sparingly still.) But in the black-and-white key, silver and steel and mirror can scarcely be overdone.

MODERN USE OF COLOR AND LIGHT

A room that appeals to me personally, more than Modern rooms do usually, is one designed by Adelaide de Voo in pale apricot pink with touches of Chinese red, much silver, glass, steel and brown wood. It is indicated in Figure 160. The armchair shown, when pushed next to a reversed duplicate, makes a sofa. Together or apart these chairs have genuinely inviting charm.

In Paris, as well as in Germany, color is much used; sometimes frightfully as in a favorite combination of terra-cotta, grass-green, yellow, and tan. But other combinations are interesting and a few beautiful. Very beautiful are the harmonies of gray-greens, smoke-browns, amethyst, and steel. Interesting and possibly suggestive is a high-ceilinged room with plain oyster-gray walls. The floor is of black and white marble lozenges. The curtains on either side of the vrall-sized windows are of vermilion. One enormous chair is upholstered in brilliant blue velvet. Every other object is gray, silver or black.

Emd Fahrenkamp (Diisseldorf) especially uses single notes of vermilion and cobalt-blue, with silver, cream, and brown. Linoleum, by the way, when used merely as a base or a painted and varnished floor, has tempting possibilities.

Most of the Austrian and German rooms are very light in color and very simple in effect. An exception by Spaeth, in Nuremberg, has walls of elephant’s-breath polished wood with a gram like moire, black carpet, ebony furniture, blue-

PALE PASTEL PINK, LACQUER RED, AND SILVER

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

white ceiling, and palest gray-blue upholstery and curtains, with silver-lined book-shelves.

At the Paris Expositions book-shelves have been an outstanding feature. Instead of having the shelves not more than an inch higher than the books to fit in them, and fitting these books in a solid mass, the French designers invariably line the inside of each shelf with especially beautiful wood or

TYPICAL BOOK. RECESSES

silver or gold lacQuer, and make it almost double the height of the books; and then they fill the shelves perhaps half full. Behind the books are very dim lights, just bright enough to accent the shelf lining.

The lighting of these same shelves or square niches used for ornaments would depend upon the becomingness to the ornament. Glass, porcelain, jade, and other translucent objects have lights set behind them. Others can easily be lighted from above by setting very small flashlight bulbs behind small recesses in the upper front corners. (See Fig. 161.) This drawing also shows an interesting and thoroughly com-

fortable desk by J. Ruhlmann that was exhibited at the 1929 Salon des Artistes Decorateurs in Paris.

Especially suitable to the modern use of books for producing decorative color effects is the idea, already suggested (page 439), of covering your own books with materials that ' accent the color scheme of your room. Any book-binding company will send you samples of cloth or leather for this purpose if you write for what you want. You can, moreover, have stamped labels made for books that are important — or importantly placed.

Other wall decorations include plaster bas-reliefs in the manner of Adam Brothers’ use of Wedgwood plaques. The decoration is made in white plaster and applied to plaster which is colored. Penonally I prefer white insets in white walls and sand insets in sand walls, but this is merely because the white on color is apt to look cheap. Design painted and slightly rubbed off— suggestive of a grown-dim stencil— is very satisfactory, whether of color or painted in silver.

TURNING HANDICAPS INTO ASSETS

One of the most practical decorative expressions of the Moderns is the use made of the steel beams which are eyesores to most of us, and to level which we sacrifice from one to four feet of room space. In a Modern room in a steelframed apartment building, therefore, you need not worry a bit about odd juts and jogs, piers and crossbeams, since

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

they all fit in perfectly with Modern design. A decorator could do no better for you.

The sketch, Figure 162, is of a room exhibited by M. Guillemard at the 1929 Paris Salon des Artistes Decorateurs. It has an excellent suggestion for treatment of the spaces between steel ceiling-beams. Figure 163 was suggested by another interior at the same Salon, by Renee Kinsbourg. This shows an interesting door treatment, both in the door which opens into a screen, and in the very plain structural embellishment of the trim. There is also a suggestion for balancing a too-uneven wall by means of beams of step-back lengths applied as ornament. The objection is, of course, that they serve no structural purpose, though they might be made to do so by being fitted between the uprights and the crossbeams and made to brace the ceiling.

IDEAL FURNITURE FOR THE SLIM PURSE

The writing-table shown in Figure 164, designed for the Byrd Antarctic Expedition, offers unending suggestions for

the personal as well as practical furnishing of the simplest house. A great objection to many of the very expensive examples of Modern furniture is that the rare materials, the ivory inlays and jeweler’s workmanship, gloss over but do not really improve the old sewingmachine outline or the packing-crate designs.

But genuine packingboxes and whatever lengths of wood of whatever sort may have accumulated in the woodshed, fastened together

AS A SCREEN

as ingenuity may find expedient, are as interesting as they are Modern. The tables, sidechairs, stands, bookcases of the Byrd Expedition should be comparatively easy to contrive out of almost anything. The wood must be smoothly planed and sandpapered and painted; decorated, perhaps, or perhaps finished in a solid color, or its various layers of different woods left natural, or painted in contrasting values. At all events, it must be shellacked and sandpapered and shellacked again, so that its lacquered or polished surface may be as fine as possible.

In the drawings below are several suggestions for the making or assembling of adequate furniture at moderate cost. The sofa (Fig. 165) is a box-spring, set upon whatever sort of frame is easiest to construct; and it has box-edged cushions over it. The book-end is screwed to the floor, so

that it cannot slide away or be knocked over. The room is twice as wide as the window and the book-end of the sofa is also used to sit on facing either right or left.

Another suggestion is the desk shown in Figure

166, which could be made by any one. Its modern effect is produced by set-back boards at top and bottom.

The expertly made desk, Figure 167, is the best piece of modern furniture that I happen to know of, despite its waste of space and its too small knee-hole two faults which could easily be overcome by cutting the arch higher or making the desk larger and dovetailing other drawers at sides or back.

Remember that no matter which outline pleases your fancy, upholstered furniture must be comfortable. One tendency to guard against is the making of chairs that take up an inordinate amount of room, but are actually so small they are comfortable to none but fat dwarfs. This dwarf tendency of many designers is curious. We live in an age

that exalts athletics, and we are rather typically a tall, long-legged race; yet all of us except midgets are put to the discomfort of attempting to sit at dining-room tables so low that only by stretching our feet out straight in front of us can we get our knees under the table’s edge. Those of us who can sit flat on the floor with

knees unbent and without any back-support do not mind so much (I can do it for hours on end, so this is not a personal plaint) ; but those who like to bend their knees have no choice but to sit with them thrust against their chins, and sideways to the edge of the table.

Xhe intention of all this over-low furniture is obviously to increase the effect of a room’s size — which it does. But a chair very close to the ground should be rather deep and have a proportionately high and supporting back. Even if you are turning your barn into a Modern room, do not buy unnecessary chair-frame thickness. In other words, be sure that every eighth of an inch adds to the depth and resilience of springs and cushioning. If a chair is so completely restful that you can scarcely keep awake in it, there is an excuse for its overfatness; not otherwise.

Straight chairs and stools such as Figure 168 are among the things to contrive for yourself; otherwise it is better to use conventional ones. But avoid the pipe-legged and steel-strapped varieties if you would give becomingness to a room. Some of the steel-strapped glass tables are attractive, but steel and leather chairs are too suggestive of a dentist’s oflEce for appeal.

One great advantage of the Modern interior’s intentional emptiness, is that those who are furnishing for the first time and have few or no possessions can feel that their home is quite complete with none but the bare essentials. If you, for instance, are a bride and are choosing the Modern style, you need not worry a bit because your bedrooms are without a stick of furniture other than beds and a painted starch box or two! (Provided you have plenty of closet space.) In a Modern house that is literally a palace the owner’s bedroom has a gigantic bed in it, a rug, two miniature stands about SIX inches from the floor, and two glimmering lights

A FIRE-STOOL OF CONSIDERABLE CHARM

THE STYLE WE KNOW. AS MODERN

on them; two cube-shaped hassocks on which to sit, or to lay dressing-gowns, and not another object. Figure 169, for instance, has not an item of furniture except the bed and its accessories.

The essential furnishings of a dining-room comprise a table and some chairs, a rug, perhaps curtains or perhaps not. But for the living-room there must be chairs of ease — the fundamentals of comfort which perfectly serve the purpose for which they are intended. And remember always that things which are strikingly unusual, and therefore challenge attention, must have greater beauty than things which are primarily useful.

In nothing (so it seems to me) has Modern Art been so successful as in glass and steel and pottery, whether for the table or for ornament. Much of the glass is transcendently beautiful, and no word but "beguiling” describes many of the toy animals of chromium steel.

BUILT-IN BED-TABLES AND SLIPPER-STOOL

The outstanding disadvantage of the Modern decorative background is that it is often not in accord with the possessions of the majority of us. And to most of us certain of our possessions are an integral part of ourselves.

Practically everything that is Chinese or derived from ^e Chinese is in accord with typical Modern expression. With ease this can be made to include such furniture as, for instance, Chinese Chippendale. The lacquer cabinet shown in igure 170 (really composed of three objects standing one upon the other) and the window-setting in Figure 171 are unmistakably Chinese. The French designers of best interiors are making special use of Directoire lines. In our own Southwest the rugs and pottery of the Navajo and Pueblo Indians ^e quite m harmony with walls of redwood or adobe, as well as with many of the Modernistic paintings.

novelty in any house of size. If you must, box it in down to the floor (without a valance), then at least decorate it with color or do something to it, no matter what, to make it look unlike a cofl&n or a bathtub.

Solidly built-in furniture with not an inch of space between the solidly packed objects is a sensible arrangement in a room too small to furnish'otherwise! But beautiful it is not. Innovation trunk-compartment effects in furniture, although suitable for staterooms on trains and ocean liners, are scarcely suggestive of the bedroom of a woman of charm and taste. A contradiction that no one has as yet explained, is that although Modern style may not be solely based on novelty, its ballyhoo is novelty. Novelty necessitates constant change. Built-in furniture admits of no change what-

ever! Perhaps the intention of the Modernists is novelty now, but never again?

In every type of room a dressing-table can be made of almost anything — a table of glass or wood with a mirror stood or hung above it. The Modern style despises petticoats; but you might have them, even so, by choosing strictly Modernistic materials. A quite marvelous dressingtable — at least one that greatly appealed to me — ^is made of a semicircular console table of wood and steel, with a mirror top, and set against a screen made of three leaves of steel-bound mirror.

BATHROOMS

In a few of the ultra-modern bathrooms the open plumbing, which for the last thirty years we have imagined to be an improvement on the old plumbing that was shut in, is now shut in again — not with match-boarding of wood, but with flutings or accordion-pleating of chromium steel or with colored marble. Bathrooms, being usually very small, lend themselves to rather extreme decoration, whether Modern or otherwise. (See Index.)

LIGHTING

Whether you find beauty in the dazzling glare of groundglass boxes, cylinders, triangles, and groups of right-angled Peter Hewitt tubes, depends perhaps upon how kindly your eyes take to unshaded glare. In its dimmer phases of lowpowered bulbs, a house equipped only with entrapped glowworm effects is not very satisfactory— unless you want to sit ^d look at the lights, as you may sit and look at the moon. That would be quite different.

But discomfort in lighting, as a matter of fact, is the fault of the buyer who is led astray by novelty. There are innumerable lamps with shades — ^lovely ones, such as those shown in Plate 62. There are plenty of practical chromium

THE STYLE WE KNOW AS MODERN

desk-lamps that can be stood on something high near a chair to read by.

It should be easy enough, for that matter, to think of innumerable devices that would obviate certain quite unnecessary discomforts which those who think only of novelty seem as yet unaware of. The achievement of comfort, convenience, and durability is merely a question of individual thought, ingenuity, and adjustment.

THE FINAL QUESTION IS: IS MODERN ART AN END OR

A BEGINNING?

Between the milder revolutionists, to whom even Modernity’s ugliest decadence appears exactly as the ass’s head appeared to Titania, and the conservatives, to whom the entire Modern idea is without a single merit to bless itself with, lies the unsolved question: Is this a Midsummer Night’s Dream, from which we shall awake to the realization of its deformity? Or is it the dawn of a great Period?

There is no doubt whatever about the ultimate survival of the fit, though what is fit and what is freakish can never be judged until we recognize the head of an ass, or of an Apollo, for what it is. At the moment, we are deep in new visions — or are they realities? In short, what value will remain when novelty grows stale? How much of the present expression of Modern decorative art will become permanent? Who can tell?

New art phases have become epidemic every so often. A few outstanding examples have been added to the ranks of the permanent Periods, but most have passed into the limbo of forgotten things. Within the memory of many now living, Pseudo-Gothic, Eastlake English, the black trim and furniture of the ebony eighties, followed by crewel work and cat-tails, American Mission, the drooping flowerstalk motive of Nouveau Art, have come and gone, leaving not a trace.

But manners and customs and points of view have so

radically changed in the last few years that some expression of this change is inevitable. And it would be both dull and absurd to say that from now on and forevermore, our houses must be everlastingly Tudor, Italian, French, Colonial, or so on. But this, at least, is certain: Whatever style we finally accept or evolve, must pass the fundamental requirements of beauty — exactly as food must pass the requirements of human digestibility. Sawdust is not food. Objects that are deformed, blatant, or inane are not beautiful.

Having throughout several thousand years accepted as permanent nothing less than beauty, it is not likely that we are beginning a permanent esthetic decline that will ultimately accept plaids and cubes and prisms on the walls, plaids and cubes and prisms under foot, a tiger skin flung across a sloppily made up bed, coflSn sideboards, crypts built into the walls, furniture made in the image of vegetables with the legs of insects, or sausages skewered on bent lengths of gas pipe— simply because it has not been done before. Mere novelty created without sincerity of purpose and without consideration of the laws of design, has no more chance of survival than any other flimsy imitation that soon wears through to rusty wire, torn paper, and glue.

It makes not an atom of difference whether a style be that of the past, the present, or the unevolved future; neither does it matter in which of the five continents it had its inception. But fundamental principles have always mattered, and the chances are that they always will matter.

The principles of utility, suitability, form, line, scale, harmony, distinction, and sincerity of purpose, are immutable. Obviously, therefore, the more nearly a theme of decoration or an object of furnishing expresses these principles, the more likely is it to contribute permanently to the

beauty, charm, and distinction— in short, to the personality — of a house.
